<meta>This page begins the author’s preface, following the frontispiece and publication credits. It introduces the historical and cultural synthesis of Indian, Chinese, and Japanese mythology regarding the dragon.</meta>

# PREFACE.

The student of Chinese and Japanese religion and folklore soon discovers the powerful influence of Indian thought upon the Far Eastern mind. Buddhism introduced a great number of Indian concepts and legends—not specifically Buddhist in origin—into Eastern countries, clothed in a Buddhist attire. In China, Taoism was ready to gratefully adopt these foreign elements, which in many ways resembled its own ideas or were of a similar nature. In this way, the collection of ancient Chinese legends was not only greatly enriched, but was also blended with Indian fables. The same process occurred in Japan when Buddhism reached the shores of Dai Nippon (Japan) in the sixth century of our era, after having spread through Korea. Before a hundred years had passed, the Japanese mind became steeped in foreign ideas, partly Chinese and partly Indian. To the mixture of these two elements, a third—consisting of original Japanese concepts—was added, and a very intricate, complex whole was formed. Anyone who studies Japanese legends faces the difficult task of analyzing this complexity into its constituent parts. —

<insert>No mythical creature is more familiar to Far Eastern art and literature</insert> than the dragon. It is interesting to observe how three different kinds of dragons, originating from India, China, and Japan, are found side by side in Japan. To a superficial observer, they all belong to the same class of rain-bestowing water gods who arouse thunder and storms, but a careful examination teaches us that they are distinct from each other.

The Indian serpent-shaped *Nāga* was identified in China with the four-legged Chinese dragon because both were divine inhabitants of seas and rivers and givers of rain. It is no wonder that the Japanese, in this blending of Chinese and Indian ideas, recognized their own serpent- or dragon-shaped gods of rivers and mountains, to whom they used to pray for rain in times of drought. Thus, the ancient legends of three countries were combined, and features of one were used to adorn the others. In order to shed light upon these facts, we must examine the

<meta>Digitized by Microsoft®</meta>

<vocab>Buddhism, Taoism, Far-Eastern, China, Japan, Korea, India, Dai Nippon, Nāga, dragon, folklore, religion</vocab>

<summary>The author explains how the mythology of the dragon in East Asia is a synthesis of Indian, Chinese, and Japanese traditions, primarily spread through the influence of Buddhism and Taoism. He notes that while these various dragon types may appear identical as water deities, they possess distinct origins that require careful analysis.</summary>
<keywords>dragon mythology, Buddhism, Taoism, Nāga, cultural synthesis, Far Eastern folklore, Dai Nippon, Indian influence, religious history</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, detailing the sources and methodology used to analyze the Indian, Chinese, and Japanese influences on dragon mythology, while acknowledging the scholars who assisted in the work.</meta>

# PREFACE.

Buddhist ideas concerning the Nāgas, which came from India to the East. Since we are not acquainted with the Sanskrit language, we have to refer to the works of European scholars and to translations in order to explain the Western elements found in Chinese and Japanese dragon legends. Since this is our only aim regarding the Nāgas, we will deal with them only by way of introduction.

In the First Book, we have systematically arranged the most interesting quotations concerning the dragon in China, selected from the enormous number of passages on this divine animal found in Chinese literature from the remotest ages down to modern times. To present the original concepts, we did not quote the numerous poems about the dragon because the latter, although based upon those concepts, expanded upon them in their own poetic way. The Second Book deals with the dragon in Japan, considered in light of the facts provided in the Introduction and Book I.

I take this opportunity to express my sincere thanks to Professor DE GROOT, whose kind assistance enabled me to greatly expand the Chinese portion of this paper. Not only was his very rich and interesting library at my disposal, but he himself was an invaluable guide to me through the labyrinth of many a difficult Chinese passage. Moreover, from the very beginning, his splendid works—especially the *Religious System of China*—formed the basis of my studies in Chinese and Japanese religion and folklore.

I also offer my best thanks to Professor SPEYER, who with great kindness gave me most valuable information concerning the Nāgas, and to Miss E. SCHMIDT, who kindly put her knowledge and time at my disposal by undertaking the tedious task of reading through the manuscript and correcting its language.

Leiden. &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; &nbsp; M. W. DE VISSER.

<summary>In this concluding part of the preface, the author outlines the book's structure and methodology while acknowledging the contributions of scholars like Professor De Groot and Professor Speyer. The work is divided into a study of Indian Nāgas, Chinese dragon literature, and their combined influence on Japanese mythology.</summary>
<keywords>Nāgas, Sanskrit, dragon legends, China, Japan, J.J.M. de Groot, J.S. Speyer, Religious System of China, methodology, acknowledgments</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

## THE NĀGA IN BUDDHISM, WITH REGARD TO HIS IDENTIFICATION WITH THE CHINESE DRAGON.

### § 1. The Nāga according to European scholars.

To understand Buddhist concepts of the Nāga’s nature, and the reasons why the Chinese identified this serpent with their four-legged dragon, we must consult the works of several authorities on Buddhism: Kern, Hardy, Grünwedel, and others. The Nāga known in the Far East is presented in a *Buddhist form*, and the legends about him that became popular in China and Japan were all influenced by Buddhism. Kern, in his *History of Indian Buddhism* <note>1. *History of Buddhism in India*, Annals of the Guimet Museum, Library of Studies, X and XI, Vol. I, p. 310 (295).</note>, states that the Nāgas occupy the eighth rank in the system of the world, following the Buddhas, Pratyekabuddhas, Arhats, Devas, Brahmas, Gandharvas, and Garuḍas, and preceding the Yakshas, Kumbhāṇḍas (goblins), Asuras (demons), Rākṣasas (giants), Pretas (ghosts or specters), and the inhabitants of hell. “They are water spirits, usually represented in human form with a crown of serpents on their heads.” And in his *Manual of Indian Buddhism* <note>2. P. 59 onwards.</note> we read that they are “snake-like beings that resemble clouds.” Regarding the list of beings, this varies in some other texts, as we learn from a note in the same *Manual* <note>3. P. 60, note 1.</note>. In the opening phrase of all the *Avadānas*, Buddha is said to be worshipped by men, Devas, Nāgas, Yakshas, Asuras, Garuḍas, Kinnaras, and Mahoragas <note>4. LÉON FEER, *Avadāna-çataka*, Annals of the Guimet Museum XVIII, p. 2.</note>. These are, however, not exactly the “Eight classes” frequently mentioned in Chinese and Japanese Buddhist works. Those are Devas, Nāgas, Yakshas, Gandharvas, Asuras, Garuḍas, Kinnaras, and Mahoragas <note>5. The phrase “Devas, Nāgas and (the rest of) the eight classes” (天龍八部) is very often found in Chinese sutras. Edkins (*Chinese Buddhism*, p. 217) says: “Beings inferior to the Devas are called collectively the ‘Eight classes’.” This is a mistake, for, as Eitel (*Sanskrit-Chinese Dictionary* under the word Nāga, p. 103) rightly explains, the</note>

Hardy’s *Manual of Buddhism*<note>1. See Hardy, Manual of Buddhism.</note> gives the following details concerning the Nāgas. “The Nāgas reside in the *loka* (world) under the Trikuta rocks that support Meru, and in the waters of the world of men. They have the shape of the spectacle-snake, with the extended hood (*coluber nāga*); but many actions are attributed to them that can only be done by one possessing the human form. They are demi-gods and have many enjoyments; and they are usually represented as being favorable to Buddha and his adherents; but when their wrath is roused, their opposition is of a formidable character.” With regard to Mount Meru, Hardy says: “The summit is the abode of Sekra (Çakra), the regent or chief of the *dewaloka* called Tawutisa (Trāyastrimçat); and around it are four mansions, 5,000 yojanas in size, inhabited by nāgas, garudas, khumbandas, and yakshas” <note>2. See Hardy's description of the mansions surrounding Meru.</note>. In describing the *dewalokas* he says: “The palace of Virūpāksha is on the west. His

---

Devas also belong to the Eight classes. But according to Eitel, the ancient Chinese phrase speaks of “Nāgas, Devas and (others of) the eight classes (龍天八部). I have never found them listed in this order in the Chinese sutras, for the Devas were always placed before the Nāgas. Moreover, in the *jātakas* and *avadānas*, the Devas always precede the Nāgas in the often-repeated order of beings. In the “Sutra on the Original Vow of the Bodhisattva Kshitigarbha” (Nanjō’s Catalogue, nr 1003, translated from Sanskrit into Chinese at the end of the seventh century), p. 2*b*, the terms 天龍鬼神, “Devas, Nāgas, Demons and Spirits,” and 天龍八部, “Devas, Nāgas, and (the remaining of) the Eight Classes,” are found side by side. I have often found the phrase *Tenryū hachibu* in Japanese works. This is, of course, the logical order, as the Devas are of higher rank in the system of the world than the Nāgas and therefore ought to be mentioned before the latter. The fact that the Devas belong to the eight classes is stated in the *Ta-Ming san-tsang fah shu*, “Numbers (i.e., numerical terms and phrases) of the Law of the Tripitaka, collected under the Great Ming dynasty” (Nanjō, nr 1621), Ch. 33, p. 13 sq., s.v. 八部, where they are listed as Devas, Nāgas, Yakshas, Gandharvas, Asuras, Garuḍas, Kinnaras, and Mahoragas.

There is, however, a second phrase, namely “Men, Devas and (the remaining of) the Eight Classes,” 人天八部, which we find in the *Sūtrālaṃkāra çāstra* (Nanjō, nr 1182, Great Japanese Tripitaka of Leiden, Ch. X, p. 4*a* and *b*), in two passages where the Buddhas Shakyamuni and Maitreya are said to honor Mahākāçyapa “before men, Devas and (the remaining of) the eight classes.” Huber (*Sūtrālaṃkāra*, nr 56, pp. 278 seq.) translates: “The eight classes of Devas,” but the Devas are not divided into eight classes and the character 人 (men) belongs, of course, to the same sentence and not to the preceding one. Men precede Devas when the different beings are listed, and the initial phrase of the Avadānas gives us their names: Men, Devas, Nāgas, Yakshas, Asuras, Garuḍas, Kinnaras, and Mahoragas (cf. also Huber, l.l., pp. 462 seq.; Chavannes, *Cinq cents contes et apologues extraits du Tripiṭaka chinois* (1910), Vol. III, p. 61).

If the former phrase actually is found sometimes in ancient Chinese books in the wrong form given by Edkins, with the Nāgas being placed before the Devas (I think I saw it once also in a Japanese work), this mistake must have arisen from blending the former phrase with the latter, which mentions the Devas in the second place.

attendants are the Nāgas, a *kela-laksha* in number, who wear red garments, hold a sword and shield made of coral, and are mounted on red horses <note>1. Page 24.</note>.

GRÜNWEDEL <note>2. *Mythology of Buddhism in Tibet and Mongolia*, p. 181.</note> states that the attributes of this Virūpāksha—one of the four *lokapālas* or Guardians of the World, also called the "Four Great Kings" (*Caturmahārājas*)—are a *caitya* (a sanctuary) or a jewel in the form of a *caitya* in the right hand, and a serpent in the left hand.

Before Gautama attained Buddhahood, a Nāga king named Kāla became aware of the approaching event by the sound the Bodhisattva's golden vessel produced when it struck against the vessels of the last three Buddhas in Kāla's abode. For they all, like Siddhārtha, had thrown their golden bowls into the river <note>3. KERN, *Manual*, p. 19; *History of Buddhism in India*, Vol. I, p. 70 (64) (there he is called “king of the underworld”).</note>.

As we shall see below, the Nāga king Mucilinda, who lived in the lake of the same name, sheltered the Lord from wind and rain for seven days with his coils and hoods. Indian artists often represented the Buddha sitting under Mucilinda's extended hoods.

However, the Nāga kings were not always so full of reverence toward the Buddha; but in the end, of course, even the most obstinate one was converted. Nandopananda, for example, tried to prevent the Lord's return from the Tushita heaven to the earth, but was conquered by Maudgalyāyana in the form of a Garuda and was then instructed by the Buddha himself <note>4. HARDY, *loc. cit.*, pp. 302 and following.</note>. When the Master had delivered a *sūtra* in one of the heavenly paradises, the Devas and Nāgas came forward and said: "We will henceforth protect the correct doctrine" <note>5. EDKINS, *loc. cit.*, p. 39.</note>. After the Buddha's death, the Nāga kings struggled with the kings of the Devas and eight kings of India to obtain a share of the Buddha's relics <note>6. EDKINS, *loc. cit.*, p. 58.</note>; they received one-third. Ashōka gave Nanda a hair from the Buddha's mustache, while threatening to destroy his kingdom if he refused. Nanda erected a pagoda of rock crystal for it on Mount Sumeru <note>7. *Ibid.*, p. 59.</note>.

According to Northern Buddhism, Nāgārjuna (circa 150 A.D.), the founder of the Mahāyāna doctrine, was instructed by Nāgas in the sea, who showed him unknown books and gave him his most important work, the *Prajñā pāramitā*, with which he returned

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Nāgas and their relationship with Buddhist figures, specifically mentioning how the monk Nāgārjuna brought Buddhist teachings to India and the role of the eight Great Nāga kings.</meta>

to India. For this reason, his name, originally Arjuna, was changed to Nāgārjuna <gloss>1</gloss>, and he is represented in art with seven Nāgas over his head <gloss>2</gloss>.

The Mahāyāna school recognizes a long list of Nāga kings, among whom the eight so-called "Great Nāga kings" are the following: Nanda (called Nāgarāja, the "King of the Nāgas"), Upananda, Sāgara, Vāsuki, Takshaka, Balavān, Anavatapta, and Utpala <gloss>3</gloss>. These eight are often mentioned in Chinese and Japanese legends as "the eight Dragon-kings," 八龍王, and were said to have been among Buddha's audience, with their retinues, while he delivered the instructions contained in the "Sūtra of the Lotus of the Good Law" (Saddharma Pundarīka sūtra, Hokkekyō, 法華經) <gloss>4</gloss>.

The Nāgas are divided into four castes, just like men, and form whole states. "They are," says GRÜNWEDEL <gloss>5</gloss>, "the Lords of the Earth more than anyone else, and when they have been insulted, they send drought, bad crops, diseases, and pestilence among mankind."

Regarding the Nāgas in Indian art, we have an excellent guide in GRÜNWEDEL'S *Buddhist Art in India*. After stating that the Vedas do not yet mention them <gloss>6</gloss>, but that they belong to Indian popular belief—which was later expanded by the official Brahmanic religion—he further remarks that they often entered the Master's presence in human form and even tried to be accepted among his followers, as we see on a relief of Gandhāra (p. 102, Fig. 47; the Nāga's true shape was discovered while he slept). For this reason, one of the questions still asked today of those who wish to be admitted into the Order is: "Are you perhaps a Nāga?" There are three ways in which Indian Buddhist art has represented the Nāgas. First: as fully human, with a cobra-like snake on the head, emerging from the

neck and often provided with several heads. This form has been adopted in Tibet, China, and Japan <note>1</note>. Second: common serpents; and third: a combination of both—that is, snakes in which the upper part of the body looks human, with snake heads appearing above their human heads, while the lower part of the body is entirely snake-like <note>2</note>. The first-mentioned shape can be seen in Figure 5 (p. 29), a relief representing Nāgas worshipping a small stūpa on a throne, and in Figure 103 (p. 103), where a Garuda in the shape of an enormous eagle flies upward with a Nāgī (a Nāga woman) in his claws, biting the long snake that comes out of the woman’s neck. A pillar figure of the stūpa of Bharhut represents Cakravāka, the Nāga king, standing on a rock in the water with five snake heads at his neck, while snakes are visible in holes in the rock <note>3</note>. Once, when Nāgas appeared before Buddha to listen to his words, he ordered Vajrapāṇi to protect them against the attacks of their enemies, the Garuḍas. An Indian relief shows us these Nāgas—the Nāga king Elāpatra and his consort—standing in the water with snakes upon their heads and worshipping Buddha, while in the background Vajrapāṇi brandishes his scepter against the expected Garuḍas. This Vajrapāṇi’s main function is, according to GRÜNWEDEL, to provide rain; as a rain god, he is the protector of the rain-giving snake gods, the Nāgas <note>4</note>.

FOUCHER’s very interesting paper on the Great Miracle of the Buddha at Çrāvastī <note>5</note> repeatedly mentions the Nāga kings Nanda and Upananda, who are represented at the base of the Buddha’s lotus seat. At the request of King Prasenajit, the Buddha performed two miracles: walking through the air in different positions, he alternately emitted flames and waves from the upper or lower part of his body; and second, he preached the Law after multiplying himself innumerable times, reaching up to the sky and in all directions. According to the *Divyāvadāna*, the Buddha, after completing the first miracle, conceived a worldly idea which was immediately executed by the gods. Brahma and Çakra placed themselves at the Buddha’s right and left sides, and the Nāga

<meta>This page continues the description of Nāga iconography, specifically focusing on the "Great Miracle of Śrāvastī" and the evolutionary link between Indian Nāgas and Chinese dragons in art, before transitioning to a discussion of translated Buddhist texts.</meta>

Kings Nanda and Upananda (who were said to have bathed the newborn Buddha and to have played a part in many episodes of his life) created an enormous, magnificent lotus upon which the Master sat down. Then the Buddha, by means of his magic power, created a great number of Buddhas—seated on lotuses or standing, walking, and lying—above his head, up to the highest heavens, and on all sides. This scene is recognized by **Foucher** on several Indian monuments. Often the two Nāga kings are seen under or on both sides of the lotus they created. They are represented supporting the lotus in a kneeling attitude, entirely human but with five serpents over their heads <note>1</note>, or with human upper bodies and scaly serpent tails <note>2</note>.

In the *Jātakas*, the Nāgas are always described as enormous serpents; sometimes, however, they appear in later Indian (i.e., Graeco-Buddhist) art as real dragons, although with the upper part of the body being human. So we see them on a relief from Gandhāra <note>3</note>, worshipping Buddha's alms bowl, in the shape of large water dragons, scaled and winged, with two horse-like legs and the upper part of the body human. Most remarkable is a picture <note>4</note> that represents Garuḍas fighting with Nāgas before the preaching saint Subhūti. The Nāgas are depicted there in all three of their forms: common snakes guarding jewels; human beings with four snakes at their necks; and winged sea dragons, where the upper part of the body is human but with a horned, ox-like head, and the lower part of the body is that of a coiling dragon. Here we find a link between the snake of ancient India and the four-legged Chinese dragon.

---

### § 2. The Nāga according to some translated Buddhist texts.

After having referred to European scholars regarding the Nāga in Buddhism, we may compare their results with some translated Indian texts. Not being acquainted with the Sanskrit language, we thankfully make use of these translations in order to illustrate the Buddhist dragon tales of China and Japan; for, as I stated already in the Preface, this is the only aim of this Introduction.

Professor **Cowell's** <note>5</note> translation of the *Jātaka*, the canonical

### § 2. The Nāga according to some traditions

The Pāli text, composed of those marvelous stories of the Buddha’s former births told by himself, contains seven tales which are vivid pictures of the great magic power of the Nāgas—especially of their kings—of the splendor of their palaces, and, on the other hand, of their helplessness against their deadly enemies, the Garuḍas <note>1 Vol. II, p. 10, Book II, no. 154, the *Uraga-Jātaka*; Vol. III, p. 174, Book VI, no. 386, the *Kharaputta-Jātaka*; Vol. IV, p. 281, Book XV, no. 506, the *Campeyya-Jātaka*; Vol. V, p. 42, Book XVI, no. 518, the *Paṇḍara-Jātaka*; Vol. V, p. 84, Book XVII, no. 524, the *Saṁkhapāla-Jātaka*; Vol. VI, p. 80, Book XXII, no. 543, the *Bhūridatta-Jātaka*; and Vol. VI, p. 126, Book XXII, no. 545, the *Vidhurapaṇḍita-Jātaka*.</note>. The Nāgas are semi-divine serpents which very often assume human shapes and whose kings live with their retinues in the utmost luxury in their magnificent abodes at the bottom of the sea or in rivers or lakes. When leaving the Nāga world, they are in constant danger of being grasped and killed by the gigantic semi-divine birds, the Garuḍas, which also change themselves into men <note>2 In Japan these birds have been identified with the Tengu; compare my treatise on the Tengu, *Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan*, Vol. XXXVI, Part. II, pp. 25—98.</note>. Buddhism has, in its usual way, declared both Nāgas and Garuḍas—mighty figures of the Hindu world of gods and demons—to be the obedient servants of Buddhas, Bodhisattvas, and saints, and to have open ears for their teachings <note>3 See CHAVANNES, *Contes et apologues*, no. 343 (Vol. II, p. 288), where a Garuḍa does not grasp a Nāga who has fled into the house of an ascetic on a small island in the sea; see Vol. III, p. 82, where a wicked Nāga king is forced by an Arhat to go away, and Vol. I, no. 151, p. 423, where the Buddha converts a very evil Nāga, whom innumerable Arhats could not convert.</note>. In the same way, Northern Buddhism adopted the gods of the countries where it introduced itself and made them protectors of its doctrine instead of its antagonists.

Sometimes <note>4 Vol. II, p. 10, no. 154.</note> we read that the Buddha, in a previous existence, succeeded in reconciling even such bitter enemies as a Nāga and a Garuḍa king. He himself was sometimes born as a mighty Nāga king. Thus he reigned as King Campeyya in his “jewelled pavilion” in the river Campā <note>5 Vol. IV, 281, Book XV, no. 506.</note>, as King Saṁkhapāla in the lake of this name <note>6 Vol. V, p. 84, Book XVII, no. 524.</note>, and as King Bhūridatta in the sacred river Yamunā <note>7 Vol. VI, pp. 80—113, Book XXII, no. 543.</note>. In all these three cases, he desired to be reborn in the world of men, and in order to attain this aim, he left his palace on fast days and lay down on the top of an ant heap, observing the fast and offering his magnificent snake body to the passers-by.

<summary>This page explores the portrayal of Nāgas and Garuḍas in Pāli Jātaka stories, highlighting their magical powers, their historical enmity, and their eventual conversion into servants of Buddhist doctrine. It specifically mentions instances where the Buddha was reborn as a Nāga king who practiced asceticism to achieve human rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Pāli text, Jātaka stories, Nāga kings, Garuḍas, Buddha's former births, King Campeyya, King Saṁkhapāla, King Bhūridatta, religious conversion, Buddhist mythology</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Nāga in Pāli literature, specifically detailing Jātaka stories where the Bodhisattva takes the form of a serpent or a Garuḍa king.</meta>

He patiently underwent the most terrible tortures without using his enormous power against the insignificant rogues who caused him so much pain. In the form of Saṁkhapāla, he was freed by a passing merchant, whom he then hosted as a guest in his palace for a whole year; this merchant later became an ascetic. In the two other cases, however, he fell into the hands of a snake charmer. This man used magical herbs, which he spat upon him, and a "charm which commands all things of sense." By squeezing and crushing the royal snake, he weakened him, placed him in a basket, and carried him to various villages and towns where he made him dance before the public. In both legends, the Bodhisattva is performing before the King of Benares when he is released because of the appearance of another Nāga—either Sumanā, his queen, or Sudassana, his brother <note>1 A similar tale is found in Chavannes’s *Contes et apologues extraits du Tripiṭaka chinois*, Vol. I, pp. 189 ff., no. 50.</note>.

We find the Bodhisattva in the form of a Garuḍa king in another tale <note>2 Vol. V, pp. 42 ff., Book XVI, no. 518.</note>, where he discovers the secret way by which the Nāgas often succeed in conquering and killing the Garuḍas. They do this by swallowing large stones and making themselves so heavy that their attackers, while trying to lift them, drop dead into the stream of water flowing from the Nāgas' wide-open mouths. Pāṇḍara, a Nāga king, was foolish enough to trust an ascetic whom both he and the Garuḍa used to visit and honor. At the ascetic's repeated request, Pāṇḍara told him the valuable secret of the Nāga tribe. The treacherous ascetic immediately revealed it to the Bodhisattva, who then succeeded in capturing Pāṇḍara by seizing him by the tail and holding him upside down. This caused the Nāga to vomit up the stones he had swallowed, making him an easy prey. However, moved by Pāṇḍara’s lamentations, the Bodhisattva released him and they became friends. They then went together to the treacherous ascetic. The Nāga king caused the man's head to split into seven pieces, and the man himself was swallowed by the earth to be reborn in the Avīci hell.

In the *Kharaputta-jātaka* <note>3 Vol. III, p. 174, Book VI, no. 386.</note>, we read about a Nāga king who was nearly killed by boys while seeking food on earth, but was saved from them by Senaka, the king of Benares. We do not read what made the mighty Nāga so powerless against those children; for there was apparently no question of fasting, as in

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Nāga legends, focusing on their magical powers, splendid palaces, and quick tempers, as well as their interactions with human kings and protective spells.</meta>

...the legends of the Bodhisattva mentioned above. He returned to the Nāga world and brought many jewels from there as a gift for the King, while also appointing one of his countless Nāga girls to stay near the King and protect him. He also gave him a charm by which he would always be able to find the girl if he could not see her, and later presented him with another charm that granted knowledge of all sounds, so that he understood even the voices of ants 1. Thus, we find the Nāga king in possession not only of countless jewels and beautiful girls, but also of powerful charms that bestow supernatural vision and hearing. The palaces of the Nāga kings are always described as extremely splendid, overflowing with gold, silver, and precious stones; and the Nāga women, when appearing in human form, were beautiful beyond description. However, the entire race was terribly quick-tempered, which—given their deadly poison and great magical power—made them very dangerous creatures 2. Even the breath of their nostrils was enough to kill a man, as we read in the previously mentioned *Kharaputta-jātaka*. In that story, the Nāga king was angry because the girl he had appointed to protect King Senaka returned to the Nāga world, falsely complaining that the King had struck her because she would not obey his commands. Consequently, the Nāga king immediately sent four Nāga youths to destroy Senaka in his bedroom with the breath of their nostrils.

We often find stories of men staying as guests in a Nāga king’s palace and enjoying all its luxury, sometimes for seven days 3, and sometimes even for an entire year 4. The most interesting of all the Nāga tales is the *Bhūridatta-jātaka* 5. There we read about “the Nāga world beneath the ocean” 6 and the Nāga palace “beneath the Yamunā’s sacred stream” 7. At the same time, the Nāga maidens—frightened by the Ālambāyana spell, a serpent spell obtained from a Garuḍa king 8—“sank into the earth,” and the “jewel of luck” 9 which “grants all desires” 10, when falling on the ground, “passed through it and was lost in

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Nāga legends, specifically their ability to move between the earth and water, their association with wish-granting jewels, and their role in protecting the Buddha.</meta>

...the Nāga world" ¹. Thus, we see that whatever belongs to that world can disappear into the earth and does not need to enter the water, because both are the Nāgas' domain ². The "jewel which grants all desires," which was guarded by the Nāga maidens but forgotten in their terror of the Garuḍa spell, is none other than the "Nyo-i hōju," 如意寶珠, mentioned in Chinese and Japanese legends. The same story teaches us that children of men and Nāgi (Nāga women) are "of a watery nature" and cannot stand sunshine or wind, but are happiest when playing in the water ³.

So far, these are the Jātakas of Cowell's edition. It is a strange fact that in all these tales no mention is made of the Nāga's nature as a *god of clouds and rain*, although this is the main reason why the Chinese identified him with their dragon. In the legends translated from the Chinese *Tripiṭaka* by Chavannes ⁴, however, so much emphasis is placed on the rain-giving capacity of the Nāga that we need not doubt its prominence in Northern Buddhism.

From the *Lalita Vistara* ⁵ we learn that in the fifth week after reaching perfect Enlightenment, the Buddha went to Lake Mucilinda. The Nāga king of the same name, who lived there, came out of the water and shielded the Lord from the rain with his coils and hoods for seven days, after which he took the form of a youth and worshipped the Great Being. In the *Mahāvagga* ⁶, the name of the lake and the Nāga king is Mucalinda, and "in order to protect the Lord against the cold and the humidity, he surrounded him seven times with his coils and extended his hood over him." According to Hardy ⁷, "in the sixth week, he went to the lake Mucalinda, where he remained at

<meta>This page continues the comparison of various Buddhist traditions regarding the legend of the Nāga Muchalinda (or Mucilinda) protecting the Buddha from a storm following his Enlightenment.</meta>

...the foot of a Midella tree. At that time, rain began to fall and continued for seven days without interruption across all four continents. The Nāga Muchalinda, having risen to the surface of the lake, saw the darkness produced by the storm. In order to shelter Buddha from the rain and wind, and to protect him from flies, mosquitoes, and other insects, he spread his extended hood over him, which served as a canopy.

It is highly interesting to compare these passages with the version of the same legend found in the Chinese *Tripiṭaka* <note>1</note>. There, he is said to have gone to Mucilinda's river (not a lake) immediately after reaching Enlightenment. While he was sitting under a tree, his brilliant light penetrated into the Nāga's palace, just as in former times his three predecessors of this *kalpa* had spread their light while sitting on the same spot. The Nāga, delighted to see the new Buddha's light, arose from the water and, surrounding the Lord with seven coils, covered him with his seven heads (not hoods). "*The Nāga, delighted, caused wind and rain for seven days and nights*" <note>2</note>. During all that time, the Lord sat motionless, protected by the royal snake, the first of all animals to be converted. This legend is found in the *Luh-tu tsih king* <note>3</note>, number 143 of Nanjo's Catalogue, translated by Seng-hwui <note>4</note>, who died in A.D. 280 <note>5</note>.

The same work contains many *jātakas*, in which the Nāgas are frequently mentioned, sometimes in the company of Çakra, Brahma, the four *devarājas*, and the gods of the earth <note>6</note>. One day, when the Bodhisattva and Ānanda were Nāgas in order to complete

the expiation of their former evil deeds, “expanding their majestic spirit, they made heaven and earth shake; they raised the clouds and caused the rain to fall” <note>1 奮其威神、震天動地、與雲降雨. The *Kiu tsah p'i-yü king* <note>3 舊雜譬喻經.</note>, the “Old version of the *Samyuktāvadāna sūtra*” (miscellaneous metaphors), translated in the third century AD by the same Seng-hwui (Nanjō’s Catalogue, no. 1359), mentions the Nāgas as bringers of rain in some of its stories. Such a being, by its rain, made the dike along which a <term>çrāmaṇera</term> carried his master’s rice so slippery that the man repeatedly tumbled down and dropped the rice into the mud. His master summoned the Nāga, who, in the shape of an old man, prostrated himself before the <term>Arhat</term> and invited him to dine in his palace for the rest of his life. The Arhat accepted this offer and daily flew with his bed to the Nāga’s palace after having entered into deep meditation. But his pupil, anxious to know where his master had obtained the splendid rice grains which he discovered in his alms bowl, hid himself under the bed and, clinging to one of its legs, arrived with the Arhat at the Nāga’s abode. The latter, his wife, and the whole crowd of beautiful women respectfully saluted the çrāmaṇera, but the pupil was warned by his master not to forget that he, the çrāmaṇera himself, was a much higher being than the Nāga, despite all the latter’s treasures and beautiful women. “The Nāga,” said he, “has to endure three kinds of suffering: his delicious food turns into toads as soon as he takes it into his mouth; his beautiful women, as well as he himself, change into serpents when he tries to embrace them; and on his back he has scales lying in a reverse direction, and when sand and pebbles enter between them, he suffers pains which pierce his heart. Therefore, do not envy him.” The pupil, however, did not answer; day and night he thought of the Nāga and forgot to eat. He fell ill, died, and was reborn as the Nāga’s son, even more fearsome than his father, but after death he became a man again <note>4 Chavannes, loc. cit., Volume I, no. 94, pages 358 and following (Tripiṭaka XIX, 7, page 19; great Japanese edition of...</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Nāga legends found in Buddhist texts, specifically focusing on their power over rain and their interactions with humans and fire.</meta>

Another time, the Buddha's disciples are compared to a great Nāga who liked to provide rain for the earth, but, fearing that the earth might not be able to bear the weight of the water, decided to make the rain fall into the sea <note>1 In the place cited, Vol. I, no. 138, p. 410 (Tripiṭaka XIX, 7, p. 24).</note>.

In the *Tsah p'i-yü king* <note>2 雜譬喻經; see Chavannes, in the place cited, Vol. II, p. 1, note 1. Both this work and the *Chung king chwen tsah p'i-yü king*, 衆經撰雜譬喻經 (Nanjō, no. 1366), are said to have been compiled by the <term>bhikṣu</term> Tao Lioh, 道畧, but are probably two different editions of his work; Kumārajīva seems to have translated Tao Lioh's work in 401 AD.</note>, a work from the Korean Tripiṭaka not found in Nanjō's Catalogue (for no. 1368, which bears the same title, is a different work), we find the following Nāga tales. A Nāga ascended to the sky and caused abundant rains to fall: for the <term>devas</term>, they brought the seven precious things; for mankind, fertilizing water; and for the hungry demons, a great fire which burned their whole bodies <note>3 Chavannes, in the place cited, Vol. II, no. 167, p. 23 (Tripiṭaka XIX, 7, p. 3).</note>.

Another Nāga who, by means of a single drop of water, could give rain to one, two, or three kingdoms—even to the whole <term>Jambudvīpa</term>—placed it in the great sea so that it might not dry up <note>4 In the place cited, Vol. II, no. 193, p. 63 (Tripiṭaka XIX, 7, p. 8).</note>.

An exorcist of Nāgas went with his pitcher full of water to the pond of such a being and, by his magic formulas, surrounded the Nāga with fire. As the water in the pitcher was the only refuge the serpent could find, it changed into a very small animal and entered the pitcher <note>5 In the place cited, Vol. II, no. 179, p. 42 (Tripiṭaka XIX, 7, p. 5).</note>.

Here we see the Nāgas not only as rain gods, but also as beings wholly dependent on the presence of water and much afraid of fire, just like the dragons in many Chinese and Japanese legends.

With regard to the precious pearls in the possession of the Nāgas as gods of the waters, we may mention a tale to be found in the *Mo ho seng chi lüh* <note>6 摩訶僧祇律, Mahāsāṃghika <term>vinaya</term>.</note> or "Discipline of the Mahāsāṃghikas" (Nanjō, no. 1119), translated in 416 by Buddhabhadra and Fah-hien <note>7 Nanjō, Catalogue, Appendix II, nos. 42 and 45.</note>. There we read about a Nāga who wore a necklace of pearls, which he liked so much that he preferred it even to his friendship with a hermit. The latter, daily tortured by the Nāga's coils wound around his body, succeeded in getting rid

The *Avadāna-çataka*, a hundred legends translated from the Sanskrit by **LÉON FEER** <note>2 *Annales du Musée Guimet*, Volume XVIII (1891).</note>, contains a few passages concerning the <term>Nāgas</term>. After having been devoured, the <term>Nāga</term> was reborn as <term>Subhūti</term> and, by following the Buddha’s teachings, reached <term>Arhatship</term>. He remembered having had five hundred rebirths among the <term>Nāgas</term> because of a long series of wicked thoughts in previous existences. Then, he used his supernatural power to convert both <term>Nāgas</term> and <term>Garuḍas</term> by protecting the former against five hundred <term>Garuḍas</term> and the latter against a gigantic <term>Nāga</term>, which he caused to appear. In this way, the law of love was taught to them, and they followed his teachings.

In another legend <note>4 No. 59, pages 227 and following.</note>, a Brahman is said to have been reborn as a <term>Nāga</term> because he had broken his fast; seven times a day, a rain of burning sand came down upon him until he succeeded in keeping a special fast. Then, after dying while abstaining from food, he was reborn in the <term>Trāyastriṃçat</term> heaven.

In a third passage <note>5 No. 19, p. 83.</note>, <term>Virūpāksha</term>, one of the four guardians of the world who reigns on the West side of Mount Meru, is said to be surrounded by <term>Nāgas</term> (his subjects, who live in the West).

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Buddhist legends involving Nāgas, likely drawing from the Sūtrālaṃkāra and other Tripiṭaka texts mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

The "<term>Saṃgha</term> (alone) can receive the great rain of the Law."¹ When a merchant named *Kotikarṇa* visited a town of <term>pretas</term>, these hungry demons uttered a long complaint containing the following verse: "When the Heavenly Dragons (the <term>Nāgas</term>) cause the sweet dew to fall on the mountains and valleys, it turns into bubbling fire and pours onto our bodies."² "<term>Elāpatra</term> the <term>Nāgarāja</term> violated the commandments by harming the leaves of a tree; after death, he was reborn among the <term>Nāgas</term>, and none of the Buddhas has predicted when he will be able to leave them."³

"The tears (of those who, upon hearing the Law of the twelve <term>Nidānas</term>, are moved by pity and weep with compassion) can entirely destroy the <term>Nāga Vāsuki</term>, who exhales a violent poison."⁴

"The *Rākṣasas* and the *Piçācas*, the evil <term>Nāgas</term>, and even robbers do not dare to oppose the words of the Buddha."⁵

An evil <term>Nāga</term> guarded a large tree that stood in a big pond and killed all those who took a branch or a leaf from it. When the <term>bhikṣus</term> came to cut down the tree to build a <term>stūpa</term>, the people and a Brahman warned them not to do so because of the danger, but the <term>bhikṣus</term> answered: "Regarding the poisonous <term>Nāga</term>, you, Brahman, glorify yourself. But we rely on the <term>Nāga</term> of men (the Buddha), and, placing our trust in Him, we glorify ourselves . . . . . Among all the poisonous <term>Nāgas</term>, you show yourself full of respectful thoughts for this <term>Nāga</term> king. The Buddha is gentle and calm; He is the King of all beings. It is Him whom we revere—the Perfect One, the <term>Bhagavat</term>. Who would be able to subdue the poisonous <term>Nāga</term>, if not the Buddha's disciples?" Then they cut down the tree, and, to the Brahman's astonishment, no clouds, no thunder, and no miraculous signs signaled the <term>Nāga</term>'s wrath, as had previously been the case even when a single leaf of his tree was taken by a human hand.⁶ The Brahman, after expressing his amazement and anger,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Buddhist legends involving Nāgas, concluding a story about a Brahman and a Nāga-guarded tree, and introduces the Kathāsaritsāgara as a historical source for these myths.</meta>

Because he thought that they had used magic incantations, he fell asleep and was addressed in a dream by the <term>Nāga</term> as follows: 
> “Do not be angry; what they did was done to show me their respect. They have neither despised nor wounded me, for my body supports the <term>stūpa</term>; moreover, the tree has become a beam of the <term>stūpa</term>, and I can protect it. As for the <term>stūpa</term> of the <term>Daçabala</term>, of the Exalted One—would I ever have been able to protect it (if not in this way)? . . . There was still another reason why I did not have sufficient power (to resist the Buddha). I am going to tell you this reason; listen attentively: <term>Takṣaka</term>, the <term>Nāga</term> king, came here in person and took possession of this tree; could I protect it? <term>Elāpatra</term>, the <term>Nāga</term> king, himself came to this spot with <term>Vaiçramaṇa</term>: was my power sufficient to resist those Devas and <term>Nāgas</term>, who are full of majesty?” 
When the Brahman awoke, he became a monk.

This remarkable story shows us the <term>Nāga</term> as an inhabitant of a pond, but at the same time as a tree demon—a role in which we often find the serpent in Chinese and Japanese tales, but never in Indian <term>Nāga</term> legends. As a rain and thunder god, he is said to produce clouds and thunder when he is angry. <term>Takṣaka</term> and <term>Elāpatra</term> are mentioned here as the mightiest of the <term>Nāga</term> kings, and <term>Vaiçramaṇa</term>, the guardian of the North and king of the <term>Yakshas</term>, is probably confused with <term>Virūpākṣa</term>, the guardian of the West and king of the <term>Nāgas</term>. The whole legend is a typical example of the way in which Buddhism subdued other cults.

After having learned the <term>Nāga</term>'s nature from these Buddhist writings which made him known in China and Japan, we may venture in another direction by turning to the *Kathāsaritsāgara* or “Ocean of the Streams of Story.” This “largest and most interesting collection” of tales was composed by the Kashmiri court poet SOMADEVA, “one of the most illustrious Indian poets”¹, in the eleventh century of our era², but the original collection that served as its source, titled the *Bṛhatkathā*, is much older. According to Professor SPEYER³, it “must have been arranged in that period of Indian history when Buddhism exercised its influence over the Hindu mind side by side with <term>Çaivism</term> and so many other various sectarian and local creeds, rites, and theosophies.” “The main story and a large number of the episodes are

---

<note>1 Compare SPEYER, *Studies about the Kathāsaritsāgara*, Proceedings of the Royal Academy of Sciences in Amsterdam, Literature Department, New Series, Volume VIII, no. 5 (1908); p. 2.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from discussing Buddhist legends of Nāgas to examining Shaiva tales within the Kathāsaritsāgara, specifically focusing on the evolution of the Nāgas' role from fearsome entities to rain-bringers.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>

Shaiva tales, as was to be expected from the supposed first narrator being none other than the Supreme God Shiva himself <note>1. In the same place.</note>. Alongside legends of the Buddhists, even mythological narrations from the Vedic age are to be found in this work, with smaller collections being incorporated into it <note>2. In the same place.</note>. Among the great number of interesting legends contained in the *Kathāsaritsāgara*, translated by Tawney (1880—1884), there are several in which the <term>Nāgas</term> play a more or less important part.

The first thing which strikes us is the total absence of passages devoted to their capacity for *giving rain*. Combining this with the same observation made above regarding the *<term>jātakas</term>* of Cowell's edition, we feel inclined to believe that this part of the <term>Nāgas'</term> nature was particularly developed by the Northern Buddhists. The original conceptions regarding these semi-divine serpents, living in the water or under the earth, seem to have attributed to them the power of raising clouds and thunder, and of appearing as clouds themselves, but not as rain-giving beings. It is, of course, a very obvious conclusion that cloud gods produce rain, but it seems that this idea, which made them the benefactors of mankind, first arose in the minds of the adherents of the <term>Mahāyāna</term> school. According to the original ideas, on the contrary, they seem to have only given vent to their *anger* by terrifying mankind by means of dense clouds, thunder, and earthquakes. Highly interesting in this respect is the following story, found in the *Kathāsaritsāgara* <note>3. Ch. LXX, Vol. II, p. 149 and following.</note>.

In the Vindhya forest in the northern quarter there was a solitary Ashoka tree, and under it, in a lake, stood the great palace of a mighty <term>Nāga</term> king, <term>Pārāvatāksha</term> by name, who obtained a matchless sword from the war of the gods and the <term>Asuras</term>. In order to get this sword an ascetic, assisted by a prince and his followers, threw enchanted mustard seed upon the water, thus clearing it from the dust which concealed it, and began to offer an oblation with snake-subduing spells. “And he conquered by the power of his spells the impediments, such as earthquakes, clouds, and so on. Then there came out from that Ashoka tree a heavenly nymph, as it were, murmuring spells with the tinkling of her jeweled ornaments, and approaching the ascetic she pierced his soul with a sidelong glance of love. And then the ascetic lost his self-control and forgot his spells; and the beautiful woman, embracing him, flung from his hand the vessel of oblation."

And then the snake Pārāvatāksha gained his opportunity, and he came out from that palace *like the dense cloud of the day of doom*. Then the heavenly nymph vanished; and the ascetic, beholding the snake—terrible with *flaming eyes and roaring horribly* <note>1. This probably refers to thunder and lightning.</note>—died of a broken heart. When he was destroyed, the snake laid aside his awful form and cursed Mṛigānkadatta (the prince) and his followers for helping the ascetic, with these words: "Since you did what was quite unnecessary by coming here with this man, you shall be separated from one another for a certain time." Then the snake disappeared, and all of them simultaneously had their eyes dimmed with darkness and were deprived of the power of hearing sounds. And they immediately went in different directions, separated from one another by the power of the curse, though they continued looking and calling for each other.

<term>Nāgas</term> injuring the crops are mentioned in another passage, where Svayamprabhā, queen of the <term>Asuras</term> residing in <term>Pātāla</term> land, "acts as a guarantor (to King Merudhvaja) that the <term>Nāgas</term> shall not injure the crops" <note>2. Ch. CXIX, Vol. II, p. 551.</note>. The seven Pātālas are the netherworld <note>3. Vol. II, p. 549, note 1.</note>, the "home of the serpent race below the earth" <note>4. Vol. I, p. 185, note 3.</note>. However, the <term>Asuras</term>, "who escaped from the slaughter in the great fight long ago between the gods and asuras," had also fled to Pātāla <note>5. Ch. CXVIII, Vol. II, p. 520.</note> and lived there. Regarding the <term>Nāgas</term> having their abode in <term>Pātāla</term> land, we may refer to the following passages of the *Kathāsaritsāgara*: "On the farthest shore he set up a pillar of victory, looking like the king of the serpents emerging from the world below to beg for immunity for Pātāla" $^6$. "Do you not remember how he went to Pātāla and there married the daughter of a <term>Nāga</term>, whose name was Surūpā?" $^7$ When Kadrū and Vinatā, two wives of Kaçyapa, had a dispute regarding the color of the Sun’s horses, they made an agreement that the one who was wrong should become a slave to the other. Kadrū, the mother of the snakes, persuaded her sons to defile the horses of the Sun by spitting venom over them; thus they appeared black instead of white. Vinatā, the mother of Garuḍa, king of birds, was defeated by this trick and made into Kadrū’s slave. When Garuḍa came to release her, the snakes requested the nectar from the sea of milk—which the gods had begun to churn—as a substitute.

and Garuḍa went to the sea of milk and displayed his great power in order to obtain the nectar. “Then the god Vishṇu, pleased with his power, saw fit to say to him: ‘I am pleased with you; choose a favor’. Then Garuḍa, angry because his mother had been enslaved, asked a favor from Vishṇu — ‘Let the snakes become my food’ ”. Vishṇu consented, and Garuḍa, after having obtained the nectar, promised Indra that he would enable him to take it away before the snakes could consume it. He placed the nectar on a bed of Kuça grass and invited the snakes to take it there after they had released his mother. They did so, and Garuḍa departed with Vinatā, but when the snakes were about to take the nectar, Indra swooped down and carried off the vessel.

“Then the snakes, in despair, licked that bed of Darbha grass, thinking that there might be a drop of spilled nectar on it, but the result was that their tongues were split, and they became double-tongued for no reason. What except ridicule can ever be the lot of the excessively greedy? Thus, the snakes did not obtain the nectar of immortality, and their enemy Garuḍa, on the strength of Vishṇu’s blessing, began to swoop down and devour them. And this he did again and again. And while he was attacking them in this way, the snakes in *Pātāla* were dying of fear, the females miscarried, and the whole serpent race was nearly destroyed. And Vāsuki, the king of the snakes, seeing him there every day, realized that the serpent world would be ruined all at once. After reflecting, he presented a petition to the irresistibly powerful Garuḍa and made this agreement with him — ‘I will send you one snake to eat every day, O king of birds, on the hill that rises out of the sand of the sea. But you must not act so foolishly as to enter *Pātāla*, for by the destruction of the serpent world, your own objective will be frustrated’. When Vāsuki said this to him, Garuḍa consented and began to eat one snake sent by him every day in this place; and in this way, innumerable serpents have met their death here.” So spoke a snake, whose turn it was to be devoured by Garuḍa, to *Jīmūtavāhana*, “the compassionate incarnation of a *Bodhisattva*” <note>1. and Garuḍa stopped eating, but was requested by Jīmūtavāhana himself to continue. Then the snake on whose behalf he had sacrificed his life arrived and cried from afar: "Stop, stop, Garuḍa! He is not a snake; I am the snake meant for you." Garuḍa was greatly grieved and was about to enter the fire to purify himself from guilt; however, following Jīmūtavāhana's advice, he determined never again to eat snakes and to revive those he had killed. The goddess Gaurī, by raining nectar on Jīmūtavāhana, made him safe and sound, and Garuḍa brought the nectar of immortality from heaven and sprinkled it along the entire seashore. "That caused all the snakes there (whose bones were lying there) to rise up alive; then that forest, crowded with the numerous tribe of snakes, appeared like Pātāla come to behold Jīmūtavāhana, having lost its previous dread of Garuḍa" ¹.

Pātāla-land—the seven under-worlds, one of which was called Rasātala ² (sometimes equivalent to Pātāla) ³—was inhabited by Nāgas, Asuras, Daityas, and Dānavas (two classes of demons who opposed the gods and were identified with the Asuras). There were temples of the gods (Çiva ⁴, Durgā ⁵, the Fire-god ⁶) worshipped by the demons. As for its entrances, these are described as mountain caverns ⁷ or "openings in the water" ⁸; or wonderful flagstaffs rising out of the sea with banners on them showed the way there ⁹. Sometimes human kings were allowed to visit this Fairy land. Chandraprabha, for example, after making offerings to Çiva and Rudra with his queen and his ministers (led by Siddhārta), entered an opening in the water pointed out by Maya and, after traveling a long distance, arrived there ¹⁰. And King Chaṇḍasinha, with Sattvaçīla, plunged into the sea and, following the sinking flagstaff, reached a splendid city ¹¹. Also King Yaçaḥketu, after diving into the sea, suddenly beheld a magnificent city with palaces of precious stones, gardens, tanks, wishing-trees that granted every desire, and beautiful maidens ¹². This agrees with the description of the Nāga palaces which we found in the Jātakas.

A temple of Vāsuki, the king of the snakes, is mentioned in the

His idol stood in the shrine, which was full of long wreaths of flowers like serpents, "and which therefore resembled the abyss of Pātāla." In the former case, Garuḍa himself persecuted the Nāga in human form; in the latter, the snake-god, discovering that he was deceived by his wife during his sleep, "discharged fire from his mouth, and reduced them both (her lover and herself) to ashes."

### § 3. The Nāga as a giver of rain.

We have seen above that the Nāga’s capacity for raising clouds and thunder when his anger was aroused was cleverly converted by the Mahāyāna school into the highly beneficent power of giving rain to the thirsty earth. In this way, through the influence of Buddha’s Law, these fearful serpents had become blessers of mankind. It is clear that in this form they were readily identified with Chinese dragons, which were also blessing, rain-giving gods of the water.

The four classes into which the Mahāyānists divided the Nāgas were:
1. *Heavenly Nāgas* (天 龍), who guard the Heavenly Palace and carry it so that it does not fall.
2. *Divine Nāgas* (神 龍), who benefit mankind by causing the clouds to rise and the rain to fall.
3. *Earthly Nāgas* (地 龍), who drain off rivers (removing obstructions) and open sluices (outlets).

---

His daughter Ratnaprabha is mentioned in Vol. I, p. 544. He cursed a Nāga king who had fled from battle, Vol. II, p. 171. The serpent Vāsuki served as a rope to whirl around mount Mandara when the sea was churned and produced Shri or Lakshmi, Vol. II, p. 568, note 1.</note>
<note>2. Ch. LXI, Vol. II, p. 54; Ch. LXIV, Vol. II, p. 98.</note>

<meta>This page continues the list of the four classes of Nāgas and discusses their role as rain-bringers, citing the Taiheiki and Beal's translations of Chinese Buddhist texts.</meta>

4. *Nāgas who lie hidden* (伏藏龍), guarding the treasuries of the "Kings of the Wheel" (輪王, <term>Cakravartī-rājas</term>) and blessing mankind¹.

The *Taiheiki*², a Japanese work, relates an Indian tale in which a Dragon (that is, a <term>Nāga</term>) king is said to have caused rain. A *sien* (仙, the Chinese equivalent for a wonder-working ascetic), annoyed by this, caught all the large and small dragons of the inner and outer seas and shut them up in a rock. Because of their absence, not a drop of rain fell for a long time, and the crops were ruined by the severe drought. Then the king, moved with compassion for his people, asked his advisers how this ascetic's power could be broken and the dragons set loose. The answer was that a beautiful woman could seduce him and thus put an end to his magical abilities. So the King dispatched the greatest beauty of his harem to the cottage of the ascetic, who immediately fell in love with her. Losing his supernatural power, he became an ordinary man and died. The dragons, no longer under his influence, flew away into the sky and caused the winds to blow and the rain to fall.

A passage from **Jin-Ch'au**'s *Buddhist Cosmos*³, dealing with the <term>Nāga</term> kings and translated by **Beal** in his *Catena of Buddhist scriptures from the Chinese*⁴, mentions four *sūtras*, one of which, the *Mahāmegha sūtra*, shall be discussed below in § 4. As for the *Lau-Tán (?) sūtra*, the title of which is not explained by **Beal**—so that we know neither the Chinese characters nor the Sanskrit equivalent—this *sūtra* is said there to contain the following passage: "To the North of Mount Sumeru, under the waters of the Great Sea, is the Palace of **Sāgara Nāgarāja**, in length and breadth

<note>1 See the Japanese Buddhist dictionary *Bukkyō iroha jiten*, 佛教いろは字典, written in 1901 (second edition 1904) by **Miura Kensŭke**, 三浦兼助, Vol. II, p. 56 under the entry for 龍; the Chinese work *Ts'ien k'ioh kü léi shu*, 潛確居類書, written during the Ming dynasty by **Ch'en Jen-sih**, 陳仁錫. The same Chinese work lists the three sorrows (患) of the Indian dragons as follows:
1. Hot winds and hot sand, which burn their skin, flesh, and bones.
2. Sudden violent winds, which blow away the palaces of the dragons and make them lose their treasures, clothes, etc., so that they can no longer hide their forms.
3. Golden-winged bird-kings (Garuda kings) who enter the dragons' palaces and devour their children.</note>

80,000 yōjanas; it is surrounded by precious walls, a beautiful railing, gardens, and parks, and is adorned with every kind of decoration." This Sāgara, one of the eight Great Nāga kings mentioned above, apparently held the primary rank among the rain-bestowing Nāgas of the sea who are worshipped by Northern Buddhists.

From the *Saddharma smṛtyupasthāna sūtra*$^1$—which Beal, without providing the Chinese title, mistakenly calls the *Saddharma Prākāsa sāsana sūtra*, but which I found in Nanjō’s Catalogue under number 679—Beal quotes the following passage: “Deep in the depths of the Great Sea at 1,000 yōjanas is a city named Hi-loh, with a length and breadth of 3,000 yōjanas; it is occupied by Nāgarājas. There are two kinds of Nāgarājas: 1. Those who practice the Law of Buddha; 2. Those who do not. The first protect the world; the second are opposed to it. Where the good Nāgas dwell, it never rains hot sand, but the wicked Nāgas are subject to this plague, and their palaces and followers are all burned up. Whenever people obey the Law, cherish their parents, and support and feed the Shamans, the good Nāgarājas are able to acquire increased power, so that they can cause a light, fertilizing rain to fall, by which the five kinds of grain are perfected in color, scent, and taste . . . . If, on the contrary, people are disobedient to the Law, do not reverence their parents, and do not cherish the Brahmans and Shamans, then the power of the wicked dragons increases, and the opposite effects follow; every possible calamity happens to the fruits of the earth and to the lives of men.”

Finally, the *Buddhāvataṁsaka mahāvaipulya sūtra*$^2$ contains a large number of interesting passages regarding the Nāgas as gods of clouds and rain. Beal translates as follows: “In the middle of the Palace of the Nāga-rāja Sāgara, there are four precious gems from which all the gems of the ocean are produced. Here also is the Palace of Jambuketu, the Nāga-rāja’s eldest son; as well as the palace of Vāsuki Nāga-rāja, and eighty myriads of other dragons, each having his own separate palace.”

“There are five kinds of dragons: 1. Serpent-dragons; 2. Lizard-dragons; 3. Fish-dragons; 4. Elephant-dragons; 5. Toad-dragons.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Nāga king Sāgara and his role as a rain-bringer, citing specific Buddhist sūtras and their descriptions of the Nāga realms.</meta>

> “Sāgara Nāga-rāja, assuming the appearance of Maheshvara and exerting his great strength, mightily assists all sentient creatures. His influence extends from the four continents up to the Paranirmita Vaçavartin Heaven. He spreads out the *clouds* diversified with every color, excites the varied brightness of the *lightning*, causes the changing peals of *thunder*, raises propitious *breezes*, and distills *fertilizing showers*. But though this Nāga-rāja is kindly disposed toward *men*, the good principles which prevail in the world are the real source of propitious rain falling. Again, it is said that *Anavataptā* Nāga-rāja raises the wide-spreading vaporous clouds which cover Jambudvīpa and distill soft and nourishing rain, causing the various herbs and grains to spring up and flourish, and the fountains and rivers to swell with refreshing streams.”

Besides this passage translated by Beal, the same sūtra often mentions Sāgara and the other Nāga kings as givers of rain. In the Chinese translation from the *end* of the seventh century A.D. (Nanjō, no. 88) we read, for example: “Further, there are innumerable Great Nāga kings, called Virūpāksha, Sāgara, etc., etc. . . . , who by raising the clouds and diffusing the rain put an end to the vexations caused to all living beings by burning heat” <note>復 有 無 量 諸 大 龍 王 所 謂 毗 樓 博 叉 王 、 娑 竭 羅 龍 王 . . . . . . . . 與 雲 布 雨 令 諸 眾 生 熱 惱 消 滅 。 Chapter I, pages 18a, b.</note>.

> “When the Great Sea-Nāga-king (Sāgara) sends down the rain, He (the Enlightened One) can separately count the drops, and in one thought determine their number” <note>Chapter XV, page 18b.</note>.

Comparisons of the rain-giving Nāga kings to Buddha and his Law, especially in stanzas, are very numerous <note>Compare Chapter XV, page 21b; XVII, 19a; XXXVIII, 22b; XLII, 6b, 15b; LI, 11b; LII, 1b; LII, 3b; LXXX, 22a.</note>.

> “The Supreme Nāga king Sāgara, when raising the clouds, covers the whole earth and distributes the rain over all places, and in his heart there is but one thought—so also do the Buddhas, the Kings of the Law: great clouds of compassion spread everywhere, and, on behalf of all those who practice religious austerities, rain down on each and on all without distinction” <note><unclear>...</unclear></note>.

> “Just as Anavatapta Nāgarāja sends down the rain everywhere on Jambudvīpa and thus can cause all the plants and trees to shoot up and grow, and it (the rain) does not come forth from his body but from his heart—in the same way also the

<summary>This page describes the roles of the Nāga kings Sāgara and Anavataptā as benevolent rain-bringers in Buddhist scripture. Their ability to provide life-giving rain and relief from heat is used as a frequent metaphor for the Buddha's universal compassion and the spread of his Law.</summary>

<keywords>Sāgara, Nāga-rāja, Anavataptā, Jambudvīpa, Virūpāksha, Buddhism, rain rituals, Beal, Nanjō, Maheshvara, compassion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the examination of Buddhist sūtras that describe the Nāga kings as rain-bringers, moving from the general influence of Sāgara to specific liturgical texts used in rain ceremonies.</meta>

"beautiful words of the Buddhas everywhere rain upon the Universe (Dharmadhātu)" ¹.

Thus this sūtra is striking evidence of the great blessing power attributed by Northern Buddhism to the Nāga kings as givers of rain.

### § 4. Sūtras recited in rain ceremonies.

The most important of the sūtras recited by the Northern Buddhists for causing rain in times of drought is the *Mahāmegha sūtra*, "The Sūtra of the Great Cloud." NANJŌ'S *Catalogue of the Buddhist Tripiṭaka* contains four Chinese translations of this text: nos. 186—188, and 970. The titles of the translations are slightly different from one another ², but the original work is the same. JÑĀNAGUPTA translated it first between A.D. 557 and 581 (no. 187), and a second time between A.D. 589 and 618 (no. 186). In A.D. 585 another translation was made by NARENDRAYAÇAS (no. 188). No. 970, which has the same Chinese title as no. 188, is a later translation. The Sanskrit text still exists, and an extract of it is given by BENDALL in the *Journal of the Royal Asiatic Society* ³; this agrees with no. 186, while BEAL, in his *Catena of Buddhist Scriptures from the Chinese*, gives an abstract of no. 188. According to DE GROOT ⁴ the sūtra was translated by AMOGHAVAJRA, the second patriarch of the Yoga school in China and a disciple of VAJRABODHI (the first patriarch of the same school, who arrived in China in 719). This is apparently no. 970 of NANJŌ'S Catalogue, designated as "a later translation."

From BENDALL'S extract we learn that the contents of the *Mahāmegha sūtra* are as follows. "On one occasion the Venerable One dwelt in the palace of the Snake-Kings Nanda and Upananda, in the summer pavilion of the circle of mighty clouds filled with

No. 188: 大 雲 輪 請 雨 經, "Great Cloud-wheel sūtra for asking rain". On p. 11b of the Chinese text we find the name of the Tathāgata "Great Cloud-wheel". precious gems and jewels, accompanied by a great assembly of bhikshus, and by a great assembly of bodhisattvas, and a great host of kings—namely, Nanda the Snake King, and Upananda (here follows a list of 185 snakes) ¹, attended, I say, by 84 hundred thousand million crores of snakes assembled and seated together. All the Nāgas saluted the Lord, bowing their clasped hands toward him, after which they stood to one side and made supplications. "Let us worship, let us reverence, esteem, and honor the samudras (infinite numbers) of Bodhisattvas... *riding upon the sea-clouds, immeasurable and innumerable, with samudras of cloud-bodies*." Then the "Great Supreme King of Snakes" asks: "How, O Venerable One, may all the troubles of all the snakes subside; (and how) may they (thus) gladdened and blessed, send down torrents of rain here, at the proper season for Jambudvīpa; make all grasses, bushes, herbs, and forest trees grow; produce all grain; and give rise to all juices, whereby the men of Jambudvīpa may become blessed?" The Master answers that all the troubles of the Nāgas may be calmed and they may be reborn in the Brahma-world by practicing charity. Further, they must put into action the *Sarvasukhandadā dhāraṇī*, and repeat the names of the Tathāgatas, "whose families and races are descended from a single hair-tip of Vairocana, swift producers of happiness [consisting of] a circle of clouds." Here follow a large number of names of Tathāgatas, among which in the Chinese text ² such are found as: "Tathāgata who stores up the great clouds," ³ "Tathāgata whose nature’s display sends forth the clouds," ⁴ "Tathāgata who holds in his hands (and directs) the clouds and the rain," ⁵ "Great raiser of the clouds," ⁶ "Great disperser of wind and—"

> “By the speaking of these names of the <term>Tathāgatas</term>, O snake-king, all the troubles of all snakes are put to rest; and though they are full of misfortunes, they create timely showers here in <term>Jambudvīpa</term>, and make all grass, shrubs, herbs, forest trees, and crops grow.”

> “He who desires a heavy rain must perform this rite in an open space, covered by a blue canopy, shaded by a blue banner, on a clear patch of ground; as a teacher of the Law, sitting on a blue seat, fasting according to the <term>ashṭāṅga</term>, with well-washed limbs, dressed in clean clothes, scented with fragrance, and wearing the three white stripes, he must recite it for a day and night continuously while facing the east; he must place four full vessels filled with pure blue water, and also offer prayers to the <term>Tathāgatas</term>, along with an offering of food, flowers, and scents according to his ability; then the teacher of the Law, after having painted toward the four directions with liquid cow dung on a reed, must depict the snake-king called <term>Triçīrshaka</term> (Three-crested) in the eastern direction, three <term>hastas</term> high, using cow dung: in the southern direction, the one called <term>Pañcaçīrshaka</term> (Five-crested), five <term>hastas</term> high; in the western, seven <term>hastas</term> high, <term>Saptaçīrshaka</term> (Seven-crested); in the northern, <term>Navaçīrshaka</term> (Nine-crested), nine <term>hastas</term> high..... Afterward, in a season of drought, he shall recite this chapter, ‘The Great-cloud-circle,’ for one or two days, until it inevitably rains for seven nights.”

Then the snake kings are summoned through numerous invocations. On page 309, we read that this “Whirlwind” chapter, also called “The Heart of all Serpents,” must be recited by the teacher of the Law after three snake kings and their followers have been painted with cow dung for twenty-one consecutive days: a three-crested one in the East, a seven-crested one in the West,

The same Nāga kings, except for Mucilinda and Elāpatra, are mentioned in the so-called *Anumantraṇa*, an invocation of the Nāgas found in the Bower Manuscript from Mingai, about which R. MORRIS <note>8 *Journal of the Pali Text Society*, 1891–1893, pages 63 and following, "Notes and Queries" by the Reverend R. MORRIS, number 44. > “Regarding the contents of the manuscript, folio 3 apparently contains a charm which is intended to force the Nāgas, or snake-deities, to send rain. The mutilated line 1 lists, it seems, various plants which are to be used as ingredients for an offering. Line 2 gives the Mantra for the offering... The end of line 2 and the following lines to the end of the page contain the so-called *Anumantraṇa*, a further invocation of the snake-deities, intended to propitiate them by a declaration of the worshipper’s friendly relations with various individual Nāgas. This snake-charm, which appears to be Buddhist, was probably composed in Southern India. For it mentions ‘the district on the banks of the Golā,’ which is the Godāvarī... The language of this piece is incorrect Sanskrit, mixed with Prākrit forms, which is common in the Buddhist works of *the early centuries of our era*, as well as in the Buddhist and Jaina inscriptions of the same period.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *Anumantraṇa* (invocation) from the Bower Manuscript, comparing its list of Nāga kings to other Buddhist sūtras and describing Chinese Buddhist rain rituals.</meta>

Each instance is preceded by the words "I keep friendship with," along with those mentioned in the *Great Cloud-wheel Rain-asking sūtra* in Beal’s *Catena*, those found in the *Saddharma Puṇḍarika sūtra*, and those of Southern Buddhism. Nanda and Upananda, Anavatapta, Takshaka, Dhṛtarāṣṭra, and Virūpāksha are mentioned in all these lists. Sāgara (mistakenly called Samhāraka in the Mingai manuscript) is mentioned in the first three, as well as Vāsuki, while Varuṇa and Manasvin are not found in the *Lotus Sutra* or in Southern Buddhism. Furthermore, the manuscript provides several other names, such as Naīrāvana, Kṛshṇa, Gautamaka, Maṇi, Daṇḍapāda, and others. Dhṛtarāṣṭra and Virūpāksha are the regents of the East and the West, respectively, and are also Nāga kings. Regarding Naīrāvana, according to Morris, this is perhaps Vaiçravaṇa, the regent of the North. Kṛshṇa and Gautamaka are mentioned in the *Divyāvadāna* as two Nāga kings.

Professor De Groot <note>1 *Code du Mahāyāna en Chine*, Chapter VIII, pages 148 ff.</note> provides a very interesting description of the entire rain ceremony as it is performed in Chinese Buddhist monasteries during times of drought, by order of the authorities or influential laymen. An altar is erected, usually in the courtyard before the great temple of the Triratna, but sometimes at the foot of the mountain where the monastery is situated; there, a Kwan-yin temple is often designated for these ceremonies and for the prayers for rain offered by the mandarins and the people. Once or twice, De Groot saw a shrine dedicated to Sāgara Nāgarāja, the special sea-god of the Chinese Buddhists; it was opened only during times of drought.

The altar corresponds to the requirements of the sūtra mentioned above <note>2 Compare also number 177 of the Supplement of the Tripiṭaka (third volume of bundle 3), page 380 b: 大雲經祈雨壇法, "Doctrine concerning the altar for praying for..."</note>. Dragons are painted on the gates of the four sides, two on each, with their heads turned inward. The cow dung used by the Hindus is replaced in China by a yellow-reddish clay, which is used for decorating the platform inside the enclosure. The dais upon this platform is covered with blue silk, as are the tables for the sūtras, utensils, offerings, and the chairs of the performing monks; the leader faces East, while the others face North and South.

According to De Groot, the color blue is chosen in China because it is the color of the East, from where the rain must come; this quarter is represented by the Azure Dragon, the highest in rank among all the dragons. We have seen, however, that

<summary>This page compares lists of Nāga kings across several Buddhist traditions and provides a detailed description of the ritual altar and procedures used in Chinese Buddhist rain ceremonies.</summary>
<keywords>Nāga kings, rain ceremony, Chinese Buddhism, Sāgara Nāgarāja, Azure Dragon, De Groot, Triratna, ritual altar, sūtra</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the rain-making ritual, specifically addressing the symbolism of the color blue, the orientation toward the East, and the physical setup of the altar and its tablets.</meta>

The original sūtra already prescribed using the color blue and facing the East. Furthermore, the Azure Dragon has nothing to do with Buddhism. Chinese Buddhists only copy an ancient Indian rite. Indra, the rain god, is the patron of the East, and Indra's color is *nīla*, dark blue or rather blue-black, which is the regular epithet for rain clouds <note>1 Professor Speyer was kind enough to point this out to me. One of the many passages where a blue-black color is mentioned is *Mahābhārata*, Book III, 16, 13.</note>. If the priest were to face the West instead of the East, this would align with the fact that the Nāgas were said to live in the Western quarter and that in India the West corresponds with the blue color. Facing the East, however, seems to point to an old rain ceremony in which Indra was invoked to raise the blue-black clouds.

On the eastern, southern, western, and northern tables, tablets are placed on which the principal dragons of these quarters, whose Indian names were mentioned above, are painted. These are depicted with three, five, seven, and nine heads instead of the crests or hoods of the Nāgas. Often, other tablets representing the attendants of these great dragons stand at their sides. All the dragons have waves at their feet and clouds above their heads. Finally, twenty-eight black poles with long blue flags—each with a burning oil lamp between four flower vases filled with fresh flowers—represent the twenty-eight constellations. We find these twenty-eight blue banners mentioned on page 21*a* of the Chinese text of the sūtra (Nanjō, nr 186); Bendall’s translation of the Sanskrit text, however, speaks only of one blue banner <note>2 Pages 303, 309.</note>. De Groot explains the fact that all the poles are black by the connection of this color with the North, with Yin, and with water <note>3 *Black horses* were the principal offerings to the rain gods of Japan; see below, Book II, Chapter III, § 2.</note>. This may be correct, as the sūtra itself does not mention the color of the poles, so the Chinese could follow their own ideas in this respect.

In the morning of the first day of the ceremonies, the leading priest, along with the abbot and the highest authorities of the monastery, offers incense in the great temple of the Triratna. While the dhāraṇīs of Kwan-yin are recited, the temple and the rain altar are purified by sprinkling pure water (as amṛta) upon them. Now the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas, dragon-kings, and saints may descend upon the altar without contaminating themselves. The leading monk and the abbot rise from their seats and offer incense; at the same time, the choir thrice sings a lamentation about the
