<meta>This page continues the description of the rain-making ritual, concluding a sentence from the previous page regarding the connection between certain ritual elements and the</meta>

drought and a prayer for rain, followed by an invocation of the Triratna. Then, some moments of profound silence allow the officiating monk to sink into <term>dhyāna</term> and to see with his mental eyes the Buddhas, Bodhisattvas, dragon-kings, and saints descending and listening to the prayers. Upon awakening, he orders the recitation seven times of the <term>dhāraṇī</term> of the “Light-king of the Great Wheel” (that is, the sun), in order to correct any mistakes that might have been made in the ritual. Thereupon, the monks invoke by name all the 187 Nāga kings mentioned in the <term>sūtra</term> and thrice recite the first kind of <term>dhāraṇīs</term> given by the Buddha to these kings according to the same holy text. These magic formulas are accompanied by the sound of <term>vajra</term> bells and followed by a loud noise of drums and cymbals to make them more powerful. 

Then follows the invocation of all the 54 rain-giving <term>Tathāgatas</term> enumerated in the <term>sūtra</term>; each monk holds a small incense burner in his hand, which they also used when invoking the Nāga kings. After a second <term>dhyāna</term> by the leading monk renders the second kind of <term>dhāraṇī</term> effective (given by the Buddha and recited by the monks in the same way as the first), the ceremony is closed by expressing the hope that rain may soon come, sent by the Triratna and the dragon kings. A little later, during the course of the forenoon, the offerings placed on the altar are solemnly presented to the dragons, and songs and prayers are offered to them, as well as to the Triratna and all the devas. Often, a paper figure of one of the Taoist “Celestial Generals,” holding a written request for rain in his hand, is burned so that he may take it to Heaven.

In the afternoon, the leading monk, the abbot, and as many other monks as they desire take their seats upon the altar and recite the Great-Cloud-Wheel <term>sūtra</term>. All these ceremonies are repeated daily until it rains sufficiently. If the drought lasts too long, Kwan-yin’s <term>dhāraṇīs</term> and prayers for rain are continued night and day, with small groups of monks relieving one another in all the buildings of the monastery. The main point of the ceremony is the purity of the altar and of the priests themselves; for the drought, like all calamities caused by the crimes of men, can only be stopped by pure ceremonies performed by pure priests. Particularly because they never eat animal food, the monks are religiously cleaner and therefore much more capable of producing rain than laymen.

As to the ceremonies for stopping excessive rains, called “praying for good weather,”これらの are described by **DE GROOT**

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Buddhist rain rituals, though there is a slight disconnect from the previous page's ending. It shifts from ceremonies to bring rain to rituals intended to stop excessive wind and rain.</meta>

in the same chapter. The same sūtra may be used because it has the power of ruling the rain, but these ceremonies are seldom performed on such an extensive scale. As a rule, a yellow paper tablet with an invocation of the Buddhas and Bodhisattvas who reside above the rays of the sun—mentioned in the "Sūtra of the Vajra Brilliant Flames (the beams of the sun), which stops wind and rain"¹—is erected in the hall of the Triratna, and offerings are made to them. Then Kwan-yin is invoked and this Bodhisattva's dhāraṇīs are recited, or those of the "Medicine-Master, Tathāgata of the *liu-li* (one of the *sapta-ratna*, probably the bluish precious stone called vaidūrya) light"², that is, the sunlight, and the latter's name is invoked a thousand times. <term>De Groot</term> explains this Medicine-Master to be the Eastern Sun, who cures Nature and drives away all illnesses caused by the demons of Darkness. His cult, the counterpart of that of Amitābha, the Western Sun, is based upon a sūtra which we find mentioned in <term>Nanjō</term>'s Catalogue under number 171³. This Tathāgata is the well-known *Yakushi Nyorai* of Japan. It is quite clear that he is considered to be most powerful in causing the rains to stop and refreshing the earth by his rays. Thereupon Shakyamuni, the Buddhas who are above the brilliant flames, and all the Nāgas are petitioned to grant good weather; besides the first two, the Medicine-Master and Kwan-yin are each invoked three times while kneeling. Finally, the Buddha, Dharma, and Saṅgha are, as always, praised as the refuge of all. The same ceremonies are repeated by other monks until the rain stops, and then a larger number of them celebrate the rites one last time as a sign of gratitude and satisfaction.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rain ceremonies, transitioning from general descriptions to specific historical examples in Japan and China involving various Buddhist sūtras.</meta>

continuous and heavy rains than China, ceremonies for stopping rain are frequently mentioned in the annals, as we shall see below $^1$. But rain prayers were also very frequent, and the Buddhist priests eagerly took advantage of the opportunity to surpass the Shintōists and extend their sphere of influence. Thus the *Great Cloud-Wheel Sūtra* (**Nanjō**, no. 188), mentioned above, was recited by fifteen Buddhist priests in the Sacred Spring Park (*Shinsen-en*) at Kyoto, in the sixth month of the year 875 of our era $^2$. At the same time, sixty other priests in the Taikyokuden, one of the buildings of the Imperial Palace, recited parts of the *Mahāprajñāpāramitā Sūtra* $^3$, which is very often mentioned in the Japanese annals as having been partly read in rain ceremonies $^4$. Sometimes, the *Vajra-prajñāpāramitā Sūtra* $^5$ was also used. In the fifth month of A.D. 880, the *Kanjō-kyō* $^6$, "Sūtra on Washing the Top of the Head (Baptism)," was recited in the Sacred Spring Park to stop the heavy rains.

Also in China, other sūtras are used in rain ceremonies, such as the *Vajra-prajñāpāramitā Sūtra*, the *Buddhabhāshita Sāgara Nāga-rāja Sūtra* $^7$, "Sūtra on the Sea-dragon-king (that is, Sāgara), spoken by Buddha," etc. This is logical because, as **De Groot** $^8$ remarks, according to the 39th commandment of the Mahāyāna code, all punishments for crimes committed—and drought is such a punishment—are to be removed by reciting the sūtras and vinayas of the Mahāyāna.

# BOOK I.

## THE DRAGON IN CHINA.

### CHAPTER I.

#### THE DRAGON IN THE CHINESE CLASSICS.

##### § 1. Yih king.

The oldest Chinese work that mentions the dragon is the *Yih King*¹. We read there the following explanation of the lowest line of the first of the diagrams, which corresponds with Heaven: “*First, nine: a dragon hidden in the water is useless*”². According to the commentators, the meaning of this sentence is that the lowest line of this diagram, representing the dragon lying in the deep, is a sign that it is not the time for action. Therefore <term>LEGGE</term>³ translates: “In the first (or lowest) line, undivided (we see its subject as) the dragon lying hidden (in the deep). It is not the time for active doing”. This translation is more explanatory than literal, for the text simply gives the words: “First, nine: a dragon hidden in the water is useless”. As for the word *nine*, this is explained by the commentary entitled “*Traditions of Ch'eng*”⁴ to mean the “fullness of <term>Yang</term>,” because it is three times three—that is, a multiplication of the indivisible number which represents <term>Yang</term>. As the undivided strokes of the diagrams are symbols of <term>Yang</term> and the divided ones are of <term>Yin</term>, the meaning of the first two words of the sentence is, as <term>LEGGE</term> translates, that the lowest line is undivided. The characters 勿用, however, do not mean “it is not the time for active doing,” but simply “useless”⁵. The dragon, symbolized by the lines of the diagram of Heaven because he is the <term>Yang</term> creature κατ’ ἐξοχήν, is represented by the

---

¹ <note>Book *Yuzuan Zhouyi Zhezhong* (御纂周易折中), Ch. I, *Shang Jing* (上經), *Qian* (乾).</note>
² <note>初九 (Chu jiu). 潛龍勿用 (Qian long wu yong).</note>
³ <note>Section I, p. 57.</note>
⁴ <note>*Cheng Chuan* (程傳).</note>
⁵ <note>Prof. <term>DE GROOT</term> kindly pointed out to me the simple and clear meaning of this and the following sentences.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the earliest Chinese references to the dragon, specifically within the Yih King (I Ching), where the dragon is linked to the first diagram of Heaven. It critiques James Legge's translation, favoring a more literal interpretation of the dragon as a symbol of Yang.</summary>

<keywords>Yih King, I Ching, dragon, Yang, Yin, James Legge, De Groot, Chinese classics, symbolism, divination</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the *Yih King* (I Ching) diagrams, specifically exploring how the lines of the first hexagram (Qian/Heaven) describe the dragon’s movements as a metaphor for both natural phenomena and the character of a "great man."</meta>

lowest line as still lying in the depth of the waters. In this condition, the heavenly giver of fertilizing rains is still useless to mankind. This must be the original meaning of these words, but the diviners concluded from this uselessness of the hidden dragon that one had to abstain from active doing.

The second line of the same diagram is explained by the *Yih king* as follows: “*Nine, second; a dragon is seen in the rice fields; advantage; a great man will be seen*”¹. <term>LEGGE</term> translates: “It will be advantageous to meet with the great man.” Although this translation follows the commentators, the meaning is clearer if we divide the sentence as we have done above. The appearance of a dragon in the rice fields gives advantage; that is, the fertilizing rain gives good crops. The original meaning of the character 利, which consists of *rice* and a *knife*, is apparently harvest, which was, of course, identical to advantage. Further, “a great man will be seen.” Here we see the dragon representing great (especially holy) men, who are as full of <term>Yang</term> as the dragon himself. Even in those ancient times, his appearance apparently was considered to be an omen of the birth of great and holy men, especially of Emperors, the holiest men on earth.

In the third line, the dragon is not mentioned, but in the fourth, we read that he is “*perhaps leaping in the pool*” (but not yet rising above the surface). “*There will be no evil* (咎)”². The word *evil* seems to be more logical in a divinatory sentence than “mistake.”

The fifth line is described as “*A flying dragon in the sky; advantage; a great man will be seen*”³. It is, of course, of the utmost benefit to mankind if the rain-bringing dragon is soaring in the sky. At the same time, it is an omen of the appearance of a great man.

Finally, the topmost line is explained as “*The dragon exceeding the proper limits (that is, flying too high). There will be regret*”⁴. The simplest explanation of these words is that, if a dragon flies too high, he is too far from the earth to return and the rain does not reach it, a reason for regret for himself and to mankind. At the same time, the great man, symbolized by the dragon, repents all exaggeration on his part.

1 Use Nine. A group of dragons appearing without heads is seen. Good fortune.

The *Yih King* continues as follows: "*The number nine is used* (in this hexagram). *If a group of dragons is seen appearing without their heads, this means good fortune*" <note>1</note>.

<margin>
2 Top Six. Dragons fight in the open field; their blood is dark and yellow.
3 Shwoh-kwa chw'en, 説卦傳 (Ch. 17), p. 12. LEGGE, Appendix V, p. 429, Chapter VIII, 12.
4 K'ien is a horse. Kw'un is a cow. Chen is a dragon.
5 Ch. 17; LEGGE, p. 430, Ch. XI, 17.

</margin>

The lowest line of the second hexagram, which represents Earth (坤, *Kw'un*), is explained as "*Dragons fighting in the open field; their blood is dark* (not purple, as Legge translated) *and yellow*" <note>2</note>. Apparently, a thunderstorm with dark and yellow clouds flying through the sky is described in this way. For in a passage of Appendix V of the *Yih King* <note>3</note>, attributed to Confucius, we read: "*K'ien* (Heaven) is a horse, *Kw'un* (Earth) is a cow, *Chen* (Thunder) is a dragon" <note>4</note>. And, again, in the same Appendix <note>5</note>: "*Chen* is thunder, is a dragon, is dark and yellow" <note>6</note>. The same diagram also represents *Spring* and the *Eastern quarter*, which are identified with the *Azure Dragon* <note>7</note>.

<margin>
8 A dragon lying in the deep is useless. Yang is below.
</margin>

In Chapter 11 (page 2) of the *Yih King*, the words "*A dragon lying in the deep is useless*" are illustrated by the phrase "*Yang is below*" <note>8</note>, which means: The Sun is below the horizon; that is, the dragon lying in the deep is as useless as the sun below the horizon.

<margin>
9 A dragon is seen in the fields. Beneficial power is spread everywhere.
</margin>

In the same chapter (on the same page) we read: "*A dragon is seen in the rice fields; beneficial power* (德) *is spread everywhere*" <note>9</note>. This is a clear explanation of the word *advantage* in the previously mentioned passage regarding the fifth line of the first hexagram.

<margin>
10 Dragons fighting in the open field. Their way is exhausted.
</margin>

Regarding the "*Dragons fighting in the open field*," in this chapter these words are followed by: "*Their way (tao) is exhausted*" <note>10</note>, meaning their beneficial actions are completed to the very end. Since rain is the blessing bestowed upon humanity by the dragons, this sentence can easily be explained by the fact that in a thunderstorm, when the dragons fight in the sky, the rain falls in torrents.

---

<meta>Digitized by Microsoft®</meta>

<summary>This page analyzes specific lines from the *I Ching* involving dragons, interpreting them as metaphors for celestial phenomena like thunderstorms and the movement of the sun. It identifies the dragon with the element of thunder and the beneficial arrival of rain.</summary>
<keywords>Yih King, I Ching, dragons, symbolism, K'ien, Kw'un, Chen, Azure Dragon, Legge, De Groot, Tao, Confucius, thunderstorm, Spring, Yang</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the modernization of a scholarly analysis of the *Yih King* (I Ching), specifically focusing on the symbolic role of dragons within the hexagrams and their association with natural forces like thunder and rain.</meta>

An appendix of the *Yih king* <note>1</note> says: “*The hibernation of dragons and snakes is done in order to preserve their bodies*” <note>2</note>. Here we see dragons and snakes being closely connected and regarded as belonging to the same kind of animals. This same fact is also observed in later times.

Upon considering the passages of the *Yih king* mentioned above, we arrive at the conclusion that the ideas about the dragon prevailing in China today are exactly the same as those from the most ancient times. It is a water animal, akin to the snake, which is accustomed to sleeping in pools during winter and emerging in spring. It is the god of thunder, who brings good crops when he appears in the rice fields (as rain) or in the sky (as dark and yellow clouds); in other words, when he causes the rain to fertilize the ground. But when he flies too high and cannot return, the thirsty earth must wait in vain for his blessings, and sorrow prevails. Because this beneficent being is full of <term>Yang</term>, it symbolizes those among men who are fullest of Light—namely, great men—and its appearance is considered an omen of their coming (that is, of their birth). Primarily, the greatest and most full of <term>Yang</term> among them all, the Emperor, is, of course, symbolized by the dragon. He is, indeed, the representative of Imperial power, as we shall see later.

When black and yellow clouds covered the sky and thunder and lightning raged, the ancient Chinese said, just as people do today: “The dragons are fighting; look at their blood spreading over the sky.” At the same time, the heavenly dragons caused the rain to pour down upon the grateful earth.

Even when the dragons were only leaping in their pools, no disaster was to be feared; and when a group of them, even headless, was seen in the sky, this was a lucky sign. Winter, when they hibernate and sleep in pools, is the dry season in China. But in spring—during the third of the twenty-four seasons into which the year was divided even in ancient times—the “Resurrection of the hibernating animals” <note>3</note> takes place, and it begins to rain a little. In the “beginning of summer” <note>4</note>, however (that is, in the first of the six summer seasons), “the winds arrive

<summary>This page explains the symbolic and ecological associations of the Chinese dragon, identifying it as a water-dwelling relative of the snake that serves as the god of thunder and rain. The dragon's cycle of hibernation and emergence corresponds with the agricultural seasons and serves as a symbol for great men and the Emperor.</summary>

and the dragons ascend to the sky” $^1$, for this is the time when the abundant rains fall, a blessing to mankind.

### § 2. Shu king.

In the *Shu king* $^2$ we read the following words of the Emperor Shun to Yu: “I wish to see the emblematic figures of the ancients: the sun, the moon, the stars, the mountain, the dragon, and the variegated animals (pheasants) which are depicted (on the upper sacrificial garment of the Emperor).” So we see that even in the early times of Shun’s predecessors—that is, in the days of Hwang Ti (who is said to have reigned in the 27th century BC) and Yao—the dragon belonged to the six symbolic figures painted on the upper garment of the Emperor. This was, no doubt, due to its power to bless as the rain-giving god of thunder and clouds.

### § 3. Li ki.

The *Li ki* $^3$ says: “What are called the four *ling* (靈)? The unicorn, the phoenix, the tortoise, and the dragon; they are called the four *ling*. Because the dragon is considered a domestic animal, fish and sturgeons do not flee away” $^4$. Couvreur translates *ling* as “animals that give omens,” but it has a stronger meaning, as we may learn from De Groot’s *Religious System* $^5$. Therefore, I would prefer to translate it as “spiritual beings.” The effective operation of the *tsing* (精) or vital spirit of these four creatures is indeed enormously strong, and therefore they may be justly called “the four spiritual animals par excellence.” It is no wonder that their appearance was considered to

XXVII, p. 24, gives the names of the nine banners ruled by the *Szĕ shang*, “Banner rulers.” “Joined dragons form the *k'i*, 旂, (the second banner),” 交龍為旂.</note>)”. The azure dragon is, as we stated above, the symbol of Spring, the season when “thunder resounds, lightning begins to flash, and the hibernating animals all move, open their doors (that is, emerge from their chrysalises) and begin to come out” <note>8. *Yueh ling* (Monthly Precepts), same citations as note 5.</note>.

Further, the *Cheu li* has taught us (above p. 40, note 7) that the banner called *k'i*, 旂, was adorned with *dragons joined* (that is, twisted about each other). <margin>✓</margin> The same work <note>1. Section *Ti kwan*, 地官, see *chang tsieh*, 掌節, Ch. XIV, p. 39.</note> states the following: "In general, as for the *tsieh* <note>2. 節.</note> (official tablets) of the envoys of the Empire, in mountainous countries *tiger tablets* are used, in plain countries *tablets painted with human figures*, and in watery countries *dragon tablets*. The tablets are all made of metal" <note>3. 凡邦國之使節。山國用虎節。土國用人節。澤國用龍節。皆金也。</note>. <margin><unclear>nún</unclear></margin> It is clear why the decorations on these official tablets were divided in this way. For, as the commentator CHING K'ANG-CH'ING remarks on this passage, "in the mountains are many tigers, in the plains many men, and in the waters many dragons" <note>4. The soil represents the level ground. In the mountains are many tigers. On the level ground are many people. In the marshes are many dragons.</note>. Thus the dragon symbolized the water.

A third passage of the *Cheu li* <note>5. Section *Tung kwan*, 冬官, Ch. XLII, 考工記、畫繢 (painting and embroidery), p. 5*b*.</note>, which deals with the Winter officials, says that, in painting and embroidery, "Water is represented by means of dragons" <note>6. 水以龍.</note>. <margin>✓</margin> CHAO P'UH's <note>7. 趙溥, a commentator of the Sung dynasty. Although only his family name is mentioned, and there was another commentator of the same family name, namely CHAO KW'ANG, 趙匡, of the T'ang dynasty, we are likely dealing with the former here.</note> commentary explains these words as follows: "The dragon is a divine being in the water. If one represents water without representing dragons, there is nothing to show the divinity of its manifestations" <note>8. 龍水中神物。畫水不畫龍則無以見變化之神。</note>. As for CHING K'ANG-CH'ING, he simply states: "The dragon is a water creature; it is (depicted or embroidered) on clothing" <note>9. 龍水物。在衣。</note>.

### § 5. I li.

A dragon banner is mentioned in the *I li* <note>10. 儀禮, Sect. 鄉射禮記, Ch. X, p. 48*a*: 於竟。則虎中。龍旂。</note>, where Imperial hunting parties are described. We read there: "In the frontier

<meta>This page continues the analysis of ancient Chinese texts regarding the dragon, completing a commentary on frontier rituals and summarizing the dragon's early symbolic roles before transitioning to a new chapter structure.</meta>

regions: when a tiger is hit: dragon banner." This is, at least, probably the meaning of the very short text. CHING K'ANG-CH'ING explains it as follows: "'In the frontier regions' (竟 is used here for 境) means shooting with the rulers of neighboring countries. They paint a dragon on the banner^1; moreover, it is a variegated pattern. 'Full silk' forms the banner"^2. In hunting parties with foreign rulers, a signal was probably given with this dragon banner when a tiger (the dragon's deadly enemy) was shot.

The ancient texts referred to in this chapter are short, but sufficient to give us the main concepts of ancient China regarding the dragon. In those early days, just as now, the dragon was the god of water, thunder, clouds, and rain, the harbinger of blessings, and the symbol of holy men. Since the Emperors are the holy beings on earth, the idea of the dragon as the symbol of Imperial power is based on this ancient conception.

For the sake of clarity, further texts will be treated in separate chapters according to the type of information they provide. In each chapter, however, chronological order will be observed.

# CHAPTER II.

## DIVINATION.

### § 1. Lucky omens.

The birth of great sages and Emperors was preceded by the appearance of dragons and phoenixes. On the night of Confucius' birth (551 B.C.), two azure dragons descended from the sky and came to his mother's house. She saw them in her dream and gave birth to the great sage ¹. The biography of Emperor Wu ², the famous ruler of the Han dynasty (140—87 B.C.), contains the following passage regarding his birth: "Emperor Hiao Wu of the Han dynasty was the son of Emperor King. Before he was born, Emperor King dreamed that a red hog descended from the clouds and directly entered the *Ch'ing fang koh* (Exalted Fragrance Corridor). Emperor King awoke and sat down under the corridor. In reality, there was a red dragon. It was like fog and, as it arrived, darkened the doors and windows. When the imperial harem went to look at what was happening, there was a cinnabar-colored vapor above the corridor which increased enormously and rose. After the vapor had dispersed, they saw a red dragon coiling and revolving between the rafters. Emperor King called a diviner named Old Yao and asked him about the matter. The old man said: 'This is a lucky omen. This corridor will certainly produce a man who shall rule the world. He shall expel the barbarians and thus bring lucky omens with him. Therefore, he shall be the most glorious ruler of the Liu family. But it may also signify a great wonder.' Emperor King ordered the Imperial Consort Wang to move to the Exalted Fragrance Corridor, wishing to act in accordance with Old Yao's words. Thereupon he changed the

---

¹ <note>*Shih i ki*, 拾遺記, written by **Wang Kia**, 王嘉, probably in the 4th century; Ch. III, 周靈王, p. 4b.</note>

² <note>*Wu Ti néi chwen*, "Inner traditions on the Emperor Wu", 武帝內傳, ascribed to the famous historian **Pan Ku**, 班固, who died A.D. 92; p. 1a.</note>

the name of the corridor to *I lan tien*, "Hall of the Flourishing Orchid" <note>1</note>. After more than ten days, Emperor King dreamed that a divine woman held up the sun in both her hands and gave it to Consort Wang. She swallowed it, and after fourteen months, she gave birth to Emperor Wu. Emperor King said: "I dreamed that a red vapor changed into a red dragon. The diviners considered this to be a lucky omen; therefore, he (the newborn son) must be called Lucky (*kih*)."

One of the ten lucky signs seen in the course of a single day during the reign of Yao—one of the five holy Emperors of ancient times—was a dragon that appeared in his palace pond <note>2</note>.

The appearance of *yellow* or *azure* dragons, often mentioned in the annals <note>3</note>, was nearly always considered a very good omen. Only if they appeared at inappropriate times or in the wrong places were they harbingers of evil, as we shall see below. They were mostly seen at night, spreading a brilliant light throughout the neighborhood. Such a nightly apparition illuminated the palace of Kung Sun-shuh <note>4</note> during the reign of Emperor Kwang Wu (25–57 A.D.). The former considered it such a good omen that in 25 A.D. he proclaimed himself Emperor of Shu (White Emperor) and changed the name of the era to *Lung-Hing* <note>5</note>, "Dragon's Rise" <note>6</note>. A black, horned dragon was seen one night by Lü Kwang <note>7</note>, who lived in the fourth century A.D. Its glittering eyes illuminated the whole vicinity, so that the huge monster was visible until it was enveloped by clouds that gathered from all sides. The next morning, traces of its scales were visible over a distance of five miles, but they were soon wiped out by the heavy

rains. Then one of Lü Kwang's attendants said to him: "A dragon is a divine animal and an omen of a man's rise to the position of a ruler. So you will attain this rank." On hearing this, Lü Kwang was greatly rejoiced; and actually, he became a ruler after some time <note>1</note>. Since dragons were such important omens, it is no wonder that Imperial proclamations were often issued because of their appearance <note>2</note>.

Finally, we may quote a divinatory work <note>3</note> which says: "When the initial rise of an Emperor or King is about to take place, a dragon appears in the Yellow River or in the Loh. Everyone examines its head: if the head is black, men are correct; if white, the Earth is correct; if red, Heaven is correct" <note>4</note>.

### § 2. Bad omens.

#### A. *Fighting dragons.*

From ancient times, great floods, tempests, and thunderstorms have been attributed by the Chinese to dragons fighting in rivers or in the air. Although, according to the *Yih king* <note>5</note>, "the *tao* of dragons, fighting in the open field, is exhausted"—meaning their blessing power makes the rain pour down in torrents—on the other hand, such severe thunderstorms often cause much damage and calamities. Therefore, however welcome a dragon fight in the air might be in times of drought, in ordinary circumstances the threatening armies in the sky were viewed with great fear. Moreover, the people believed the damage produced by dragon fights in rivers or in the air was not limited to the actual calamities of the present, but extended into the near future; in other words, they were considered to be very bad

<note>2 Emperor Wen of the Han dynasty, for example, did so in 165 B.C., *Books of the Early Han Dynasty*, Annals of Emperor Wen (文帝本紀), Ch. IV; compare Emperor Süen's proclamation in the summer of 52 B.C. (ibid., Annals of Emperor Süen (宣帝本紀), Ch. VIII, p. 14*a*).</note>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragons as bad omens, specifically focusing on how dragon fights were interpreted as signs of natural disasters, political instability, and upcoming warfare.</meta>

omens, foretelling floods, disorder, war, and even the dynasty's fall. As gods of water, clouds, and rain, they caused severe floods through their fights, and as representatives of Imperial power, their victory or defeat signified rebellion, war, and even the fall of the reigning House.

According to the *Tso chwen* <note>1</note>, a severe flood was attributed to dragons fighting in a pool in the nineteenth year of the reign of Chao, Duke of Lu (523 B.C.). "There were great floods in Ch'ing, and [some] dragons fought in the pool of Wei, outside the She gate. The people asked permission to sacrifice to them, but Tsze-ch'an refused, saying: 'When we fight, the dragons do not look at us. Why should we look at them when they are fighting? If we offer a sacrifice to appease them, they will leave their dwellings. If we do not seek out the dragons, they also will not seek us.' Then the matter was abandoned."

The *Yih lin* <note>2</note> says: "If six dragons have an angry fight with one another under an embankment, and the azure or yellow dragons do not conquer, the travelers will meet hardships and trouble" <note>3</note>. As we have seen above, the azure and yellow dragons especially were heralds of good fortune; so their defeat was a sign of coming trouble, probably caused by floods.

Regarding impending war and ruin, we may quote the following passages from the Histories.

In the *Books of the Sui dynasty* <note>4</note> we read: "In the Liang dynasty (A.D. 502—557), in the second year of the T'ien kien era (503), there were dragons fighting in a pool in Northern Liang province. They sprayed fog over a distance of several miles. Regarding the evils of dragons and snakes, the *Hung fan wu hing chwen* <note>5</note> says: 'These are the troubles and damages of dragons and beasts. That which belongs to Heaven is a symbol of the Ruler. If the Heavenly breath is injured, and the Way of the Ruler is wounded, the dragons are also injured. Their fights are symbols of weapons and shields [warfare].'"

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragons as bad omens, specifically focusing on historical accounts of dragon fights and their interpretations as predictors of political turmoil, rebellion, and the fall of dynasties.</meta>

KING FANG<note>1</note> says in his *Yih fêi heu*<note>2</note> ("Flying observations on divination"): "When the hearts of the masses are not at peace, dragon fights are the bad omens of it"<note>3</note>. At that time, the Emperor had just ascended the throne, and there was a riot involving Ch'en Poh-chi and Liu Li-lien. Danger and fear prevailed in the empire.

The same annals<note>4</note> contain the following passage: "In the sixth month of the fifth year of the P'u t'ung era (524 A.D.), dragons fought in the pond of the King of K'üh o (?). They went westward as far as Kien ling ch'ing. In the places they passed, all the trees were broken. The interpretation was the same as in the second year of the T'ien kien era (503 A.D.): namely, that their passing Kien ling and the breaking of the trees indicated that there would be a disaster of war for the dynasty, and it was a sign that the Imperial tombs would be destroyed. At that time, the Emperor considered holding discussions to be his only task and did not think of agriculture. His fighting generals were negligent, his soldiers idle, and the Way (*Tao*) of the Ruler was harmed. Therefore, there was a corresponding occurrence of the dragons' evil omen. The Emperor did not become aware of the danger at all. In the first year of the T'ai Ts'ing era (547 A.D.), there was another dragon fight in the waters of Li cheu. The waves seethed and bubbled up, and clouds and fog gathered from all sides. White dragons were seen fleeing to the South, followed by black dragons. That year, Heu King arrived with troops to surrender, and the Emperor accepted his submission without taking precautions. The people of the realm were all frightened, and suddenly rebellion broke out. As a consequence, the Emperor met a tragic death." He died in 549, and eight years later the Liang dynasty ended.

In A.D. 579, a black dragon was killed by a red one. Moreover, in the same year, there was a fight between a white dragon and a black one, the result of which was that the white one ascended—

Then more than ten fireballs fell from the sky, having the size of houses with ten rooms. The two dragons immediately ascended (to the sky), for Heaven, afraid that they might cause a calamity, sent out sacred fire to drive them away. Had Heaven been even slightly negligent for a moment, then within a hundred miles everything would have turned into gigantic torrents. ### B. Dead dragons.

When dragons were wounded in a battle, tumbled down, and died, this was believed to be a very bad omen. The *Books of the Han Dynasty* <note>2 *Shuh Han shu*, Ch. XVII, Sect. 五行, no. 5, 龍蛇孽, p. 2a.</note> relate the following: "On the day *jen-tszě* of the sixth month of the seventh year of the Yen-hi era (A.D. 164), during the reign of Emperor Hwan, a dragon died on Mount Yé Wang in Ho néi (one of the districts of that time). Its length was several dozen <term>chang</term>. Siang K‘iai held the following opinion: 'Considering that the dragon is a favorable symbol of an Emperor or King, and that the *Yih lun ta jen* says: "In the T‘ien-feng era (A.D. 14—19), there was a dead dragon in the Hwang-shan palace. The Han troops killed Mang (that is, the Emperor Wang Mang, killed in A.D. 22), and Shi Tsu (that is, Kwang Wu, the first Emperor of the Eastern Han dynasty) rose again (ascended the throne in A.D. 25)," this omen must be a sign of change (of the dynasty)'. In the 25th year of the Kien-ngan era (A.D. 220), Emperor Wen of the Wéi dynasty replaced the House of Han" <note>3 桓帝延熹七年六月壬子河內野王山上有龍</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dead dragons as omens of dynastic change, completing the account from the previous page regarding the Han and Wei dynasties, before moving on to the Later Cheu dynasty and the concept of dragons appearing at inappropriate times.</meta>

...died; its length was several dozen *chang*. Siang K‘iai believed that because the dragon is a favorable symbol of an Emperor or King, and because the *Yih lun ta jen* states that in the T‘ien-feng era (A.D. 14—19) there was a dead dragon in the Hwang-shan palace—after which the Han troops killed Mang (the usurper Wang Mang) and the founder of the Eastern Han rose to power—this was an omen of a change of dynasties. In the twenty-fifth year of the Kien-an era, Emperor Wen of the Wei dynasty replaced the Han.

In the fifth year of the Kien-teh era (A.D. 576), under the Later Cheu dynasty, a black dragon fell from the sky and died. The dragon is the symbol of the Ruler, black was the color of the dynasty, and falling and dying is a most unlucky omen <note>1 *Books of the Sui dynasty, Sect.* 五行志: In the fifth year of the Kien-teh era of the Later Cheu dynasty, a black dragon fell at Bozhou and died. The dragon is the symbol of the ruler; black was the favored color of the Cheu dynasty; falling and dying is an omen of extreme misfortune.</note>. So it was a foreboding of the Emperor’s death, which happened two years later (A.D. 578), and of the dynasty’s fall (A.D. 581), which was also announced by the dragon fights mentioned above.

### C. *Dragons appearing at wrong times.*

When dragons appeared at wrong times, they were forebodings of evil instead of omens of good fortune. The time is wrong for a dragon to appear when the Son of Heaven himself does not walk in the Tao, thus throwing both the Tao of Heaven and men into disorder. This was true of the Emperor K‘ung Kiah of the ancient Hia dynasty, twenty centuries before Christ. SZĚ-MA TS‘IEN <note>2 *Historical Records, Ch. II*, 夏本紀, Japanese edition with commentaries and notes, 史記評林 (Yao edition), Vol. II, Ch. II, p. 21b. Compare CHAVANNES’ translation, Vol. I, p. 168.</note> says the following about this monarch: “After the Emperor K‘ung Kiah had ascended the Throne, he loved matters concerning the <term>kwéi</term> and the <term>shen</term> and was disorderly (in his behavior, meaning he disturbed the Tao). As the virtue of the House of the Hia rulers was declining, the feudal lords rebelled against it. Heaven sent down two dragons, a female and a male. K‘ung Kiah could not feed them; he had not yet found the Dragon-rearer Family <note>3 *Hwan-lung shi*, 豢龍氏.</note>. Since T‘ang of Tao (that is, the House of the Emperor Yao) had declined, one of his descendants was Liu Léi, who learned to tame dragons from the Dragon-rearer family in order to serve K‘ung Kiah. K‘ung Kiah bestowed upon him the family name of Yü-lung <note>4 <meta>Text for footnote 4 is missing from the original source.</meta></note>.”

SZE-MA TS'IEN. "In ancient times, when the rulers of the Hia dynasty were declining in virtue and power, two divine dragons stopped at the palace of the Emperor and said: 'We are two rulers of Pao.' The Emperor tried to determine through divination whether he should kill them, send them away, or keep them, but he received no favorable answer to any of these questions. However, when he cast lots regarding whether he should request the dragons to give him their foam to store away, the answer was favorable. Then a piece of cloth was spread, and a written message was offered to them. The dragons disappeared and their foam remained; it was placed in a case and stored away. When the Hia dynasty fell, this case was passed to the House of Yin; when the House of Yin fell, it was passed again to the House of Cheu. During these three dynasties, no one dared to open it; but at the end of the reign of King Li, it was opened and inspected. The foam spread through the palace and could not be removed. King Li ordered his wives to undress and to shout in unison while naked against the foam. The foam transformed into a black lizard <note>1 玄 黿, *hūen yuen*. A young concubine in the harem, who had reached the age when one loses their baby teeth (seven years old), encountered it. By the time she reached the age when young girls pin up their hair (that is, the age of fifteen, when they become marriageable), she was pregnant. Without having a husband, she gave birth to a child, whom she abandoned in fear. During the time of King Süen (King Li’s son), a little girl sang, saying: 'A bow of wild mulberry wood and a quiver of reeds are sure to destroy the Cheu dynasty.' King Süen heard this, and because there was a married couple who sold these items, he ordered them to be seized and executed. They escaped and, while on the road, saw the child lying there whom the young concubine of the harem had just abandoned. They heard her crying in the night, pitied her, and picked her up. The man and his wife then fled to the land of Pao. The people of Pao, having committed a crime, asked for permission to present the girl whom the young concubine had abandoned to the King, in order to atone for their misdeed. fell in love with her. She gave birth to a son, Poh Fuh. Finally, the King demoted Queen Chen and the Crown Prince, and made Pao Szĕ queen and Poh Fuh crown prince. It is clear that in the passages above, the dragons were omens of evil because the Emperors did not follow the *Tao*.

In A. D. 553, a dragon was seen ascending near the Imperial Palace, and the next year a huge black serpent rose from the Palace moat to the sky, spreading a dazzling light and followed by a small snake. Calamity was predicted because of these apparitions, and the Emperor tried to prevent the evil through offerings of money², magic, Buddhist prayers, and philanthropy; but it was all in vain, for at the end of the same year he was killed³.

### D. *Dragons appearing in wrong places.*

If a dragon, a symbol of Imperial power, is born in a commoner's house or emerges from his well, this is a very bad omen for the dynasty, the Emperor personally, or one of his feudal lords. It signifies a degradation from the highest rank to a common state, and the death of the ruler or one of his representatives.

The *Books of the Tsin Dynasty*<note>History of the Tsin (AD 265—420), Chapter XXIX, number 19, Section on the Five Elements, Part 2, page 24a (Omens of Dragons and Snakes).</note> contain the following passage: "Under the reign of Sun Hao of the Wu dynasty (the fourth and last Emperor of that dynasty, AD 242—283), in the T'ients'eh era (AD 275—276), a dragon was hatched in the house of a family in Ch'ang-sha and ate the chickens. King Fang<note>Ching Fang, the famous diviner of the first century BC, mentioned above on page 47, note 1.</note> says in his <term>Yih yao</term><note>Omens of the I-Ching (the Book of Changes).</note>: 'If a dragon is hatched in a man's house, a

According to the *Ching-fang I-yao* (Ching Fang's Omens from the *I-ching*), if a dragon is born in a commoner's house, the king will become a commoner. The frequent appearance of blue dragons means that the virtue of the sovereign and the fate of the dynasty are in internal conflict with each other<note>The frequent appearance of blue dragons signifies that the virtue of the sovereign and the fate of the dynasty are mutually destructive.</note>. "According to Liu Hiang's<note>Liu Hiang (80–9 BC), a famous author and minister; see Giles, *Biographical Dictionary*, p. 501, no. 1300.</note> explanation, when a dragon—the symbol of dignity—is imprisoned in a well, it signifies a calamity in which a feudal lord is about to be secretly seized. In the Wei dynasty, there was no dragon that did not appear in a well. It was an omen of the oppressive measures taken by those men who occupied the highest ranks<note>According to Liu Hiang's theory, the dragon symbolizes nobility, and its being trapped in a well represents the disaster of a feudal lord being imprisoned. In the Wei era, every dragon appeared in a well, which was a sign of those in power being under pressure or restraint.</note>. The poem on the 'Dragon lying in the deep,' written by Kao Kwei Hiang Kung, has this meaning."

In the second year of the reign of Emperor Hui (193 BC), on the morning of the *hwei-yiu* day of the first month, two dragons appeared in a well at Li-wen-ling (a village) east of the Palace of Lan-ling. They were visible until the evening of the *yih-hai* day, and then they departed. Liu Xiang held the following opinion: "If a dragon, a symbol of dignity, is trapped in the well of a commoner, it signifies a calamity in which a feudal lord is about to be secretly seized" <note>Liu Xiang believed that because the dragon is a noble symbol, its confinement in a commoner's well symbolized the misfortune of a feudal lord being secretly imprisoned.</note>. Afterward, Empress Dowager Lü secretly killed Ru-yi, the King of Zhao <note>Compare Giles, page 553, number 1442, under *Lü Hou*: "To make the throne secure, she poisoned the Prince of Zhao, another son of the late Emperor by a concubine."</note>, and the Lü clan was finally destroyed. Jing Fang says in his *Yi Chuan* <note>Interpretations of the *Book of Changes* (I Ching).</note>: "When those who possess virtue meet with injury (that is, are put to death), the bad omens for this are dragons appearing in wells." Furthermore, he says: "In cases of execution or violent cruelty, black dragons emerge from wells" <note>Jing Fang's *Yi Chuan* states: "When the virtuous suffer harm, the omen is a dragon appearing in a well." It also says: "When executions are cruel and violent, black dragons emerge from wells."</note>.

The "Biography of Zhang Wen-biao of Chu" <note>The Biography of Zhang Wen-biao of Chu, quoted in the *T’u-shu chi-ch’eng*, Ch. 129, section on Dragons, Record 2, page 14a.</note> provides the following story: "When Wen-biao was plotting his rebellion and was still preparing it, having not yet finalized his plans, one of his followers dreamed at night that a dragon was coiled above Wen-biao’s chin. Wen-biao was very delighted and said, 'This is Heaven's appointment' (to the Throne, meaning it was a sign that he would become Emperor). He then finalized his plans, raised troops, and was defeated. Men of knowledge said: 'Since the dragon is a divine being and yet emerged from his chin, this was an omen that calamity was at work and that his <term>shen</term> (soul) was departing'" <note>Wise observers interpreted the dragon being a divine creature and yet coming out of his chin as a sign of impending disaster and the departure of his spirit.</note>. Here again, the dragon appeared in an inappropriate place.

### § 3. Dragon horses.

The *Li-ji* <note>Legge, *Sacred Books of the East*, Vol. XXVII, Book VII (*Li yun*), Section IV, number 16, page 392. Couvreur, *Li-ji*, Volume I, page 536: "The River produced the horse-map."</note> says: "The He (River) sent forth the horse with

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mythological origins and omens, specifically transitioning from dragons in wells to the "River Map" and the "dragon horse" associated with the legendary ruler Fuh-Hi and the creation of the eight trigrams.</meta>

the map (on its back)." This was the "River Map" from which Fuh-Hi fashioned the eight *kwa* (八 卦), the diagrams used in divination. The *Shu king* <note>1</note> mentions this map among the precious objects preserved at the Court in 1079 BC. Legge <note>2</note> discusses it in his Introduction to the *Yi king* regarding the well-known passage from an Appendix of this Classic <note>3</note>, which reads as follows: "The Ho gave forth the scheme or map, and the Lo gave forth the writing, (both of) which the sages copied." According to one of the commentators on the *Yi king*, "the water of the Ho sent forth a dragon horse; on its back there was curly hair, like a map of starry dots. The water of the Lo sent forth a divine tortoise; on its back there were split veins, like the writing of character-pictures" <note>4</note>. This concept, apparently based on the above passage from the *Li ki*, became common in later times, and the *San ts'ai t'u hwui* <note>5</note> provides a picture of this dragon horse.

As for the appendix of the *Yi king* <note>6</note>, quoted by Sima Zheng in the "Annals of the Three Sovereigns" <note>7</note>, neither the river nor the horse are mentioned there; it is simply stated that Fuh-Hi was the first to draw the eight diagrams.

Its height is eight *chi* and five *cun*; its neck is long, and its body is covered with scales. It has wings at its shanks, and its hair hangs down its sides. Its cry consists of nine tones, and it walks on the water without sinking. It appears during the reigns of famous sovereigns." This reminds us of the description given

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "dragon horse" (longma), specifically citing K'ung Ngan-kwoh's commentary on the *Shu King* and other historical records such as the *Tszĕ-chi t'ung kien* and *Tso-chwen* regarding its appearance as an omen during the reign of Fuh-Hi.</meta>

by K'UNG NGAN-KWOH <note>1</note> in his commentary on the *Shu King* <note>2</note>, which runs as follows:

> “A dragon horse is the vital spirit of Heaven and Earth. As a being, its shape consists of a horse’s body, yet it has dragon scales. Therefore, it is called a ‘dragon horse.’ Its height is eight *chi* and five *cun*. A true dragon horse has wings at its sides and walks upon the water without sinking. If a holy man is on the throne, it emerges from the midst of the Ming River, carrying a map on its back” <note>3</note>.

The *T'ung kien ts'ien pien wai ki* <note>4</note>, which refers to this passage, says:

> “At the time of Tai Hao (that is, Fuh-Hi), there was a lucky omen consisting of a dragon horse which carried a map on its back and came out of the Ho River. Therefore, in giving titles to the officials, he began to arrange them by means of the dragon and called them ‘Dragon-officers’” <note>5</note>.

Regarding these titles, we read in the *Annals of the Three Sovereigns* <note>6</note>: “He (Fuh-Hi) had the lucky omen of a dragon; by means of the dragon, he arranged the officials and called them ‘Dragon-officers’”. The *Tso-chwen* <note>7</note> provides the same information in an extensive passage regarding the titles of the officials of the first emperors.

The *T'ai-p'ing yü-lan* <note>8</note> describes a dragon horse which appeared

--- Page 75 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of a dragon horse that appeared during the reign of the Tang Emperor Xuanzong, as recorded in the T'ai-p'ing yü-lan.</meta>

in AD 741 and was considered to be a good omen for the Emperor. It was spotted with blue and red and was covered in scales. Its mane resembled that of a dragon, and its neighing sounded like the tone of a flute. It could travel three hundred miles. Its mother was an ordinary horse that had become pregnant by drinking water from a river in which she was bathed. This aligns with the statement of the *Shui ying t'u* quoted above regarding the dragon horse being the vital spirit of river water. The same horse is described as follows in another work of a much later date¹: "A horse with dragon scales, the tail of a massive serpent, frizzy hair, round eyes, and a fleshy crest." When the Emperor fled west from the capital, this horse entered a river, transformed into a dragon, and swam away.

Another dragon horse, which appeared in AD 622, had a scaly dragon's body spotted with five colors and a horse's head with two white horns. In its mouth, it carried an object about three or four *ch'ih* <note>A Chinese unit of length, often called a Chinese foot.</note> long. This horse was seen on a river, walking about a hundred paces on the surface of the water, looking around and then disappearing².

Finally, we may refer to a passage in the *Shih i ki*³, where we read that Emperor Muh of the Cheu dynasty, in the thirty-second year of his reign, drove around the world in a carriage drawn by eight winged dragon horses⁴.

### § 4. Geomancy.

The so-called *fung-shui* (風水, "wind and water") is a geomantic system prevalent throughout China from ancient times down to the present day. The tiger and the dragon, the gods of wind and water, are the cornerstones of this doctrine. I consider it unnecessary to discuss it at length, because Professor

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on geomancy (Fung-shui) following the previous page's introduction to the topic and its connection to the dragon and tiger as gods of wind and water.</meta>

De Groot¹ has already given a full account of its origin, elements, meaning, and influence. "It is," he says, "a quasi-scientific system, supposed to teach men where and how to build graves, temples, and dwellings, so that the dead, the gods, and the living may be located there exclusively, or as far as possible, under the auspicious influences of Nature" ². The dragon plays a most important part in this system, being "the chief spirit of water and rain" ³, and at the same time representing one of the four quarters of heaven (that is, the East, called the Azure Dragon ⁴, and the first of the seasons, spring) ⁵. "The word Dragon comprises the high grounds in general, and the water-streams which have their sources there or wind their way through them. Hence it is that books on Fung-shui commonly begin with a bulky set of dissertations, grouped under the heading: ‘Rules concerning the Dragon’ (龍法), in reality dealing with the doctrines about the situation and contours of mountains and hills and the direction of water-courses" ⁶.

Finally, we may quote the following passage from the same work ⁷: "Amoy is unanimously declared by all the wise men of the town to be indebted for its prosperity to two knolls flanking the inner harbor, and commonly styled *Hó-tʻaô soaⁿ* (虎頭山), or ‘Tiger-head Hill’, and *Lêng-tʻaô soaⁿ* (龍頭山), or ‘Dragon-head Hill’. The latter, which is situated on the opposite shore, on the islet of Kulangsu, is crowned with huge boulders poised in a fantastic manner, upon which experts have had several blocks of granite arranged for the purpose of helping the imagination to discover the outlines of a dragon on the spot. The costs of these improvements were borne by some wealthy citizens, anxious to promote their own prosperity and that of their fellow townsmen". A "Dragon’s head Mountain" is mentioned in the *Sin shi San Tsʻin ki* ⁸, where we read the following: "The Dragon’s head Mountain is 60 miles long; its head enters the water of the Wei (a large tributary of the Yellow River), and its tail reaches the Fan river. The height of its head is 20 chang <note>A Chinese unit of length, approximately 3.3 meters.</note>, and the tail goes

# CHAPTER III.

## GENERAL INFORMATION.

### § 1. Enormous light-giving mountain gods.

The *Shan hai king*¹ describes the god of Mount Chung as follows: 

> “The god of Mount Chung is called ‘Enlightener of the Darkness.’ By looking (that is, by opening his eyes) he creates daylight, and by closing his eyes he creates night. By blowing he makes winter, by exhaling he makes summer. He neither eats nor drinks, nor does he rest. His breath causes wind. His length is a thousand miles. He is in the East of Wu-k‘i (‘Without bowels’). As a living being, he has a human face, the body of a snake, and a red color. He lives ‘at the foot of Mount Chung’.” 

The commentator **Kwok P'oh**² explains this passage in the following words: 

> “‘Enlightener’ is a dragon; he enlightens the nine *yin* (darknesses, that is, the nine points of the compass at the opposite, dark side of the earth, which is a flat disk; these nine points are North, South, East, West, Northeast, Northwest, Southeast, Southwest, and the Center).” 

According to the *Hwai nan tszĕ*, it is “a god with a human face and a dragon’s body, but without legs”³.

We may quote here a passage from the *T‘ung ming ki*⁴, a work from the beginning of our era, to which **De Groot**⁵ refers as follows: “The *T‘ung ming ki* says that in the year **99** before our era, the Emperor Wu convoked a meeting of magicians and

<meta>Continuing from the description of the "Enlightener of the Darkness," this page completes the account of Tung Fang-soh regarding the blue dragon and introduces philosophical perspectives on the nature of dragons from the *Kwan tszĕ* and *Han Fei tszĕ*.</meta>

learned men, at which Tung Fang-soh spoke as follows: ‘I made a journey to the North Pole and came to a mountain covered in fire, which neither the sun nor the moon ever illuminates, but which is lit to its farthest reaches by a *blue dragon* using a torch which it holds in its jaws’¹.

Since the dragon is full of Yang, it is quite logical that he should emit light, as we have also seen above (Ch. II, § 1, p. 44). The *Yih lin*² says: “A black dragon breathes out light and turns Darkness (Yin) into Light (Yang).”

### § 2. Nature of the dragons.

In Kwan Chung’s philosophical work titled *Kwan tszĕ*³, “The Philosopher Kwan,” we read the following: “Those who, hidden in the darkness, can exist or vanish are the *shi* (蓍, a plant whose stalks are used in divination), tortoises, and dragons. The tortoise is born in the water; she is made to reveal what she knows through fire, and then becomes the first of all creatures, the judge of misfortune and happiness. *A dragon in the water takes on five colors.* Therefore, he is a god (*shen*). If he wants to become small, he takes a shape like a silkworm; and if he wants to become large, he lies hidden throughout the world. If he wants to rise, he reaches toward the clouds; and if he wants to descend, he enters a deep well. He whose transformations are not limited by days, and whose rising and falling are not limited by time, is called a god (*shen*).”

The philosopher Han Fei⁴ says: “Ah, a dragon, as an

animal, is so mild that one may approach it (be familiar with it, i.e., tame it) and ride on it. But under its throat it has scales, lying in a reverse direction, one *ch'ih* (foot) in diameter. If a man touches them, the dragon is sure to kill him.”

The Classics have taught us that the dragon belongs to the four creatures that have the most *ling* (靈), i.e., whose *shen* manifests itself in the most powerful way. The *'Rh ya yih*¹ goes further and states that the dragon possesses the most *ling* of all creatures. According to the *Shui ying t'u*² “the yellow dragon is the quintessence of *shen*, and the chief of the four dragons. If a king does not drain off ponds and lakes, their water can penetrate into deep pools, and the yellow dragons, following their nature, swim in ponds and lakes.”

Lü Puh-wei³ relates the following: “Confucius said: ‘A dragon (*lung*) eats what is pure and moves about in what is pure⁴. A *chi* (螭) eats what is pure and moves about in what is muddy. A fish eats what is muddy and moves about in what is muddy. Now I, in ascending, do not reach the dragon (i.e., I am not such a high being as the dragon), and in descending, do not reach the fishes (i.e., I am not such a low creature as the fishes); I am (like) the *chi*.’”

The *yü-kia* (羽嘉) produced ~~the flying dragon, the flying dragon gave birth to the~~ phoenixes, and after them the *lwan-niao* (鸞鳥) and all birds—in general, the winged beings—were born in succession. The *Ta tai li ki* ³ states that “the essence of the scaly animals is called the dragon,” and that “the dragon does not ascend if there is no wind.”

LI TAO-YUAN <note>1</note>, in his commentary on the *Shui king*, states that the expression 'fishes and dragons consider the autumn days as night' means that "at the autumnal equinox the dragons descend and then hibernate and sleep in pools."

The *'Rh ya yih* <note>2</note> quotes the following passage from a work of WANG FU <note>3</note>: "When rain is to be expected, the dragons cry out and their voices are like the sound made by striking copper basins. At the present day on rivers and lakes there are sometimes people who see one claw and the tail (of a dragon), but the head is not to be seen. In summer, after the fourth month, the dragons divide the regions among themselves and each of them has his territory. centipedes <note>1</note>, the leaves of the *lien* tree (*Melia Azederach*), and five-colored silk thread. Therefore, those who have eaten swallows avoid crossing the water, and those who pray for rain use swallows; those who suppress water calamities (inundations) use iron; those who stir up dragons (to cause them to make rain) use the *wang* plant; and those who offer sacrifices to K'üh Yuen use leaves of the *Melia Azederach* and colored silk thread, wrapping dumplings in them which they throw into the river. Also, when physicians use dragon bones, they must know these particulars about the dragon's nature regarding its likes and dislikes" <note>2</note>.

The beautiful gems remind us of the Indian dragons; the pearls of the sea were, of course, considered to be in the special possession of the dragon-shaped sea gods in India as well as in China and Japan. Regarding the *k'ung-ts'ing*, this is explained to be a hollow stone with water inside, or the vital spirit (精, *tsing*) of copper. Swallows are also mentioned as food for the *shen* (蜃) <note>3</note>. The same particulars are to be found in the *Nan pu sin shu* <note>4</note>, where we read that dragons are afraid of wax, and that their fat makes silk garments waterproof.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon lore, specifically focusing on the dragon's aversion to iron and a detailed description of its physical form based on historical Chinese texts.</meta>

legend to be found in the *T'ien chung ki* <note>1</note>, where we read the following. In 762 AD, a river dike broke, and every time the repairs were nearly complete, it broke again. Finally, someone mentioned that during the reign of Emperor Wu of the Liang dynasty (who ruled from 502 to 549 AD), in a similar case, thousands of pounds of iron were buried under the dike, after which the work was finished. On hearing these words, the superintendent of the work ordered the same to be done, and behold! The thundering noise beneath the ground was no longer heard at the spot where the iron was placed, but gradually faded away, and the dike was soon repaired. "The reason may be," says the author, "that the dragons' eyes are hurt by the pungent nature (literally, the taste) of iron or gold, and they flee to protect their eyes."

### § 4. Shape of the dragons.

Wang Fu <note>2</note> says: "People paint the dragon's shape with a horse's head and a snake's tail. Furthermore, there are expressions such as 'three joints' and 'nine resemblances' regarding the dragon, namely: from the head to the shoulder, from the shoulder to the breast, and from the breast to the tail. These are the joints; as for the nine resemblances, they are the following: its horns resemble those of a stag, its head that of a camel, its eyes those of a demon, its neck that of a snake, its belly that of a clam (<term>shen</term>, 蜃), its scales those of a carp, its claws those of an eagle, its soles those of a tiger, and its ears those of a cow. Upon its head, it has a feature like a broad eminence (a large lump) called <term>ch'ih muh</term> (尺木). If a dragon lacks a <term>ch'ih muh</term>, it cannot ascend to the sky."

The *Pi ya* <note>3</note> states that "the dragon's 81 scales form a number

### § 5. Male and female dragons.

<margin>✓</margin>
The difference between male and female dragons is described as follows: "The male dragon's horn is undulating, concave, and steep; it is strong at the top, but becomes very thin below. The female dragon has a straight nose, a round mane, thin scales, and a strong tail" <note>3</note>.

The *Shing i ki* <note>4</note> tells of a painter who was very skilled in painting dragons, but whose work one day was criticized by a man and a woman. They said that he did not distinguish male from female dragons, although they were different in reality. When he became angry and asked them how they knew this, they

<note>2</note> Chapter 43, "Scales I", "Dragon", p. 1a: "At the sides of the mouth there are whiskers; under the chin is a bright pearl. Under the throat are reversed scales. Upon the head is a Boshan pattern named *ch'ih muh*. A dragon without a *ch'ih muh* cannot ascend to heaven. Its breath turns into clouds, which can then change into water and also change into fire... The Buddhist scriptures (*Shih tien*) say: 'When dragons copulate, they turn into two small snakes.'"

### § 6. **Different kinds of dragons.**

The *Shuh i ki*<note>1</note> says: "A water snake (水虺, *shui yuen*) changes into a *kiao* (蛟) after five hundred years; a *kiao* changes into a *lung* (龍) after a thousand years; a *lung* changes into a *kioh-lung* (角龍, "horned dragon") after five hundred years; and after a thousand years, it becomes a *ying-lung* (應龍)."

Quite different, however, is the statement made by Liu Ngan in his work titled *Hwai nan tszě*<note>2</note>, which we have seen above (p. 65). According to him, the "flying dragons" are the offspring of the bird *yü-kia*<note>3</note> (this is why these dragons have wings, according to the commentary on this passage). The *ying-lung* are the offspring of a quadruped called *mao-tuh*<note>4</note>; the *kiao-lung* are the offspring of a fish called *kiai-lin*<note>5</note>; the *sien-lung*<note>6</note> are the offspring of an armored beast called *kiai-t'an*<note>7</note>; and the *k'üh-lung*<note>8</note> are produced by a sea plant called *hai-lü*<note>9</note>. When the yellow dragon—born from thousand-year-old yellow gold—enters a deep place, a yellow spring gushes forth; if any particles<note>10</note> arise from this spring, they become a yellow cloud. In the same way, blue springs and blue clouds originate from blue dragons born from eight-hundred-year-old blue gold; and red, white, and black springs and clouds originate from red, white, and black dragons born from gold of the same colors that is a thousand years old.

The *Poh ya*<note>11</note> gives the following definition of the principal

<margin>v</margin>dragons: “If a dragon has scales, it is called *kiao-lung*; if it has wings, *ying-lung* (應 龍); if it has a horn, *k'iu-lung* (虯 龍); and if it has no horn, it is called *ch'i-lung* (螭 龍).” <note>The *Kang-muh*, Ch. 43, 鱗 之一, p. 6b, under the entry for *kiao-lung*, quotes the text in this form without stating that it is borrowed from the *Poh ya*. The original text of the *Poh ya* provides different characters for the names of the last two dragons. These characters are not found in standard dictionaries—one being the 205th radical under the 140th, and the other being *duo* (多) combined with *zhai* (宅)—but the pronunciations added to them are *kiu* (巨 彪) and *ch'i* (恥 支).</note> In the Japanese Buddhist dictionary entitled *Bukkyō iroha jiten* <note>See above, Introduction, p. 22, note 1; Vol. II, p. 56, under the heading 龍.</note>, we find the same list with the addition of a fifth class, the *p'an-lung* (蟠 龍), or “coiled dragon,” which does not yet ascend to heaven. This dragon is also mentioned in the *Fang yen* <note>*Fang yen* (方 言), or “Local Terms,” according to De Groot (*Rel. Syst.* Vol. III, p. 1073), is “a small vocabulary composed by Yang Hiung (揚 雄), an ethical philosopher and statesman who died in 18 AD.” Ch. XII, p. 7a: “Dragons which do not yet ascend to heaven are called *p'an-lung*.”</note>, where we read:
> “Dragons which do not yet ascend to heaven are called *p'an-lung*.”

In the same passage of the mentioned Japanese dictionary, another division into five classes is given, namely: crow-dragons, snake-dragons, toad-dragons, horse-dragons, and fish-dragons <note>*Wu-lung, she-lung, hia-ma-lung, ma-lung,* and *yü-lung* (烏 龍、蛇 龍、蝦 蟆 龍、馬 龍、魚 龍).</note>. This list is found in a Buddhist work, the *Sü-men ts'ang king* <note>The *Sü-men ts'ang king* (須 彌 [Sumeru] 藏 經) is quoted in the *Ts'ien-k'ioh kü lei shu* (潛 確 居 類 書), a cyclopedia compiled in 1632 by Ch'en Jen-sih (陳 仁 錫). See Wylie, *Notes on Chinese Literature* (2nd ed.), p. 187.</note>, where we read that of these five classes, the snake-dragons are the most important; they are the “right kind of dragon.”

<note>The following paragraph is underlined in the original manuscript.</note>
The *Shui ying t'u* ⁴ says that the *yellow dragon* is the head of the four dragons, the essence of divine manifesting power ⁵, and that he can become large and small, appearing and disappearing in a moment; the *blue dragon* is the vital spirit of water. The azure, blue, yellow, black, white, and red dragons as good or bad omens and givers of light or rain are mentioned above.

The legend about the *ying-lung*, the winged dragon, which—after having killed the rebel Ch'i Yiu (the first to raise rebellion in 2637 B.C.)—could not return to the Southern peak where he used to live, which is why frequent droughts occurred afterward, will be given below (Chapter VI).

A nine-headed, eighteen-tailed dragon is mentioned in a passage of the *Lang hüen ki* ⁶, referred to by DE GROOT ⁷. There, a Taoist doctor is said to have recited this spell: "I came from the East and found a pond on the road; in its water lived a venerable dragon with nine heads and eighteen tails. I asked what it fed on; it ate nothing but fever-demons."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of various creatures classified as dragons or dragon-like beings in Chinese lore, moving from the nine-headed dragon mentioned on the previous page to smaller creatures like lizards and gavials.</meta>

*tokage* or *imori* (lizard), which is born among stones in the mountains and has earned the name "little dragon" because it was (and still is) believed to cause hail with its breath and to bring rain to those who pray to it $^1$.

The connection between the snake and the dragon is evident from the description of the so-called *t'eng-shé*, 騰蛇, a wingless serpent "which can cause the clouds to rise and, riding upon them, can fly a thousand miles. It can transform into a dragon. Although there are males and females, they do not mate. Their cry foretells pregnancy" $^2$. And Koh Hung $^3$ states that "tortoises turn into tigers and snakes into dragons." In the *Yiu-yang tsah tsu* $^4$ we read: "Dragons and snakes are considered by scholars to be related."

The gavial $^5$ also belongs to the category of dragons. The *Pen-ts'ao kang-muh* $^6$ describes it as follows: "There are numerous gavials in rivers and lakes. They resemble the group of the *ling-li* $^7$, and their length is one or two *chang* <note>A unit of length roughly equal to 10 feet or 3.3 meters</note>. Both their backs and tails are covered with scales. By exhaling, they can create clouds and cause rain. It is a kind of dragon. They live in deep holes and can fly only horizontally, not vertically. Their cries are like the—"

sound of a drum, and when they cry at night, this is called "the gavial-drum." When the local people hear it, they predict rain.

About the *shen* (蜃), a huge clam, the same work¹ says the following: “It is a kind of *kiao* (蛟). Its shape also resembles that of a snake, but it is larger. It has a horn like a dragon, a red mane, and the scales under its lower body all lie in a reversed direction. It eats young swallows. When it exhales, its breath takes the form of towers and castles, which are seen when it is about to rain; these are called ‘clam-towers’², or ‘sea-markets’³. Candles are made of its fat mixed with wax, which one may smell at a distance of about a hundred paces. Also, in the flames of these candles, the shapes of towers and steeples are to be seen. LUH TIEN [the author of the *Pi ya*, who lived during the reign of Emperor Hwui Tsung (1101—1126)] says: ‘If a *kiao* mates with a tortoise, they produce a tortoise, and when it mates with a pheasant, a clam (*shen*) is produced’”.

### § 7. Kiao lung (蛟龍).

The *Shan hai jing*⁴ describes the *kiao* as follows: “(From the Tao Kwo mountains) water comes forth in waves and flows to the South, where it flows into the sea. In this water there are ‘tiger-kiao.’ Their shapes consist of the body of a fish and the tail of a snake. Their voices are like those of mandarin ducks. Those who eat them do not have boils, and their flesh may be used to cure piles.” In three other passages⁵ of the same ancient work, many *kiao* are said to live in specific mountain rivulets.

According to the *Yang yü jing*, “Classic on the Rearing of Fish”⁶, “if there are exactly 360 fish, the *kiao lung* is made their leader, and leading the fish, it flies away.”

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the kiao-lung dragon, transitioning from the description of its appearance in ancient texts to its role in Taoist literature and classical poetry.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>

<margin>✓</margin>
From the ancient Taoist treatise known as the *Wen tszě*¹, we learn the following: "As for him who accumulates the virtue of the Tao, phoenixes fly in his courtyard, *k'i-lin* roam about in his suburbs, and *kiao-lung* reside in his pond." Furthermore, we read there: "On the highest peaks of the mountains, clouds and rain arise, and in the deepest depths of the water, *kiao-lung* are born" ².

<margin>✓</margin>
*Kwan tszě*³ says: "The *kiao-lung* is the god of the water animals. If he rides upon the water, his spirit is in full vigor, but when he loses water (if he is deprived of it), his spirit declines. Therefore I (or they) say: 'If a *kiao-lung* finds water, his spirit can be in full vigor'." The same philosopher states that "when people drain marshes to catch fish, the *kiao-lung* do not dwell in those pools" ⁴.

<margin>✓</margin>
Also, the *Hwai nan tszě*⁵ mentions the *kiao-lung* with the following words: "The *kiao-lung* lie hidden and sleep in pools, and yet their eggs hatch (that is, the young ones come out of them) on the hills." The commentator remarks: "The *kiao-lung* lay their eggs on hills and hide in pools. Their eggs come to life spontaneously" ⁶.

K'ÜH YUEN⁷, the famous nobleman and poet of Ts'u—who was banished by King Hwai toward the end of the fourth century B.C. and who composed his celebrated poem titled *Li Sao*⁸ around 299 B.C.—describes in the ninth section of this poem his journey to the mysterious K'wan-lun mountains in the West, in a chariot

<meta>This page continues the description of the kiao-lung dragon, moving from its mythological references in poetry to specific accounts of its physical appearance and legendary transformations as recorded in various historical Chinese texts and encyclopedias.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>

<margin>✓</margin>
The *Shih i ki*<note>1. 拾遺記 (Shih i ki), Ch. VI, p. 3b.</note> (4th century) tells us that Emperor Chao of the Han dynasty (86–74 BC), while fishing in the Wei River, “caught a white *kiao*, three *chang* long, which resembled a large snake but had no scaly armor. The Emperor said, ‘This is not a lucky omen,’ and ordered the Ta kwan to make a condiment from it. Its flesh was purple, its bones were blue, and its taste was very savory and pleasant.”

<margin>✓</margin>
The ancient Chinese apparently considered the *kiao*—a four-legged water animal—to be a common and dangerous creature, but later it was believed to be related to the dragon and was called a dragon itself. Thus, it became the principal god of rivers and streams.

<margin>✓</margin>
According to the *Shuh i ki*<note>2. 述異記 (Shuh i ki) (sixth century), Ch. 上 (Part 1), p. 19b: “Old tiger-fishes become *kiao*.”</note>, “old tiger-fishes become *kiao*,” and the author of the *Yiu-yang tsah-tsu*<note>3. Quoted in the T. S. Sect. 禽蟲 (Animals and Insects), Ch. 132, 蛟部雜錄 (Miscellaneous Records of the Kiao Section), p. 1b: “When fishes weigh two thousand *kin* (catties), they become *kiao*.”</note> informs us that “when fishes weigh two thousand *kin* (catties), they become *kiao*.” Another work, however, the *Yuh hu ts’ing hwa*<note>4. 玉壺清話 (Yuh hu ts’ing hwa), quoted in the same source, p. 2a.</note>, states that eggs left by snakes or pheasants become *kiao* after being in the ground for a thousand years.

<margin>✓</margin>
The *Pi ya*<note>5. S. v. 蛟 (Under the entry for *kiao*); Ch. I, p. 9a: “The *kiao* belongs to the same species as the *lung*. Its shape resembles a snake, yet it has four legs and a thin neck. It has a white ring around its neck. Large ones are several spans thick. They are born from eggs. Their eyebrows meet (交), which is the reason why they are called *kiao* (蛟).”</note> describes this animal as follows: “The *kiao* belongs to the same species as the *lung*. Its shape resembles that of a snake, and yet it has four legs and a thin neck. Around its neck, it has a white necklace. The large *kiao* are several spans thick. They are born from eggs. Their eyebrows are joined (交), which is why they are called *kiao* (蛟).”

The *Mih k’oh hwui si*<note>6. 墨客揮犀 (Mih k’oh hwui si), according to DE GROOT (Rel. Syst. Vol. V, p. 864, note 2) “a work in ten chapters by P’ENG SHING, 彭乘, of the eleventh century,” quoted in the T. S., Sect. 禽蟲 (Animals and Insects), Ch. 132, 蛟部雜錄 (Miscellaneous Records of the Kiao Section), p. 1b.</note> says: “The *kiao*’s shape is like that of a snake, and its head is like that of a tiger. Its length reaches several *chang*. Many of them live in streams and pools and under rock caves. Their voices are like the bellowing of a cow. When people walk on the shore or in the valleys of brooks, they are

<meta>This page continues the description of the kiao dragon, transitioning from its origins to its predatory habits, its specific physical characteristics as cited in the Pen-ts'ao kang-mu, its role as a divine messenger for the River Lord, and a legend regarding its transformation into a human child.</meta>

troubled by the *kiao*. When they see a person, they first surround them with stinking saliva, and after causing them to fall into the water, they suck their blood from under their armpits. When there is no blood left, they stop sucking.

<margin>✓</margin>In the *Pen-ts'ao kang-mu*¹ LI SHI-CHEN quotes the following passage from the *P'ei yuen kwang cheu ki*²: 
> "The *kiao* is over a *chang* long. It resembles a snake but has four feet, and its body is broader, resembling the beam of a railing. It has a small head and a thin neck. Around its neck, it has white tassels (a white necklace³). The upper part of its breast is reddish-brown, the top of its back is spotted with blue, and the sides of its ribs (flanks) are like brocade. Its tail has a fleshy ring. Large *kiao* are several spans thick, and their eggs are also larger (than those of other *kiao*). They can lead fish and fly. If people catch turtles, the *kiao* can escape."

As messengers from the River Lord (河伯), the god of the Yellow River, the *kiao* are mentioned in a story found in the *Poh wuh chi* (3rd century)⁴. This god wished to deprive an official—who was crossing the river with a jade badge of office—of this precious object and sent two *kiao* to seize the vessel. However, both were killed by the bold man, who, after having crossed the river three times, threw the badge into the water as a gift to the River Lord, who danced with joy and took it home.

<margin>✓</margin>Transformations of *kiao* into human shapes are the subjects of several tales. The *Wu ki*⁵ tells the following: 
> "During the reign of Emperor Ta Ti of the Wu dynasty (AD 228–251), in the seventh month of the third year of the Ch'ih-wu era (AD 240), a certain Wang Shuh was gathering medicinal herbs on Mount T'ien-tai. During the hottest part of the day, he took a rest under a bridge, when suddenly he saw a small blue boy, over a foot long, in the brook. The boy held a blue rush in his hand and rode on a red carp. The fish went straight into a cloud and gradually disappeared. After a long while, Shuh climbed a high mountain peak and looked in all directions. He saw wind and clouds rising above the sea, and in a moment, a thunderstorm broke out. Suddenly it was about to reach Shuh, who, terrified, hid himself in a hollow tree. When the sky cleared, he again saw the red carp on which the boy rode and the
