<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *kiao* dragon's ability to transform, specifically recounting a legend from the *Sheu shen heu ki* and another from the *I yuen*, both illustrating the *kiao*'s connection to rainfall and its supernatural powers.</meta>

little boy returning and entering the brook. It was a black *kiao*!”

<margin>✓</margin>
In the *Sheu shen heu ki*<note>1</note> we read about a *kiao* who, in the shape of a man about twenty years old, came to a farmer’s cottage. He rode on a white horse under a state umbrella and was escorted by four followers, all dressed in yellow robes. “They came from the East and, arriving at the gate, they called: ‘Child of Yin (the little son of the farmer, thirteen years old, who was alone at home), we have come to sit down for a little while and rest’. Thus they entered the house and sat down on a couch in the lower part of the courtyard. One of them grasped the umbrella and turned it upside down. Yin’s child looked at their clothes and saw that they were entirely without seams. The horse was spotted with five colors and looked as if it had scaly armor and no hair. In a moment a rainy vapor arrived, whereupon the man mounted the horse and rode away. Turning and looking back, he said to the child: ‘Tomorrow I must come again’. Yin’s child watched where they went and saw them treading on the air, turning westward and gradually ascending. In a moment, cloudy vapors assembled from all sides and the daylight was darkened by them. The next day a heavy rain came violently down; the water gushed over mountains and valleys, and hills and ravines were overflowed. When it was about to overflow the cottage of Yin’s child, he suddenly saw a big *kiao*, over three *chang* long, which protectively covered the cottage with its coils.”

<margin>✓</margin>
The revenge of a *kiao*, transformed into a girl, is told in the *I yuen*<note>2</note>. A man who had hit a *kiao* with an arrow met a crying girl with the same arrow in her hand. When he asked her what this meant, she said that she came to return to him the burning pain it had caused her, after which she gave him the arrow and disappeared. Before he reached his house, he caught a high fever and died on the road.

The passages mentioned above clearly show that the *kiao*, just like the *lung*, were believed to assume human shapes and to cause rain and thunderstorms. This is not surprising, for we have seen that the *kiao* were called *lung* themselves.

### § 8. Rearing and taming dragons.

In Chapter II (pages 50 and following) we have referred to the *Historical Records* regarding the Emperor K'ung Kiah of the Hia dynasty, in whose service Liu Léi tamed two dragons sent down by Heaven. This Liu Léi had learned the art from the Dragon-rearer family, and he himself received the family name of *Yü lung*, "Dragon-ruler."

The *T'so chwen*¹ gives the same legend in the following passage: "In autumn (of the 29th year of Chao kung, that is Chao, duke of Lu, who reigned 541—509 BC) a dragon appeared in the suburbs of Kiang. Wéi Hien tszě asked Ts'ai Mih, saying: <note>The following sentence is underlined in the manuscript:</note> *'I have heard that no animal is the dragon's equal in knowledge, and for this reason the dragon cannot be caught alive. Can we believe that it is right to ascribe this (his not being caught alive) to his knowledge?'* Mih replied: 'Men really do not know; it is not that the dragon is truly knowledgeable. The ancients kept dragons; therefore the State had a Dragon-rearer family (*Hwan-lung shi*²) and a Dragon-ruler family (*Yü-lung shi*³). Hien tszě said: 'I have also heard about those two families, but I do not know their origin; what is it said to be?' The answer was: 'In ancient times there was Shuh Ngan of Liu, who had a distant descendant called Tung Fu, who was very fond of dragons and able to find out their tastes and preferences, so as to supply them with drink and meat. Many dragons sought refuge with him, and he reared the dragons according to their nature in order to serve the Emperor Shun, who gave him the surname of Tung and the family name of *Hwan-lung* (Dragon-rearer). He was <note>also</note> invested with <note>the principality of</note> Tsung-chw'en, and the family of Tsung I is of his posterity. Thus in the time of the Emperor Shun, and for generations after, dragons were reared. We then come to K'ung Kiah of the Hia dynasty, who was so obedient <margin>✓</margin> and acceptable to the Emperor of Heaven that the latter gave him riding dragons: two (a male and a female) from the Hwang-ho, and two from the Han river. K'ung Kiah could not feed them and had not yet found <note>members of the</note> *Hwan lung* family. T'ao T'ang (Yao)'s family having declined, one of his descendants was Liu Léi, who learned the art of rearing dragons from the 'Dragon-rearer' family. With this, he undertook to serve K'ung

### § 9. Dragons ridden by sien, or drawing the cars of gods and holy men.

The "Traditions on the Lives of Immortals", *Lieh sien chu‘en* <sup>1</sup>, repeatedly mentions *sien* who rode away on dragons through the air. We often read also of flying dragons or *ying-lung* drawing the cars of gods or holy men. As we shall see below (Ch. VII), Hwang Ti rode on a dragon, and Yü's carriage was drawn by two of these divine animals. In the *Li Sao*, quoted above <sup>2</sup>, K‘üh Yuen's car was drawn by four *k‘iu* or by flying dragons. The Emperor Wu of the Han dynasty (140-86 BC) once ascended the Yen ling tower and, after the second night watch, saw Si wang mu, the "Royal Mother of the West", arriving in a carriage of purple clouds, drawn by nine-colored, spotted dragons <sup>3</sup>. These ideas are, of course, closely connected with those about dragon-horses—winged and scaly horses of extraordinary size—treated above in Ch. II, § 3, pages 56 and following.

### § 10. Dragon-boats.

<margin>✓</margin>
Dragon-boats are mentioned in the *Hwai nan tszě* <sup>4</sup>, where these ships are called "*dragon-boats (and) yih-heads*" (龍舟鷁首). This is explained as follows by the commentator: "Dragon-boats are big ships adorned with carved dragon-ornaments (文); <note>A horizontal pencil line is drawn through the next two lines</note> the *yih* is a big bird, the painted shape of which is attached to the prows of ships". WELLS WILLIAMS <sup>5</sup> describes the *yih* as "a kind of seabird that flies high, whose figure is gaily painted on the sterns of junks, to denote their swift sailing; the descriptions are contradictory, but its picture roughly resembles a heron". On these boats, which were used by the Emperors for pleasure

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon-boats from the previous page, specifically explaining the decorative motifs of the dragon and the "yih" bird used to protect and adorn these vessels in Chinese and Japanese traditions.</meta>

...trips, on which occasions music was performed on board. The bird was painted not to indicate swift sailing, but to subdue the water-gods, according to the commentary on a passage <margin>✓</margin> of the *Wen süen* $^1$. It appears that the ships depicted dragons with yih-heads, and that the "dragon-ornaments" were the dragon's scales carved into the sides of the vessels.

The Japanese courtiers of the eleventh century, however, who wished to imitate all the customs prevailing at the Chinese court, <margin>✓</margin> misunderstood the words of the *Hwai nan tszě* and commissioned two types of ships which they referred to by a single term: "Dragon-heads (and) *Yih-heads*", **龍頭鷁首**, "*Ryōtō-gekisu*". The combination of these two words is reminiscent of the term "*shishi-komainu*", used at the Japanese Court in the same era to refer to the images of the lion and the unicorn—not as separate entities, but as one name for both together $^2$. Therefore, I would be inclined to think that the <margin>✓</margin> term *Ryōtō-gekisu* originally referred to one kind of ship adorned with a dragon-head in front and a yih-head behind, were it not for a passage in the *Jikkinshō* $^3$ stating that during a pleasure trip in Emperor Shirakawa's time (1072—1086), "Koresue played the flute on board the 'dragon-head', but there was no flute playing on board the 'yih-head'". Regarding Murasaki Shikibu's *Diary* $^4$, where we read that the new ships were very beautiful, and the *Hamamatsu Chūnagon Monogatari* $^5$, these works from the beginning and middle of the eleventh century—as well as the *Eigwa monogatari* $^6$ (about 1100), which states that the Emperor took a pleasure trip with "*ryōtō-gekisu*"—seem to speak of a single kind of ship. The *Kagakushū* $^7$, however, which dates

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the distinction between dragon-head and yih-head ships, and moves into the symbolic use of dragons in Chinese festivals and palace architecture.</meta>

A source from 1444 says: "'Dragon-head' and 'Yih-head' are two different names for ships," which agrees with the words of the *Jikkinshō* <note>Compare the *Nambakō* (難 波 江), written by Okamoto Yasutaka (岡 本 保 孝), who lived from 1798 to 1878; Chapter II, part 2, *Hyakka setsurin*, Vol. 續 下 一, p. 636.</note>.

These Chinese ships are different from the "dragon-boats" used in China on the fifth day of the fifth month during the water festival. The latter are actual boats used in regattas, or decorative dragon-boats carried through the streets and burned at the seashore as substitutes that take away all evil influences. No doubt De Groot's <note>*Fêtes annuelles à Émoui*, Vol. I, pp. 372 ff.</note> explanation of this festival, as being based on sympathetic magic, is correct. As we shall see below <note>In this book, Chapter VI (on causing rain).</note>, the Chinese used to make clay dragons to cause rain. In the same way, their dragon-boat races are certainly intended to represent fighting dragons, in order to provoke a real dragon fight, which is always accompanied by heavy rains. The dragon-boats carried through the streets may also serve to bring rain, although they are at the same time considered to be substitutes.

As for the enormous dragon made of linen, bamboo, and paper, and carried in procession through the streets on the 15th of the first month with a red ball being carried in front of him, this was formerly explained by De Groot <note>*Fêtes annuelles*, Vol. I, p. 369.</note> as an imitation of the Azure Dragon. The head of this dragon (a star) in ancient times at the beginning of spring rose and set at the same time as the sun (the fiery ball), as if it pursued this celestial globe and finally succeeded in swallowing it <note>Compare Schlegel, *Uranographie Chinoise*, pp. 55 ff.</note>. Regarding his later explanation concerning the thunder belched out by the dragon, we may refer to Chapter IV (Ornaments), § 4 of this book.

### § 11. “Dragon-tail-road” and other words connected with the dragon.

The “*Dragon-tail-road*”, 龍 尾 道, *Lung-wěi-tao*, was the road ascending straight southward to the *Shé yuen tien*, 舍 元 殿, a building belonging to the Chinese Emperor’s palace. Along this road, visitors came to be received in audience (北 面) by His Majesty, who always faced the South (南 面). In imitation, the road before the Taikyokuden, a building belonging to the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon terminology and symbolism in Japan and China, moving from ship names and festivals to architectural terms, flora, and the legend of the Dragon-gate.</meta>

Japanese Palace was also called *Ryūbidō*, "Dragon-tail-road" <note>*Ryūan zuihitsu*, 柳菴隨筆, written in 1819 by Kurihara Shinjū (Nobumitsu), 栗原信充; Chapter IX; *Hyakka setsurin*, Vol. 續下二, p. 488.</note>.

Other words borrowed from China are the following: *Ryūteki*, 龍笛, "dragon-flute"; *ryūbin*, 龍鬚, "dragon's whiskers", a mat woven from rush <note>藺, *lin*; *Kokushi daijiten*, p. 2338, s. v. *ryūbin*.</note>; according to the *Pao P'oh-tszě* (Section 登渉, Chapter IV, no. 17) it is the name of a kind of grass produced by the whiskers of the dragon ridden by Hwang Ti. The officials who could not ascend the dragon caught hold of its whiskers, but by their weight pulled them out. Where the whiskers fell, the "Dragon's whiskers herb" grew up (compare below, Book I, Chapter VI, § 1); *ryūtan*, 龍膽, pronounced *rindō*, "Dragon's liver", a species of gentian; three of these flowers, together with five *sasa* (笹, a kind of small bamboo), formed the badge of the Minamoto Family (*sasa-rindō*) <note>*Ryūan zuihitsu*, same as above, pp. 485 ff.</note>.

### § 12. Dragon-gate.

The *Sin shi San Ts'in ki* <note>辛氏三秦記, written by a certain Sin, 辛; quoted T. S., Section 禽蟲, Chapter 128, 龍部紀事一, p. 13a: 河津一名龍門。大魚集龍門下數千、不得上。上者為龍、不上者魚。</note> says: "Lung men (龍門, "Dragon-gate") is another name for *Ho tsin* (河津, "Ford of the Hwang Ho"). Several thousand big fish assemble under the Dragon-gate without being able to ascend it (that is, to swim against the current). Those which succeed in ascending it become dragons; those which fail remain fish."

A fish changing into a dragon is represented on the altar table of the Yuh-Fuh-tien in the Fah-yü temple on P'u t'o shan (Boerschmann, *Die Baukunst und religiöse Kultur der Chinesen*, Vol. I, p. 65), and dragons trying to grasp the mysterious fiery "pearl", which is hanging in the Dragon-gate, are seen in the same temple (in the same place, pp. 46, 87, compare below, Book I, Chapter IV, § 4).

As we shall see below (Book II, Chapter XI, § 2, B), there are in Japan several Dragon-gate waterfalls, and also, in the province of Kii, a Dragon-gate mountain. The latter reminds us of the Lung-men mountain between the rivers I and Lo, not far from the confluence of these rivers <note><unclear>Cf. Chavannes, Ch. L... ... ... T. I ... </unclear></note>.

### § 13. Dragon's dens.

We read in the *Sheu shen heu ki*<note>1 武昌虬山有龍穴。居人每見神虬飛翔出入歲旱。禱之卽雨。 (On Mount K'iu in Wu-ch'ang there is a dragon's den. Whenever the residents see a divine k'iu fly in and out, the year is dry. Praying to it brings rain.)</note>:

> "On Mount K'iu in Wu-ch'ang (in Hu-kwang province) there was a dragon's den. Whenever the residents saw a divine *k'iu* (虬) fly out of and into the den, the year was dry; but when they prayed to this dragon, it rained."

Another dragon's den is mentioned in the *Cheh-kiang t'ung-chi*, "General Memoirs concerning Cheh-kiang,"<note>2 浙江通志 (Refer to Wylie, loc. cit., p. 45: 16th century, revised 1684 and 1736), quoted in T. S., loc. cit. Chapter 129, 紀事二, p. 13b: 湖州卞山有黃龍洞。頂有洞出泉、名金井泉、亦名金井洞。竇穴深邃莫窺其際。梁時黃龍見於洞。吳越王因立宮以祀。 (On Mount Pien in Hu-cheu there is a Yellow Dragon's Cavern. At the top there is a spring that gushes forth from the cave, called the "Golden Well Spring"; the cave is also called the "Golden Well Cave." The cavern is so deep that its end cannot be seen. During the Liang dynasty, a yellow dragon appeared in it. For this reason, King Yueh of Wu built a shrine to offer sacrifices to the dragon.)</note> where we read:

> "On Mount Pien in Hu-cheu there is a Yellow Dragon's Cavern. At the top, there is a spring which gushes forth from the cave, called the ‘Golden Well spring’; the cave is also called the ‘Golden Well cave’. The cavern is so deep that its end cannot be seen. At the time of the Liang dynasty, a yellow dragon appeared in it. For this reason, King Yueh of Wu built a shrine in order to sacrifice to the dragon."

Another dragon's den, mentioned in the *Kwah i chi*, will be discussed below in connection with the Indian Nāga-kings (Chapter IX).

### § 14. Dragon herds.

According to the *Shih cheu ki*<note>3 十洲記, written during the Han dynasty; p. 9a.</note> herds of dragons gather at Fang chang Island in the center of the Eastern Sea. The *Luh i ki*<note>4 錄異記, "Writings on Recorded Wonders," written by TU KWANG-T'ING, a Taoist priest who lived in the latter part of the ninth century (cf. De Groot, *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. V, p. 630, note 2), quoted in T. S. loc. cit., Chapter 129, 紀事二, p. 14a.</note> tells of a so-called "Blue Smoke Temple" situated on an island. For several days, a cloud of smoke hung over the sanctuary. Suddenly one morning, the waves rose up violently, a herd of dragons appeared at the surface and entered the Han River. The large ones were several *chang* long, and the small ones were over a *chang*. Some were yellow, others black, red, white, or blue, and

<meta>This page continues the description of dragon herds and transitions into discussions regarding the legendary properties of dragon pearls and dragon eggs in Chinese folklore.</meta>

They resembled cows, horses, donkeys, or sheep. Forming a row of fifty, they followed one another into the mouth of the Han River; then they returned to the temple. Thus they went back and forth for several miles, sometimes hidden and sometimes visible. This lasted for three days and then stopped.

### § 15. Dragon’s pearls.

According to *Chwang tszĕ* <note>1 Lie Yukou chapter: Indeed, a pearl of a thousand gold pieces must be in the ninefold abyss under the chin of a black dragon.</note>, a “pearl of a thousand pieces of gold (<term>ts'ien kin</term>)” is certainly to be found in a pool of nine layers (meaning very deep) under the throat of a <term>li-lung</term> or “horse-dragon.” The *Shuh i ki* <note>2 Chapter Upper, p. 3b: Generally, pearls consist of dragon pearls, which are spat out by dragons, and snake pearls, which are spat out by snakes.</note> (sixth century) states that so-called dragon pearls are spat out by dragons, just as snake pearls are spat out by snakes. In the *Lung-ch’ing luh* <note>3 *Lung-ch’ing luh*, written during the T'ang dynasty by **Liu Tsung-yuen**, Chapter II.</note>, we read about a dragon which, in the shape of a little child, was playing with three pearls before the entrance of its den. When a man approached, it fled into the cavern and, reassuming its dragon form, put the pearls in its left ear. The man cut off the ear in order to take possession of the pearls, but they vanished together with the dragon itself.

Another legend <note>4 *Lang hüen ki* (see above p. 74, note 6), Middle Volume.</note> tells us about a man who was very fond of wine and obtained a pearl from a female <term>sien</term> in the mountains; she said the pearl was kept by dragons in their mouths to replace wine.

**De Groot** mentions “Thunder pearls” (雷珠, <term>léi-chu</term>), “which dragons have dropped from their mouths, and which may thoroughly illuminate a whole house during the night.” “Perhaps,” says **De Groot**, “these objects may be the relics of a stone age.”

### § 16. Dragon’s eggs.

Dragon eggs are beautiful stones picked up in the mountains or at the riverside and preserved until they split amidst thunder,

This “Dragon-mother,” as the people called her because fishes (the subjects of the dragons) used to dance before her when she was washing clothes in the river, became so famous for her true prophecies that even the Emperor wished to consult her. She died, however, on her way to the capital and was buried on the eastern bank of the river; but the dragons caused a violent storm to rise and moved the grave to the opposite side of the stream. <note>2 *Taiping Guangji*, same source.</note>

She brought wealth to the house where she lived, but at last she ran away and, in the form of a snake, disappeared into a crack in a mountain rock.

...very small, and it gave a hollow sound. This egg had been found in the T'ien shing era (1023–1032) in the middle of the Great River, and by Imperial order, it had been presented to the monastery. That very year, however, a great flood washed away a large number of houses nearby, and the people attributed this to the dragon's egg.

According to a work from the sixteenth century <note>1 *Suh wen hien t'ung k'ao*, **續文獻通考**, written by WANG K'I, **王圻**, who obtained official rank in 1561; Ch. 224.</note> of our era, dragon's eggs are found in times of heavy rain. Furthermore, we read there that in 1469 a fisherman picked up a large egg, as big as a human head and five-colored, with a pointed lower end and a round upper end. If one shook it, there was a sound like water inside the egg, which was very heavy and lukewarm. The people worshiped it, looking upon it as a supernatural thing. A diviner declared it to be a dragon's egg.

### § 17. Dragon's bones, ~~skins, teeth, horns, brains, livers, placentas and fetuses,~~ used as medicines.

Among the nine ingredients of specter-killing pills mentioned by DE GROOT <note>2 *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. VI, p. 1087.</note>, we find "dragon's bones," which are "certain fossil bones to be found in the shops of leading apothecaries." There is, indeed, an extensive medical literature on the curative power of these bones, which are probably the remains of prehistoric animals.

The *Pen-ts'ao kang-mu* <note>3 Sect. **鱗魚**, Ch. 43, p. 1 ff.</note> is, as in all medical matters, the best source of our knowledge about these bones and the use made of them by Chinese physicians. According to some of the authors referred to by LI SHI-CHEN—the learned author of this standard medical work—dragon's bones are the cast-off skins of living dragons, for these animals are said to shed not only their skins but also their bones; according to others, they are the remains of dead dragons. LI SHI-CHEN, on comparing all the different views and stories, arrives at the conclusion that the dragon, although a divine being, certainly dies like other animals, and that the *Pen king* <note>4 **本經**. Under this abbreviated title, the *Shen Nung Pen ts'ao king*, "Classical work on Medicines of (the Emperor) Shen Nung," the oldest medical work, is quoted in the *Pen-ts'ao kang-mu*. The work itself is lost. See BRETSCHNEIDER, *Botanicon*</note>, one of his principal sources, is right in declaring that dragon's bones belong to dead dragons.

<summary>This page describes historical accounts of dragon eggs being discovered during floods and rains, and introduces the medicinal use of "dragon bones" (fossil remains). It cites the Pen-ts'ao kang-mu to conclude that these bones come from deceased dragons rather than shed skins.</summary>
<keywords>dragon eggs, T'ien shing era, dragon bones, Chinese medicine, Pen-ts'ao kang-mu, Li Shi-chen, fossil bones, specter-killing pills, Shen Nung Pen ts'ao king</keywords>

As to the places where they are found, the *Ming i pieh luh* <gloss>1</gloss> says: "They come from (literally, are produced in) the valleys of Tsin land (Shansi province) and from spots where dead dragons are lying in caverns on the steep water banks in T'ai Shan. They are gathered at any time" <gloss>2</gloss>. "Today," says the same author, "many bones are exported from the center of Liang, Yih, and Pa (Sz'-ch'wen province)." <gloss>3</gloss>

**Lei Hiao** <gloss>4</gloss> remarks: "Those from Yen cheu, Ts'ang cheu, and T'ai yuen are the best. Among these bones, those which are thin and have broad veins are from female dragons; those which are coarse and have narrow veins belong to male ones. Those which have five colors are the best; the white and the yellow ones belong to the middle kind, and the black ones are of the most inferior quality. As a rule, those with veins running lengthwise are not pure, and those which have been gathered by women are useless."

In **Wu P'u's** <gloss>5</gloss> opinion, the blue and white ones are good, and **Su Kung** <gloss>6</gloss> says: "Today, all (the bones) come from Tsin land. The fresh and hard ones are not good; those having five colors are good. The blue, yellow, red, white, and black ones also correspond with the viscera according to their colors, just as the five *chih* (felicitous plants), the five crystals (*shih ying*), and the five kinds of mineral bole (*shih chi*)." The meaning of the last sentence is as follows: the five colors (blue, white, red, black, and yellow) correspond to the five viscera (liver, lungs,

heart, kidneys, and spleen) and to the so-called mansions (gallbladder, small and large intestines, bladder, and stomach), as we learn from the list given by **De Groot**, *Rel. Syst.* Vol. IV, p. 26. For this reason, the use of dragon’s bones as medicines was likely different according to their colors, in respect to the color of the organ to be cured.

The preparation of the bones is described as follows by **Lei Hiao**: “To use dragon’s bones, first cook fragrant plants; wash the bones twice in hot water, pound them into powder, and put this in gauze bags. Take a couple of young swallows and, after having taken out their intestines and stomach, put the bags in the swallows and hang them over a well. After one night, take the bags out of the swallows, grind the powder, and mix it into medicines for strengthening the kidneys. The efficacy of such a medicine is, as it were, divine!” <note>*Pen-ts'ao kang-muh*, in the place cited, p. 2a: Lei Hiao says: “Whenever using dragon's bones, first boil fragrant herbs, wash the bones in the liquid twice, pound them to powder, and place it in a silk bag. As to the illnesses cured by means of dragon’s bones, their number is large. Dysentery, gallstones, fever and convulsions of babies, boils in the bowels and internal ulcers, paralysis of the legs, illnesses of pregnant women, remittent fever, and abscesses are all driven away by this powerful medicine. The large ones are several tens of *chang* or at least ten *chang* long, while the small ones are only one or two *ch'ih* or three or four *ts'un*. The bodies are all complete. I saw them as they were being gathered." ³

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon bones used in Chinese medicine, moving from their general locations to specific historical accounts of dragon bones and skins, including theories on their origin and their use as glowing treasures.</meta>

Li Chao¹ says in his *Kwoh shi pu* (“Commentary on the Dynastic Histories”)²:
> “When the spring water arrives and the fish ascend the Dragon Gate <note>Compare to the discussion above in this chapter, § 12, p. 86</note>, there are a great many cast-off bones, which are gathered by the people to make medicines from them. Some of them are five-colored. The Dragon Gate is in the land of Tsin, which agrees with the statement of the *Pen king* <note>Compare to the discussion above</note>. Are these dragon bones perhaps the bones of these fish?”

Su Sung³, who quotes this passage, informs us that in his time these bones were found in many districts of the Ho tung province.

Another work from the eleventh century⁴ tells us about a man who, on a dark night, saw a tree branch that spread a brilliant light. He broke it off and used it as a torch. The next morning, he discovered that the light was due to the cast-off skin of a dragon, which in size resembled the new shell of a cicada and consisted of a head, horns, claws, and a tail. It was hollow inside, yet it was solid, and when he knocked against it, it produced a sound like precious stones. The brightness of its light blinded the eye, and in the dark, it served as a shining torch. He kept it as a treasure in his house.

The strong light spread by the cast-off dragon skins is, of course, due to the strong Yang power of the dragons.

In 1553, when the water was very low, a dragon’s skeleton was discovered on a small island in a river; the people were all very eager to obtain one of the bones.⁵

Dragon teeth were also considered to be good medicine. The *Pen-ts'ao kang-muh*⁶ quotes Sü Chi-ts'ai⁷, who said: “Generally,

According to CHEN K'ÜEN<note>Quoted in the same place: 鎮心，安魂魄。 Regarding the *hwun* and the *p'oh*, see DE GROOT, *Religious System*, Vol. IV, Part I, Ch. CHEN JEH-HWA<note>陳日華 (Chen Rehua), who lived in the Sung dynasty and wrote the *King yen fang*, 經驗方 (BRETSCHNEIDER, ibid., p. 161, nr 338). They also cure liver diseases, for "as the *hwun* which is stored in the liver can change itself, those whose *hwun* is wandering and is not fixed are cured by means of dragon's teeth."<note>HÜ SHUH-WEI, 許叔微, who lived in the time of the Sung dynasty and wrote the *Pen shi fang*, 本事方 (BRETSCHNEIDER, ibid., p. 179, nr 588). Quoted in the same place: 肝藏魂能變化、故魂遊不定者治之以龍齒。</note> LI SHI-CHEN gives the following explanation: "Because the dragon is the god of the Eastern quarter, his bones, horns, and teeth all conquer liver diseases."<note>Ibid.: 龍者東方之神、故其骨與角齒皆主肝病。</note>

Dragon's horns are used for curing roughly the same illnesses as those mentioned regarding dragon's teeth.<note>*Pen-ts'ao kang-muh*, ibid., p. 4b.</note>

Dragon's brains were believed to stop dysentery<note>T'AO HUNG-KING, quoted in the same place. The "brain of a dragon a thousand years old" is mentioned among a hundred medicines in the *Shuh i ki*, Ch. II, p. 5a.</note>, and the *liver* of this divine animal—sometimes from a *living one*—was prescribed by some physicians in difficult cases. Sometimes a royal patient for this reason even ordered the killing of the dragon of a pond, which was accustomed to hearing the people's prayers for rain in times of drought.

### § 18. Dragon's blood, fat and saliva.

The *Yiu-yang tsah tsu* <note>Yiu-yang tsah tsu (酉陽雜俎), ninth century, Chapter XI, p. 6b: "When dragon's blood enters the earth, it becomes amber."</note> says: "When dragon's blood enters the earth, it becomes *hu-poh*, or amber."

Regarding dragon's fat, we learn from the *Shih i ki* <note>Shih i ki (拾遺記), fourth century, Chapter X, Section on Mount Fang-chang, p. 3b.</note> that a tower, lit by means of it, spread such a brilliant light that it was seen at a distance of a hundred miles. Some people said this light was a lucky omen and worshipped it from afar. The wick was made of "fire-washed cloth" (asbestos cloth which can be cleaned by fire), twisted into a rope.

With regard to the dragon's saliva, we read the following in the *Pen-ts'ao kang-muh* <note>In the place cited above, p. 5a.</note>: "**WANG KI** <note>Wang Ki (汪機), a celebrated physician of the 16th century, author of the Pen-ts'ao hui-</note> says: 'Perfume is made from the saliva spat out by dragons.' **LI SHI-CHEN** (the author himself) says: 'Dragon's saliva is seldom used as a medicine; it is only mixed into perfumes. It is said that it can bind camphor..."

The *Yiu hwan ki wen* <note>遊宦紀聞 (*Yiu hwan ki wen*), written by Chang Shi-nan (張世南) during the Song dynasty; Chapter VII; quoted in the *T’u-shu chi-ch’eng* (T.S.), in the same place as above, Chapter 130, Miscellaneous Records (雜錄), p. 5a.</note> informs us that the most precious of all perfumes is dragon's saliva, and that the inhabitants of Ta-shih land used to watch the vapors arising for half a year or even two or three years from the same spot of the sea. When they vanished, this was a sign that the dragons which had been sleeping there all the time had gone away. Then the people went to the spot in order to gather the saliva of those dragons. According to another explanation, found in the same passage, the dragons lived in whirlpools in the open sea. The saliva which they emitted was hardened by the sun, and these hard pieces were blown ashore by the wind. When fresh it was white, gradually it became purple, and finally black (amber <note>Likely referring to ambergris.</note>, generally considered to be the excrement of cachalots—that is, sperm whales—is yellowish).

This perfume reminds us of the “Dragon-fight perfume,” mentioned in the *Tsu t’ing shi yuen* <note>祖庭事苑 (*Tsu t’ing shi yuen*), quoted in the Japanese Buddhist dictionary titled *Bukkyō iroha jiten*, Vol. II, p. 63, under the entry *Ryū-tō* (龍鬪).</note>, which is said to be produced by fighting dragons. One pill of it makes a large cloud of perfume arise.

According to the *Lang hüen ki* <note>瑯嬛記 (*Lang hüen ki*), written by Yi Shi-zhen (伊世珍) during the Yuan dynasty; T.S., in the same place as above, Chapter 131, p. 1b.</note> the Emperor Shun used the saliva of a purple dragon as ink in writing the names of holy ministers on tablets of jade, those of sages on tablets of gold, and those of talented ministers on tablets of quartz crystal; those of ordinary ministers were written with ordinary ink on tablets of wood. In order to obtain the saliva, he ordered Yü Hu to raise a purple dragon. The latter daily made the animal salivate by holding a swallow, which he had cooked (the favorite food of the dragons, see above, p. 68), before it without immediately giving it to the dragon to eat. This made the dragon’s mouth water, and a large quantity of saliva dripped down. Then Yü Hu filled a vessel with it, whereupon he gave the swallow to the

# CHAPTER IV.

## Ornaments.

### § 1. Symbols of Imperial dignity and fertilizing rain, represented on garments, honorary gates, coffins etc.

As we have seen above (Ch. I, § 2, p. 39), the *Shu king* states that the dragon was among the symbolic figures depicted on the Emperor's upper sacrificial garment. It is not surprising that this divine giver of rain—who is also a symbol of a good sovereign and his prosperous government—should be represented among the Imperial ornaments.
The so-called *shah* (翣) are described by <term>De Groot</term>¹ as square wooden boards covered with white linen, with handles five feet long. In ancient times, these were carried behind the funeral carriages of nobles and were placed inside the grave once the coffin had been lowered. These *shah* displayed the rank of the nobles through symbolic figures. "The Qianlong edition of the Three Rituals suggests that the two *shah* which the Son of Heaven had in addition to the six of a feudal prince were painted with a dragon, the characteristic symbol of the imperial dignity" ².
Four pedestals of the five-part decorative gate at the Imperial Ming tombs "display, on every face, an Imperial Dragon, soaring in the midst of the usual emblems accompanying this divine distributor of fertilizing rains, namely clouds and stars" ³. "The shaft of each (of the four columns in the continuation of the diagonals of the tablet-house in the avenue leading to the Ming Tombs) is sculpted with a gigantic dragon, coiling itself around it as if climbing the skies" ⁴.
With regard to honorary gates, <term>De Groot</term> remarks that the tablet placed perpendicularly underneath their highest roof,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon motifs in Imperial architecture and transitions into their use on funeral items and garments, as described by De Groot and historical Chinese texts.</meta>

...displaying the characters 御旨, “By Imperial Decree,” or 聖旨, “By decree of the Holy One,” is supported by a pair of dragons, “the symbols of the blessed reign of the Son of Heaven”¹.

The azure dragon, the symbol of the eastern quarter in ancient China, was to be seen on the left side of the coffins of high-ranking officials in the Han dynasty, while on the right side, a white tiger represented the West. We learn this from a passage in the *Books of the Early Han Dynasty*², quoted by DE GROOT³, who also refers to <margin>✓</margin> the *Books of the Later Han Dynasty*⁴, which state that the imperial coffins “used to be decorated and painted with a sun, a moon, a bird, a tortoise, a dragon, and a tiger.” This was also the case in the T‘ang dynasty⁵. At the present day, the use of ornamental dragons is not limited to the funerals of Emperors or high officials, but even common people are allowed to enjoy their blessing power. “On the front curtain⁶ (of the catafalque) are a pair of dragons rising out of the waves, surrounded by clouds and with a sun between them; the back displays a tiger or unicorn, the top exhibits dragons, various ornamental flowers, and figures representing clouds. Thanks to these clouds and to the dragons which produce them in their role as water-gods, the greatest blessings which the Universe can bestow—namely, fertilizing rains causing crops to grow and so providing food, clothing, and wealth—surround the dead”⁷. The funeral clothes for women in Amoy, called the “dragon-petticoat”⁸, “dragon-mantle”⁹, and “clouds-mantilla”¹⁰, are adorned with embroidered dragons amidst clouds, bats, phoenixes, stags, tortoises, and cranes—emblems of fertilizing rains, old age, joy, financial profits, and happiness¹¹.

### § 2. Nine different kinds of dragons, used as ornaments.

A well-known work from the end of the sixteenth century, the *Wuh tsah tsu*⁴, informs us about the nine different offspring of the dragon, whose shapes are used as ornaments according to their nature. The *p'u-lao*⁵, dragons which like to cry out, are represented on the tops of bells, serving as handles. The *szĕ-niu*⁶, which like music, are used to adorn musical instruments. The *ch'i-wen*⁷, which like swallowing, are placed on both ends of the ridgepoles of roofs (*to swallow all evil influences*). The *chao-fung*⁸, lion-like beasts which like precipices, are placed on the four corners of roofs. The *ai-hwa*⁹, which like to kill, serve as ornaments on sword-hilts. The *hi-pi*¹⁰, which have the shape of the *ch'i-lung*¹¹ and are fond of literature, are represented on the sides of grave monuments. The *pi-han*¹², which like litigation, are placed over prison gates (in order to keep guard). The *swan-i*¹³, which like to sit down, are represented upon the bases of Buddhist idols (under the feet of the Buddhas or Bodhisattvas). The *pa-hia*¹⁴, finally, are large tortoises which like to carry heavy objects and are placed under grave monuments.¹⁵

Furthermore, the same author lists nine other kinds of dragons—there are so many, he says, because the dragon’s nature is very promiscuous, so that he mates with all animals¹—which are depicted as ornaments on various objects or buildings according to their preferences: prisons, water, the pungent smell of newly caught fish or newly killed meat, wind and rain, ornaments, smoke, closing the mouth (used for decorating keyholes), standing in high places (placed on roofs), and fire.

### § 3. Ornaments used by Wu-ist priests and mediums.

De Groot's description of the religious dress of the Wu-ist priests (the *sai kong*² of Amoy) contains the following passage. “On the left and right (of the mountain range, representing the continents of the world, embroidered on the back of the principal vestment of the *sai kong*), a large dragon rises high above the waves in an attitude suggesting a soaring motion toward the continent; these animals symbolize the *fertilizing rains*, and are therefore surrounded by gold-thread figures which represent *clouds*, and some which resemble *spirals* and denote *rolling thunder*....... There is also a broad border of blue silk around the neck, stitched with *two ascending dragons which are breathing out a ball, probably representing thunder*”³.

A similar secondary vestment of a *sai kong* is decorated with “an oblong piece of blue silk, embroidered with two dragons which breathe out a ball, as well as a continent and waves over which they soar”⁴.

“It is then obvious that the priestly dress of the *sai kong* is a magical dress. The priest who wears it is invested by it with the power of the Order of the World itself, and is thus enabled to restore that Order whenever, by means of sacrifices and magical ceremonies, he is averting unseasonable and disastrous events, such as *drought, untimely and excessive rainfall, or eclipses*. Besides, since the Tao is the mightiest power against the demon—

### § 4. The dragons and the ball.

As to the ball "belched out by the two dragons," this reminds us at once of the Dragon festival on the 15th day of the first month; the ball carried in front of the dragon on that day might also be explained in the same way, that is, as thunder belched out by the dragon, and not as the sun being pursued by him. This fact was orally pointed out to me by Prof. De Groot himself <note>See above, this Book, Chapter 4, Section 10.</note>. The ball between the two dragons is often depicted as a spiral, and in an ancient charm represented in Koh Hung's *Pao P'oh-tszĕ* (17th section), "a spiral denotes the rolling of thunder from which issues a flash of lightning" <note>Religious System, Vol. VI, p. 1036, Figure 3.</note>. "In the sign expressing lightning, the projecting stroke signifies the flash; therefore its effect as a charm is indefinitely increased by lengthening that"

<summary>This page describes the use of dragon embroidery on the ritual garments and equipment of spirit mediums (ki tông), emphasizing their connection to the Emperor of Heaven. It also explores the symbolism of the "dragon and the ball," arguing the ball represents thunder rather than the sun.</summary>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic representation of thunder and its relation to dragon iconography and Japanese ornaments, specifically focusing on the mitsu-tomoe and the work of Friedrich Hirth.</meta>

stroke so that it looks like a spiral, which at the same time represents the rolling of thunder."¹

This theory agrees with Hirth’s explanation of the "Triquetrum" in connection with the dragon in Chinese and Japanese ornaments². Hirth identifies the "Triquetrum"—that is, the well-known three-comma-shaped figure, the Japanese *mitsu-tomoe*—with the ancient spiral representing thunder. He provides a Japanese picture of the thunder god with his drums, all emitting flames and decorated with the *mitsu-tomoe*. But this ornament is not at all limited to the drums of the thunder god³; on the contrary, it is very frequently seen even on the drums beaten by children at the Nichiren festival in October. At many Japanese temple festivals that have no connection whatsoever with the thunder god or the dragon, the same ornament is seen on lanterns and flags. Hirth explains its frequent appearance on tiles as a means of warding off lightning, based on the rule "*similia similibus*" (like attracts like). This is contrary to the use of "sympathetic magic," which is very common in the Far East⁴; according to this practice, the symbol of thunder would not avert thunder but attract it, thereby destroying and driving away evil influences. Apparently, both ideas are found side by side, for images of dragons were used to attract them, thus causing rain and thunder; yet at the same time, the thunder god of Mount Atago (with whom Shōgun Jizō was identified as Atago Gongen) was worshipped as the primary protector against fire. But the symbol of thunder on the tiles may also serve to drive away all evil influences from the buildings, similar to the dragons represented on both ends of the ridgepoles mentioned above (p. 101).

Hirth provides a picture from a Japanese work on ornaments entitled *Nairyu kira ga ōsa*, but the ancient Chinese "Triquetrums" (numbers 23, 25, 26, and 27) are different from the Japanese forms. The former have a circle in the center and five or eight commas, all placed separately and turned toward the center (except in number 23, where they originate from the center), while the latter consist of two or three black commas interlaced with white and often joined in the center. Yet the turning motion is evident in all,

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Friedrich Hirth's theories regarding the tomoe symbol, comparing Japanese and Chinese interpretations of thunder and its relationship to the dragon motif.</meta>

and the more I reflect upon it, the more I feel inclined to accept HIRTH'S explanation of the *mitsu-tomoe* and *futatsu-tomoe* (two commas) as the rolling thunder. Its frequent appearance on lanterns, flags, tiles, and, in olden times, on the *tomo* or leather shield worn around the wrist by archers, and its frequent use as a badge of arms may be explained by its magic power, averting evil and, in some cases, bringing fertilizing rains. I formerly believed it to be the Yang and Yin symbol, the third comma being the *T'ai Kih* (太極, the primordium, from which Yang and Yin emanate). This primordium, which in China is represented by the whole figure, was perhaps mistakenly represented by the Japanese by means of a third comma ¹. Yang and Yin, Light and Darkness, however, are represented by one white and one black figure, somewhat resembling commas and forming a circle together. It would be very strange if the ancient Japanese, who closely imitated the Chinese models, had altered this symbol in such a way that its fundamental meaning was lost; for replacing the two white and black commas with two or three black ones would have had this effect. Moreover, in Japanese divination, based on the Chinese diagrams, the original Chinese symbol of Yang and Yin is always used and placed in the midst of the eight diagrams. Thus the *futatsu-tomoe* and *mitsu-tomoe* are apparently quite different from this symbol, and HIRTH rightly identifies them with the ancient Chinese spiral, representing thunder. Moreover, I found the same explanation of the *tomoe* in the Japanese work *Shiojiri* ², which gives a picture of two kinds of spirals, ancient symbols of thunder and clouds. Finally, on Japanese prints the dragon is often accompanied by a huge spiral, representing the thunderstorm caused by him.

Is the ball, so often seen in connection with the dragon, and often represented as a spiral emitting flames or as a ball upon which something like a spiral is delineated, identical with the spiral denoting thunder? HIRTH and DE GROOT suppose so. The latter, considering the dragon's nature as a thunder god, arrived at the conclusion that the dragon must *belch out* the ball instead of swallowing it, for why should he, who causes thunder, pursue it and try to swallow it? HIRTH ³ speaks about a dragon which with his claw is putting the thunder into rotation. This is,

<note>2 鹽尻, written by AMANO NOBUKAGE, 天野信景, who lived 1660—1733; new edition (1907), Chapter XXXI, p. 497.</note>
<note>3 Loc. cit. p. 233.</note>

<summary>The author argues that the Japanese tomoe symbol represents rolling thunder rather than the Chinese Yang and Yin, supporting this with historical evidence and its association with dragons and protective magic. He also explores whether the dragon's pearl is actually a symbol for thunder that the dragon creates rather than consumes.</summary>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the symbolic meaning of the dragon and the spiral or ball, moving from the concept of thunder to the theory that the "ball" represents the moon.</meta>

However, this is not the ordinary way of representing the dragon with the ball or spiral. *Two dragons flying with open mouths toward a ball or spiral between them*—this is the most frequent and apparently the most ancient representation. Artists, especially those of later times, often varied this subject, so that we sometimes see more than two dragons rushing toward one ball, or one dragon trying to swallow it or having caught it with his claw; sometimes there are even two balls and only one dragon. But they never give the impression of *belching* out the ball; their whole attitude, on the contrary, indicates their eagerness in trying to catch and swallow it. Moreover, how can *two* dragons belch out *one* ball? And the dragon of the festival constantly follows the ball with his mouth, apparently in order to swallow it. Yet I was inclined to accept De Groot's theory, although it was very difficult to make it agree with the eager attitude of the dragons, until Mr. Kramp was kind enough to point out his own opinion on this subject to me. After drawing my attention to Hirth's paper, mentioned above, he showed me a small Chinese picture, represented in Blacker's *Chats on Oriental China* (London, 1908), on page 54, where we see two dragons rushing toward a fiery, spiral-shaped ball, under which the following
<margin>✓</margin>
characters are to be read: 兩竜朝月, "A couple of dragons facing the moon." The moon! These were the first written characters I ever saw regarding this interesting subject, for the sea of texts concerning the dragon, ancient and modern, did not provide a single word. Leaving aside the character 朝, which is apparently not well chosen to denote the aggressive attitude of the dragons, we have only to consider the character 月.

Would it be absurd to represent dragons trying to swallow the moon? Not in the least, for the dragons are, as we have seen above, the clouds, and the ancient Chinese may easily have imagined that these dragons, quickly approaching and covering the moon, actually devoured it. When they did so, the fertilizing rain soon trickled down upon the thirsty earth, a great blessing to mankind. For this reason, they might be represented so often trying to swallow the moon—namely, as a symbol of fertilizing rains. Owing to the close connection between the moon and the water, the moon, having been swallowed by the dragon, might have been believed to strengthen the rain-giving power of the latter. The dragon of the festival, chasing the moon, might be carried along the streets in order to cause rain by sympathetic magic.

---

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the symbolic meaning of the "ball" or "pearl" in Chinese iconography, moving from the concept of the moon to its associations in Buddhist and Taoist contexts.</meta>

The Chinese themselves, however, mostly call the ball a “precious pearl.” We find it explained this way in BOERSCHMANN’s highly interesting work on *P'u t'o shan*¹, where a gilded glass ball is said to hang from the center of the roof of the Great Hall of the Buddhist temple Fa(h)-yü-sze (法雨寺, “Temple of the Rain of the Law”), while eight dragons, carved around the surrounding “hanging pillars,” eagerly stretch their claws toward the “pearl of perfection”². This term sounds Buddhist and is appropriate for the Buddhist surroundings, as well as the number of eight dragons, which is indeed fixed by the form of the roof, but is also found on the staircase of the Yü(h)-fo(h)-tien (p. 57). Dragons trying to seize a fiery “pearl” hanging in a gate (the Dragon-gate, cf. above, p. 86) are represented twice in the same temple (pp. 46, 87). Setting aside BOERSCHMANN’s fantastic ideas about the “dragons playing with the pearl” (p. 43), we may be sure that Chinese Buddhists, identifying the dragon with the Nāga, also identified the ball with their *cintāmaṇi* or precious pearl that grants all desires. The question arises: “Was the ball originally also a pearl, not of Buddhism but of Taoism?”

Mr. KRAMP pointed out to me that the character 玥, composed of *jewel* and *moon*, though not found in the dictionaries of WELLS WILLIAMS, GILES, or COUVREUR, is given in the K'ang-hi dictionary. I also found it in the Japanese lexicon entitled *Kanwa daijiten* (p. 852), explained as a “divine pearl” (神珠), and with the Sino-Japanese pronunciations *getsu* and *gwachi*. This is evidently based upon the K'ang-hi dictionary, where we read under the entry: <margin>✓</margin> 魚厥切、音月、神珠也。 The same pronunciation and meaning are given in the lexicon entitled *Tszĕ-wei*. This sacred <insert>yueh pearl</insert> probably owes its holiness to its connection with the moon, for the second part of the character 玥 may not only form the phonetic element, but it may indicate that this is “the pearl of the moon,” as there is also a “pearl of the bright moon” (明月之珠, COUVREUR’S Dictionary under the entry 月). It is possible that in the short sentence mentioned above: 兩竜朝月, the last character has taken the place of the fuller form 玥, in which case the two dragons would be said to “face the moon-pearl.”

---

2 Dragons and pearl: pp. 18, 35, 57, 59, 77, 124. One dragon with the pearl in his claw, other dragons flying from both sides to the spot, p. 35.

<summary>This page explores the identification of the dragon's "ball" as a "precious pearl" or Buddhist cintāmaṇi, while also investigating a Taoist lunar origin. The author uses linguistic evidence from the K'ang-hi dictionary to suggest that the pearl specifically represents the moon.</summary>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the symbolic meaning of the "ball" or "pearl" in Chinese iconography, specifically addressing the difficulties of the "moon theory" versus the "thunder theory" regarding the dragon's pearl.</meta>

Difficult points in the moon theory are the red color of the ball and its spiral-shaped form. If it is a *pearl*, however, representing the moon or at least closely connected with it, the red color may represent the luster of this brilliant, fiery gem, which in the temple on P'u t'o shan, mentioned above, is represented by a glass ball covered with gold. The red ball, carried by the Dragon girl in the Hall of the Law of the same temple (BOERSCHMANN, l. l., p. 122, no. 7) is evidently also a pearl. The spiral is much used in delineating the sacred pearls of Buddhism, so that it might have served also to design those of Taoism; although I must acknowledge that the spiral of the Buddhist pearl goes upwards, while the spiral of the dragon is flat.

We know the close connection of dragons and pearls in both religions. This connection is quite logical, for the masters of the sea are, of course, the possessors and guardians of its treasures. When the clouds approached and covered the moon, the ancient Chinese may have thought that the dragons had seized and swallowed this pearl, more brilliant than all their pearls of the sea.

These are, however, all mere suppositions. The only facts we know are: the eager attitude of the dragons, ready to grasp and swallow the ball; the ideas of the Chinese themselves as to the ball being the moon or a pearl; the existence of a kind of sacred “moon-pearl”; the red color of the ball, its emitting flames, and its spiral-like form. As the three last facts are in favor of the thunder theory, I should be inclined to prefer the latter. Yet I am convinced that the dragons do not *belch out* the thunder. If their trying to grasp or swallow the thunder could be explained, I should immediately accept the theory concerning the thunder-spiral, especially on account of the flames it emits. But I do not see the reason why the god of thunder should persecute thunder itself. Therefore, after having given the above facts so that the reader may take them into consideration, I feel obliged to say: “non liquet” <note>Latin for "it is not clear."</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dragon's relationship with natural phenomena, transitioning from the symbolic "pearl" and "moon" theories to the dragon's role as a deity of thunder, clouds, and rain.</meta>

# CHAPTER V.

## CAUSING RAIN, THUNDER AND STORM.

### <u>§ 1. The gods of thunder, clouds and rain.</u>

The Classics have taught us that the dragon is thunder and, at the same time, a water animal related to the snake, sleeping in pools during winter and rising in spring. When autumn comes with its dry weather, the dragon descends and dives into the water to remain there until spring arrives again. When thunderclaps were heard and a little rain fell during the first month of the year, the ancients were convinced that this was the work of the dragons, who appeared in the sky in the form of dark clouds. If our interpretation of the words of the *Yih king* is correct, the "advantage" they provided when they were seen soaring over the rice fields, and the "blessing power then spread by them everywhere," was nothing but the fertilizing rain they poured down upon the earth. In later texts, at any rate, we have seen them clearly identified as the gods of clouds and rain, <u>whose breath turned into clouds</u> <gloss>1</gloss> and whose power manifested itself in heavy rains. KOH HUNG <gloss>2</gloss>, for example, in the *Pao P'oh tszě*, states the following: "If on a *yin* day there is a being in the mountains who calls himself a 'forester,' it is a tiger... and if on a *ch'en* day a being calls himself 'Rain-master,' it is a dragon... If one only knows these, their animal names, they cannot do him any harm." The tiger, indeed, is the god of the mountains and woods, just as <u>the dragon is the divinity of water and rain.</u>

---

**1** Compare the *Rh ya yih*, quoting WANG FU, above, Book I, Ch. III, § 2, p. 66; HAN YÜ, 韓愈, (A.D. 768—824), quoted T. S., Sect. 禽蟲, Ch. 127, p. 8b, says the same: **龍嘘氣成雲.**

**2** Ch. IV, Sect. 登涉, quoted by DE GROOT, *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. V, p. 601: **山中寅日有自稱虞吏者虎也。...... 辰日稱雨師者龍也。...... 但知其物名則不能為害也。**

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dragon’s identity and its relationship to natural forces, moving from its role as a god of clouds and rain to specific mythological entities like the khwei and the drought-causing demons that oppose them.</meta>

According to the *Kwoh yü* ^1, Confucius stated that “apparitions (怪, ‘strange beings’) in the water are called *lung* (龍) and *wang-siang* (罔象), while apparitions between trees and rocks are called *khwei* (夔) and *wang-liang* (魍 魎)”. As to these *khwei*, we learn from <term>De Groot</term> ^2, who quotes the *Shwoh wen* ^3 and the *Shan hai king* ^4, that this is a class of one-legged beasts or *dragons* with human faces, which were imagined in ancient China to be amphibious and to cause wind and rain. The *Shan hai king*, as quoted by <term>De Groot</term>, describes them as follows:

> “In the Eastern seas is a Land of rolling Waves, extending seaward over seven thousand miles. There certain animals live, shaped like cows with *blue* bodies, but hornless and one-legged. Whenever they leave or enter the waters, winds are sure to blow, and rains to fall. Their glare is like that of the sun and the moon, and their voice is like thunder. They are named *khwei*. The Emperor Hwang caught some and made drums of their hides, which, when beaten with bones of the ‘thunder-beast’, resounded over a distance of five hundred miles, and thus struck the world under heaven with awe.”

“In this description,” says <term>De Groot</term>, “we immediately recognize the *lung* or Dragon, China’s god of Water and Rain.”

Further, <term>De Groot</term> ^5 quotes the *Tszĕ puh yü* ^6, which states the following:

> “There are three species of drought-causing *pah* (旱 魃). Some are like four-legged animals; another kind are transformations of *kiang shi* (僵 尸, corpse specters), and both of these species are able to produce drought and stop wind and rain. But the principal, superior drought-demons, called *koh* (or *koh-tszĕ*, 格. 格 子), cause even more damage; they resemble men but are taller, and have one eye on the top of the head. They devour dragons, and all the *Rain Masters* (雨 師) fear them greatly, for when they

<meta>This page continues the description of the drought-demons known as *koh*, detailing their ability to disperse rain clouds and cause dry weather. (the *koh*) see clouds arise, they raise their heads and disperse them (the clouds) in all directions by blowing, while the sun increases in intensity. No man can conquer them. Some say that when it is Heaven’s will for there to be a drought, the vapors of the mountain streams (山川之氣) condense and become these demons. When the latter suddenly vanish, it will rain.

The term “Rain-master” (*yü-shi*, 雨師) for a dragon is also mentioned by Wu Shuh <note>1</note>. The Japanese applied it especially to one of their dragon-shaped river gods, who was most famous for his power to bestow rain <note>2</note>.

Ascending dragons cause rain, but if they descend from the sky, this is not always the case. According to the “Various Divinations of Farmers” <note>3</note>, when black dragons descend, it signifies drought or at least very little rain; hence a proverb says: “Many dragons, much drought.” The descending of white dragons, however, was explained to be a sure sign of coming rain.

### § 2. Violent rains accompanied by heavy winds and thunderstorms.

In a passage from the *History of the Sung Dynasty*, mentioned above <note>4</note> regarding dragon omens, the appearance of a black dragon above the capital was said to be an omen of great floods which, in the following year, destroyed the fields and houses in 24 prefectures. We also read there that a dragon, which rose from a well in the fourth month of the sixth year of the K’ai Pao era (973), caused violent rains that destroyed a large number of houses and trees and swept away the inhabitants. And in the sixth month of the following year, when the tower of a castle gate was struck by lightning, this accident is described as follows: “In Ti cheu, a fire fell from the air upon the tower of the Northern gate of the castle. There was a creature which embraced the eastern pillar. It had the shape of a dragon and

After a short time, the animal began to wriggle and seemed to grow. A well bubbled up in the hole, and suddenly a black vapor like incense smoke rose and went straight out from under the eaves. The crowd nearby was afraid and ran home, convinced that it was a dragon. But before they were even a few miles away, the wind suddenly rose, the rain came down, and several heavy thunderclaps were heard.

### § 3. Rain magic and prayers.

Since the dragon is the god of rain, his images have been used since ancient times during droughts to make him ascend through sympathetic magic. The *Shan hai king* ⁷ says: “In the northeastern corner of the Great Desert (Ta hwang), there is a mountain called Hiung-li earth mound; a *ying lung* (according to the commentator, a winged dragon ⁸) inhabited its southern extremity.

---

After having killed Ch'i Yiu (the first rebel) and Kw'a Fu (?), he (the dragon) could not ascend again, and for this reason drought often prevails on earth. In times of drought, an image of a *ying lung* is made and then heavy rain is obtained." The commentator KwOH P'OH¹ (A.D. 276—324) adds: "The earthen dragons of the present day find their origin in this" ².

<margin>✓</margin>
WANG CH'UNG ³ of the Later Han dynasty, who in his work titled *Lun Heng* ⁴ severely criticizes the superstitions of his time, refers to the following statement by TUNG CHUNG-SHU ⁵: "At the rain sacrifices in spring and autumn, earthen dragons are set up in order to call down the rain. The idea of this is that by this means clouds and dragons are caused to come. The *Yih king* says: 'Clouds follow the dragon, wind follows the tiger'. They are invited to come by means of their likenesses; therefore, when earthen dragons are set up, Yin and Yang follow their likenesses and clouds and rain arrive on their own account."

<margin>✓</margin>
Also the *Lü shi ch'un-ts'iu* ⁶ states that "by means of dragons rain is made," and LIU NGAN ⁷ says: "Earthen dragons cause the rain to come." According to a commentary on this passage, "the Emperor T'ang (the founder of the Shang dynasty, 1766 B.C.), in a time of drought, made an earthen dragon in order to symbolize the dragon being followed by the clouds" ⁸. "The duke of Cheh in the land of Chu," says WANG CH'UNG ⁹, "liked dragons and had them painted on all his walls and trays, certainly considering

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical use of earthen dragons in Chinese rain-making rituals, citing various classical texts and commentaries to explain the specific ceremonial procedures and their alignment with Taoist cosmology.</meta>

their pictures to be like real dragons. Thus, there was always rain (that is, there never was a drought) in this duke’s country."

In the *Supplement to the Books of the Han Dynasty* <note>1</note>, a description is given of the ceremonies performed when praying for rain. An extensive commentary explains the words: "The underlings raise the earthen dragons." First, the passage from the *Shan Hai King* mentioned above is quoted, along with Guo Pu's commentary regarding the earthen dragons of his time. This is followed by a long description of rain ceremonies found in the *Ch'un-ch'iu Fan-lu* <note>2</note> by Tung Chung-shu, the second-century B.C. author quoted earlier.

In this passage, the rain ceremonies for spring, summer, the last month of summer, autumn, and winter are described. The details all align with the Taoist system pointed out by De Groot in his *Religious System* <note>3</note>, and Wu-ist priests were the performers of the rites. Accordingly, in the ceremonies for spring, summer, the last month of summer, autumn, and winter, the eastern, southern (twice), western, and northern gates of the towns and villages are mentioned. The colors of the silk banners on the altars and the robes of the officiating priests were azure <gloss>blue</gloss>, red, yellow, white, and black. Furthermore, the numbers eight, seven, five, nine, and six were used for the square altars erected at the five different ceremonies, for the tanks in which shrimps or frogs were placed, and for the number of days spent on the various preparations <note>4</note>.

Regarding the *earthen dragons* mentioned in this description: the days on which they were made, their sizes, colors, and numbers, the directions in which they were placed, and the positions where they stood—as well as the colors of the robes worn by those who carried and erected them, and the numbers and ages of those people—all corresponded with the same Taoist system.

> "On *kiah* and *yih* days <note>5</note> (in spring), one large blue dragon, eight feet long, was placed in the center, along with several small ones..."

<meta>This page continues the description of seasonal rain rituals, specifically the construction and arrangement of earthen dragons according to the directions, colors, and numbers associated with the seasons of spring, summer, and autumn.</meta>

[a dragon], eight *chang* [long], is made and stands in the center; seven small ones, each four *chang* long, are made (and placed) on the east side. They are all directed toward the *East*, with a distance of eight *ch'ih* between each other. *Eight little boys*, who have all observed religious abstinence for three days and are dressed in *blue robes*, brandish the dragons. The *T'ien seh fu*$^1$ (Superintendent of Harvesting), who also has observed religious abstinence for three days and is dressed in *blue robes*, erects them.

In the same way, in *summer* on *ping* and *ting* days, one big red dragon was made, *seven chang* long, and placed in the center, while six small dragons, each three *chang* and five *ch'ih* long, stood on the *south* side; they were all directed to the *south*, with a distance of *seven ch'ih* between each other. *Seven full-grown men*, who had observed religious abstinence for three days and were dressed in *red robes*, brandished the dragons, and the *Szě k'ung seh fu*$^2$ (Superintendent of Works), who likewise had observed religious abstinence for three days and was dressed in *red robes*, erected them.

When the mountains and hills were prayed to in the *last month of summer*, on *wu* and *szě* days, one big yellow dragon, *five chang* long, was placed in the center, and four$^3$ small ones, two *chang* and five *ch'ih* long, stood on the *south* side; they were all directed to the *South*, with a distance of *five ch'ih* between each other. *Five elders*, after three days of religious abstinence and dressed in *yellow robes*, brandished the dragons, and five men (or a senior$^4$) in *yellow robes* erected them.

In *autumn*, on *keng* and *sin* days, one big white dragon was made, *nine chang* long, and placed in the center; eight small ones, four *chang* and five *ch'ih* long, were placed on the *west* side. They were all directed to the *West*, and the distance between them was nine *ch'ih*; *nine old unmarried men* (or widowers$^5$) in *white robes* brandished them, and the *Szě ma*$^6$ (Inspector of Horses), also dressed in *white garments*, erected them.

<summary>This page details the specific ceremonial protocols for rain-making rituals across different seasons, including the dimensions, colors, and placement of earthen dragons, as well as the requirements for the participants' clothing and ritual fasting.</summary>
<keywords>earthen dragons, rain-making rituals, T'ien seh fu, Szě k'ung seh fu, Szě ma, religious abstinence, five elements, seasonal ceremonies</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the descriptions of seasonal rain-making rituals involving earthen dragons, moving from the autumn rituals to winter practices, and discusses historical sources and related magical objects.</meta>

Finally, in *winter*, when prayers were made to famous mountains, one big *black* dragon, made on *jen* and *kwéi* days and *six* chang long, was placed in the center, and five small ones, each three chang long, stood on the *north* side; they were all directed to the *North*, and the distance between them was *six* ch'ih. *Six* old men, all dressed in *black* robes, brandished the dragon, and a *wéi*¹ (military officer), also wearing *black* garments, erected them².

In the ceremonies used for stopping rain, no dragons are mentioned. We learn from the *Sung-ch'ao shi shih*³ that in the Sung dynasty the same magic was performed; the dragons were sprinkled with water and, after the ceremony, thrown into the water.

DE GROOT⁴ discusses this custom in order to show that, since this kind of rain magic was very common in ancient China, the dragon processions on the 15th day of the first month and the dragon boats on the fifth day of the fifth month may be easily explained in the same way. He also refers to a passage from the *Yiu-yang tsah tsu*⁵, where a Buddhist priest, who in the K'ai-yuen era (A.D. 713—742) was ordered by the Emperor to pray for rain, said that he wanted a utensil engraved with the figure of a dragon. Nothing of the kind could be found until, after two or three days, an old mirror, the handle of which had the form of a dragon, was discovered in the Emperor's storehouse. The priest took it into the chapel and prayed; and behold, that very evening the rain poured down!

The same sympathetic magic is mentioned in the *Pih ki man chi*⁶, where a mirror, adorned on the back with a "coiled dragon," *p'an lung*, **盤龍**, is said to have been worshiped (or rather, used in a magical way) in order to cause rain⁷.

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon-related magic, specifically focusing on rituals used to either compel dragons to produce rain or to stop excessive rainfall through the use of sympathetic magic and images.</meta>

The goal of this magic was to force the dragons to follow their images and to rise from their pools. It is no wonder that drastic measures were sometimes taken to make them obey this human command when it failed to succeed. Thus, in the tenth century A.D., the head of two districts did not hesitate to have an earthen dragon flogged in order to force the unwilling dragons to rise; and he was right, for that very day a sufficient rain fell ¹.

As we have seen above, Buddhist priests also used images of dragons to make rain. There is another story from the K'ai-yuen era, found in the same work ², which teaches us how they sometimes employed them to stop rain. An Indian monk was requested by the Emperor to stop the incessant rains caused by one of his Chinese colleagues, who, by order of the sovereign, had prayed for rain and had fulfilled his task so successfully that several people drowned as a result of the floods. The Indian priest made five or six clay dragons, placed them in water, and scolded them in his mother tongue. Then he took them out of the water and laid them elsewhere, laughing loudly. After a little while, the rain stopped. The meaning of this magic was apparently different from the ancient Chinese ideas. By placing the dragons in their element, water, he gave them life, just as a fourth-century Buddhist priest did with a dead dragon he had excavated. The latter, however, after reviving the dragon in this way, used incantations to cause it to ascend to the sky and end a severe drought. ³ On the contrary, his Indian colleague from the K'ai-yuen era removed the dragons with a scornful laugh after giving them life, in order to cause their counterparts to leave as well. We may compare this with several instances of a

---

or the beginning of the 11th century (compare <term>De Groot</term>, *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. IV, p. 74), where we read about a dragon painted on a wall with a well in front of it. People from far and near prayed to it for rain, and it used to hear their prayers. Once, during a drought, a drunken man had the audacity to rail at the dragon. He shouted over the well's balustrade: “If Heaven sends a drought like this, what use are you?” and hit one of the painted dragon's feet with a large stone. The mark was still visible in the author's time. When the man returned home, he suddenly felt an unbearable pain in his foot. Although he sent a messenger to burn incense before the dragon and apologize, it was all in vain, and he died within a few days. T. S., same section, Ch. 129, p. 11*b*.

similar magic mentioned by FRAZER in his *Golden Bough*¹. There, we read of plagues caused by vermin, scorpions, or serpents that were stopped by burying or removing images of these harmful creatures.

A curious prescription for making rain is given in the *Yiu-yang tsah tsu*², where we read the following: "Take four water-lizards and, after filling two earthen jugs with water, put two lizards in each. Then cover the jugs with wooden lids, place them in two different quiet spots, prepare seats before and behind them, and burn incense. If you then have more than ten boys, ten years old or younger, strike the jars incessantly with small green bamboo sticks day and night, it will certainly rain." This advice was followed, and after one day and two nights, the rain fell. "Tradition says," the author adds, "that dragons and water-lizards belong to the same species." The idea of annoying dragons with noise to stir them up is also found in Japan where, as we will see below³, Court officials played music and danced on a dragon boat on the pond of the Sacred-Spring-Park to force the dragon to emerge and bring rain.

Another way to make rain is to provoke the dragons' anger by throwing poisonous plants⁴, ashes⁵, pieces of wood, stones⁶, or tiger bones⁷—the tiger being the dragon’s deadly enemy—into their pools, or by pulling a tiger's head by means

by pulling a rope through a river inhabited by a dragon <sup>1</sup>. As we will see later <sup>2</sup>, the Japanese, following the same methods, threw horse dung, old sandals, and other dirty things into dragon ponds, or stirred the dragons up using iron utensils or metal shavings; for, as we saw above <sup>3</sup>, these animals were believed to detest and fear iron.

The *Wu tsah tsu* <sup>4</sup> describes a remarkable way in which the people of Ling-nan caused rain. Because dragons are very lustful and fond of women, a naked woman was placed on an elevated point in order to attract a dragon. As soon as one arrived and flew around her, he was magically prevented from approaching her, so that his anger was aroused and heavy rains fell. The same work <sup>5</sup> says that at the beginning of summer, the dragons are divided so that each of them has his special territory, which he does not exceed. This is the reason why in summertime it rains very much in one place and not at all a little further on.

Apart from these means of stirring up the dragons, we often read about prayers recited to them so that they might give fertilizing rains. This was done in shrines or at ponds inhabited by dragons, or at the entrances of their dens. The *Mao t'ing k'oh hwa*, for example, mentions a Dragon-woman's shrine, dedicated to a female dragon which in A.D. 740 appeared in a dream and promised to give

### § 4. Buddhist rain ceremonies.

In the Introduction (§ 4, pp. 25 and following), we dealt with the Buddhist rain ceremonies prescribed in the Mahāmegha Sūtra and those described by <term>De Groot</term> in his *Code du Mahāyāna*. As we will see later (Book II, Ch. III), Buddhist priests in Japan also gradually conquered this field, which was formerly the domain of the <term>Shintoists</term>. They used the same sutras as the Chinese Buddhists. The latter flourished during the Tang dynasty, when sometimes—as we read in the *Tuh i chi* <sup>3</sup>—eleven hundred Buddhist priests read sutras in order to bring rain. Regarding these ceremonies, we may refer the reader to the Introduction.
