<meta>This page begins Chapter VI, shifting from Buddhist rain ceremonies to the mythological connections between Chinese Emperors and dragons, focusing on the legends of Hwang Ti.</meta>

# CHAPTER VI.

## EMPERORS CONNECTED WITH DRAGONS.

### § 1. Hwang Ti rode on a dragon.

Since the dragon is the symbol of the Emperor and his prosperous reign, a large number of legends point to the close connection between this divine animal and the Son of Heaven. Naturally, the holy Emperors of the earliest times are mentioned first in this regard.

The *Historical Records*<note>1 Section 封禪書, Chapter XXVIII, number 6, page 30a (CHAVANNES, *Mémoires Historiques*, Volume III, page 488).</note> contain the following passage: “The Emperor Hwang gathered copper from Mount Sheu and cast a tripod at the foot of Mount King. When the tripod was ready, a dragon appeared, lowering its whiskers as it descended to meet Hwang Ti. The Emperor mounted the dragon and rode on it, after which the ministers did the same—more than seventy men in total. Then the dragon ascended and flew away. The remaining lower ministers had no opportunity to climb upon the dragon and all grabbed its whiskers at once; the weight caused the whiskers to be pulled out and fall to the ground.”

According to the *Ku kin chu*<note>2 古今注, written around the middle of the 4th century by TS'UI PAO, 崔豹 (compare WYLIE, page 159; DE GROOT, *Rel. Syst.*, Volume IV, page 244, note 1), quoted in T. S., Section 禽蟲, Chapter 130, 龍部雜錄, page 4b.</note>, Hwang Ti was melting cinnabar (to prepare the elixir of immortality) in the Tsoh ye mountains when he became a *sien* and rode a dragon to the sky. When the ministers clung to the animal's whiskers, the whiskers fell off. To the question of whether they produced the so-called “Dragon's whiskers herb,” the answer given is that this is a false tradition caused by the other name of the same herb: “Red clouds herb.” The same monarch commanded a winged dragon (*ying lung*) to attack and repel the troops of the rebel Chi Yiu<note>3 *Shan hai king*, Section XVIII, number 14, 大荒東經, page 6b; *Bamboo Annals* (*Chuh shu ki nien*, 竹書記年), Chapter I, LEGGE, *Chinese Classics*, Volume III, Part I, page 108.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the legendary connection between ancient Chinese Emperors and dragons, focusing on Emperor Hwang Ti's ascension to heaven on a dragon and his use of a winged dragon in battle.</summary>
<keywords>Hwang Ti, Yellow Emperor, dragon legends, immortality, Historical Records, Shiji, Ku kin chu, sien, ying lung, Chi Yiu, Dragon's whiskers herb</keywords>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of legendary Chinese Emperors and their mythical associations with dragons, specifically detailing the accounts of Yao, Kao Tsu, Shun, and Yü.</meta>

### § 2. Yao and Kao Tsu were sons of dragons.

The Emperor Yao was said to be the son of a red dragon, who came to his mother while carrying an inscription on its back: "You also receive Heaven's protection." Darkness and wind rose on all sides, and the dragon touched her. As a result, she became pregnant and gave birth to Yao in Tanling fourteen months later.<note>1 *Bamboo Annals*, Chapter II, LEGGE, in the place cited, page 112.</note>

A similar story is told about Kao Tsu (206—195 B.C.), the founder of the Han dynasty. His father, Taigong, saw a *kiao lung* above his wife amidst thunder, lightning, and total darkness while she was asleep on the bank of a large pond. She dreamed that she had intercourse with a god and later gave birth to Kao Tsu. This Emperor, who was very fond of wine, was always protected by a dragon when he was drunk.<note>2 *Historical Records*, Chapter VIII (高祖), page 2; CHAVANNES, in the place cited, Volume II, pages 325 and following.</note>

### § 3. Shun was visited by a yellow dragon.

The Emperor Shun, Yao's famous successor, was visited by a yellow dragon that emerged from the Loh River. On its scaly armor, the inscription "Shun shall ascend the Throne" was visible.<note>3 *Yuh fu shui t'u*, 玉符瑞圖; T. S., same section, Chapter 128, 紀事二, page 2b.</note> As we have seen above, the same holy sovereign established the "Dragon-rearer family," whose members were tasked with raising dragons for the Emperor.

### § 4. Yü drove in a carriage drawn by dragons, and was assisted by a ying lung.

Yü, the famous founder of the Hia dynasty, traveled in a carriage drawn by two dragons. They had descended into his courtyard because the virtuous power of Hia reached its highest point during his reign.<note>4 *Poh wuh chi*, Chapter II, page 2a.</note> When he had completed the regulation of the waters, blue dragons stopped in the suburbs of the capital.<note>5 *Bamboo Annals*, Chapter III, LEGGE, in the place cited, page 117: 青龍止于郊。</note> According to a later tradition, a *ying lung* assisted Yü in his work by marking the ground with its tail.<note>6 *San ts'ai t'u hwui*, 三才圖會, in the *Wakan sansai zue*, Chapter XLV, page 675. According to the *Bamboo Annals* (Chapter III, in the place cited) the spirit of the Ho river, a man with a fish body, gave him a chart of the Ho.</note>

<summary>This page recounts the legendary connections between several early Chinese Emperors and dragons, describing myths of draconic parentage, divine omens of succession, and dragons serving as celestial steeds or laborers.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Yao, Kao Tsu, Han dynasty, Emperor Shun, Emperor Yü, Hia dynasty, kiao lung, ying lung, Dragon-rearer family, Chinese mythology, imperial symbols, Bamboo Annals</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of legendary Chinese emperors and their interactions with dragons, specifically focusing on instances where dragons assisted Ming Hwang and Wu Suh, challenged Yü, and the consequences faced by Shi Hwang for killing one of the creatures.</meta>

### § 5. Ming Hwang’s vessel was moved forward by a dragon.

In later times, dragons were also said to assist Emperors. This was the case during the T‘ien pao era (742—755), when a small dragon emerged from a pond the evening before Emperor Ming Hwang, defeated by the rebel Ngan Luh-shan, left the capital and fled to the South. The dragon traveled in the same direction, and when the Emperor crossed a river, the animal appeared in the water and carried the ship forward on its back. His Majesty, deeply moved by the dragon’s loyalty, thanked it and gave it wine <note>1 *Ts‘zě-liu shi kiu wen*, 次柳氏舊聞, written during the T‘ang dynasty by LI TEH-YÜ, 李德裕. In the same way, the vessel of Wu Suh, king of Wu and Yueh (that is, Ts‘ien Liu, A.D. 851—932), which ran onto a rock in 909 and could not advance, was carried forward by two dragons amidst heavy rain, thunder, and lightning (*Shih-kwoh Ch‘un-ts‘iu*, 十國春秋, written in the latter half of the 17th century by WU JEN-CH‘EN, 吳任臣; according to DE GROOT, *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. IV, p. 327, “a rather apocryphal history” of ten small states which existed between the T‘ang and Sung dynasties) (WYLIE, p. 41). T. S., same section, Ch. 129, 紀事二, p. 14a.</note>.

### § 6. Two yellow dragons threatened to upset Yü’s vessel.

Sometimes, however, the dragons of rivers and seas caused trouble even to Emperors. Thus, two yellow dragons threatened to upset Yü’s vessel by taking it on their backs when His Majesty crossed the Yangtze River (Yang-tszě kiang). But Yü, not in the least frightened, laughed and said: “I received my appointment from Heaven and do my utmost to nourish men. To be born is the course of nature; to die is by Heaven’s decree. Why be troubled by the dragons?” Upon hearing these words, the dragons fled, dragging their tails <note>2 *Bamboo Annals*, Ch. III, LEGGE, in the place cited, p. 118; cf. *Hwai nan tszě*, Ch. VII, 精神 n 9</note>.

### § 7. Shi Hwang died on account of having killed a dragon.

Another Emperor was severely punished for having killed a dragon. This was Shi Hwang, the founder of the Ts‘in dynasty (246—210 B.C.), who was so anxious to have a long life that he was highly rejoiced when two *sien* arrived, pretending to know

After being favored with high dignities and salaries, they set sail with a crowd of six thousand girls and boys, no older than fifteen years, to seek the Island of the Blessed ^2. However, although they sought it for a long time, it was all in vain. The *sien*, who were afraid of punishment on account of their lies, now invented a new scheme. Upon returning to the Court, they advised the Emperor to go on board himself and set out with a large army. Again the foolish monarch believed them and put to sea with no fewer than three million soldiers, who made a terrible noise by shouting in unison and beating drums (in order to frighten the sea-gods and thus be able to reach the Island of the Blessed). The dragon-god, aroused by the din, appeared at the surface of the sea in the shape of an enormous shark, five hundred *ch'ih* (feet) long, with a head like that of a lion. He was immediately surrounded by the fleet and killed with poisonous arrows, so that his blood colored the sea over a distance of ten thousand miles. That night the Emperor dreamed that he had a battle with the dragon-god; and the next day he fell ill and died within seven days ^3.

# CHAPTER VII.

## TRANSFORMATIONS.

### § 1. The dragon's transformations are unlimited.

From the *Kwan tzĕ* and the *P‘i ya*, quoted above <note>Pages 63 and 65.</note>, we have learned that *the dragon’s ability to transform is unlimited*. Therefore, it is no wonder that Chinese literature abounds with stories about dragons that assumed the shapes of men, animals, or objects. *When they transformed themselves into human beings, they mostly appeared as old men or beautiful women*; the latter remind us of the Nāga maidens in Indian tales. Sometimes fish—which, when being cooked, emitted a five-colored light or spoke with human voices—were recognized as dragons. Likewise, four-legged animals, such as dogs, rats, or cows, sometimes proved to be the temporary shapes of these divine creatures. Snakes, of course, being closely related to dragons, often served as their disguises to hide their true nature, and newborn dragons were said to crawl out of their eggs in this form. Finally, tree trunks or other objects floating in the water sometimes suddenly resumed their actual dragon shapes. One passage <note>*Chen chu ch‘wen*, 珍珠船 (compare above, page 119, note 7), quoted in the *T‘u-shu chi-ch‘eng*, in the same section, Chapter 130, 龍部雜錄 (Dragon Department, Miscellaneous Records), page 6b.</note> says that dragons can always transform themselves except at the time of their birth, when they sleep, or when they are angry or lustful; however, this stands alone among the innumerable other statements regarding their nature and abilities.

### § 2. Appearing as old men or beautiful women.

Regarding their appearance as old men, we may refer to the *Süen shih chi* <note>宣室志, written by <term>CHANG TUH</term>, 張讀, in the ninth century. Quoted</note>, where a yellow dragon is said to have visited a house

<summary>This chapter details the unlimited shape-shifting abilities of Chinese dragons, which can appear as humans, various animals, or inanimate objects. It notes that while dragons typically disguise themselves as old men or beautiful women, their true nature is often revealed through supernatural signs like multi-colored light or human speech.</summary>

<keywords>dragon transformations, Chinese mythology, Nāga maidens, shape-shifting, Kwan tzĕ, P‘i ya, Süen shih chi, CHANG TUH</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon transformations, specifically focusing on their appearance as human beings—such as old men and beautiful women—and begins a section on their transformation into fish.</meta>

in the mountains in the shape of an old man with a yellow robe. The *Kwang-sin-fu chi* <note>**廣信府志**, “Memoirs of the department of Kwang-sin (in Kiang-si province),” quoted in T. S. l. l., p. 16*a*.</note> contains a story about a *wu*-sorcerer who, at the beginning of the Sung dynasty, was praying for rain above a well when he fell into it while trying to catch the white cow horn he had blown, which suddenly dropped from his hands. At the bottom of the well, he saw a majestic old man sitting in a tower in the water with the horn in his hands. This was the dragon of the well, who for that time allowed him to return and gave him back the horn on the condition that he should never make a noise near the well again. However, during the next drought, the man forgot his promise and blew the horn above the well as before. This was too much for the dragon, who caused both the horn and the man to tumble into the water, and this time the sorcerer drowned. Afterward, he appeared to one of the villagers in a dream, and upon his advice, a shrine was erected in honor of the dragon, who from then on heard their prayers for rain.

The *Yiu-yang tsah tsu* <note>Chapters II and VI; T. S., same section, Chapter 131, **龍部外編**, p. 12*a*.</note> also mentions dragons that assumed the shapes of old men, as well as *beautiful women* <note>Chapter VI.</note>. <term>Liu Tsung-yuen</term> <note>**柳宗元** (A.D. 773—819), one of the most celebrated poets and essayists of the T'ang dynasty. **謫龍說**, T. S., same section, Chapter 127, **藝文一**, p. 8*b*. Another punishment of a dragon is mentioned in the *Yun sien tsah ki* (**雲仙雜記**, about which work <term>De Groot</term> (*Rel. Syst.*, Vol. IV, p. 289) says: “Ten chapters of miscellanies of doubtful authenticity, ascribed to one <term>T'ung Chi</term>, **馮贄**, of whom nothing is known but the name. More likely, perhaps, the author was the learned <term>Wang Chih</term>, **王銍**, also named <term>Sing-chi</term>, **性之**, who flourished in the middle of the 12th century”), where a disobedient dragon is said to have had his ears cut off as a punishment from Heaven; the blood that dripped upon the earth produced a plum tree with fleshy fruits without pits. T. S., same section, Chapter 129, **紀事二**, p. 12*a*.</note> tells how a dragon punished by the Emperor of Heaven fell to earth in the shape of a woman, spreading a brilliant light. She had to stay there for seven days; then, after drinking some water, her breath became a cloudy vapor, she changed into a white dragon, and flew up to Heaven.

### § 3. Appearing as fishes.

Transformations of dragons into fish are also to be found

in the Dynastic Histories <note>*Books of the Tsin dynasty*, 列傳, Chapter VI, 張華傳 (the fish emitted a five-colored light while being cooked).</note> as well as in books of tales and legends like the *Lang hüen ki* <note>Chapter I (the fish spoke with a human voice).</note> (Yuan dynasty) and even in a geographical work like the *Yih t'ung chi* <note>一統志, “Memoirs concerning the whole Empire” (1647); T. S., same section, Chapter 129, 龍部紀事二, p. 13a.</note>, where we read about a white eel that was caught by some villagers. They were about to cook it when an old man said: "This is a dragon from the Siang River. I fear a calamity." But the others considered this to be foolish prattle and did not listen to his words. The next day the whole village collapsed.

In the *Shwoh yuen* <note>說苑, written by <term>Liu Hiang</term>, 劉向 (who lived 80—9 B.C.), the famous author of the *Lieh sien chw'en* (列仙傳); Section 正諫.</note> a white dragon is said to have assumed the shape of a fish and to have been hit in the eye with an arrow by a fisherman. The dragon accused the man before the Emperor of Heaven, but the Emperor remarked that it was the dragon's own fault because he had been foolish enough to change himself into a fish. The fisherman was not to be blamed for having treated him like any other fish. This story is often referred to in Japanese literature; for example, in the *Zoku kojidan* <note>續古事談, Chapter II, *Gunsho ruijū*, Vol. XVII, p. 661.</note>, where the fish is said to have fallen into the fisherman's net and to have lodged a complaint with the *Dragon king* (an Indian concept; see the Introduction and the next chapter), who gave him a similar answer and advised him not to do such a foolish thing again. In the *Taiheiki* <note>Chapter XX, p. 9a. The same comparison is found in Chapter XXXI, p. 12, of the same work.</note> Nitta Yoshisada, who died in battle, is compared to the dragon of this legend, which, instead of hiding itself in the depths of a pool, came to a shallow place and was caught in the net.

As we have seen above <note>Book I, Chapter III, § 12, p. 86.</note>, fish were believed to become dragons when they succeeded in ascending the Dragon Gate (apparently a waterfall), and old tiger-fish or fish weighing two thousand *kin* became *kiao* <note>Book I, Chapter III, § 7, p. 79.</note>.

### § 4. Appearing as snakes, dogs, or rats.

The *Poh mung so yen* <note>北夢瑣言, attributed to <term>Sun Kwang-hien</term>, 孫光憲, also called <term>Meng-wen</term>, 孟文, a high official under the founder of the Song dynasty (T'ai tsu, 960—976) (compare De Groot, *Religious System*, Vol. V, p. 527, note 2). T. S., same section, Ch. 129, p. 14*a*.</note> tells the story of a child who, during the T'ong-kwang era (923–926), met a white snake on the road, tied it with a rope, and swung its head back and forth until it fell down. In a moment, a thunderstorm arose and the child was carried into the air, where they were struck by lightning and dropped dead on the ground. On the child's back, vermilion writing could be read, announcing that Heaven had punished the child for having killed a Celestial dragon.

Two dragons in the shape of *mao* dogs (茅狗), ridden through the air by *sien* <note>immortals</note>, are mentioned in the *Lieh sien chw'en* <note>T. S., same section, Ch. 131, p. 2*b*.</note>. A *sien* brought them to a diviner, more than 100 years old, and invited him to ride on them along with an old woman. According to the *Lang hüen ki* <note>Quoted in the same work, p. 3*b*.</note>, two guardian gods of a cave palace were dragons. The *Kiang-si t'ung-chi* <note>T. S., same section, Ch. 129, p. 12*b*.</note> speaks about a very deep “Dragon-rearing pond” near the castle of Kwang ch'ang district in Kien ch'ang fu, which was inhabited by a dragon. Over the pond there was a stone tray in which remains of food were always placed for the animal, which used to change into a black dog to eat the food. This pond was still there in the author’s time, and a “Dragon-well temple” had been built on the spot.

In the seventh year of the Kia-yiu era (1062), an enormous white rat was seen smelling the sacrificial dishes offered in the temple on the Great White Mountain in Fu fung district (Shen-si province). This is a mountain with much *ling*—that is, where the divine power of its god clearly manifested itself in hearing the prayers of the believers, just as was the case on the Japanese mountain of the same name (Hakusan). Old people declared the rat, which only smelled the dishes but did not eat them, to be a dragon <note>*Tung-p'o chi-lin*, 東坡志林, miscellaneous notes by <term>Su Tung-p'o</term>, 蘇東坡, also known as <term>Su Shih</term>, 蘇軾, a famous poet who lived 1036–1101; T. S., same section, Ch. 130, p. 4*a*.</note>.

### § 5. A cow transformed into a dragon.

The author of the *Hwai-ngan-fu chi* <note>淮安府志, “Memoirs concerning Hwai-ngan-fu (in the province of Kiang-su); T. S., in the same place, p. 12*b*.</note> tells us how a cow

became a dragon. A rich farmer who owned a large herd of cattle dreamt one night that one of his cows said to him: “I have become a dragon and have fought with the dragon of Sang-k'ü Lake, but without defeating him. You must tie small knives onto my horns.” The next day, he discovered that an extremely large cow in the herd had scales under its belly. When he had attached knives to its horns, the cow defeated the other dragon, which was wounded in the eye and retreated into its lake. The cow itself became the dragon of the Great Lake. Up until the author's time, those who passed this lake avoided the character 牛 (cow), and those who passed Sang-k'ü Lake avoided the character 瞎 (blind in one or both eyes); otherwise, a storm would suddenly break out and large waves would rise.

### § 6. Appearing as objects.

With regard to *objects* that proved to be dragons, we may refer to the *I yuen* <note>Ch. I, p. 2. The same work tells a story about a large piece of driftwood that broke the boat of a man who seized it, turned into a dragon, and swam away.</note>, where we read how a man, while fishing in a river, found a weaving shuttle and took it home. After a short while, the tool, which he had hung on the wall, changed into a red dragon and ascended to the sky amidst thunder and rain. A dragon that had assumed the shape of a tree growing underwater is mentioned in the *Shuh i ki* <note>述異記 (see above, p. 72, note 1), Ch. 下, p. 16b.</note>. A woman who touched this tree when going into the water to catch some fish became pregnant and gave birth to ten sons. Afterward, when the dragon appeared in his true form above the water, nine of the boys ran away in fear, but the tenth climbed onto his dragon-shaped father’s neck and in later years became the king of the land <note>Compare above, Book I, Ch. VI, p. 123.</note>. The same work tells us about a girl in the Palace, under the Hia dynasty, who changed into a terrifying dragon and then, regaining her human form, became a very beautiful woman who devoured men <note>Ch. 上; p. 4a.</note>.

In the *Books of the Tsin dynasty* <note>晋書 Ch VI 張華傳 These swords, which turned into a male and a <unclear>...</unclear></note> an astrologer is said to have discovered the vital spirits (精) of two precious swords among the stars and pointed out the spot where they were buried.

<summary>This page describes how dragons can manifest as cows or inanimate objects such as weaving shuttles, underwater trees, and celestial swords. It highlights legends where these transformations lead to battles, the birth of kings, or supernatural events tied to local taboos.</summary>

<keywords>dragon transformations, Sang-k'ü lake, Great Lake, I yuen, Shuh i ki, Hia dynasty, Tsin dynasty, astrologer, vital spirits, 牛, 瞎, 述異記, 精, 晋書, 張華傳</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragons manifesting as inanimate objects. The text transitions from the conclusion of a story about a dragon's offspring to the legendary account of two supernatural swords that transform into dragons.</meta>

There, a stone box was dug up from which a brilliant light shone; but as soon as the swords were taken out of the box, their spirits in the sky were extinguished. On one of the swords, the characters 龍泉, *lung-ts'üen* ("Dragon-spring"), were written, and on the other, 太阿, *t'ai-o*. According to the astrologer, such supernatural swords could not remain in human hands for long. In fact, one of them soon disappeared, and the other later jumped by itself out of its sheath into a river that its owner was crossing. When it was sought, nothing was found except two dragons, two or three <term>chang</term> long, coiled together and emitting a brilliant light which illuminated the water. Then they vanished, raising turbulent waves with their violent movements. Evidently, the swords had transformed into dragons and were united once more.

The story illustrates the belief that such powerful, celestial objects cannot remain in the possession of humans indefinitely.</summary>

# CHAPTER VIII.

## THE INDIAN NĀGA IN CHINA.

### § 1. Reborn as a dragon.

Regarding the Indian dragon (*Nāga*) in China, we may refer to the Introduction and to the following legends.

Buddhist reincarnation into a dragon was said to have been the fate of Emperor Wu's Consort K'ih (first half of the sixth century A.D.), who was so jealous that she was reborn as a dragon. This dragon lived in a well inside the enclosure of the Palace and frightened her husband in his dreams. When he was in love with another woman, the water of the well was violently disturbed. To appease the spirit, the Emperor had a palace built over the well and placed all kinds of clothes and utensils there, as if she were still a human being; and he never married again <note>History of the South (南史, Nan-shi), Biographies of Imperial Consorts, Part 2.</note>.

### § 2. Ponds inhabited by Dragon-Kings.

According to another Buddhist legend <note>Yiu-yang tsah tsu (ninth century), Ch. V (T. S., l. l., Supplement, p. 11a).</note>, a Dragon-King who lived in a palace at the bottom of a pond called *Kwun ming ch'i* <note>Kunming Pond (昆明池).</note> appeared as an old man to a hermit who lived in the neighborhood. He begged this man to save his life, because a Buddhist priest—under the pretext of praying for rain by order of the Emperor—was making the water of his pond decrease more and more. The priest intended to kill the dragon and use its brain to prepare some medicine. The hermit advised the dragon to go to SUN SZE-MOH <note>Sun Simiao, a famous physician and Daoist of the Tang dynasty.</note>, who was studying in the mountains in order to become a <term>sien</term> <note>A Daoist immortal.</note>. When the dragon did so, this man promised to

...promised to save him on the condition that he should teach him how to prepare the three thousand kinds of medicine found in the Dragon Palace at the bottom of the pond. The dragon accepted the condition, after which the water of the pond rose higher and higher, and the monk died of anger and shame. The dragon kept his promise, and thus **SUN SZĔ-MOH** obtained the knowledge preserved in his famous medical work, titled *Ts'ien kin fang* <note><term>千金方</term>. Dragon's pearls were called <term>千金珠</term>; see the text above in Chapter III, § 15, page 88.</note>.

Other ponds inhabited by Dragon-Kings are mentioned in the *Loh-yang kia-lan ki* <note><term>洛陽伽藍記</term>; according to **WYLIE** (page 55) this is "a descriptive account of the various Buddhist establishments in Loh-yang, the capital during the Northern Wei; written by **YANG HÜEN-CHI** (<term>楊衒之</term>), an official of that dynasty." It was written in the sixth century (refer to **DE GROOT**, *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. I, page 344). T. S., same section, Chapter 131, page 5a.</note> and in the *Po-chi kwoh chw'en* <note><term>波知國傳</term>, quoted in the T. S., same section, Chapter 129, page 4b.</note>, but these were in foreign western countries. Sacrifices were offered to them; to the latter by travelers (there were three ponds: in the largest lived the Dragon-King himself, in the next lived his consort, and in the smallest lived his child) because otherwise they were certain to be troubled by wind and snow. The former pond was near a Buddhist monastery in the west of the land of Wu-yih; the king of that land prayed to the dragon and threw gold and jade into the pond. When these precious objects were washed out of the pond, he ordered the monks to take them.

### § 3. Temples of Dragon-Kings.

A "Dragon-rearing well" <note><term>豢龍井</term></note> in a "Dragon-King's temple" <note><term>龍王廟</term></note> was said to be inhabited by a dragon. No one dared to draw water from this well, because if anyone did so, strange things happened, and the person who had ventured to provoke the dragon's anger in this way fell ill <note>*Mih k'oh hwui si* (11th century), T. S., same section, Chapter 130, page 3b.</note>.

Another temple of a Dragon-King on a mountain, near a white dragon's pond and a dragon's den (on the top of the mountain), are mentioned in the *Kwah i chi* <note><term>括異志</term>; see **DE GROOT**, *Rel. Syst.*, Vol. IV, page 210, note 1. This must be the later work of this name, dating from the thirteenth century (see above, page 120, note 1), as the Shun-hi era (1174—1190) is mentioned. T. S., loc. cit., Chapter 130, page 7a.</note>. In times of drought, the peasants used to pray before the cavern, which always contained water in spring and summer, and when they took this water—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Dragon-King lore, specifically focusing on legendary sites and the palaces where these beings are said to reside.</meta>

and worshipped it, abundant rains fell. Near the same spot was the Dragon-mother’s grave, mentioned above <note>Book I, Ch. III, § 16, p. 89.</note>.

### § 4. Palaces of Dragon-Kings.

A Dragon-King’s Palace is mentioned in the *Luh i ki* <note><term>錄異記</term> (ninth century, see above, p. 87, note 4). T. S. same section, Ch. 129, p. 14a.</note>. According to a tradition among sailors, it was situated under a small island about five or six days' sailing from Su-cheu (in Kiang-su province). Even when there was no wind, the waves there were so high that no vessel dared to approach it directly. However, at every high tide, when the water overflowed the island and the high waves were no longer visible, ships could pass through. At night, a red light was seen from afar above the water at this spot, as bright as sunlight, which extended over more than a hundred square miles and reached the sky.

The *Wuh tsah tsu* <note><term>五雜俎</term> (about 1592), in a passage translated into Japanese in the *Heishoku wakumonchin* (<term>秉燭或問珍</term>), written in 1710 by **KOJIMA FUKYŪ** (<term>兒嶋不求</term>) and printed in 1737; referenced by **INOUE ENRYŌ** in *Yōkwaigaku kōgi*, Vol. II, Ch. XXVII (<term>龍宮仙鄕</term>), p. 123 ff.</note> describes the same island, but without mentioning the light, says that it lies above the water, red like the sun. Although no human being dared to approach it, a sound was heard on the island as if several thousand men were busy there cutting and transporting trees. On clear nights, one could see that all the trees on the mountains had been felled. It was said that this was done to build the Dragon-King’s abode. Evidently, the Taoist ideas concerning the island of the blessed—the land of the <term>sien</term>—are conflated here with Indian concepts regarding Nāga palaces.

Finally, we may quote a passage from the *T‘ai-p‘ing yü-lan* <note>Ch. 424.</note>, where a magistrate is said to have often received a beautiful dragon-woman in his house; she arrived each time in a magnificent carriage, accompanied by female postilions. In his former existence, he had promised to marry her; he now kept his word and finally disappeared with her. The people said that he had gone to the Dragon Palace and had become a “water-<term>sien</term>” (<term>水仙</term>).

## BOOK II.

# THE DRAGON IN JAPAN.

### CHAPTER I.

#### THE ORIGINAL JAPANESE DRAGON-GODS OF RIVERS, SEAS AND MOUNTAINS.

When discussing Japanese dragon legends, we must first consider the original beliefs of the natives and separate them from the concepts imported from India and China. In the oldest annals, dragons are mentioned in various ways, but mostly as water-gods in the shape of serpents or dragons.

->§ 1. **Okami.**<-

In the *Nihongi*¹ we read that when Izanagi's consort, Izanami, died while giving birth to the fire-god Kaguzuchi, Izanagi cut the child into three pieces, each of which became a god. The blood that dripped from the upper part of the sword turned into three gods: *Kura-okami* (闇 龗), *Kura-yama-tsumi* (闇 山 祇), and *Kura-mitsu-ha* (闇 罔 象). Professor FLORENZ provides extensive notes on these three gods in his "*Japanische Mythologie*"². He says that *Kura* is explained as "abyss, valley, or cleft," although the character's meaning is "dark." The second character, 龗, which in FLORENZ'S note 26 consists of characters representing rain and dragon, but in the Japanese text (K. T. K. I, 13) is a combination of the upper part of the character 靈 with "dragon," is explained

Of the same kind are the *Shiseki shūran* (史籍集覧) and the *Gunsho ruijū* (群書類従) (1795), while the *Hyakka setsurin* (百家説林) contains a great number of works from the Tokugawa period.

as "dragon"; in the *Bungo Fudoki*<note>1 豊 後 風 土 記, written in 713; *Gunsho ruijū*, Vol. XVII, no. 499, p. 1126.</note> the characters 蛇 龗, "snake-dragon," are read as "*okami*." This and subsequent ideas about <term>Kura-okami</term> show that this divinity is a dragon or snake. He is the deity of *rain and snow*, and in the *Manyōshū* (2, 19), it is said that he was prayed to for snow. The *Engishiki* states that this god, <term>Okami</term>, had Shintō temples in every province. In a variant version<note>2 *Nihongi*, Ch. I, p. 16; Florentz, as cited above, p. 63: 高 龗。</note> we read that one of the three gods who emerged from the three pieces of <term>Kaguzuchi's</term> body was *Taka-okami*. This name is explained by one commentator as "the dragon-god residing on the mountains," to distinguish him from *Kura-okami*, "the dragon-god of the valleys."<note>3 According to Aston (*Shinto*, p. 153), it is simply "*O Kami*," or "August god," so that the names Kura-o-kami and Taka-o-kami should mean "God of the valleys" and "God of the heights." However, in my opinion, Florentz's arguments are correct.</note>

The passage from the *Bungo Fudoki* cited by Florentz states that in the village of Kutami in the Naori district, there was a well from which water was drawn for Emperor Keikō (71–130 AD) (not Suinin, as Florentz claims) when he visited the place. Then a snake-dragon (蛇 龗, *okami*) appeared, at which point the Emperor said: "This water is certainly foul-smelling (*kŭsai*). Drawing water from it should not be permitted." Consequently, the well was named Kūsa-izumi.

### § 2. Yamatsumi and Mitsuha.

Regarding the second god mentioned in the *Nihongi*, *Kura-yama-tsumi*, his name means "Lord of the Dark Mountains," but one commentator explains it as "Mountain-snake" (*yama-tsu*[之]-*mi*). The name of the third divinity, *Kura-mitsu-ha*, might be translated as "Dark-water-snake" or "Valley-water-snake."<note>4 Florentz translates this as "dark water-dragon" (*dunkler Wasserdrache*), but in note 29, the word *ha* is explained as "snake," not "dragon." Compare my treatise on the *Snake in Japanese superstition*, Ch. II, A, 2 (Serpent-shaped gods of the water), pp. 13 ff., *Reports of the Seminar for Oriental Languages in Berlin, Vol. XIV, Dept. I.*</note> Florentz believes that this god is identical to *Mitsuha no Me* mentioned in the previous text (Ch. I, p. 11), although the latter is a female deity. In that passage, we read that Izanami, while dying as a result of the birth of the fire-god, gave birth to the earth-goddess <term>Hani-yama-bime</term> and the water-goddess *Mitsuha no Me* (水 神 罔 象 女). Florentz⁵ devotes an interesting note to the

A Japanese commentator explains *mitsu-ha* as "Water-snake" (水津蛇), and quotes several names and words in which *ha* means "snake"; if this is true, *Mitsuha no Me* is "Female Water-snake." Another commentary, however, explains the word *ha* as 生, "to produce," so that the name of the goddess would be: "The Woman who produces the water." <term>FLORENZ</term> does not know which explanation is correct, nor can I decide.

### § 3. Watatsumi.

In another passage of the *Nihongi*<note>5 Chapter I, p. 12; <term>FLORENZ</term>, as cited above, Ch. IV, p. 39; 又生海神等。號少童命。</note> Izanami and Izanagi are said to have given birth to "gods of the sea," called "*Watatsumi no Mikoto*" (少童命), or, as in Ch. III, p. 76 (Jimmu Tennō), 海童, "little boys" or "boys of the sea." The Chinese characters with which this name is written agree with <term>YÜ PAO'S</term> above-mentioned description of the *wang-siang* as little children; these terms are apparently identical with "sea-gods," 海神. <term>FLORENZ</term> explains the name "*Wata-tsu-mi*" as "Lords of the sea," *wata* being an old word for sea, and *mi* a kind of honorific epithet. The same commentator, however, who saw in *Mitsuha no Me* a "Female Water-snake," considers *Wata-tsu-mi* to be "Snakes of the Sea," *mi* being an old word for snake. It is not impossible that he is right, and that the old Japanese sea-gods were snakes or dragons.

The name of the river-gods, "*mizuchi*," or "water-fathers,"

> “379 AD (67th year of Emperor Nintoku). This year, at a fork of the River Kahashima, in the central division of Kibi Province, there was a great water-dragon (*mizuchi*) which harassed the people. Now when travelers were passing that place on their journey, they were certain to be affected by its poison, so that many died. At this, Agatamori—the ancestor of the Omi of Kasa, a man of fierce temper and of great bodily strength—stood over the pool of the river-fork and threw into the water three whole calabashes, saying: ‘You are continually spitting up poison and thereby plaguing travelers. I will kill you, water-dragon (虬). If you can sink these calabashes, then I will leave; but if you cannot sink them, then I will cut your body to pieces.’ Now the water-dragon changed itself into a deer and tried to pull down the calabashes, but the calabashes would not sink. So, with upraised sword, he entered the water and killed the water-dragon. He further searched for the water-dragon’s companions. The whole tribe of water-dragons filled a cave at the bottom of the pool. He killed every one of them, and the river water turned to blood. Therefore, that water was called the pool of Agatamori’” <note>4. Footnote 4 refers to this legend in the Nihongi.</note>.

**ASTON** also refers to another passage of the *Nihongi* (Ch. 11, p. 197), where we read about a similar experiment with two calabashes, by which a man who was to be sacrificed to a river-god saved his life. It was in the eleventh year of Emperor Nintoku’s reign (323 AD), and the Emperor had dreamed that a god pointed out two men to him who were to be sacrificed to the god of the Northern river in order to allow the people to complete the embankment, which had collapsed in two places. ### § 5. Oho-watatsumi, the sea-god.

Finally, we must mention the sea-god *Oho-wata-tsu-mi no Mikoto*, in whose name we again find the term "Sea-lord" or "Sea-snake," spoken of in the preceding text. He is also called *Toyo-tama hiko no Mikoto* ("Abundant-Pearl-Prince"), and his daughter's name is "*Toyo-tama-bime*" ("Abundant-Pearl-Princess," 豐 玉 姫). This god had his magnificent palace at the bottom of the sea, and when his daughter announced to him that she had seen reflected in the well before the gate the face of a beautiful youth who was sitting in the cassia tree close by, he received Hiko-hohodemi—for this was the youth—in a hospitable way. Afterwards the guest married the princess and lived in the palace for three years. Then, however, he returned to the earth (according to the *Kojiki*, on the back of a *wani*, 和 邇, one fathom long) and was followed by his consort, for whom he had built a "parturition-house" on the seashore. She begged him not to look at her while she was giving birth, but he was too curious and peeped in, seeing that his wife had become a *wani* (*Kojiki*), or *dragon* (*Nihongi*). Angry and ashamed, she abandoned her child, the father of Jimmu Tennō, and returned to the Sea-god's palace <note>1. *Nihongi*, Ch. II, pp. 62 and following; *Kojiki*, 上, 傳 十 七, 神 代 下, Kokushi Taikei Vol. VII, p. 59: 化 八 尋 和 邇 而 匍 匐 委 蛇。 *Nihongi*, p. 63: 化 爲 龍; p. 66 (a variant): 化 爲 八 尋 大 熊 鰐 (wani) 匍 匐 透 迤。</note>.

### § 6. wani.

The word *wani*, which is written either phonetically (和 邇) or with the character 鰐, indicating a crocodile, is found once more in Chapter I of the *Nihongi* (p. 40). We read there: "Further it is said that Koto-shiro-nushi no kami changed himself into a bear-*wani*, eight fathoms long (*ya-hiro no kuma-wani*,

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the term wani and the legend of the sea-princess Toyotama-bime, identifying the creature's characteristics and the linguistic origins of its name.</meta>

(Eight-fathom-bear-wani, <term>八尋熊鰐</term>). The epithet "bear" means "strong as a bear" <note>1. FLORENZ, in the place cited, p. 148, note 89.</note>. Regarding the word <term>wani</term>, one version of the <term>Hohodemi</term> legend <note>2. *Nihongi*, Ch. II, p. 66.</note> says that the sea princess became a <term>wani</term>, and according to another version she changed into a *dragon*; in the former, the same words are used as in the previously mentioned passage about <term>Koto-shiro-nushi no kami</term>: "Toyotama-bime changed into a big *bear-wani*, eight fathoms long, which crawled about." ASTON <note>3. *Nihongi*, Vol. I, p. 61, note 3.</note>, in a note to this passage, suggests that the word <term>wani</term> is not a Japanese, but a Korean word, *wang-i*, which simply means: king. FLORENZ <note>4. In the place cited, p. 148, note 89.</note> agrees with him, and they base their opinion upon the fact that the legend has strong Chinese features. Although the *Indian notions about the <term>Nāga-kings</term>* discussed above (Introduction) can be easily recognized in the Japanese legend, I believe we must not go so far as to consider the entire story Western, nor do we have the right to doubt the ancient word <term>wani</term> simply because *a part of the legend* is of foreign origin. Why would the ancient Japanese or Koreans have called these sea monsters "kings," omitting the word "dragon," which is the most important part of the combined term "dragon-king"? And if the full term were used in Korea, certainly the Japanese would not have adopted only its last part. In my opinion, the <term>wani</term> is an ancient Japanese dragon- or serpent-shaped sea-god, and the legend is an ancient Japanese tale, clothed in an Indian style by later generations. The oldest version probably related how <term>Hohodemi</term> went to the sea-god, married his daughter, and obtained from him the two jewels of ebb and flood, or some other means to punish his brother by nearly drowning him; afterwards, having returned to the earth, he built the *parturition-house*, and breaking his promise not to look at his wife when she was giving birth, saw that she had changed into a <term>wani</term>, that is, an enormous sea monster. Regarding the pearls, although mysterious jewels are very common in the Indian tales about the <term>Nāga-kings</term>, it is possible that Japanese sea-gods were also believed to possess them, as the sea conceals so many treasures in its depths; however, it may also be an Indian concept. When later generations became acquainted with Chinese and Indian dragons, they identified their <term>wani</term> with the latter, and embellished their old legends with features borrowed from the Indian <term>Nāga</term> tales. The magnificent palace is of Indian origin, and, as ASTON <note>5. [Note continued on next page]</note>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between the Japanese legend of Hohodemi and regional folklore, specifically exploring potential Indonesian origins for the myth and the identity of the wani.</meta>

points out, the castle gate and the (cassia) tree before it, as well as the well which serves as a mirror, form a combination not unknown to European folklore. Europe probably also received them from India, the cradle of both Western and Eastern legends.

After writing this, I became acquainted with the interesting fact, pointed out by F. W. K. MÜLLER <note>1. *Myths of the Kei Islanders and Related Matters*, Zeitschrift für Ethnologie, Vol. XXV (1893), pp. 533 and following. Dr. H. H. JUYNBOLL kindly pointed out to me the existence of these Kei and Minahassa myths and Dr. MÜLLER’S interesting article. See KERN, in the periodical titled “Contributions to the Language, Land, and Ethnology of the Dutch East Indies,” 1893, p. 501; JUYNBOLL, in the same place, 1894, p. 712, note 1.</note>, that a similar myth is found on the Kei islands as well as in the Minahassa. The resemblance of several features of this myth to the Japanese one is so striking that we may be sure the latter is of Indonesian origin. Probably the foreign invaders who conquered Japan in prehistoric times came from Indonesia and brought this myth with them. In the Kei version, the man who had lost the hook lent to him by his brother *enters the clouds in a boat and finally finds the hook in the throat of a fish*. In the Minahassa legend, however, *he dives into the sea and arrives at a village at the bottom of the water. There, he discovers the hook in the throat of a girl and is brought home on the back of a big fish.* And just as Hohodemi punished his brother by nearly drowning him using the jewel of the flood-tide, so the hero of the Minahassa legend, through his prayers, caused the rain to come down in torrents upon his evil friend. In Japan, Buddhist influence evidently changed the village in the sea into the palace of a Dragon king, but in the older version, the sea-god and his daughter have kept their original shapes as <term>wani</term>, probably a kind of crocodile, as the Chinese character indicates. An old painting by Sensai Eitaku, reproduced by MÜLLER, shows Hohodemi returning home on the back of a crocodile. It is quite possible that the form of this Indonesian myth introduced into Japan mentioned crocodiles, and that the vague conception of these animals was retained under the old name of <term>wani</term>, which may be an Indonesian word.

On p. 149 of the same work ASTON says:
> “There can be little doubt that the <term>wani</term> is really the Chinese dragon. It is frequently so represented in Japanese pictures. I have before me a print which shows Toyotama-hiko and his daughter *with dragons’ heads appearing over their human ones*. This shows that he was conceived of not only as a Lord of Dragons, but as a dragon himself . . . In Japanese myth the serpent or dragon is almost always asso-

associated with water in some of its forms.” He provides the print on the same page, and we see at once that we are here dealing not so much with *Chinese* as with *Indian* territory. In the Introduction (pp. 4 and following), I have referred to Grünwedel’s description of the dragon in Indian art, so I need not explain that “the dragon’s heads appearing over the human one” constitutes quite an Indian motif, transferred to China and from there to Korea and Japan. As the sea-god in his magnificent palace was an Indian conception, Japanese art naturally represented him in an Indian way. This is, however, no proof that the *wani* was originally identical to the Nāga, or to the Chinese-Indian dragon-kings.

### § 7. The jewels of flood and ebb.

Regarding the jewels of the flood-tide and ebb-tide, we may refer to the *Mizu kagami* <note>1. **水鏡**, First Volume, **神功**, K. T. K. Vol. XVII, p. 351; written in the second half of the twelfth century.</note>, which contains a legend apparently created in imitation of the Hiko-Hohodemi tale found in the *Kojiki* and the *Nihongi*. It runs as follows: In the year 200, when Empress Jingō (200—269) arrived in Korea, she took some sea water in her hand and prayed from afar to the gods of Kashima (in Hitachi) and Kasuga (Takemikazuchi, who had a famous old temple at Kashima and another on the hill of Kasuga at Nara, under the name of Kasuga-daimyōjin; the latter, however, was not built before 710). Then the gods of Kasuga, Sumiyoshi, and Suwa, clad in armor and wearing helmets on their heads, came to the Empress’s ship. Kasuga sent the Great God (Daimyōjin) of Kawakami <note>2. **河上**, the “Rain-Master” (**雨師**), see below, Ch. IV.</note> as a messenger to the Dragon Palace (龍宮, *ryūgū*) at the bottom of the sea, and this mighty river-god took the “pearl of ebb” and the “pearl of flood” from the Great Dragon-king Sāgara <note>3. **沙竭羅**, also mentioned in the *Fusō ryakki*, Emperor Shōmu, K. T. K. Vol. VI, p. 564. He is one of the eight Great Dragon-Kings; cf. above, p. 4.</note> and brought them back to the surface with him. While the Korean warships were drawn up in battle formation, the pearl of ebb, thrown into the sea, caused the water to suddenly dry up <note>4. **大龍王沙竭羅龍王二干珠滿珠ノ一ノ玉ヲ** (The Great Dragon-king Sāgara's two jewels: the Ebb-jewel and the Flood-jewel.)</note>. Then the king of Koma entered the seabed with his troops in order to destroy the Japanese fleet; but as soon as he did so, the god of Kawakami, following Kasuga’s order, threw the pearl of flood

This book describes them as being about five *sun* long; the former is white and the latter is blue.

In Higo province, Aso district, there is a body of water in the mountains (*numa*, 沼, which is not only a swamp, but a body of water larger than a pond and smaller than a lake) called "The Sacred Pond" (神靈池, Shinreichi). For many years past, even in times of major floods or severe droughts, the water of that pond did not rise or fall. Now, however, it has decreased by more than twenty <term>jō</term> without any apparent reason. According to the diviners, this signifies a calamity of drought (旱疫, *kan-eki*, literally "pestilence of drought") <note>The same information can be found in the *Nihon isshi*, 日本逸史, Ch. IV and XIII, K. T. K. Vol. VI, pp. 39 and 363.</note>.

In 840, the water level fell 40 <term>jō</term> <note>*Shoku Nihon kōki*, 續日本後紀, written in 869; Ch. IX, K. T. K. Vol. III, p. 285.</note>, and the Emperor issued a proclamation ordering the people to pray to avert this bad omen <note>Same work, Ch. IX, p. 288. See also Ch. X, p. 293.</note>.

### § 9. An Emperor's dragon-tail.

In the *Ainōshō* <note>壒囊鈔, an encyclopedia written in 1446 by the Buddhist priest Gyōgo, 行譽; Ch. VII, no. 21, p. 19.</note>, a humorous explanation is given for the use of the word *birō* (尾籠) in the sense of *dōtai* (同躰, "same body"). According to some people, says the writer, this is due to the fact that Emperor Ōjin (270—310, the son of Empress Jingō, deified as Hachiman in 712) had a dragon’s tail, because he was a descendant of the sea god (Jimmu Tennō, his ancestor, was the grandson of the sea god’s daughter <note>See above, page 139.</note>). In order to hide this tail, he invented the *suso*, or skirt. One day, however, when he left the room, the tail was still inside when a lady-in-waiting shut the sliding doors and pinched the tail between them. Then the Emperor exclaimed: "Biryū," ("I am a tailed dragon"). Afterward, this word *biryū* was changed into *birō* with the meaning of "same body," because the Emperor had meant to say that what was caught in the door also belonged to his body (!). The author of the *Ainōshō* believes the legend of Emperor Ōjin’s dragon tail because, he says, Toyotamabime’s son Ugaya-fuki-aezu no Mikoto married his own aunt—who was also a daughter of the sea god and a younger sister of his mother, called Tamayori-hime—with whom he fathered four sons, the youngest of whom was Jimmu Tennō. Therefore, in his opinion, it is quite possible that Jimmu’s descendants had dragon tails!

<meta>This page transitions from Chapter I's discussion of imperial physical anomalies (like Emperor Ōjin’s dragon tail) to Chapter II, which focuses on the interpretation of dragons and dragon-horses as omens in Japanese history, specifically referencing records from the Nihongi and Fusō ryakki.</meta>

# CHAPTER II.

## THE CHINESE DRAGON AND THE DRAGON-HORSE AS OMENS IN JAPAN.

In China, the dragon often—and the dragon-horse always—belong (as we have stated above¹) to the category of very good omens. The Japanese, who have fully adopted Chinese opinions on the subject of forebodings, did not hesitate to also believe in the truth of their assertions regarding the appearance of dragons.

### § 1. Flying dragon as horse of a ghost or a sien.

The Chinese dragon, flying through the air, is mentioned in the *Nihongi*², where we read: “On the first day of the fifth month of the first year of Empress Saimei’s reign (655), a man riding on a dragon appeared in the sky. In form, he resembled a Chinese person, and he wore a blue (broad-rimmed bamboo) hat covered with oiled silk. Galloping from Katsuragi peak, he disappeared into the Ikoma mountains; at noon, he galloped away from the top of Sumiyoshi's (Sumi no e, 住吉) Pine-tree Peak in a western direction.”

The *Fusō ryakki*³ provides the same legend and adds: “The people of that time said: ‘It is the soul of Soga Toyora no Ō-omi Emishi’.” This was a famous minister who died in A.D. 645, the son of Umako and grandson of Iname, the first protectors of Buddhism. Iname had built the first Buddhist temple, Kōgenji (also known as Katsuragi-dera), which was destroyed in 645 during the fall of the Soga family. Although it is not stated in the text of the *Nihongi*, the appearance of this dragon as the horse of a <term>sien</term>⁴ at the beginning of the Empress’s reign was probably considered a very good omen, as

<note>2 Ch. XXVI, p. 457.</note>
<note>3 *Fusō ryakki* (扶桑略記), written about 1150 by the Buddhist priest Koen (皇圓), teacher of the famous Genku. K. T. K. Vol. VI, p. 546, Ch. IV.</note>

...as well as that of the yellow dragon, which was seen ascending from the northwestern mountains to the sky in A.D. 887, at the Emperor Uda's accession to the Throne <note>1</note>.

### § 2. Dragon-horses.

In the *Nihongi* <note>2</note> we read: “The Emperor (Kōtoku Tennō, in the sixth year of his reign, i.e., 650) said: ‘When a holy sovereign appears in the world and reigns over the empire, Heaven, in response, gives *good omens*. In ancient times, under the reign of the monarchs of the Western country (China), Cheng Wang of the Zhou dynasty and Ming Di of the Han dynasty <note>in reality of the Jin dynasty</note>, white pheasants appeared. Under the reign of the Japanese Emperor Honda (Ōjin Tennō, 270–310 A.D.) a white raven nested in the Palace, and in the time of the Emperor Ōsazaki (Nintoku Tennō, 311–399 A.D.) a *dragon-horse* (龍馬, *ryū-me*, or *tatsu no uma*) <note>3</note> appeared in the West. Thus, from ancient times down to the present day, there are many instances of the appearance of lucky omens corresponding with the presence of virtuous men.’”

The *Engishiki* <note>4</note> also lists the dragon-horse among the lucky omens (祥瑞). It is called a “divine horse” (神馬) there and is described as follows: “It has a long neck and wings at its sides. When it treads on the water, it does not sink” <note>5</note>. The dragon is mentioned in the same list, with the following description borrowed from China: “It has five colors and walks (or flies) about; it can make itself invisible or visible, small or big.”

The *Shoku Nihongi* <note>6</note> and the *Shoku Nihon kōki* <note>7</note> quote Chinese...

<keywords>dragon-horse, ryū-me, Nihongi, Engishiki, omens, Emperor Kōtoku, Emperor Nintoku, Japanese mythology, 祥瑞, 神馬</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragons and dragon-horses as auspicious omens, following the mention of the Shoku Nihon kōki quoting Chinese texts on the subject.</meta>

expressions regarding tortoises and dragons appearing as signs of the reign of a good emperor.

The *Nihon Sandai jitsuroku* <note>1</note> compares a cloudy vapor, which hung under the sun on the 27th day of the 7th month of A.D. 883, to a dragon-horse, and states that in A.D. 885 the "dragon-star" (龍星) appeared twice <note>2</note>, which was the reason the era name was changed (apparently it was considered a bad omen). As the Emperor announced to the people in a proclamation, Gwangyō 9 was replaced by Ninna 1.

In the *Konjaku monogatari* <note>3</note> we find a much-damaged passage about a dragon-horse which flew through the air in Emperor Shōmu's time (724–749).

The *Masu kagami* <note>4</note> mentions the dragon-horse only in regard to its capacity for crossing broad rivers. In 1221, when Hōjō Yoshitoki marched from Kamakura to Kyoto against the Emperor Juntoku, the rivers Fujigawa and Tenryūgawa (天龍川, "Celestial Dragon River") were swollen by the rains to such a degree "that even a dragon-horse could not have crossed them."

An interesting passage regarding the dragon-horse is found in the *Taiheiki* <note>5</note>, where such an excellent horse <note>6</note> is said to have been presented by Enya Takasada to the Emperor Go-Daigo (1318–1339). His Majesty praised it highly and said that it was certainly a "Heavenly horse" (*temma*, 天馬). At his question whether the fact that such a horse had appeared during his reign was a good or a bad omen, the courtiers answered that it was an extremely lucky sign, due to His Majesty's own virtues. As phoenixes appeared during the Chinese Emperor Shun's time

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the dragon-horse as an omen, specifically comparing the horse presented to Emperor Go-Daigo to legendary creatures from Chinese history and the differing interpretations of its significance.</meta>

(who is supposed to have reigned from 2255–2205 B.C.), and a *kilin* in the age of Confucius, so this heavenly horse was considered an excellent omen for the era, foreboding both the Emperor's long reign and life, as well as the glory of Buddhism. They further related how during the time of the Chinese Emperor Mu Wang of the Zhou dynasty, eight heavenly horses had appeared, each having a different name, and how the Emperor, drawn by all of them, had visited every place in the world <note>Compare the previous reference on page 59.</note>. So everyone present congratulated Go-Daigo on his horse, except for Fujiwara no Fujifusa. When his opinion was asked, he declared that he was convinced it was *not* a good omen, and he also referred to Chinese examples to confirm his statement. The houses of two Emperors of the Han dynasty, Wen and Guangwu, who had refused such presents, had enjoyed long and prosperous reigns, he said, while the house of Mu, who had used the eight heavenly horses, had soon declined. Those horses were merely a transformation of the Fang constellation (房, the eleventh of the zodiacal constellations), and a sign of the fall of the Zhou dynasty. Go-Daigo, upon hearing these words, was angry and put a stop to the festivities of the day. Not believing Fujifusa's pessimistic prediction, he accepted the horse, and a few years later (1336) the great schism of the Southern and Northern Courts seemed to prove the truth of Fujifusa's words.

The same work <note>Chapter XIV, page 14.</note> relates how the Emperor Go-Daigo gave the aforementioned dragon-horse to Nitta Yoshisada when he dispatched him to Owari province (1335). It was expected to cover the distance, which would have required four or five days with an ordinary horse, in half a day, so that he could be back in Kyoto that very evening. In a few hours he arrived in Ōmi province, but there the animal suddenly died, which was, of course, a very evil omen.

Finally, we may mention a dragon-horse which certainly was not a harbinger of evil, namely the one on which the Empress Jingō, after the death of her consort Chūai (200 A.D.), flew through the air to Sugiyama at Ikeda in Buzen province. There, she prayed to the gods for assistance regarding her expedition against Korea. Then the Four Heavenly Kings, holding eight white flags (Hachiman, 八幡) in their hands, descended from Heaven <note>*Sansha takusen ryakushō* (三社託宣略抄); author unknown; the year 1650 (Keian 3) is mentioned as the date of the epilogue. *Zoku zoku gunsho ruijū*, Vol. I, p. 741.</note>.

### § 3. The carriage of a ghost drawn through the air by eight dragons.

Regarding the same Emperor, a third tale in the *Taiheiki*¹ may be mentioned. Ōmori Morinaga, who had defeated Go-Daigo's loyal general, Kusunoki Masashige (1336), one evening saw Masashige's ghost appearing in the garden and trying to take his sword. He questioned the spirit about who accompanied him, and Masashige answered that Emperor Go-Daigo, the Emperor’s son Prince Morinaga (killed at Kamakura in 1335), and Nitta Yoshisada had come with him. Ōmori lit a torch and, looking upwards, discovered twenty demons in a large cloud carrying the Imperial sedan-chair on their shoulders; then followed the Prince in a carriage *drawn by eight dragons*², and Yoshisada rode in front with more than three thousand horsemen. This reminds us of a sentence in the *Gempei seisuiki*³, a quotation from the *Ba-iku-kyō*⁴, which says that "in heaven a horse is made into a dragon, and among men a dragon is made into a horse"⁵. The number eight is a standard feature in these legends about dragons ridden by kings or gods, or drawing their carriages. For example, we read about a Buddhist god with twelve faces and forty-two arms brandishing swords and lances, riding eight dragons through the air amidst rain and wind⁶.

### § 4. A dragon appears as a good omen.

The *Kanden jihitsu*⁷ describes a dragon which was seen under a bridge near Unawa village in Harima province, at the foot of Mount Shiko. It was seven <term>shaku</term> long, had one horn, hands, and feet, and its body was the color of tree leaves tinged with a golden luster. It was a beautiful animal, exactly like the red dragons in paintings. When the villagers descended from the

# CHAPTER III.

## CAUSING RAIN.

### § 1. Shintō gods.

The ancient annals of Japan very frequently speak of heavy droughts that threatened the country with hunger and misery. These were considered to be punishments, or at least plagues from the gods, which could only be stopped by earnest prayers and offerings to those same divinities. The ancient, dragon-shaped river-gods (the “river-uncles,” 河伯, *kawa no kami*) in particular—who from ancient times were believed to be the givers of rain—were entreated not to withhold their blessings any longer from the parched and suffering land.

The *Nihongi*<note>1 Ch. XXIV, K. T. K. Vol. I, p. 410.</note> tells us that in the first year of the Emperor Kōgyoku’s reign (642), there was a long drought that could not be stopped by the Shintō priests. In <term>ASTON</term>’s translation, this passage runs as follows:

> “25th day. The Ministers conversed with one another, saying: — ‘In accordance with the teachings of the village hafuri [Shintō priests], horses and cattle have been killed in some places as a sacrifice to the gods of the various (Shintō) shrines; in others, there have been frequent changes of the marketplaces [both old Chinese customs<note>2 See <term>ASTON</term>’s note on this passage (*Nihongi*, Vol. II, p. 174, note 4), and <term>FLORENZ</term>’s note 3 (*Nihongi*, *Japanische Annalen*, Book XXII—XXX, second ed., p. 75).</note>], or prayers to the River-gods. None of these practices have had any good result so far.’ Then Soga no Oho-omi [Iruka, the last of the Sogas, who was killed in 645 along with his father Emishi; all the Sogas—Iname, Umako, Emishi, and Iruka—were powerful ministers and great protectors of Buddhism] answered and said: — ‘The <term>Mahāyāna Sūtra</term> should be read by way of excerpts<note>3 轉讀, *tendoku*; <term>ASTON</term>, p. 175, note 1: “the reading of passages of a book to”</note> in the temples, our sins should be repented as Buddha teaches, and thus rain should be prayed for with humility.’”

> “27th day. In the South Court of the Great Temple, the images of Buddha and of the Bosatsu (Bodhisattvas), and the images of the four Deva Kings, were magnificently adorned. A multitude of priests, by humble request, read the Mahāyāna Sūtra. On this occasion Soga no Oho-omi held a censer in his hands, and having burnt incense in it, offered a prayer.”

> “28th day. A light rain fell.”

> “29th day. Since the prayers for rain were unsuccessful, the reading of the Sūtra was discontinued.”

> “8th month, 1st day. The Emperor made a journey to the river-source of Minabuchi. Here he knelt down and prayed, worshipping toward the four cardinal directions and looking up to Heaven <note>Chinese style, as the Buddhist prayers had been unsuccessful</note>. Immediately there was thunder and a heavy rain, which eventually fell for five days and plentifully drenched the Empire. <note>One record states: — ‘For five days there was continuous rain, and the nine grains ripened’</note>. At this, the peasantry throughout the Empire cried with one voice: ‘Banzai,’ and said: ‘An Emperor of great virtue’.”

Among the eighty-five Shintō shrines to which messengers were dispatched by the Court to pray for rain, the *Engishiki*<gloss>1</gloss> mentions several river and water deities, such as the gods of Kibune<gloss>2</gloss> and Nibu no kawakami<gloss>3</gloss>, but also the Wind gods of Tatsuta<gloss>4</gloss>, the Thunder god of Kamo<gloss>5</gloss> and many others. The *Nihongi*<gloss>6</gloss> repeatedly uses the same words regarding these prayers, namely: “The Emperor sent *daibu* (high-ranking officials) as envoys to the various Shintō shrines in order to pray for rain; he also dispatched messengers to pray to the god Ō-imi of Hirose and to the Wind gods of Tatsuta (龍田, ‘Dragonfield’).” Was it accidental that the Wind gods, who appeared to be also givers of rain, had their shrine at a place called

<keywords>Emperor Kōgyoku, rainmaking, Soga no Oho-omi, Minabuchi, Banzai, Engishiki, Nihongi, Shinto deities, Tatsuta Wind-gods, Kibune, Nibu no kawakami, drought rituals</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Shinto rain-making rituals, specifically addressing the indigenous Japanese concept of dragons (tatsu) and the historical blending of Shinto and Buddhist ceremonies to end droughts.</meta>

<page-num>154</page-num>

"Dragon-field"? The word *tatsu* (dragon) is, as far as I know, not found in the *Nihongi* except in this name. However, the fact that the ancient Japanese had such a word indicates that they themselves were familiar with a type of dragon before they were taught by Koreans and Chinese about the existence of Chinese dragons. They identified these *tatsu* with the *lung* (龍), and, as we have seen above (p. 138), wrote the name of their "water-fathers," *mizuchi*, with the character 虬 (*k'iu*, the horned dragon), while the word *okami* was written using a character partly consisting of "rain" and "dragon."

Their dragons were *kami* (gods)<sup>1</sup> who lived in rivers and seas, valleys and mountains (in rivulets, lakes, and ponds), granting rain to their worshippers. We learn that those river-gods could also cause wind from the passage of the *Nihongi* quoted above<sup>2</sup>, where the god of the Northern River is said to have caused a whirlwind to arise in order to submerge the calabashes. Thus, the three kinds of dragons found in Japan—original Japanese, Chinese, and Indian—all have one feature in common: the ability to cause rain; meanwhile, the winds belong to the domain of the first two.

The *Shoku Nihongi*<sup>3</sup> states that in 715, Empress Gemmei dispatched messengers to pray for rain at "famous mountains and large rivers" (名山大川), after which the rain fell in torrents within a few days. It is noteworthy that at the same time, she established religious festivals in the two great Buddhist temples of Nara—Kōfukuji and Hōryūji—and dispatched messengers to the different Shinto shrines with *nusa* (幣帛, offerings of hemp and bark-fiber<sup>4</sup>). We often see this dualism in the measures taken by the Emperors to stop drought or excessive rain, especially in later times when Buddhism became increasingly powerful<sup>5</sup>.

The Shinto gods who were believed to cause rain were also considered able to stop it, and we often read of prayers offered to them for that purpose. In times of drought, messengers were mostly dispatched to the different rain-bestowing gods within the so-called *gokinai* (五畿內), the five provinces adjoining the capital: Yamashiro, Yamato, Kawachi, Izumi, and Settsu<sup>1</sup>. The most powerful in this respect was apparently the river god of *Nibu Kawakami* (丹生川上神), mentioned in the *Engishiki*<sup>2</sup> among the ten temples of the Yoshino district in Yamato province. Not only were hemp and fiber offered to this river god, but occasionally a black horse was also offered to induce him to give rain<sup>3</sup>. His dragon form is evident from the term "Rain-Master" (雨師, *U-shi*), by which he was often designated in imitation of the Chinese dragons<sup>4</sup>, and which appears to have

> [Note: 月 is a misprint for 日]. On the eighteenth day it thundered, and a little rain slightly moistened the earth. On the nineteenth, there was an earthquake, and the light rain immediately stopped. The reading of the sutra was prolonged for two days more, because a good, moistening rain had not yet been obtained.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Shinto rain-making rituals, specifically focusing on the gods of the five provinces near the capital and the prominent role of the river god of Nibu Kawakami as the "Rain-Master."</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>

been given to him as a special title. People also prayed to him to stop wind and rain ¹.

The *Kimpishō* ² states that Court nobles were responsible for the offerings sent by the Emperor to the Nibu and Kibune shrines to pray for rain or to ask the dragon-gods to stop continuous rains. These nobles, however, did not go there themselves; instead, they dispatched officials of the *Jingikwan*, or, on special occasions, Court officials (*kurabito*). There were sixteen *Shintō* shrines where the gods were worshiped for the purpose of causing or stopping rain: the seven "Upper shrines" (those of Ise, Iwashimizu, Kamo, Matsuo, Hirano, Inari, and Kasuga), and additionally those of Ōharano, Yamato Ishigami, Hirose, Tatsuta, Sumiyoshi, Nibu, and Kibune ³.

Finally, in the Kamige district of Buzen province, there was a place called *Tatsu no fuchi* (龍の淵), or "Dragon's Pool," home to a native Japanese dragon who was famous for granting rain to those who prayed to him ⁴. In the Sakai district of Echizen province, there is still a *Shintō* shrine today dedicated to *Kokuryū Myōjin* (黑龍明神), "the Black Dragon-god." It is located on the bank of the *Kuzuryū-gawa* (九頭龍川), or "River of the Nine-headed Dragon," which is also called the *Kokuryū-gawa*, or "Black Dragon's Flood." If one prays there for rain, the prayer is certainly heard ⁵.

### § 2. Horses offered to Shintō gods.

Regarding the horses offered to the rain-gods, we may refer to another passage ⁶, which states that in 838, white horses were offered twice to the god of *Kibune* (貴布禰) on Mount Kurama near Kyoto—another famous rain-god—and to the previously mentioned—

called “Rain-Master,” in order to stop the rain. The offering to him of a blue (that is, dark-colored) horse in 859 (to stop the rain) and of a white one in 875 and 883 is recorded in the *Sandai jitsuroku* <note>1</note>, while black horses were twice offered to the same god in 877, as well as in 880 and 885 <note>2</note>. It is no wonder that the Emperor repeatedly promoted this mighty river-god to a higher rank <note>3</note>. The *Kimpishō* <note>4</note>, a work written in the Kenryaku era (1211–1212) by the Emperor Juntoku, says that, when officials of the Jingikwan (the Department of <term>Shintō</term> Rites and Ceremonies) went to the shrines of Nibu (the “Rain-Master”) and Kibune to pray for rain or to beseech these gods to stop the excessive rains, they took a sacred horse with them from the Imperial stables. When Kurabito (*kurōdo*, or *kurando*, 藏人, officials of the <term>kurōdo-dokoro</term>, which was responsible for Imperial decrees) went to those temples, one of the Emperor’s ordinary horses or one taken from the stables of the retired Emperor was considered sufficient. To stop rain, a red horse was offered, and when rain was needed, a white horse was offered, because the color red was avoided when praying for rain. The

*Engishiki*¹, on the contrary, states that in the Engi era (901—922) a white horse was offered in the former case, and a black one in the latter. This may have varied at different times; red (or blue, that is, dark-colored), black, and white were at any rate the colors used, of which red was limited to cases in which the stopping of rain was prayed for.

### § 3 Buddhism Gains Ground.

Especially in the last of the six oldest Japanese Standard Histories (the *Rikkokushi*, 六 國 史), that is, the *Sandai jitsuroku* (written in 910), we see the Buddhist priests gradually prevailing in their struggle against the Shintōists. While formerly in times of drought there was only one way of averting this evil—namely, praying and making offerings to the Shintō rain-gods, and among them especially to the dragon-shaped river-gods—the Emperors now began to employ Buddhist assistance at the same time, or sometimes even without addressing the Shintō deities.

It is most characteristic that in the seventh month of 877, when such a heavy drought prevailed that the Prime Minister, Fujiwara no Mototsune, offered his resignation because he considered it a sign of his bad government, nothing was said about prayers or offerings to Shintō gods². The Emperor did not accept Mototsune’s resignation, and attributed the drought to a curse from Empress Jingō’s mausoleum at Tatanami (楯 列) in Yamato, to which he accordingly sent messengers to investigate the matter. They reported that a stag had been cut to pieces and eaten, and that peasants had cut down three hundred and thirty-two trees near the mausolea; the guilty officials were punished, but the drought continued. Then one hundred Buddhist priests were summoned to the Shishinden (a building of the Palace) and there read the *Daihannya* (Mahāprajñāpāramitā) Sutra for three days; this was the sutra usually read in autumn, but it was also used to cause rain. After two days a thunderstorm arose, and clouds covered the sky. A slight rain fell, but this was not sufficient, so the sutra reading was prolonged for two days and the *Ni-ō* (仁 王) Sutra was read. The next day, even the water of the pond in the *Shinsenen*, or Sacred Spring Park (see below, § 4), was required to irrigate the rice fields; in one day and one night the pond was quite dry. Then the Emperor sent messengers

to Empress Jingō’s mausoleum to apologize for the cutting of the trees and the killing of the stag. Sūtras were read there for five days without any result whatsoever, and some of the monks were so ashamed that they slipped away. One of them, however, the well-known high priest Dentō Daihōshi <note>1 **傳燈大法師**; compare *Fusō ryakki*, Ch. XX, K. T. K. Vol. VI, p. 598.</note>, advised having one of his pupils try his magical art of making rain using tantras. Then the pupil was summoned and was clever enough to set a limit of five days. The next day, an earthquake and a thunderstorm announced the successful result of the tantras; the rain poured down for three days, and there was great joy in the Palace and throughout the land.

Two years earlier, in 875, messengers were dispatched to fifteen great Buddhist temples, and the *Daihannya sūtra* was read to obtain rain <note>2 Chapter XXVII, pages 414 and following.</note>. Sixty Buddhist priests read the same holy text in the Taikyokuden (a building of the Palace), and fifteen others recited the *Daiunrin seiu kyō* (大雲輪請雨經, “Great Cloud-wheel Rain-praying sūtra”) <note>3 This is the *Mahāmegha sūtra*, discussed in the Introduction, § 4, pages 25 and following.</note> in the aforementioned Sacred Spring Park (Shinsenen). High officials went to the Imperial mausoleum at Fukakusa and, apologizing for any evil that might have been done, they prayed for mercy; for the Jingikwan (the Department of Shintō rites and ceremonies) had declared the drought to be a curse resulting from the cutting of trees at this mausoleum.

### § 4. The Sacred Spring Park.

The *Shinsenen* (神泉苑, “Sacred Spring Park”) was an important place in ancient times, and it is mentioned countless times in the ancient annals, from the *Nihon kōki* to the *Fusō ryakki*. The ways in which it is mentioned, however, are quite different. In the older works, the Emperors are said to have visited it many times for their amusement, to watch wrestlers, etc., but in the *Sandai jitsuroku*, it appears to have become the site where Buddhist services were held to obtain rain. In addition to 875, we read about such a ceremony in 877, when Dentō Daihōshi—the same man who a month later recommended his pupil to produce rain through tantras <note>4 See above, § 3, on this page.</note>—went to the park at the head of twenty-one other Buddhist priests and, practicing the method of reciting the “Sūtra of the Golden-Winged Bird-King”

<summary>This page details the transition from Shintō to Buddhist rituals for rain-making, including the use of tantras and specific sūtras at imperial mausoleums and the Sacred Spring Park (Shinsenen) to end severe droughts.</summary>
<keywords>rain-making, Buddhism, Empress Jingō, Shinsenen, Dentō Daihōshi, tantras, Daihannya sūtra, Shintō, drought rituals</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of rain-making rituals in 875 A.D., specifically focusing on the transition from Buddhist ceremonies to Shinto-based traditions and the significance of the Sacred Spring Park.</meta>

(No doubt it was the Garuda, intended to frighten the dragon and make it ascend)¹, they prayed for rain. The next day, another high priest, Risshi Enju, and a high-ranking member of the Board of Ceremonies, Tachibana Ason, were sent by the Emperor to the Great Buddha (*Daibutsu*) of Tōdaiji at Nara to pray there for three days; yet it was all in vain. Then the river-gods of Kakō² in Hitachi province and Karo³ in Inaba were elevated to higher ranks, and messengers were sent to all the Imperial mausoleums with the announcement that the *nengō* (era name) was changed (from Jōgwan to Gwangyō, a means of preventing the continuation of the evil—that is, the drought)⁴. What was described above (§ 3) then followed. It is interesting to observe that the assistance of the ancient Shintō deities was not requested until after the Buddhist priests had proven unable to bring rain. Even then, no prayers or offerings were made as they had been in the past; instead, the gods were merely promoted to higher ranks, and the change of the *nengō* was simply announced to the Imperial ancestors.

It was the pond in the park that led the Buddhists to choose it for their rain prayers. We read in the *Sandai jitsuroku*⁵ that on the 23rd day of the 6th month of 875 A.D., when all the rituals performed by the Buddhist priests mentioned above⁶ had only caused a slight, insufficient rain to fall, an old man said: "In the pond of the Sacred Spring Park, there is a divine dragon. In the past, during times of severe drought, the water of this pond was drained and the pond was dried up; bells and drums were beaten, and when (the dragon) answered (the request), it thundered and rained. This is certain to produce a good result." The Emperor then dispatched high officials to the park and had the water drained.
