--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of rain-making ceremonies in 875 AD at the Sacred Spring Park, moving from the failure of Buddhist rituals to the implementation of older Chinese-influenced customs involving noise and dragon-boats.</meta>

Other officials, the Court musicians, took their places on a dragon-boat (龍舟, a boat with a dragon-shaped prow, see above, Book I, pp. 83 ff.) and beat bells and drums, sang and danced, so that their voices “made heaven shake.” The next day it thundered and rained a little, but after a short while the sky became clear again, and outside of Kyōto the dust was only slightly moistened. On the 25th the result was the same, and on the 26th the officials—who had been making music on the pond incessantly, night and day—were praised by the Emperor and were allowed to stop the work.

From this passage, we learn that the dragon of the pond in the Sacred Spring Park was originally not an Indian Nāga introduced by the Buddhists, but a Chinese, or perhaps a Japanese, dragon. This dragon formerly used to be forced to ascend and to make rain by being deprived of its element, the water, or by being stirred up by a terrible noise, according to the Chinese methods described above <note>Book I, Ch. V, § 3, p. 119; cf. the Chinese legend concerning the Emperor Shi Huang, whose soldiers made a terrible noise to frighten the dragon god (Book I, Ch. VI, § 7, p. 125).</note>. The Buddhist priests identified this dragon with an Indian Nāga-king, whom they caused to give rain by reading sūtras. In the seventh century, however, Chinese ideas prevailed at the Japanese Court, and the Emperor himself sometimes proceeded to a river, and, kneeling and bowing to the four quarters of the compass, prayed to Heaven in the Chinese way. Then it thundered and continuous rains made the crops thrive <note>*Fusō ryakki*, Ch. IV, K. T. K. Vol. VI, p. 508, the Emperor Kwōgyoku in 642.</note>.

In 875, the old Chinese methods of causing rain had apparently sunk into oblivion at the Japanese Court, but they were tried again when the old man turned the attention of the Courtiers to them because the sūtras failed to have any effect.

Like the Shintō dragon-gods, the dragon in the Sacred Spring Park was believed not only to be able to make rain, but also to possess the ability of stopping it if it was pouring too abundantly. Thus, in 880, a Buddhist priest recited the *Kwanchō* (灌頂, washing the head, baptism) *sūtra* there for three days in order to stop the rain <note>*Sandai jitsuroku*, Ch. XXXVII, p. 541.</note>.

The *Nihon kiryaku* <note>日本紀略, written after 1036, K. T. K. Vol. V.</note> also contains several passages relating to Buddhist rain-prayers in the park. In 972, the so-called “Law (method) of the Rain-praying-sūtra” (*Seiukyō-hō*, 請雨經法, i.e. the doctrine of the *Mahāmēgha sūtra*, cf. above, pp. 25 ff.)

<summary>The text describes the revival of ancient Chinese rain-making rituals in 875 AD, such as using dragon-boats and drums, after Buddhist sūtras failed to end a drought. It highlights the cultural blend of local Japanese or Chinese dragon lore with imported Indian Buddhist Nāga traditions at the Sacred Spring Park.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of rain-making rituals at the Sacred Spring Park, moving from the historical annals of the 9th, 10th, and 11th centuries to legendary accounts involving the famous priest Kōbō Daishi.</meta>

...was practiced there for nine days with a splendid result, as well as in 982, 985, and 1018¹.

When leaving the Annals and turning to legendary works, we obtain the following information. The *Konjaku monogatari*² relates how, during a time of severe drought, the Emperor ordered Kōbō Daishi (774—835) to bring rain. For seven days, the saint practiced the ritual of the Rain-Praying Sūtra in the Sacred Spring Park. Then, a snake³ appeared on the right side of the altar, five shaku long, carrying a small gold-colored snake about five sun in length; after a while, both disappeared into the pond. Only four of the twenty priests who were sitting in a row could see the apparition. One of these chosen ones asked what it meant, to which another answered that the appearance of the Indian dragon-king Zennyo (善如)—who lived in the Anavatapta⁴ pond in India and was now living in the pond of the Sacred Spring Park—was a sign that the ritual would be successful. Indeed, a dark cloud rose in the northwest, and soon the rain was pouring down. From then on, whenever drought prevailed, the same ritual was practiced in the park, and never in vain.

The *Kojidan*⁵ states that this event occurred in the year 824. According to this work, the Buddhist priest Shubin (守敏) requested the Emperor’s permission to practice the rain-prayer ritual himself instead of Kūkai (Kōbō Daishi), as he claimed to be as experienced in such matters as the latter. This was granted, and he succeeded in causing thunder and rain in Kyoto, but not beyond Higashi yama. Then Kōbō Daishi was ordered to make it rain over the whole country, which he promised to do within seven days. This time limit, however, expired, and the sky remained as cloudless as before. The saint, absorbed in meditation (samādhi), concluded that Shubin, his rival,

had captured all the dragons and shut them up in a water pitcher by using magical formulas (tantras). This was the reason why his (Kōbō’s) own prayers had been in vain. However, he decided not to give up hope and continued to recite the sutra. During the night of the second day, he said: “In this pond is a dragon called Zennyo, who pities mankind. I have prayed to him, and now I see him rising out of the middle of the lake, gold-colored, about eight sun long, sitting on the head of another dragon eight shaku in length.” This was reported to the Emperor, who soon sent a messenger with offerings for the Dragon King. When the seven days of the new vow had ended, a heavy thunderstorm broke out and a torrent of rain fell over the entire country, so that the water of the pond overflowed the altar. As a reward for saving the people from starvation, Kūkai was promoted to the rank of Shōsōzu (bishop) ¹.

The *Taiheiki* ² provides another version of the same legend. After stating that the park was laid out in the time of Emperor Kanmu (781–806) in imitation of the Lingyiu (靈囿), the park of the Chinese Emperor Wen of the Zhou dynasty, the author informs us that the same Japanese monarch (who built the Palace at Kyoto, the new capital he founded and made his residence in 794) had two Buddhist monasteries built on the east and west sides of the Suzaku gate, called Tōji and Seiji, “the Eastern and the Western Monastery.” The former was under the direction of Kōbō Daishi, who was tasked with guarding the Emperor’s rank; the latter was under Bishop Shubin, who was to protect His Majesty’s person. After Kōbō Daishi’s return from China, Shubin—who had been the most influential figure during Kōbō’s absence—was overshadowed by his rival. The Emperor, who had greatly admired Shubin’s miraculous magical power, now considered Kōbō his superior. This was more than the ambitious Shubin could endure; he nurtured a deep hatred against both his sovereign and his rival. To seek revenge against the Emperor, he captured all the dragon gods of the inner and outer seas by the power of his tantras and shut them up in a water pitcher. In this way, he caused the terrible drought about which we read in the *Konjaku Monogatari* and *Kojidan*; it lasted for three full months and caused the people to suffer immensely. Then Kōbō Daishi reported to the Emperor that there was only one dragon, a

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the rain-making contest between Kōbō Daishi and the priest Shubin, detailing Kōbō's successful summoning of the Dragon King Zennyo and the subsequent defeat and death of his rival.</meta>

A Bodhisattva of higher rank than Shubin, namely the Dragon King Zennyo of the Anavatapta pond<gloss>1</gloss> in Northern India, was not within Shubin's power. Immediately, a pond was dug in front of the Palace and filled with pure water, whereupon Kōbō invited the Dragon King to come and live there. And behold, a gold-colored dragon, eight *sun* long, appeared, seated on the head of a snake more than nine *shaku* in length, and entered the pond. When Kōbō reported this fortunate news, the Emperor sent a messenger with all kinds of offerings to worship the Dragon King. The result was marvelous, for it soon rained for three days across the entire Empire. Since that day, the Shingon sect flourished more and more, and Kōbō Daishi was highly revered by people of all ranks. In vain Shubin worshipped Gundari<gloss>2</gloss> and the Yakshas to destroy his enemy; for as soon as Kōbō heard this, he began to worship Dai Itoku Myō-ō<gloss>3</gloss>, and there was a violent struggle in the air between these two parties. "To make Shubin careless, Kōbō caused a rumor of his own death to be spread, which created great sorrow among all classes of people, but great joy in his enemy's heart. As Kōbō had expected, Shubin dismantled his altar and stopped worshipping the demons, but at that same moment Kōbō's power struck him, and he fell dead on the floor. His monastery soon decayed and disappeared, and Tōji's glory increased yearly. Kōbō made a dragon out of so-called *chigaya* (*Imperata arundinacea*, a kind of reed) and placed it upon an altar<gloss>4</gloss>. Then he promised the assembled crowd that he would cause the real dragon to stay in the park and protect the country through his doctrine, while the reed Dragon King would become a large dragon and go to the Anavatapta pond in India. According to another tradition, the reed dragon ascended to the sky and flew away toward the east, but stopped in Owari Province at Atsuta's famous Shintō shrine—a fortunate omen of the spread of Buddha's Law to the East. Kōbō said: "When this Dragon King (that is, the real one) goes to another country, the pond will dry up, the land will be a wasteland, and the world will be in poverty.. Then my priests (the Shingon priests) must pray for the Dragon King to stay, and thus save the country."

So we know that the Buddhist priests, ordered by the different

<summary>Kōbō Daishi successfully summons the Dragon King Zennyo to bring rain to Japan, leading to the growth of the Shingon sect and the death of his rival, Shubin. <meta>This page continues the discussion of rain-making rituals, following the account of Kōbō Daishi’s success and the demise of his rival, Shubin.</meta>

The priests appointed by Emperors to pray in the park for rain or for the cessation of rain always belonged to the <term>Shingon</term> sect.
The *Kojidan*¹ relates how in 1016, Bishop Shinkaku (深 覺) prayed for rain in the park and achieved a splendid success within a few hours, following a very long and severe drought. The <term>Naidaijin</term>, one of the Ministers, had sent him a message to warn him that he would be mocked by the world if he failed, but the bishop answered that he would attempt it not for his own sake, but for the sake of the people. And behold, during the <term>hour of the sheep</term>, dark clouds arose, a heavy thunderstorm burst forth, and the rain fell in torrents.
In the *Gempei seisuiki*² we read that in 1179, the "Secret Doctrine of the <term>Rain-prayer-sūtra</term>" was practiced in vain in the Sacred Spring Park; nor did the prayers of other powerful priests have any effect, until at last a secret tune, played on a <term>biwa</term> at the shrine of Sumiyoshi, caused a continuous and heavy rain to fall. According to the *Hyakurenshō*³, the same <term>sūtra</term> was read in the park in the years 1215 and 1224; and the *Genkō Shakusho*⁴ relates the same thing regarding the year 1082.
The *Zoku kojidan*⁵ mentions a two-storied gate on the south side of the park, which was destroyed by the "Dragon of the Sacred Spring." During the time of Fujiwara no Saneyori (899–970), this dragon entered this gate in the form of a beautiful man. He sat down, and when he was asked where he came from, he answered that he lived in the West and had passed through the gate on his way to another place. Then he disappeared, and at the same moment, the sky became dark and a terrible thunderstorm arose. Tradition says that the Buddhist bishop Genkwa was just reciting the <term>Rain-prayer-sūtra</term> in the park when the gate was destroyed.
The *Kimpishō*⁶ tells us that in the event of a drought, the court officials were tasked first of all with cleaning the Sacred Spring Park. Then they were ordered by the Emperor to go to the

Regarding other rituals, the *Kimpishō* mentions the praying for rain at the Imperial tombs <note>2 See above, page 158 and following.</note>, and the reading of <term>sūtras</term> in the Taikyokuden, a building of the Palace <note>3 See above, in the same place.</note>, or in the seven great Buddhist temples of Nara (Tōdaiji, Kōfukuji, Genkōji, Daianji, Yakushiji, Seidaiji, and Hōryūji), or in the various Shinto temples. Sometimes, for instance in the Ōwa era (961–963), the Great Bear was worshipped in the Sacred Spring Park in order to obtain rain.

An interesting legend is told about the Dragon of the Sacred Spring Park in the *Taiheiki* <note>6 The Taiheiki is a historical epic of the 14th century.</note>. It runs as follows: In 1335, the Emperor Godaigo was invited by the <term>Dainagon</term> Saionji Kimmune, one of the Fujiwara, to come to his house in order to see a new bathroom. This invitation was given with the intention to kill His Majesty, who would have stepped upon a loose board of the floor and fallen down upon a row of swords, placed upright with the points facing upward. Fortunately, the Emperor was saved by the dragon of the pond in the park, who—in the night before he intended to go to the fatal house—appeared to him in a dream in the shape of a woman, clad in a red <term>hakama</term> and light-colored garments. She said to him: "Before you are tigers and wolves; behind you are brown and spotted bears. Do not go tomorrow." When he asked who she was, she answered that she had lived for many years in the Sacred Spring Park. Then she went away. When the Emperor awoke, he

Before leaving this subject, we may observe that according to the *Kokushi daijiten* <gloss>¹</gloss>, the park was repeatedly destroyed and restored, but the pond is still there. On a small island in the middle of it, there are two chapels: one dedicated to Zennyo, the Dragon-King, and the other to Benten. Thus, this dragon, identified with an Indian Naga, has bestowed rain upon Japan for eleven hundred years!

### § 5. The “Dragon-hole” on Mount Murōbu.

The previously mentioned "Dragon-hole" (*Ryū-ketsu*, 龍穴), where sutras were read to cause rain, is discussed in the *Kojidan* <gloss>²</gloss>, where we find the following details.

The Dragon-hole on Mount Murōbu <gloss>³</gloss> in Yamato Province is the home of the Dragon-King Zentatsu (善達, possibly Sudatta or Sudarśana <gloss>⁴</gloss>), who first lived in the Sarusawa <gloss>⁵</gloss> pond at Nara. In ancient times, when a prostitute drowned herself in that pond, the Dragon-King fled to Mount Kasuga; he lived there until the corpse of a man of low social standing was thrown into his pond. He then fled again and established himself on Mount Murōbu, where the Buddhist bishop Kenkei practiced his religious austerities. Another priest named Nittai, who for many years had cherished the wish of seeing and worshiping the Dragon-King's venerable form, entered the hole to seek him. The entrance was pitch black, but after penetrating into the inner part of the hole, he arrived at a splendid palace under a blue sky. Through the opening of a window blind (*sudare*) made of pearls, which moved in the wind, he saw a portion of the *Hokkekyō* (the *Saddharma Puṇḍarīka Sutra*) lying on a jeweled table. Then he heard a voice asking who he was. When he gave his name and explained why he had entered the hole, the Dragon-King (the invisible speaker) said: “You cannot...

<meta>This page continues the legend of the priest Nittai and his encounter with the Dragon-king at Mount Murōbu, and further explores the transition of dragon worship from Shintō to Buddhist contexts.</meta>

see me. Leave this hole, and you will meet me at a distance of about three *chō* from the entrance." So Nittai left the hole and actually beheld the Dragon-king, who rose out of the ground wearing a robe and a cap, and disappeared after being worshiped by the priest. The priest built a Shintō shrine on the spot and erected an image of the Dragon-king, which was still there during the author's time (at the beginning of the thirteenth century). Sutras were read at this shrine when people prayed for rain; and when the Dragon-king listened favorably to the prayers, a dark cloud hung over the hole. This cloud spread over the entire sky, and the rain fell. <note>1</note>

The *Kojidan* tells this story; and it is immediately striking that a Buddhist priest erected a Shintō shrine in honor of the Nāga. The legend was apparently invented by Buddhists to convert this dragon-hole—which was probably the home of one of the mountain dragons of old Japan mentioned above <note>2</note>—into a place of Buddhist sanctity. They changed the old Shintō cult into Nāga worship without, however, going so far as to replace the Shintō shrine with a Buddhist temple. The *Ryūketsu-jinja*, the “Shintō shrine of the Dragon-hole,” was later called the *Ryū-ō-sha*, or Dragon-king's temple, and was famous for the rain-bestowing power of its dragon-god. <note>3</note>

This same dragon is called Zennyo (善女, “The Good Woman”; compare the Zennyo, 善如, in the Sacred Spring Park, identified with Anavatapta <note>4</note>), instead of Zentatsu, in the *Genkō Shakusho* <note>5</note>. In that work, it is said that the Buddhist priest Ringa <note>6</note>, who died in 1150, was so powerful that Zennyo, the Dragon-king, appeared when he prayed for rain. The same work states that the Buddhist priest Keien <note>7</note> lived for a thousand days as a hermit near the Dragon-hole on Mount Murōbu. While traveling from there to another location, he was crossing a bridge over a river when suddenly

A dragon of the same name (Zennyo) was said to live in the *Zennyo ryū-ō chi* <note>善女龍王池 (*Zennyo ryū-ō chi*).</note>, or "Dragon-king Zennyo's Pond," near the "Chapel of the Thirty Guardian-gods" <note>*Sanjū banshin dō* (三十番神堂).</note> on a mountain peak in Kawachi Province, Ishikawa District. In both these places, he was prayed to for rain with great success. <note>Ibid., Chapter 4, page 51.</note>

### § 6. Reborn as a rain-giving dragon.

In the *Kojidan* <note>Chapter 3, pages 69 and following.</note>, we read about Bishop Gonkyū of Kwazan. Inside a dense cloud, a sacred dragon appeared to him along with the priest Shōkyū <note>性救 (Shōkyū).</note> from the Western Pagoda <note>Saitō (西塔).</note> on Mount Hiei. This dragon was the "true form" of Gobyō (御廟) Daishi (that is, Bishop Jie <note>慈慧大師 (Jie Daishi), a famous Tendai priest who lived from 912 to 985 and...</note>), whom Gonkyū had often prayed to see. When he asked why the priest was in the company of the dragon god, he was told that Shōkyū would become a relative of this god (meaning, he would become a dragon). As soon as Gonkyū woke up, he sent a messenger to the Saitō Monastery to inquire about Shōkyū's health. Upon hearing that the priest had been ill for

### § 7. Buddhist priests dominating the dragons.

The *Kojidan* <note>Chapter 3, page 83.</note> mentions the remarkable answer given by Bishop Jōkai <note>**定海**</note> to the Emperor when the latter expressed his admiration for the priest's power, because it had rained violently for a couple of hours after Jōkai had been praying for two days. "Your Majesty," he said, "this is not *my* rain, and I cannot accept any reward for it. My rain, however, will arise tomorrow from the northwest and fall. Then you may reward me." And actually, the next day the clouds came from the northwest, and it rained for three days.

A master in calling up and dominating the dragon-gods was also the Buddhist priest Jōkwan <note>**靜觀**</note>, who in the Engi era (901–922) freed the country from a terrible drought by causing the dragons to move about amidst thunder and rain <note>*Uji shūi monogatari* (**宇治拾遺物語**), written 1213–1218; Chapter 2, K.T.K. Vol. XII, pages 31 and following.</note>. This same monk conquered a poisonous dragon on Mount Hiei. There was on this mountain a rock in the shape of an open dragon's mouth, and the monks who lived nearby in Saitō <note>**西塔**</note>, and especially in a monastery called Senju-in <note>**千手院**</note>, all died quickly. At last it was made

<summary>This page details several accounts of Buddhist priests interacting with dragons, including Shōkyū's rebirth as a rain-giving snake and the ability of bishops like Jōkai and Jōkwan to command the weather and subdue poisonous dragons.</summary>
<keywords>Shōkyū, Gobyō Daishi, Bishop Jie, Bishop Jōkai, Jōkwan, Mount Hiei (Hieizan), rain-making, dragon-kings, Kojidan, Uji shūi monogatari, Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Buddhist priests who possessed the power to command dragons, following the mention of the priest Jōkwan.</meta>

...out that the rock was the cause of their death, and since that time it was called the "Poisonous-Dragon-rock" <note>*Dokuryū no iwa*.</note>. No one would live there anymore, and Saitō and Senju-in became quite deserted and fell into ruin. Then Jōkwan went to the location and prayed for seven days and nights before the rock. On the last night, the sky became cloudy and there was a terrible turbulence in the air, while Hieizan was covered with clouds. After a while, however, it cleared up, and behold! The rock had disappeared and only some rubble was left. From then on, it was safe to live in Saitō, and Jōkwan's name was kept in grateful memory and admiration by the monks of the mountain even in the author's day. Apparently, the poisonous dragon had left the place as a result of the prayers, which were also so powerful in stirring up the dragons and the clouds during times of drought <note>*Uji shūi monogatari*, as cited above.</note>.

According to the *Fusō ryakki* <note>Chapter 29, *K. T. K.* Volume 6, page 807.</note>, on the 21st day of the second month of 1065, the priests of Hieizan assembled in the Kamo temple at Kyōto, where they prayed for rain and recited the *Ni-ō sūtra*. Then a little snake appeared and spat out some vapor before the sanctuary, after which a little rain fell.

The *Gempei seisuiki* <note>Chapter 3, *Teikoku Bunko*, Volume 5, page 69.</note> relates that in 1174, such a severe drought prevailed that the rivers dried up and the fields could not be cultivated. Then a priest of Hieizan, named Chōken <note>**澄憲**</note>, who held the rank of *Gonshōsōzu* <note>**權少僧都**, "Vice-bishop".</note>, in order to assist the peasants, wrote a letter to the Dragon-gods and read it aloud while looking up at the sky. In this letter, he reproved and instructed the dragons, while at the same time imploring them to make it rain. He wrote that heavenly beings (gods) and dragon-gods should not be ashamed to remedy a wrong they had done; therefore, the dragons had to cause a "sweet rain" (甘雨) to fall and put a stop to this terrible drought. The dragons listened to these words and provided continuous rain, so that both the Emperor and the people were filled with admiration for Chōken's power and with devotion to Buddha's Law.

### § 8. Dragon-women in ponds.

The *Sanshū kidan* <note>*Sanshū kidan*, written in 1764 by Hotta Bakusui (**堀田麥水**); Chapter 1.</note> contains the following legends. In the

<summary>This page recounts historical legends of Buddhist priests from Mount Hiei, such as Jōkwan and Chōken, who successfully performed rituals and wrote to dragon-gods to end droughts or remove malevolent spirits. It concludes by introducing a new section on legends regarding dragon-women residing in ponds.</summary>

<keywords>Jōkwan, Poisonous-Dragon-rock, Hieizan, Chōken, rain-making, Dragon-gods, sweet rain, Buddhist priests, Japanese folklore, drought rituals</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon legends in Japan, following accounts of Buddhist priests who used their spiritual powers to influence dragons and control the weather.</meta>

In the vicinity of seven ponds in the mountains of Enuma, a district of Kaga province, many strange things happened. People said that they had heard the voices of several hundred men there in the middle of the night, and that they had seen these men lighting torches upon the ponds. Fishermen had seen the water rising without any visible reason, and the more they retreated, the higher the water rose, until they finally stopped fishing and fled home as fast as their legs could carry them. When looking back from a distance of one or two chō <note>A unit of distance equal to about 109 meters.</note> from the ponds, they saw a silver dragon (<term>銀龍</term>) in the form of a boy (*ginryū no warabegata*, <term>童形</term>) appearing above the water. There was a road between these ponds from which a huge face sometimes darted out; and one night, it was as if men were fighting there.

In times of drought, the people worshipped these ponds and prayed there for rain. One day, a little girl was found there by the inhabitants of a neighboring monastery. They took her home and educated her, but after twelve years she constantly expressed the wish to make a pilgrimage to Ise. Although she received the answer that this did not agree with the law of the empire (as she was a woman), she persisted in speaking about it. At last, her foster fathers gave in, secretly hired a sedan chair, and let her go to Ise. She went off gladly, but when she came to a lake, she said: "This must be my lake; take me to the bank." When the sedan chair carriers did so, she alighted, adjusted her clothes, and said: "I am well acquainted here; you can go home." Then, with her beautiful garments on, she jumped into the water and disappeared in the depths. She was a beautiful girl, but her face was long (a sign of something unnatural <note>Bewitching women are often described as having extraordinarily long faces. Compare *Sanshū kidan*, Chapter I, page 673, where a gigantic woman with a huge face is supposed to be a fox or a tanuki—in any case, the vital spirit (<term>精</term>) of an old creature.</note>). Although the author does not state it, this was apparently a female dragon temporarily transformed into a girl.

Another dragon woman lived in the so-called Rope-pond (Nawa-ga-ike, <term>繩ガ池</term>) in Etchū province. This was a pond in the mountains, about two ri <note>A unit of distance; one ri is about 3.9 kilometers.</note> in diameter. Heavy storms and rains often raged in this vicinity, even when splendid weather prevailed everywhere around it. Down to the author’s time, the dragon woman was said to live in the pond and to cause it to never dry up; and his contemporaries still ascribed to her a great influence on the weather <note>*Ibidem*, Chapter V, page 839.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts legends of dragons in the provinces of Kaga and Etchū, including a silver dragon appearing as a boy and a dragon transformed into a girl who eventually returns to the water.</summary>
<keywords>Enuma, Kaga province, silver dragon, ginryū, Ise pilgrimage, Rope-pond, Etchū province, dragon woman, weather myths, Sanshū kidan</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Japanese dragon and snake legends, following the account of a beautiful girl who revealed her true nature by jumping into a lake.</meta>

A similar pond is mentioned in the *Sanshū kidan kōhen* <note>¹ **三州奇談後編**, written in 1779 by the same author; Chapter V, page 952.</note>. An evil snake (<term>akuja</term>, 悪蛇) was believed to have her home there and to commit all kinds of strange acts. When someone stood on the bank of the pond and looked over the water, such a dreary wind blew that most people fled home. If someone prayed there for rain, their prayer was usually heard. The author was uncertain whether a terrible-looking woman—who appeared one night on a neighboring bridge to a man returning from a festival slightly tipsy—was the snake of the pond or a transformed wind-tanuki <note>² **風狸**, *kaze-tanuki*; compare my treatise on "*The Fox and Badger in Japanese Folklore*" in *Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan*, Volume XXXVII, Part III, page 403.</note>. She stood on the railing of the bridge, binding up her hair and laughing loudly with an open mouth so that all her black teeth were visible. Her malicious face was square and very ugly, and it seemed as if she had only one leg. When people approached with torches, she flew away. Another time, she attacked a man who had also enjoyed a good cup of sake and was on his way home in the dead of night. She threw him from the road into the grass and then disappeared, but the poor fellow was ill for an entire month. As the water of the pond flowed around the village and under this bridge, Hotta says it is possible that the woman was the snake of the pond, although her body—which she moved so easily while flying away—did not resemble a *dragon-snake* (龍蛇) (which always requires a cloud as a vehicle). The name of the pond, "Shiroshūto (白醜人) no ike," or "Pond of the White and Ugly Person," perhaps had something to do with the snake's transformation into an ugly woman.

### § 9. Stirring up the dragons by throwing iron or filth into their ponds.

If an iron utensil was thrown into the Rope-pond mentioned in § 8, darkness suddenly covered the land and a hurricane devastated the rice fields. For this reason, the villagers strictly forbade others from approaching the pond without a special reason. It was said that greedy merchants who had bought rice threw metal shavings into the pond to cause storms and rain, which would destroy the crop and thus cause the price of rice to rise <note>³ [Note marker preserved from original text structure]</note>. This way of stirring up the dragons by means of

<summary>This page describes the legend of a malicious snake or "wind-tanuki" inhabiting a pond and appearing as an ugly woman, as well as the superstitious practice of throwing iron into ponds to provoke dragons and cause destructive weather.</summary>
<keywords>Sanshū kidan kōhen, akuja, wind-tanuki, kaze-tanuki, Hotta, dragon-snake, Shiroshūto no ike, Rope-pond, weather manipulation, rice prices</keywords>

--- Page 191 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Japanese dragon and snake legends, specifically focusing on methods used to provoke these creatures into causing rain, a practice often involving the pollution of their ponds or the offering of mutilated fish.</meta>

Iron, which they disliked very much, was borrowed from China, as we have seen above ¹; this practice was also carried out at the "Pond of the Ugly Woman," mentioned in the *Sanshū kidan kōhen* (above, § 8). There, within a day after one had thrown metal shavings into the pond, a heavy storm certainly arose and the rain came down in torrents.

We may compare this with a passage from the *Matsunoya hikki* ², where we read that the inhabitants of the Tsukui district ³ (Tsukui-agata), Sagami province, used to throw horse dung, old sandals, and other filth into a pond in neighboring Toyama when drought prevailed. After having done this, they rapidly fled for fear of the angry dragon, which certainly arose, causing a terrible hurricane and heavy rains. As we have stated above ⁴, the idea of causing rain by provoking the dragon's anger is quite Chinese.

It was certainly also a pond, inhabited by a dragon or a snake, which we find mentioned on page 653 of the *Sanshū kidan* (Chapter I). In summer, when the people wanted rain, they went there, cut a mackerel to pieces, and threw these into the mountain pond while praying for rain. If they did so, their prayer was always heard, and the rain came down at once. This seems to be an offering to the dragon, but it might be another way of stirring him up by mistreating one of his subjects—the fish—before his eyes.

## § 10. **A dragon engraved on an incense pot believed to cause rain.**
### **Pine trees cause clouds to rise and rain to fall.**

The dragon was so strongly associated with rain that even an incense pot decorated with a "cloud-dragon," *unryū* (**雲龍**), was supposed to be the reason why it always rained on the day of an Inari festival. This pot was preserved among the precious objects of a temple dedicated to the Rice Goddess, but was hidden when the suspicion rose that it caused the annoying rain on Inari's day ⁵. This appeared, however, not to be the case, for the rain poured down just as much after this measure as before.

<meta>Digitized by Microsoft®. This page continues the account of how objects associated with dragons—in this case, sacred pine wood—were believed to influence the weather. It follows a discussion on how a dragon-decorated incense pot was thought to cause rain during festivals.</meta>

as soon as the day arrived that was fixed for the children's dances, who were dressed in festive clothing in honor of Inari. On the days devoted to Sannō, Suwa, and Tada Hachiman, the weather was fine; however, Inari's festival was always spoiled by rain. At last, the reason was discovered. The boards of the stage on which the dances were performed were made from the wood of several sacred pine trees. These had belonged to a neighboring Shintō temple but were sold by the villagers during a time of financial distress. The man who bought these trees placed them in the compound of the Inari temple, and as the wood was very strong, it was used in building the stage for the sacred dances of this sanctuary. 

Now, it occurred to the people that every time this timber was used—as such stages are always built temporarily and dismantled after the festival—and the sun shone upon the boards, it began to rain. Because of this, a messenger was dispatched to the village where the wood had come from to inquire about the trees in question. The man returned with the news that the two woodcutters who had cut those trees had died within a few days in a state of madness, as if they were possessed by some evil spirit. This confirmed the people's opinion that these pine trees were the cause of the rain at Inari's festival; therefore, they removed them and laid them near the worship hall instead of using the wood for building the dancing stage. 

They said: "We have heard that in China, in ancient times, during the reign of Emperor Shi Hwan of the Ts'in dynasty (246–210 B.C.), a pine tree suddenly grew into a massive tree and sheltered the emperor from the rain. Why is it that these pine trees are causing rain nowadays? It is said that pine trees, being covered with scaly armor, turn into dragons when they grow old. This may be the reason why they always had the miraculous power of summoning the clouds and the rain." Thus the people spoke, and they all admired the wonderful influence of the pine trees. <note>1 *Sanshū kidan*, Ch. II, p. 712.</note>

### § 11. The eight Dragon-kings.

A *Shintō* (!) temple dedicated to the eight Dragon-kings is mentioned in the *Seki no akikaze* <note>2 關ノ秋風 (*Seki no akikaze*), written by SHIRAKAWA RAKUŌ, 白川樂翁, "The Merry Old Man of Shirakawa"—that is, MATSUDAIRA SADANOBU, 松平定信, who lived</note>. The author of this work

<meta>This page continues the discussion of weather-related miracles, moving from the influence of sacred pine wood to stories of priests personally praying for rain and legends of dragons sacrificing themselves in response to Buddhist rituals.</meta>

personally prayed for rain there, and his prayer was heard. Then he ordered the villagers to repair the shrine. Afterwards, when the floodgates of heaven remained open too long, he successfully prayed to the dragons again, this time to stop the rain.

## § 12. A Buddhist dragon’s suicide.

The *Nihon shūkyō fūzokushi* <note>**1** Japanese Religious Customs (*Nihon shūkyō fūzokushi*), written in 1902 by <term>Katō Kumaichirō</term>, p. 247.</note> provides an ancient tradition explaining the names of three Buddhist temples in Shimōsa province. In 730 A.D., when the priest <term>Shaku-myō</term>, by order of the Emperor, prayed for rain, he achieved great success. At the same time, a dragon appeared in the air, cut its own body into three parts, and died. The middle part fell in the Imba district, where the temple called *Ryūfukuji* (龍腹寺), or “Shrine of the Dragon’s Belly,” can be found. The tail landed in the Katori district (also in Shimōsa), leading to the construction of the shrine *Ryūbiji* (龍尾寺, “Temple of the Dragon’s Tail”). The head descended on the spot where the previously mentioned priest had been praying, where even today the name of the sanctuary, *Ryūkaku-ji* (龍角寺, or “Temple of the Dragon’s Horn” at Sakai village, Shimohabu district), reminds believers of the ancient dragon.

A similar legend can be found in the *Yūhō meisho ryaku* <note>**2** Chapter III, p. 54. Compare with page 170, note 4.</note>, where the *Shasekishū* <note>**3** A Collection of Sand and Pebbles (*Shasekishū*), written by the Buddhist priest <term>Mujū</term>, who died in 1312.</note> is quoted. A blue dragon, after hearing a priest explain Buddha’s Law, was so moved by emotion that its body divided into three parts. Where the head landed, Ryūtōji, “the Temple of the Dragon’s Head,” was built (at Nara). In another part of Nara, where the dragon’s tail fell, Ryūbiji was erected. Its trunk gave rise to the name Ryūfukuji, also in the old capital; this was the only one of the three shrines that still existed in <term>Mujū</term>’s time (that is, at the beginning of the fourteenth century).

## § 13. Conclusions.

The passages mentioned in this chapter have clearly shown us that from ancient times in Japan, there were three methods for causing or stopping rain. The oldest, probably originally—

<summary>This page details legends regarding dragons that sacrificed themselves or divided their bodies into three parts following Buddhist rain-making prayers or teachings, resulting in the naming of several Japanese temples. It concludes by identifying three historical methods used in Japan to control the weather.</summary>
<keywords>Shimōsa province, Shaku-myō, Ryūfukuji, Ryūbiji, Ryūkaku-ji, Mujū, Katō Kumaichirō, dragon legends, rain-making rituals, Buddhist temples, Nara</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of rainmaking traditions in Japan, moving from specific local legends of dragon sacrifices to a broader discussion of the Chinese and Buddhist methods used to summon rain.</meta>

Japanese (though originally Chinese) methods involved offering white, black, or red horses to the dragon-shaped river-gods (red horses were offered only for stopping rain). This was followed by the Chinese custom of the Emperor praying to the four quarters of Heaven, as well as the—similarly Chinese—idea of stirring up the dragons with loud noise (as court officials did in 877 at the pond in the Sacred Spring Park). The same concept is found in the custom, common in much later times, of throwing iron into a dragon's pond. Snakes, and therefore dragons (which are considered to belong to the same species), are believed to hate and fear iron intensely<note>¹ See above, pages 67 and following.</note>, and many a mighty serpent is said to have been killed or driven away by a single needle. Therefore, when iron is thrown into a pond inhabited by a dragon, this rain-god is certain to become angry and rise from his home into the sky, which is instantly covered with clouds. Then the dragon vents his anger in a terrible thunderstorm accompanied by heavy rain, and the goal of the person who threw the iron tool or metal shavings into the pond is achieved.

The third way of causing rain—the Buddhist method—started from an opposite point of view. Instead of forcing the dragons to rise by annoying them, Buddhist priests recited sūtras which so impressed the devout minds of the <term>Nāgas</term> that they would immediately assist mankind and liberate the people from the terrible suffering caused by a long drought. Sometimes a sūtra was read concerning the <term>Garuda-kings</term>—the deadly and much-feared enemies of the <term>Nāgas</term>—likely to make the dragons feel completely dependent on Buddha's mighty protection. As Buddhism flourished, this kind of rain-prayer soon became the most dominant form in Japan. In the eighteenth century, however, the Chinese methods of stirring up the dragons seem to have been revived. Today, when continuous drought prevails in the seventh and eighth months and peasants anxiously watch the sky, fearing their crops will be ruined, they often walk in processions, beating drums and making noise, just as the court officials did in 877 A.D. The old Chinese ideas are deeply rooted in the minds of the people.

It also notes the persistence of ancient Chinese noise-making traditions among the peasantry during times of drought.</summary>

### CHAPTER IV.

#### THE INDIAN NĀGA IN JAPAN.

As we have seen above<note>¹ Book II, Chapter I, sections 5 and 6, pages 139 and following.</note>, the Indian <term>Nāga</term> legends already served in the time of the *Nihongi* (that is, in the beginning of the eighth century) to embellish the old tales concerning the Japanese sea gods. The magnificent palace of Oho-watatsumi no Mikoto at the bottom of the sea and the "Jewel which grants all desires" of the Empress Jingō left no doubt about their Indian origin. It is no wonder, then, that as Buddhism flourished in Japan, the original Japanese sea and river gods had to give way to the Indian conquerors; therefore, most of the dragons mentioned in later works are <term>Nāgas</term>. In Chapter III, we saw that rain prayers, which were first offered exclusively to various Shintō gods—especially to the dragon-shaped river deities—were also addressed to the <term>Nāgas</term> from the ninth century onward. In times of drought, the Emperors increasingly looked upon Buddhist priests as the most powerful rescuers of the country, and large crowds of Shingon priests recited their sutras in the Palace as well as at the Dragon Pond of the Sacred Spring Park to cause the <term>Nāgas</term> to make it rain all over the country.

Regarding the legends referred to in this chapter, many of them have Chinese features even though they relate to <term>Nāgas</term>. This is quite clear because all Indian tales came to Japan by way of China. Moreover, many originally Japanese dragons, to which Chinese legends were applied, were later identified with <term>Nāgas</term>, resulting in a blending of ideas.

##### § 1. The Dragon-kings revere Buddha's Law.

The *Sandai jitsuroku*<note>² Chapter V, page 82: 貺動幽明。龍王移水府之深。星容布天圃之賚. Another text gives 琛 instead of 深; then it would mean: "The Dragon-kings transpose the precious stones of the water regions."</note> (901 A.D.) quotes a written petition from the Lord of Harima, Sugawara no Koreyoshi (812—880), to

<summary>This page discusses how Indian Nāga legends were integrated into Japanese mythology, eventually displacing or blending with indigenous Shintō sea and river gods. It highlights the role of Buddhist priests in conducting rainmaking rituals as these Indian traditions became dominant via China.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Dragon-kings and their reverence for the Buddha's Law, following the mention of the Great Buddha of Nara.</meta>

the Great Buddha of Nara (in 861), in which we read these words: “You give motion to the Darkness and the Light; the Dragon-kings retreat into the depths of the water regions, and the stars spread all over the sky (that is, by the influence of your Law).” In the same supplication <note>1. Page 85.</note> we find the well-known term “<term>Ryūjin hachibu</term>”, 龍神八部, “Dragons, Spirits (or Dragon-gods), and (other beings of) the eight departments,” a variant of <term>Tenryū hachibu</term>, 天龍八部, or <term>Ryūten hachibu</term>, 龍天八部 <note>2. See above, Introduction, § 1, pages 1 and following, note 5.</note>.

The <term>Shasekishū</term> <note>3. Chapter VI, part 1, page 17. See above, page 177, note 3.</note> (written before 1312 AD) refers to a sutra entitled <term>Shinchikwan-kyō</term> <note>4. 心地觀經.</note>, where we read: “If one wears only one Buddhist sacerdotal robe, he can cross the sea without being annoyed by poisonous dragons.” So great is the reverence even of these dangerous creatures for Buddha and his believers.

### § 2. Dragons appear at the dedication of Buddhist temples.

The <term>Fusō ryakki</term> <note>5. Chapter III, K. T. K. Vol. VI, page 497.</note> (written about 1150 AD) relates how in 596, when the Buddhist temple called <term>Hōkōji</term> <note>6. 法興寺.</note> was dedicated at Nara, a purple cloud descended from the sky and covered the pagoda as well as the Buddha hall; then the cloud became five-colored and assumed the shape of a dragon or phoenix, or of a man or an animal. After a while, it vanished in a western direction <note>7. It changed into five colors; sometimes appearing as a dragon or phoenix, sometimes like a man or animal. After a long while, it departed toward the west. 變爲五色。或爲龍鳳。或如人畜。良久向西方去.</note>.

A work of much later date, the <term>Yūhō meisho ryaku</term> <note>8. Chapter VI, page 47. See above, page 170, note 4.</note> (1697), contains a legend about a Buddhist temple named <term>Unryūzan</term>, “Cloud-dragon-shrine,” in Fuwa district, Mino province. When the abbot <term>Ryūshū</term>, who lived from 1307 to 1388, was erecting this sanctuary, on the day he started the work, a dragon appeared with a pearl in its mouth—a very good sign indeed. For this reason, he called the mountain <term>Ryūshūhō</term>, “Dragon-pearl-peak” (龍珠峯). When the temple was ready, a rain of flowers fell from heaven.

### § 3. Dragons living in ponds or lakes, mostly near Buddhist shrines.

In the history of Shitennō-ji, the "Monastery of the Four Deva-kings"—the Buddhist monastery built by Shōtoku Taishi at Namba (the present Ōsaka)—we read that in the compound of one of the monastery's buildings, called Keiden-in, there was a deep pond named Kōryōchi <note>1. It was called Kōryōchi. Its bottom was deep. A blue dragon always lived there.</note>, where a blue dragon was believed to live <note>2. *Fusō ryakki*, Ch. III, p. 495.</note>.

At a distance of 36 chō from the temple of *Hakusan Gongen*, "The Manifestation of Mount Hakusan" (the Buddhist name of the ancient Shintō god of this holy mountain, which lies on the frontiers of Mino, Hida, Echizen, and Kaga provinces), there was, according to the *Kojidan* <note>3. Ch. V, K. T. K. Vol. XV, p. 119.</note> (1210—1220 A.D.), a sacred pond called Mikuriya no ike, or "August Kitchen Pond." All the Dragon-kings were said to assemble there to prepare their food (<term>kuyō</term>, 供養, food for offerings). Human beings could not approach it, for as soon as they had the audacity to do so, a violent thunderstorm burst forth and killed the culprits <note>4. It was called the Mikuriya pond. It was a pond where all the Dragon-kings gathered to prepare food for offerings. Humans did not dare to approach it. If anyone did, violent thunder and lightning would harm them, it is said.</note>. Yet two holy men prayed to Hakusan Gongen to allow them to scoop a little water out of this pond. Another priest, who heard this, stayed for thirty-seven days in the temple, continually repeating the same prayer. Then he went to the bank of the pond and earnestly practiced the *kuyō-hō* or "food-offering method." The sky was clear, and there was no thunder or rain to drive him away. However, no sooner did he scoop a little water into a pitcher than his mind became confused and he felt as if he were dying. Yet he was able to return home after concentrating his thoughts. Sick people who drank this water or rubbed themselves with it were sure to be cured by the power of Buddha's Law.

The *Uji shūi monogatari* <note>5. Ch. XI, K. T. K. Vol. XVII, p. 225. See above, p. 171, note 6.</note> (1213—1218) contains a tale about a young Buddhist priest who lived in the Nara period (719—784) and played the following practical joke. On the bank of the Sarusawa pond (near the Kōfuku temple), he put up a placard announcing that on a special day and hour, a dragon would arise from the

pond. As the passers-by who read this all believed it, on the appointed day an immense crowd flocked together from Yamato, Kawachi, Izumi, and Settsu provinces to see the miracle. The priest himself, standing at the gate of the Kōfuku temple, was highly amused by the success of his joke and laughed to himself when seeing the crowd on the tiptoe of expectation. When evening fell and no dragon appeared, they all went home greatly disappointed.

The *Gempei seisuiki* <note>1. Ch. XXXIX, p. 742. See above, p. 165, note 2.</note> (about 1250) tells us how in 717 A.D. the Zen priest Shinyu was invited by an unknown goddess—who was said to have always protected the Emperor and the people—to come to the top of Mount Hakusan in order to worship her "real shape" there. When he went there and prayed near the pond on the mountain, while uttering incantations (*kaji*) and making three sacred <term>mudrās</term> (mystic finger gestures), an enormous nine-headed, serpent-shaped dragon arose from the middle of the pond. The priest, however, declared that this was not the deity's real shape and increased the power of his mantras (magical formulas) until he finally beheld the majestic form of the Eleven-faced Kwannon.

When connecting this legend with the passage of the *Kojidan* referred to above, we may easily guess that the sacred pond on Mount Hakusan had been the home of an original Japanese dragon since ancient times, which gave rise to various Buddhist dragon legends regarding this pond.

In the *Genkō Shakusho* <note>2. Ch. XIII, p. 853.</note> (before 1346), we read that the day before the priest Jitsuhan's <note>3. 實範.</note> arrival at Daigoji (in Kyoto), Genkaku <note>4. 嚴覺.</note>, the abbot of this monastery, saw in a dream a blue dragon arising from the pond in the garden, lifting up its head and spouting clear water from its mouth. As he understood the meaning of this dream, the abbot ordered his pupils the next morning to clean the monastery thoroughly to graciously receive the venerable student, who indeed arrived.

In a much later work, the *Sanshū kidan kōhen* <note>5. [Note missing in original footer]</note> (1779), we find the following details about an old woman who could cure all kinds of diseases. She was believed to be possessed by the god of the neighboring pond, whether it was a river-otter (*kawa-oso*, 水獺) or a dragon-snake (龍蛇). She was a strange, poor old woman,

who ate nothing but boiled flour and refused to accept money from her patients. Her fame was so great that hundreds of people came from far and near to obtain medicine from her. And strange medicine it was, for in reality it was nothing at all. After a patient had told her their complaint, she went inside, put a rush mat upon her head, and after having meditated like this for a while, she came out of the house and gave imaginary medicine to the patient, saying: “Here are doses for seven days. If only you believe in me and think that you are swallowing medicine, it will certainly have a good effect. If there is no result within seven days, you must come back.” If the person followed her advice, they actually recovered. It was no wonder that patients flocked together from all quarters. Since she was busy from morning until night, she distributed charms with “Namu Amida Butsu” or something of the kind written on them and marked with her stamp, instead of continuing the longer procedure which she had followed in the beginning. If anyone tried to deceive her, she immediately discovered it. She was such a wonderful being that some people proposed to buy her for seven hundred <term>ryō</term> (perhaps from the villagers?) and take her to the capital, but this was prevented by the authorities. Her strange diet gave rise to the suspicion that she was possessed by a <term>tanuki</term>, especially because she used to eat with her face hidden in the bowl. Others supposed her to be the mother of Hō-kun (鮑君, Lord Salted Fish [?]), or the wife of the “Great King with the straw sandals”¹, meaning one of the <term>Ni-ō</term>². But the local physician was of another opinion. He said to Hotta, the author of the *Sanshū kidan kōhen*: “This old woman is assisted by some water demon. I have often heard the villagers say that she ‘purifies herself’³, as she calls it, twice a day, going into the pond and repeatedly diving underwater so that even her head is not visible. After having spoken with several patients, she washes her head with well water; if her head is not wet, she cannot see her patients. She certainly is a creature connected with the pond, whether it be a river otter or a dragon-snake. Some thirty or forty years ago, when her husband was still alive, a Buddhist nun came one winter and stayed in their house; she washed clothes and served not only herself but others as well. From then on, she stayed there every month for

<note>3. *Kori wo toru*, to perform ritual purification.</note>

Local accounts suggest her powers were tied to a nearby pond, fueling theories that she was a shape-shifting creature like a river otter or dragon-snake.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the account of a mysterious woman whose supernatural abilities were attributed to a water spirit. It then transitions to a description of the annual Dragon-god festival at Hakone and begins a legend from the Taiheiki concerning the exiled Prince Takanaga.</meta>

three or four days, and then went home. At last the man, warned by a neighbor, watched her from the upper story of the neighbor's house and saw her coming out of his own house. After having walked some distance in human shape, she was transformed into a stream of white vapor (白氣), flew to the pond, and disappeared under the water. The man, greatly frightened by this sight, went to a neighboring Buddhist temple and requested the priest to recite prayers on his behalf. Moreover, he pasted holy Buddhist texts and charms on the walls of his house in order to ward off the evil. This was sufficient, for the nun never returned. Within a couple of years, however, the man died, and now, after more than thirty years, such strange things are happening in the same house once again. "The old woman is probably possessed by the Master (主, *nushi*) of the pond." So spoke the physician, no doubt jealous of the woman on account of her medical fame, but at the same time clearly expressing the superstitious ideas of the people. The term "dragon-snake" seems to indicate the Nāga, a serpent identified with a dragon; moreover, Buddhism plays a predominant part in this story.

Before the Restoration, a so-called "Dragon-god festival" (*Ryūjin-sai*, 龍神祭) was celebrated annually by the priest of the Gongen shrine at Hakone, the well-known mountain village in Sagami province. Three hundred thirty-three *gō* (合) of "red rice" (*sekihan*), in a new wooden rice bowl, were offered to the Dragon-god of Hakone Lake in the following way. The Buddhist priest (though Ieyasu's shrine now belongs to Shinto) went in a boat to the middle of the lake and there placed the bowl on the water, whereupon the boat moved on, with neither the priest nor the boatmen looking back. Then they heard a sound like a whirlpool at the spot where the offering had been made, and the bowl disappeared under the water ¹.

### § 4. Reborn as dragons.

In the *Taiheiki* ² (about 1382) we read the following legend. The second son of Emperor Godaigo, Prince Takanaga (also called Ichi no Miya), who had been banished to Hata in Tosa province, longed so much for his consort, who had remained in Kyoto, that he dispatched his faithful vassal, Hada no Takebumi,

<meta>This page continues the account of Prince Takanaga's exile. It describes how the samurai Takebumi, after failing to prevent the kidnapping of a lady in his care, transformed into a vengeful Dragon God to intercept the kidnapper's ship at the Uwa no Naruto whirlpool.</meta>

to the capital in order to take her to his place of exile. When the latter was on his way to Tosa with the lady, and they were waiting for a favorable wind at Amagasaki in Settsu province, there was a samurai named Matsuura Goro who fell in love with the beautiful woman, kidnapped her, and after taking her on board his ship, set sail at once. As soon as Takebumi realized this trick, he called the vessel back with a loud voice, but the only answer he received was outrageous laughter, and the vessel continued on its course. Then poor Takebumi, at his wit’s end, said: “Today I will become a Dragon God at the bottom of the sea and stop that ship.” With these words, he disemboweled himself and jumped into the sea. There is a well-known whirlpool called Uwa no Naruto, the “Sounding Door” (meaning the eddy) of Uwa, between Shikoku and Awaji, which was said to be the Eastern Gate of the Dragon Palace. It was there that Takebumi’s revenge revealed itself in a terrible way; for the vessel, caught by the eddy, was spun around for three days, and in vain all kinds of precious things, such as bows, swords, and clothes, were thrown into the sea as offerings to the Dragon God. Then the crew concluded that the dragon wanted the woman herself, and Matsuura was about to throw her into the furious waves when a Buddhist priest advised him not to provoke the Dragon God’s anger by making a human offering to him—which the dragon, being a pure being and a believer in Buddha, would certainly dislike. It is better, the priest said, to recite sutras and pray. So the whole crew prayed to Kwannon, and behold! Takebumi’s spirit appeared on the waves, still beckoning to the vessel as he had done before his death, preceded by several retainers on horseback. Although mysterious things often happened at that spot, this time it was certainly Takebumi’s angry soul which caused the disaster. Therefore, they placed the woman, together with one sailor, in a small boat, hoping to satisfy the ghost in this way and to get rid of her without killing her. As soon as they had done this, the ship was immediately driven out of the whirlpool and disappeared toward the west; it was never heard of again. As for the lady, she arrived safely at an island where she was kindly received by the inhabitants, and where she remained for the rest of her life, not daring to risk being kidnapped again.

--- Page 202 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon legends in Japan, moving from the story of Takebumi to another account of a human transforming into a dragon due to intense passion or "shūnen."</meta>

The disappointed lover was said to have drowned himself, and his passion (*shūnen*, 執念) was believed to have condensed into the form of a white dragon (in other words, his soul, because of its passionate state at the time of his death, was reincarnated as a dragon). This was an old tale in **Hotta**’s time (the eighteenth century), and the lake had become ten times narrower than before, so that the dragon was no longer thought to live in the water, but in a so-called “dragon-hole” (*ryū-kutsu*, 龍窟) underground, where “dragon-vapors” or “dragon-breath” (龍氣) used to rise as a sign of the demon’s presence (these are Chinese ideas). At the beginning of the Anei era (1772—1780), people who crossed a neighboring ferry on the river that flows into the lake saw a long, white monster swimming from the lake into the river mouth. When it was at the bottom of the stream, the water became completely white. Sometimes the dragon showed its snow-white back, but not its head or tail. Some people who had seen its head, which was rarely visible, said that it was square. After spending a day swimming along the coast, it disappeared. This dragon was said to have lived in that vicinity for a long time, and as it was called “the white man” (白男, *shiro-otoko*), **Hotta** assumes it to be the same person who once drowned himself and took this shape after being deceived by his sweetheart, “the white girl.” He was believed to be living underground afterward because the lake had become too narrow. Since he could not flood the land and destroy the fields, he occasionally simply made an excursion to the neighboring sea coast. At the same ferry, there was a creature called “shiga,” which stretched itself and blocked the boats when the snow began to melt; this was also some type of “breath” (氣), probably, says **Hotta**, the same “dragon-breath” that was examined by an incredibly daring man during the Keichō era (1596—1614) according to the work titled “*Chūgwaiden*” <note>中外傳 (Date and author unknown?).</note>.

According to a modern work, the *Nihon shūkyō fūzoku shi* mentioned above <note>Page 117, note 1; page 204.</note>, there is a pond called “Sakura ga ike” (Cherry-tree Pond) in Kasahara village, Tōtōmi province. It is the home of a huge dragon, to whom those who have a special wish pray on the middle day of *higan* (彼岸, “the other shore,” a period of seven days in either equinox; the middle day is the

equinoctial day), while at the same time making an offering to him consisting of a bucket of hard-boiled rice (<term>kowameshi</term>, 強 飯, which is <term>sekihan</term>, 赤 飯, "red rice"—rice boiled with red beans), which they cause to float on the water. If they later find the bucket empty, it is a sign that the dragon has eaten the rice, accepting the offering and hearing the prayer; however, if the rice is still in the bucket, the prayer will not be granted. This dragon is the reincarnation of the Buddhist priest <term>Genkō</term> (源 皇), a <term>Tendai</term> priest from Mount Hiei (<term>Hieizan</term>) and the teacher of <term>Hōnen Shōnin</term> (法 然), who lived from 1132 to 1212. <term>Genkō</term> wished to become a dragon because his life was too short to obtain sufficient knowledge of the Buddha's doctrine. One day, he heard from one of his disciples that the aforementioned pond was an excellent place for a dragon to live. He then sat down in religious meditation (<term>samādhi</term>), placed a single drop of water in his hand—by which he created clouds and rain—and flew through the air to the pond. There he died while in meditation, and when his disciple arrived and called to him, an enormous dragon appeared above the water and wept. At the pupil's request, he assumed his former human shape and spoke with him for a long time.

We should also mention an old legend found in the *Gukwanshō* <note>1. 愚 管 抄 (*Gukwanshō*), likely written by Bishop <term>Ji-en</term> (慈 圓), who died in 1225; *Kokushi Taikei* (K.T.K.) Vol. XIV, Ch. VII, p. 597.</note>, which relates that the Imperial Princess <term>Inoue</term> (<term>Inoue no Naishinnō</term>), daughter of Emperor Shōmu and Consort of Emperor Kōnin, had become a dragon even before her death. She was accused of practicing <term>wu-ku</term> (巫 蠱), a Chinese magical art performed using small reptiles and insects <note>2. See De Groot, *Religious System of China*, Vol. V, Ch. II, pp. 826 and following.</note>, in order to have her son appointed Crown Prince. For this reason, she was imprisoned in a pit in 772 by order of the Prime Minister <term>Fujiwara no Momokawa</term>, and three years later both she and her son died. According to popular tradition, however, she had transformed into a dragon even before her death.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sea dragons and their demands for Buddhist artifacts, transitioning from the previous page's mention of Princess Inoue to the story of the monk Dōshō and an account from the *Konjaku monogatari*.</meta>

When he returned to Japan, he obtained from Hüen Tsang—the famous pilgrim who went to India in 629 and returned in 645—a small kettle for preparing medicines, in addition to a relic of Buddha and sūtras. Hüen Tsang had brought this kettle with him from India and said that it was of the greatest value because all diseases could be cured by medicines cooked in it. This proved to be true, for one of Dōshō's companions, who fell ill before they left China, was cured at once thanks to this marvelous utensil. On their way to Japan, in the middle of the ocean, the ship suddenly stopped and did not move for seven days, while wind and waves raged around it in a terrible way. Then a diviner said: "There is something on board which the Sea-god wants. I think it is the kettle." At first the abbot refused to give up his treasure and said there was no reason why the Dragon-king should ask for it. But when the others, fearing for their lives, urgently begged him to follow the diviner's advice, the priest gave in and threw the kettle into the sea. Immediately the storm and the waves subsided, the ship was able to continue its course, and they soon arrived in Japan. Apparently the Dragon-king had actually wanted the offering of the sacred kettle.

The *Konjaku monogatari*¹ describes how a Prime Minister, who was transporting a precious Buddha image across the sea for his king, was overtaken by a terrible storm. It was in vain that he threw all kinds of precious things into the sea; the Dragon-king apparently wanted something else. At last the minister understood what would appease him, and, praying for his life, offered the pearl from between the eyebrows of the Buddha image. The Dragon-king stretched out his hand and took the pearl, whereupon the storm calmed down. Although this danger was over, the minister—who was convinced that he would be decapitated when he confessed the loss of the pearl to his sovereign—wept bitterly and begged the Dragon to return the treasure. Then the Sea-god appeared to him in a dream and promised to restore the pearl to him if he would stop the nine tortures which were inflicted upon the dragons. Overjoyed, the man awoke and, addressing the sea, answered that he was willing to free the dragons from their tortures by copying and offering holy Buddhist texts. And when he had done so, the Dragon-king kept his promise and returned the pearl; however, it had lost its luster. The Sea-god again appeared to the minister in a dream

### § 6. The “jewel which grants all desires” (<term>cintāmaṇi</term>).

There lived in Northern India a Buddhist abbot named “Buddha’s Vow” <note>佛誓, Bussei.</note> who, for the sake of humanity, sought the “Precious pearl which grants all desires” <note>如意寶珠, *nyo-i hōju, cintāmaṇi*, compare above, p. 10.</note>. He went on board a ship and, when in the middle of the sea, called up the Dragon-king by Buddha’s power. After binding him by means of mystic formulas (<term>tantras</term>), he demanded the pearl from him. The dragon, unable to escape, took the pearl from his head and prepared to hand it over to the priest. The priest stretched out his left hand while making the “sword-sign”—a <term>mudrā</term> (mystic finger-gesture)—with his right hand. 

The Dragon-king, however, said: “In former times, when the daughter of Dragon-king Sāgara gave a precious pearl to Çākyamuni, the latter received it with folded hands; why should a pupil of the Buddha accept it with only one hand?” Then the priest folded his hands, releasing the <term>mudrā</term>, and was about to take the pearl when the Dragon-king, no longer suppressed by the mystic sign, freed himself from his bonds and ascended to the sky. He left the abbot behind empty-handed and destroyed his boat. The only man who was saved was the priest himself. Later, this same abbot met the patriarch Bodhidharma <note>波羅門, Baramon, the “Wall-gazing Brahman”.</note>, who came across the sea from Southern India (in 526), and together they went to Japan <note>*Fusō ryakki*, 拔萃 (Shōmu Tennō), K. T. K. Vol. VI, p. 564.</note>.

### § 7. The eight Dragon-kings.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon lore, transitioning from the power of Buddhist scriptures over dragons to specific historical and legendary accounts of Dragon-kings, including their human manifestations and their roles as protective deities in Japanese history.</meta>

Dragon-kings <note>Compare above, Introduction, § 1, p. 4.</note> crossing a large sea in ships; on the eighth vessel no dragon was to be seen. When the man asked the reason for this Dragon-king’s absence, he received the answer that the absent dragon was currently the head abbot of Hieizan. Evidently, Jie was a metamorphosis of Utpala <note>優鉢羅龍王, Uhachira Ryū-ō.</note>, the last of the eight Dragon-kings. We have seen above <note>Book II, Chapter III, § 6, p. 170.</note> that a dragon was his “real shape.”

In the *Taiheiki* <note>Chapter II, p. 9a.</note>, an exile on Sado Island prays to different gods to make a ship approach his lonely place. Among these deities are: “Gongen (Manifestations), Kongō dōji (*Vajra kumāra*), Tenryū (Heavenly Dragons), Yasha (Yakshas), and the eight Great Dragon-kings” <note>權現金剛童子天龍夜叉八大龍王.</note>. Apparently, the Nāgas mentioned last were considered to be different from the Heavenly Dragons, which formed one of the four classes of Nāgas mentioned above <note>Introduction, § 3, p. 21.</note>. The eight Dragon-kings probably belonged to the second class of Nāgas, the “Divine Dragons” (神龍).

->§ 8. The Dragon-gods of the inner and outer seas.<-

The *Gempei seisuiki* <note>Chapter VII, p. 183.</note> says that Fujiwara no Yasuyori, banished to the island called Kikai-ga-shima, invoked the compassion of “the dragon-gods of the inner and outer seas, and (the other beings of) the eight departments” <note>內海外海龍神八部. Compare above, Introduction, § 1, pages 1 and following, note 5.</note>. The same expression, meaning “dragon-gods of the inner and outer seas,” is found in the *Taiheiki* <note>Chapter X, p. 7b.</note>, where we read how, in the year 1333, Nitta Yoshisada, Godaigo’s faithful general, invoked them. He was marching toward Kamakura in order to punish the <term>Shikken</term> (Regent) Hōjō Takatoki, and when he arrived at Inamurazaki, a cape between Enoshima and Kamakura, he prayed to the “Dragon-gods of the inner and outer seas” to make the sea retreat, so that he might be able to pass with his troops along the shore and thus easily reach Kamakura. They apparently heard his prayer, for that night the tide suddenly became so low that Takatoki’s ships could not approach the coast, and the arrows of his soldiers could not reach Nitta’s troops, which marched along the dry shore straight

### § 9. Dragon-palaces.

According to the *Genkō Shakusho* <note>Chapter I, K. T. K. Vol. XIV, p. 642.</note>, the Chinese monk Kien Chen <note>鑑 眞, Kanshin.</note>, when crossing the sea on his way to Japan, was invited by a dragon-god to come to his palace and preach for him <note>The text says only: "he went to the Dragon-palace," but the commentator explains the reason why he did this.</note>. After having complied with the request, the priest continued his journey and at last (in 762) arrived in Kyūshū (then called Dazaifu).

The famous legend concerning *Tawara Tōda*, which is found in the *Honchō kwaidan koji* <note>本朝怪談故事, written in 1711 by the Buddhist priest Kōyo, 厚 譽; Chapter I, number 16, p. 29.</note>, is a blending of Chinese and Indian ideas. It runs as follows. In the Hidesato temple, a Shintō shrine near the Seta bridge in Ōmi province, Tawara Tōda <note>俵 藤 太</note>, "Rice bag Tōda," is worshipped together with *Suifushin* <note>水 府 神</note>, the "God of the Water Department." If one takes a centipede (*mukade*) to this shrine, the animal immediately dies for the following reason. In olden times, when Fujiwara no Hidesato (who lived in the first half of the tenth century) crossed the bridge, a big serpent lay across it. The hero, however, was not at all afraid and calmly stepped over the monster, which at once disappeared into the water and returned in the shape of a beautiful woman. She said she had lived under this bridge for two thousand years, but she had never seen such a brave man as he. For this reason, she requested him to destroy her enemy, a huge centipede <note>The centipede is, according to Chinese belief, the snake's deadly enemy, whose ability in killing snakes is so great that it is considered an excellent charm against them, and used to cure diseases caused by *ku*-sorcery. Compare DE GROOT, *Religious System of China*, Vol. V, pp. 863 and following.</note>, which had killed her sons and grandsons. Hidesato promised her he would do so and, armed with a bow and arrows, awaited the centipede on the bridge. There came from the top of Mikami yama two enormous lights, as big as the light of two hundred torches. These were the centipede's eyes, and Hidesato shot three arrows in that direction, whereupon the lights were extinguished.

and the monster died. The dragon woman, filled with joy and gratitude, took the hero with her to the splendid Dragon-palace, where she entertained him with delicious dishes and rewarded him with a piece of silk, a sword, a suit of armor, a temple bell, and a bag (*tawara*) of rice. She said that there would always be silk remaining as long as he lived, no matter how much he might cut from it; and the bag of rice would never be empty <note>In a later version of the legend, he received a box of white wood, three or four *sun* square, called *debebako* (出米箱, "Rice-supplying box"). This was placed above the ceiling, and if someone placed a rice box beneath and pointed at the box above, saying: "Rice for tomorrow for so many people," the next morning that exact amount of rice was certainly in the box below. This miraculous box remained in the family for many generations and kept the same ability to provide rice until it was taken down to be cleaned and was accidentally dropped on the stones in the garden. It then broke, and a dead little white snake fell out of it. After that, no more rice was provided, but the box and the snake are still preserved by the family.</note>. As for the temple bell, this was the most precious treasure of the Dragon-palace.

After his return to the world of men, Hidesato presented the bell to Miidera, the famous Buddhist monastery near Ōtsu in Ōmi province. One day, a priest of Hieizan stole it, but because it would not produce any sound except for the words: "I wish to go back to Miidera," he angrily threw it into the valley. There it was found and taken back to Miidera by the monks of that monastery. Then a small snake appeared and, stroking the cracks of the bell with its tail, made them vanish at once, so that the precious object was as undamaged as before.

The *Taiheiki* <note>Ch. XV, p. 5.</note>, which also tells the legend of Tawara Tōda, says that the bell was stolen during the war between Miidera and Hieizan when the former monastery was on fire, and that it fell to pieces in the valley but was restored by the snake in a single night. The snake was probably the dragon woman herself or a messenger from the Dragon-palace. In the version found in the *Taiheiki*, the serpent that Hidesato met on the bridge did not change into a woman, but into a strange small man; this was the Dragon-king himself. Because of the miraculous rice bag, the hero was from then on called Tawara Tōda, "Rice-bag Tōda" <note>In reality, the name Tawara was written 田原, not 俵. Tawara (田原) is the name of a noble family at Aki (Bungo province) and of a place in Mikawa.</note>.

The *Yūhō meisho ryaku* (1697) <note>Ch. X, p. 39; see above, p. 170, note 4. This passage is quoted in the *Nihon shūkyō fūzoku shi* (1902), p. 247.</note> mentions a Buddhist priest named Nanzō, who lived in the Enkyū era (1069—1073) and who for three years prayed in the temple of Kumano Gongen

<meta>This page transitions from the legend of Fujiwara no Hidesato and the Dragon Palace to various other Japanese legends involving priests transforming into dragons or encountering them, citing sources like the Genkō Shakusho.</meta>

for a long life, so that he might be able to study Buddha’s doctrine thoroughly. At last, he learned through a divine revelation in a dream that if he went to a large, deep lake on Mount Kotowake, on the borders of Hitachi and Mutsu provinces, he would become a dragon and have a very long life. Greatly rejoiced by the success of his prayers, he followed the god’s advice and took up residence in a hole near the lake, where he spent his days reading sutras and leading a strictly ascetic life. However, a female dragon, who visited him daily in the form of a beautiful woman to hear him reciting the sutras, fell in love with him and invited him to go with her to the dragon palace at the bottom of the lake. He followed her, carrying eight sutra rolls, and immediately lived with the woman in the luxurious mansion, where he transformed into an eight-headed dragon (due to the eight sutra rolls). His voice is often heard reciting the sutras in the lake. About three ri from this spot, there is another lake on Nuka ga take which was formerly inhabited by a nine-headed male dragon. This was the husband of the dragon woman mentioned above; when his place was taken by his eight-headed rival (the transformed priest), he went to the other lake and fought the intruder, but was defeated and killed. For this reason, a dragon no longer lives in the lake of Nuka ga take.

Finally, we may refer to a name formerly given to the starfish because of its resemblance to common spools for winding thread, namely *Ryūgū no itomaki*, “spool of the Dragon Palace”¹.

### § 10. Dragons connected with Buddhist priests.

The *Genkō Shakusho* says that a blue dragon appeared to the Tendai priest Eisai (榮西) when he climbed the Chinese Mount T’ai (台) in 1168, the holy ground of the Tendai sect².

In the same work, we read how the Dragon King Kwō-taku (廣澤) announced in a dream to the Chinese teacher of Fang-Ngan (方菴) and Enji (圓爾, also known as the Japanese priest Ben-en, 辨圓) that these two pupils were now ready to become priests. Because of this dream, the master sent the latter back to Japan so that he might preach the Law there³.

A third legend found in this work speaks of a daughter of Emperor Sujaku (930–946), who went mad and, dressed in
