...scanty garments, visited the cottage of a Buddhist hermit to plead with him to perform incantations on her behalf (i.e., to exorcise the evil spirit which was possessing her). The hermit agreed and the Princess returned home. In the middle of the night she (i.e., the evil spirit within her) suddenly exclaimed: "Help, help! A dragon is about to cut my throat with a sword, and a boy is tying me with a rope!" The ladies-in-waiting were very frightened, but the next morning the patient was cured. A dragon and an angel, invoked by the priest's incantations, had driven out the evil demon ¹.

### § 11. Eight dragons ridden through the sky by a Buddhist deity.

The *Taiheiki* ² describes the vision of a man who passed the night praying before the Outer Shrine (Gegu) at Ise. He saw a gigantic god with twelve faces and forty-two arms, brandishing swords and lances and riding eight dragons through the air amidst rain and wind, at the head of many others who drove in carriages above the clouds. They came from all sides, two or three thousand in all, in carriages or on horseback, while a brilliant palace, made of precious stones and silver, glittered in the sky.

### § 12. Curses wrought by dragons.

The *Shinchomonshū* ³ mentions curses of dragons in the following passages. "An old tradition said that the guardian god of the Ryūmon temple ⁴, a Buddhist sanctuary especially devoted to the religious services for the deceased relatives of Mr. Mogami Gengorō in Dewa province, was a dragon. One day the stone wall of this shrine had fallen into ruin, and a large number of men were working there together and had piled up stones, when a snake, about six or seven inches long, appeared from under the stones, was pursued and killed. Those who had killed her immediately became dizzy and died on the spot; the others, who had only pursued her, were ill for about fifty or sixty days. The body of this snake, tradition says, is now in the Keiyō temple opposite Asakusa in Yedo."

<meta>This page continues the exploration of dragon-related legends in Japan, specifically focusing on the deadly curses inflicted by dragons and the transition into a discussion about physical relics of dragons kept in Buddhist temples.</meta>

No less severe was the curse of another snake-shaped dragon. The house of the head of a village called “*Ryō no ike*” or “Dragon’s Pond,” in Uma district, Iyo province, was said to be built on a pool inhabited by a dragon in ancient times. A pond in the garden, three or four <term>shaku</term> square, which was the remainder of this pool, never went dry, not even in times of drought. On the 15th day of the 7th month (*Ullambana*, the Bon festival for the dead) in 1638, the villagers were dancing (the “*bon-odori*,” or “Bon dance”) in this garden and making such a noise that it was some time before they heard the master of the house crying for help. When they ran into the room, they found him standing in the dark, holding an animal by the throat that had swallowed one of the arms of his child, who was about eight years old. They cut the beast to pieces, but it grew larger and larger and at last filled the whole room. It appeared to be an enormous serpent, yet it had evidently entered the house through a very small opening, only large enough for an earthworm. Upon the sand of the pond a trace was visible—only a thin line—which showed that the dragon had crawled out of the pond in the shape of an earthworm. The curse of the monster soon followed in a terrible way, for the entire family—more than seventy people—died one after another, except for one blind minstrel who escaped this fate and later told the story ¹.

A man whose ship struck a huge snake, thirteen <term>ken</term> long, killed the monster with his sword. In order to escape its curse, he cut its body into three pieces, buried them together with the head, and had masses said for the animal’s soul. But this was all in vain, for thirteen years later—on the same day of the same month, and even at the same hour—he exclaimed, “I am drinking water,” choked, and died. The people were convinced that his death was caused by the snake. This water-serpent was, of course, a dragon ².

### § 13. Relics of dragons preserved in Buddhist temples.

At Noda, in Mikawa province, there is a Buddhist shrine called *Senryū-in*, or “Spring Dragon Temple” (泉 龍 院), where three dragon scales are preserved. Before the temple was built, its founder, *Mōrin Shōnin*, preached there every night, and each...

The priest, who pitied the creature, filled up the pond and built a temple over it. Three scales, left by the dragon, are preserved in the sanctuary¹.

One of the treasures of another Buddhist shrine, called *Ryūgenji*, or “Dragon-spring-temple” (龍源寺), in Hagi village, Mikawa province, is the tooth of a “hidden dragon” (潜龍, *senryū*), subdued by the priest Shūtei².

### § 14. The “Dragon-flower-meeting”.

In Miura Kensŭke’s *Bukkyō iroha jiten*³, under the entry for *Ryūge-e*, 龍華會, or “Dragon-flower-meeting,” we read that, when Maitreya will “forsake the world and find the truth of the Buddha,” he will assemble a large crowd and explain his doctrine. All the trees on earth will then take the shapes of golden dragons and will open their flowers. This is the meaning of the name of the religious meeting mentioned above.

## CHAPTER V.

### CHINESE AND INDIAN DRAGONS IDENTIFIED OR CONNECTED WITH ANCIENT JAPANESE DEITIES.

Because Chinese and Indian ideas about dragons so thoroughly influenced Japanese thought, as we have seen in the preceding chapters, it is not surprising that many ancient Shintō gods were identified with or connected to them. Sea gods or serpent-shaped mountain deities were especially prone to being viewed in this light. In the thirteenth century and later, people did not hesitate to interpret old legends of the gods in their own way, making frequent use of the terms "Dragon-god" and "Dragon-king." The following passages are examples of this trend.

#### § 1. Sāgara, the Dragon-king, the Yamato no orochi, Antoku Tennō and the Kusanagi sword.

The *Gukwanshō* ¹ (written before 1225) tells us that *Itsǔkushima no Myōjin* (嚴島ノ明神, the goddess of the island of Itsǔkushima in the Inland Sea) was, according to tradition, a Dragon-king's daughter reborn as Emperor Antoku. He was the unfortunate Emperor who drowned at the age of seven during the Battle of Dan-no-ura in 1185. His grandmother, Nii-no-ama (the widow of Kiyomori), jumped overboard with the young Emperor when she saw that the battle was lost. Thus, the Dragon-king's daughter returned to her father.

Details of this legend are found in the *Gempei seisuiki* ² (around 1250), which relates that this goddess was a grandchild of Amaterasu, the Sun Goddess, and the daughter of the Dragon-king Sāgara ³. The same work provides, in another passage ⁴, the

<note>3 婆竭羅, Shakatsura, also known as Sāgara, one of the eight Great Dragon-kings. See above, Introduction, § 1, p. 4; Book II, Chapter IV, § 6, p. 189. <meta>This page continues the discussion from the *Gempei seisuiki* regarding the legend of the Dragon-king Sāgara's daughter and the loss of the Kusanagi sword.</meta>

reason why the dragon was reborn as Emperor Antoku. The retired Emperor Go-Shirakawa, as we read there, sought in vain for the Kusanagi sword¹, one of the three treasures of the Imperial family, which Susanowo no Mikoto had found in the tail of the eight-headed serpent, Yamato no orochi. After praying for seven days in the Kamo temple, he received a divine revelation in a dream, telling him that the sword could be found at the bottom of the sea at Dan-no-ura, and that two female divers of that place—a mother and her daughter named Oimatsu and Wakamatsu—should be ordered to search for it. In consequence of this dream, Yoshitsune was dispatched to Dan-no-ura, and the two women were told to dive for the sword. They obeyed and remained underwater for a whole day (!). 

Then they returned to the surface, and the mother said that it was a very strange place down there, which she could not enter without Buddha's powerful assistance; therefore, she requested that the *Nyohō-kyō*², a *sūtra*, be copied and wound around her body. Immediately, a large number of venerable priests assembled and copied the *sūtra*; the woman wound it around her body and dove again. This time, she remained below for no less than a day and a night before she came up, still without the sword. Yoshitsune asked her what she had seen, but she answered that she could only tell the Emperor himself. 

So he took her to Kyoto, where she reported the following to the Emperor: She had entered the gate of a magnificent building, apparently the Dragon-king's palace. When she said that she came as a messenger from the Emperor of Japan to ask for the precious sword, two women led her into the garden to an old pine tree, where she could look into a room from under a half-raised blind (*sudare*). There she saw a large serpent, twenty *shaku* long, with a sword in its mouth and a child of seven or eight years old within its coils. The monster's eyes were large and glittered like the sun and the moon, and its red tongue moved up and down incessantly. 

The serpent said to the woman: "Tell the Emperor that this sword does not belong to Japan, but to the Dragon-palace. My second son³, who was driven out of my palace because of an evil deed, changed into the eight-headed serpent of the headwaters of the River Hi in Izumo (the Yamato no orochi); he was killed by Susanowo, who took the sword from the snake's tail and gave it to Amaterasu. During the reign of Emperor Keikō (71—130 A.D.), when Prince Yamato-dake

<meta>This page continues the legend of the Dragon-king's palace and the search for the Kusanagi sword, specifically detailing a supernatural encounter where a serpent claims to be the protector and true owner of the sword.</meta>

subjugated the barbarians, Amaterasu handed over the sword to Utsuki no miya¹, who gave it to the Prince. Then my second son took the form of a large snake, ten *shaku* long, and lay down in Yamato-dake’s path at the foot of Mount Ibuki (in Ōmi province) in order to frighten the Prince and take back the sword. The Prince, however, was not afraid of the snake and stepped over it, thus thwarting my son’s plan². Finally, the latter was reincarnated as the Emperor Antoku and jumped into the sea with the sword, which he returned to me. This child here is my son in his human form, and the sword which I am holding in my mouth is the one you ask for. But I cannot give it to the Emperor”. On receiving this message, Go-Shirakawa was very distressed and thought the precious object was lost. This was, however, not the case, for the real sword was preserved in the Great Shrine (*Daijingū*) at Ise, and Antoku’s sword was only a counterfeit. How strange that the Dragon-god did not know this!

Another legend connects the Kusanagi sword with a Dragon-king in a different way. In 674 AD, a Korean monk stole the sword from the Shinto temple at Atsuta in Owari province and hid it under his cloak. But a dark cloud descended before the shrine, took the treasure, and placed it back into the sanctuary. Then the priest, after praying there for a hundred days, stole the sword again and fled to Ōmi province. Once more the black cloud appeared, deprived the thief of his prize, and flew away with it in an eastern direction (toward Atsuta). A third time the theft seemed to be successful, for the priest had succeeded in secretly carrying the sword on board a ship bound for Korea, when a severe storm arose and stopped the vessel in its course. In despair, the Korean man threw the sword into the sea, and the Dragon-king took it and returned it to Atsuta³.

### § 2. The Thunder-god caught by Sukaru and identified with a Dragon-king.

In the *Gempei seisuiki*⁴, we find the following remarkable story.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of legendary history involving the Dragon-king and divine beings, moving from the stolen Kusanagi sword to the capture of the Thunder-god during the reign of Emperor Yūryaku.</meta>

> "During the time of Emperor Yūryaku (the twenty-second Emperor, 457—479), there was an important vassal of His Majesty named Oshibe Sukaru. One day, when this man entered the palace of Hatsuse Asakura and the apartments of the Emperor, who was staying there, the Emperor was being intimate with the Empress. Because a thunderstorm was raging at that moment, the monarch, ashamed at being surprised, ordered Sukaru to invite the roaring thunder to the palace in order to get rid of him. The vassal, having received the Imperial command, left the palace and rode on horseback from the road of Abe no Yamada to Toyora-dera, looking up to the sky and crying: 'You, Thunder-god who are roaring in the sky, His Majesty commands you to fall down.' The thunder, however, continued to move away, making the air resound with its echoes. Then Sukaru again spurred his horse and exclaimed: 'Although you are a Thunder-god, you are roaring in the air of Japan. How could you disobey the Emperor's order?' Then, with a loud noise, the *Dragon-king* returned and dropped to the earth between Toyora-dera and Iioka. Sukaru at once called Shinto priests, had them place the Dragon-god in a sedan chair, and returned to the palace. When he reported the matter to the Emperor, the Thunder raised his scales, stared with dilating eyes, and watched the Palace, while his radiance illuminated the whole building. This spectacle frightened His Majesty, and, after making all kinds of offerings to the Thunder-god, he quickly sent him back to the spot where he had fallen. This spot is now called 'The Thunder's Hill' (Ikazuchi no oka)."

This is a very old legend, found in the *Nihongi* and the *Ryō-i-ki*. The version from the *Nihongi*¹ is as follows: —

> "In the seventh year of Emperor Yūryaku's reign (463), on the third day of the seventh month, His Majesty said to Oshibe no Sukaru, Minister of State (Muraji, 連): 'I wish to see the shape of the god of Mimoro hill (Mimoro no oka, also called Mount Mimoro). As you excel others in strength, you shall go and, after having caught him yourself, you must bring him here.' Sukaru answered: 'I will try to do so,' and ascending Mimoro hill he caught a *big serpent* (大蛇), which he showed to the Emperor. As the Emperor had not practiced religious abstinence (in honor of the god),

The Emperor was frightened, covered his eyes, and did not look upon the god, but hid himself in the interior of the Palace and ordered the snake to be released on the hill. For this reason, the Emperor changed the deity's name to 'Ikazuchi' ('Thunderbolt')."

As to the *Ryō-i-ki*<note>1 **靈 異 記** (Record of Miraculous Events), written by the Buddhist priest KEIKAI about 750 A.D. Chapter I, Gunsho ruijū, number 447, Volume XVI, page 23.</note>, this gives the same details as the *Gempei seisuiki*, which apparently borrowed the legend from it. Instead of "Dragon-king" or "Dragon-god," however, the ancient work simply calls the deity "Thunder-god" (雷 神), which shows that the identification of this divinity with a Dragon-king dates from later times. The author of the *Gempei seisuiki*, translating the old text into the Japanese of his own era, followed the ideas of his age. Although he retained the word "Thunder-god" once, he changed it the other two times to "Dragon-king" and "Dragon-god," and he added the words: "raised his scales and dilated his eyes." The fact that the *Nihongi* spoke of a serpent-shaped mountain god made the identification with a dragon quite logical. The author of the *Gempei seisuiki* omitted the last part of the legend, which in the *Ryō-i-ki* runs as follows: "Afterwards, when Sukaru had died, the Emperor by decree ordered the funeral to be delayed for seven days and seven nights. He praised Sukaru's loyalty and had his tomb made on the same spot where the Thunder had fallen down. Over the grave, he erected a stone monument with the following inscription: 'This is the tomb of Sukaru, the Thunder-catcher.' The Thunder, angry at this insult, came down with a loud roar and trampled upon the stone monument, but while he was smashing it, he was seized (by Sukaru's ghost). When the Emperor heard this, he released the Thunder, who was not dead, but, being quite perplexed, remained there for seven days and seven nights. The Emperor ordered another stone monument to be erected with the following inscription: 'This is the tomb of Sukaru, who in his lifetime and after death caught the Thunder.' This is the reason why at the time of the old capital (that is, Empress Suiko's capital, Owarida no miya, 小 治 田 宮; Empress Suiko reigned 593—628) this spot was called 'Thunder-hill'."

### § 3. Watatsumi no kami, the Sea-god, identified with a Dragon-king.

A similar alteration of an old text by the author of the *Gempei seisuiki* is to be found in the legend about Prince Yamato-dake,

...who, when his ship was tossed about by wind and waves on its way from Musashi to Kazusa province, was saved by his talented concubine Otōto Tachibana-hime. She jumped into the sea to sacrifice herself to the Dragon-god on the Prince's behalf, thereby calming the turbulent waves¹. This legend is borrowed from the *Nihongi*², but there we read only about Watatsumi no kami³, the "Sea God" (海神).

### § 4. The dragon-hole in the Gion shrine.

A dragon's hole in a Shinto temple is mentioned in the *Zoku Kojidan*⁴. This hole was said to be in the *hōden* ("treasure hall," where the *shintai* or "divine bodies" of the gods are preserved) of the Gion shrine in Kyoto. In 1221, when the temple was destroyed by fire, Nashimoto, the Buddhist head abbot (*zasu*) of Mount Hiei, tried to measure the depth of the hole. However, even at a depth of fifty *jō* (five hundred *shaku*), the bottom had not yet been reached.

### § 5. The dragon-snake offered by the Sea-god to the Sada shrine.

The *Shokoku rijindan*⁵ says the following: "In the Shinto temple of Sada, in Akika district, Izumo province, worship is performed in several ways. Between the eleventh and the fifteenth day of the tenth month, a small snake about one *shaku* long comes from the open sea, floating on the waves and approaching the shore. It is a beautiful, gold-colored animal called a *dragon-snake* (龍蛇, *ryūja*). After purifying himself, the priest of the shrine goes to the beach and waits for the snake, which he carries back to the temple coiled upon some seaweed. It is a gift from the Sea God to the shrine."

### § 6. A dragon-snake as a tree-sprite on Kōya san.

Another tale in the same work⁶ refers to a serpent-shaped tree spirit—the spirit of a willow called *ja-yanagi* (蛇柳), or "snake-willow"—on Mount Kōya. This was a large serpent or dragon,

which from remote ages lived on this sacred mountain, until it was forced by Kōbō Daishi to retreat to a spot about half a mile away. He made the demon promise to do so by causing poisonous snakes to appear on the demon's body, so that it suffered immensely and was immediately willing to leave. From then on, Kōbō Daishi forbade bringing flutes onto the mountain, fearing that the sound of a flute, because it resembled a dragon's cry, might attract the serpent and cause it to return to its former home. This story was told by one of the monks to Hideyoshi when the latter, while staying as a pilgrim on the mountain, ordered a famous Nō actor he had brought with him to give a performance. The monk warned him not to arouse the dragon by playing the flute, but Hideyoshi laughed at him. However, no sooner had the tones of the flute echoed across the mountain than dark clouds arose in the clear sky and covered the earth. A severe thunderstorm shook the mountains and valleys, trees were uprooted, and the rain poured down in torrents. Hideyoshi, frightened by these terrible signs of the dragon's presence, fled from the monastery and took shelter in a small house at the foot of the mountain. When about two hours had passed, the storm abated, but Hideyoshi's skepticism toward Kōbō's wisdom was cured forever.

->§ 7. The “Heavenly Dragon’s Well” at the Suwa shrine.<-

According to the *Honchō zokugenshi* <note>1 *Honchō zokugenshi*, written in 1746 by Kikuoka Senryō (also called Beizan), Ch. I, p. 19, quoted in the *Shiojiri*, written in 1749 by Zansetsusha Sokyū, Ch. II.</note>, one of the seven wonders of the famous Shinto shrine of *Suwa-Myōjin* at Lake Suwa (諏訪湖, *Suwa-ko*)—where the Tenryū River (天龍川, *Tenryū-gawa*, “Heavenly Dragon River”) originates—is the *Tenryū no ido*, or “Heavenly Dragon’s Well” (天龍ノ井). Water was always dripping from the overhanging roof of the temple into this well, a phenomenon that was apparently attributed to a dragon. When Kublai Khan’s armada attacked Japan, the God of Suwa flew from the lake toward the west in the shape of a long, five-colored cloud resembling a serpent, in order to assist the Japanese against the foreign invaders <note>2 *Taiheiki*, Ch. XXXIX, p. 12.</note>.
In the neighborhood of the same “Heavenly Dragon River,”

In Tōtōmi Province, a large dragon’s head was preserved in a Buddhist temple called Zuda-dera (頭陀寺). It was taken to Edo and shown to the people there. The river’s name was said to have originated from the presence of this dragon <note>1 *Shiojiri*, Ch. II, p. 11.</note>.

### § 8. Kurikara Myō-ō, the dragon-shaped mountain god.

Another Shinto shrine, the temple of *Kurikara Myō-ō* (倶梨迦羅明王), is dedicated to a dragon-shaped mountain god who is said to live in a waterfall on Mount Ōyama in Sagami Province. As the *Nihon Shūkyō Fūzoku Shi* <note>2 P. 214.</note> (1902) tells us, in olden times the Buddhist priest Ryōben was preaching there one day when a violent thunderstorm suddenly arose, and the water in the basin carved out by the waterfall was heavily disturbed. A huge dragon emerged from it and said to the priest: “I am the guardian god of this mountain. After having heard your sermon, I wish to serve Buddha.” Then Ryōben worshipped the dragon, and afterwards a small Shinto shrine was built on the spot and dedicated to the dragon, which was called by the Buddhist name “Kurikara Myō-ō,” or “Kurikara, the Wisdom King” (that is, <term>Vidyā-rāja</term>, with the word "Light" being used in the sense of mystic knowledge, <term>Vidyā</term>).

This was apparently an original Japanese dragon-shaped mountain god who was identified by the Buddhists with Fudō Myō-ō’s dragon form; the Shinto shrine, however, remained his sanctuary. As we read in Miura’s *Bukkyō Iroha Jiten* <note>3 Vol. III, p. 57, under the entry for Kurikara; see also below, Ch. VI, § 10.</note>, Kurikara is Fudō Myō-ō’s “Samaya” (三摩耶) form—a black dragon coiled around a sword.

# CHAPTER VI.

## THE DRAGON-LANTERN.

Among the many <term>ignes fatui</term> of Japan, the Dragon-lantern (<term>Ryūtō</term>, 龍燈) occupies an important place. It mostly rises from the sea and flies from there to the mountains, where it is seen hanging in some specific old pine or cryptomeria tree before a (mostly Buddhist) temple. Old pine trees especially are famous in respect to these mysterious lights, which are evidently offerings sent by the dragons of the sea to the deities, Buddhas, or Bodhisattvas worshipped in the shrines. There is an enormous number of legends telling of the Dragon-lanterns appearing along the mountainous coasts of Japan. In order to make the people’s ideas on this point clear, however, it may be sufficient to refer to a few passages, because they closely resemble one another, and the same concepts lie at the root of them all.

The old annals do not speak of the Dragon-lantern, nor do we find any mention made of it in other books before the fourteenth century.

### § 1. Dengyō Daishi's image of Yakushi Nyorai.

The *Kigegawa Yakushi engi*¹ says the following: “The image of Yakushi Nyorai in Jōkwōji (also called Shōryūzan, 青龍山, “Blue Dragon monastery”), in the Katsushika district of Shimōsa province, was made by Dengyō Daishi ². When Jikaku Daishi ³ stayed in Asakusa-dera (the famous Kannon temple in Asakusa, the well-known district of Edo), an old man with gray hair appeared to him and said: ‘In the Northeast there is a holy

---

<note>1 木下川藥師縁起, written in 1327 by the Buddhist priest Gijun, 義純, *Gunsho ruijū*, Vol. XV, no. 442, p. 637.</note>
<note>2 傳教大師 (767—822), the founder of the Tendai sect in Japan.</note>
<note>3 慈覺大師 (794—864), appointed head (*zasu*) of the Tendai sect in 854.</note>

<meta>Digitized by Microsoft®</meta>

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the "Dragon-lantern" (Ryūtō), a mysterious light seen as an offering from sea dragons to Buddhist deities, and begins a specific legend involving the priests Dengyō Daishi and Jikaku Daishi.</summary>
<keywords>Dragon-lantern, Ryūtō, Buddhism, Yakushi Nyorai, Dengyō Daishi, Jikaku Daishi, Edo, Shimōsa province, ignes fatui</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the legend of the Blue Dragon Temple (Shōryūzan) and the origin of its dragon lantern, followed by an account of Kōbō Daishi's spirit appearing in Edo.</meta>

place where I have dedicated a miraculous image made by Dengyō Daishi.' Thereupon the man disappeared, and Jikaku went outside and looked toward the northeast. Suddenly a lucky cloud (瑞 雲, *zui-un*, a cloud of a lucky color) arose, and a blue dragon was visible within it. Then the Daishi secretly left the temple and went in search of this blue dragon until he arrived at the cottage (where the aforementioned old man had lived as a hermit and had obtained the image). There he worshipped the image and saw the blue dragon, which was still there. Jikaku turned toward the lucky cloud and addressed the dragon as follows: ‘I wish to say a few words to you, sacred dragon; listen to me. I want to build a temple here, which you must guard and protect from calamity. From this moment, I appoint you the guardian god of the shrine.’ When the Daishi had finished speaking, the dragon, which had listened motionless with its head bent down in reverence, disappeared. The priest considered this to be a good sign and called the sanctuary the ‘Blue Dragon Temple.’ To this day, a *dragon lantern* appears there from time to time as a wonderful, lucky omen, probably as a result of the facts mentioned above (that is, because the blue dragon is the temple’s guardian god).”

The *Edo meisho ki*¹ tells us that since ancient times many pilgrims have traveled to this temple—also called Jōkwōji (淨 光 寺, “Temple of the Pure Light”)—to worship the dragon lantern, which was certain to appear before the image of Yakushi Nyorai on the eighth day of every month and on New Year's morning.

### § 2. Kōbō Daishi's spirit.

In the *Tomioka Hachiman shaki* (“History of the Shinto Temple of Hachiman at Tomioka”)², we read that in 1628 Kōbō Daishi's ghost appeared in a dream to a Shingon priest and ordered all the priests of his sect in Kwantō, except for the heads of Kōya and Sekigaku, to assemble in Eitaijima (in Yedo). They obeyed the saint's command and preached sermons for ninety consecutive days. At the same time, they erected a temple dedicated to

<note>2 富 岡 八 幡 社 記, quoted by Kurihara Ryū-an, 栗 原 柳 菴 (1793–1870), in his *Ryū-an zuihitsu*, 柳 菴 隨 筆, written in 1819, Hyakka setsurin, Vol. 續 下 二, p. 487.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of dragon lanterns associated with famous Buddhist figures and sacred sites, concluding a section on Kōbō Daishi and beginning an account of Jigen Daishi.</meta>

...Kōbō Daishi's soul (Mikage-dō), and since that time, a dragon lantern has arisen before this shrine.

### § 3. Jigen Daishi's spirit.

The *Jigen Daishi den*<note>慈眼大師傳</note>, the biography of Jigen Daishi—that is, the Buddhist bishop Tenkai<note>天海</note>, who was greatly revered by Ieyasu and who died in 1643—contains the following tale:

"On the evening of the second day of the eleventh month of the twentieth year of the Kan'ei era (1643), a special service was held for Jigen's soul in the Sembakita temple in Musashi. During the service, a dragon lantern rose from a well and hung at the top of a cryptomeria tree at the southern front of the *kyakuden* (the temple's reception hall). Priests and laypeople stared at the light in astonishment and worshipped it. Immediately, a fast runner was dispatched as a messenger to the Nikkō temple to announce the news, and everyone was filled with admiration for Jigen's holiness; his soul was evidently believed to be connected with the light, much like that of Kōbō Daishi in the preceding legend."<note>Sounding curiously rationalistic alongside these passages are the following words from the *Ensei meibutsu kōhōi* (遠西名物考補遺, Ch. VIII), quoted on the same page of the *Ryūan zuihitsu*: 'The "Devil-lights" (*kirin*<note>鬼燐</note>) and dragon lanterns which appear over swamps, pools, broad plains, mountain temples, graveyards, etc., are "zwavelstofgas" coming from rotting animals and plants.' The word **"zwavelstofgas"**, written in kana, is a Dutch word and must be **"zwavelwaterstofgas"**, meaning hydrogen sulfide.</note>

### § 4. "Dragon lantern pine trees".

Mention is very frequently made of so-called "Dragon lantern pine trees" (*Ryūtō no matsu*<note>龍燈松</note>), which stood before Buddhist temples and in whose branches a dragon lantern was said to appear regularly. Occasionally, we read of such trees standing near Shinto shrines, but by far the majority of the passages concerning them, as well as those concerning dragon lanterns in general, relate to Buddhist sanctuaries.

In front of the chapel of Monju (Mañjuśrī), called the Monjudō<note>文殊堂</note>, at Ama-no-hashidate (one of the *Nihon sankei*, the three most beautiful places in Japan) in the Yosa district of Tango province, situated near the so-called Kuze-no-to or Kire-to, there stood a "dragon lantern pine tree." At midnight on the sixteenth

<summary>This page recounts a miraculous dragon lantern appearing at the Sembakita temple after the death of the monk Tenkai (Jigen Daishi) and introduces "dragon lantern pine trees" (Ryūtō no matsu) found at Buddhist temples.</summary>
<keywords>Jigen Daishi, Tenkai, dragon lantern, ryūtō, Sembakita temple, Ryūtō no matsu, Amanohashidate, hydrogen sulfide, Japanese folklore</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Dragon lantern pine trees," focusing on specific miraculous lights at Hashidate and Karida associated with Buddhist and Shinto deities.</meta>

day of every month, a dragon lantern appeared from the northeastern sea and flew to this tree. On the night of the sixteenth day of the first, fifth, and ninth months, another light called the "Heavenly Lantern" (*Tentō*, <term>天燈</term>) descended from the sky. A third light, the so-called "*Ise no go tō*," or "August Light of Ise," was also visible at this spot. It is mentioned in the *Yūhō meisho ryaku*¹ (1697), which states that it is named *Shintō* (<term>神燈</term>, the "Sacred Light") and is created by the divinity of the Grand Shrine at Ise (Amaterasu). The image of the Bodhisattva Mañjuśrī (Monju Bosatsu) worshiped there was said to be of Indian origin and to have emerged from the sea.

The same temple is mentioned in the *Kii zōdanshū*², where we read the following details concerning the light: "It comes from a deep spot in the sea, two <term>chō</term> from the 'Broken Door' (Kire-to) of Hashidate, where the Gate of the Dragon-palace is said to be located. When the weather is clear and the wind and waves are calm, it moves from Kire-to to the Monju Shrine. Unbelievers cannot see it, or if they do, they mistake it for a fisherman's light. It settles on the top of a tall pine tree that stands about 20 <term>ken</term> south of the Monjudō. After half an hour or less, it is extinguished. From time to time, a young boy is seen at the top of the tree carrying a lamp called *Tendō*, <term>天灯</term>, 'Heavenly Lantern' (this word may also be written <term>天童</term>, *Tendō*, 'Heavenly Boy'). In the past, this boy (a celestial being) appeared often, but he is rarely seen now."

The *Nihon shūkyō fūzoku shi*³ (1902) mentions an ancient "Dragon lantern pine tree" which still stands near a Shinto temple called *Uhara jinja* (<term>宇原神社</term>) in Karida village, Kyōtō district, Buzen province. There, Toyotama-bime, the Sea-god's daughter, gave birth to a son while in the form of a dragon⁴, and at the same time, a light (a dragon lantern) flew from the sea and hung in the same pine tree⁵.

We may mention here another Shinto shrine, the *Shirahige jinja* (白鬚神社) in Shiga district, Ōmi province, where a dragon-lantern was said to enter the worshiping hall (*haiden*) from time to time, instead of hanging from a pine tree¹; and the *Jōgū* (常宮), a Shinto temple in Tsuruga, Echizen province, where every New Year's night such a light arose in a “Dragon-lantern pine tree” which stood in the temple garden².

Before the Buddhist chapel of *Kasai Yakushi* (笠井藥師), situated on a mountain north of Okayama, in Bizen province, there stood a “Dragon-lantern pine tree.” Every night, especially in summer time, will-o’-the-wisps were seen there³.

### § 5. Tide-stones connected with dragon-lanterns.

On the top of Kaneyama, a mountain very near the above-mentioned chapel of *Kasai Yakushi*, there was a big stone with a hole in it, about one *shaku* square. When the tide was high, this hole was filled with water, and at low tide it was dry⁴.

It seems that such stones were considered to be connected with the dragons who sent the dragon-lanterns, for also on the Sata promontory, in Hata district, Tosa province (30 *ri* west of Kōchi), there was at the same period (1746) the so-called *Ushio-ishi* (潮石) or “Tide-stone,” a concave stone, filled with water at high tide and empty at ebb tide, while on the same spot, near the Shinto temple of Ashizuri no Myōjin (蹉跎ノ明神), a dragon-lantern used to appear from the sea simultaneously with the descent from the sky of a Heavenly Light (*Tentō*, 天燈). The latter was one of the seven wonders of the place. Another of these wonders was a *dragon-horse*, which used to come at the hour of the ox (1–3 a.m.) and eat the small bamboo, which for this reason gradually died out in the vicinity of the temple⁵.

The connection between the tide-stones and the dragons immediately reminds us of the legends concerning Toyotama-hiko, the Sea-god, who gave the tide-jewel to Hiko-hohodemi⁶, and concerning the Empress Jingō, who was assisted by the gods of Kasuga and

Kawakami by means of the jewels of low and high tide, taken from Sāgara, the Dragon-king <note>See above, Book II, Ch. I, § 7, p. 142.</note>.

### § 6. The Mountain-light and the Dragon-lantern of Gammokuzan in Etchū province.

The *Tōyūki kōhen* <note>東遊記後編, written in 1797 by Tachibana Nankei, 橘南谿 (1752—1805), Zoku Teikoku bunko, Vol. XX (*Kikō bunshū*, 紀行文集), p. 113.</note> states the following about a temple of the Zen sect in Niikawa district, Etchū province, called Gammokuzan (眼目山) or Sakkwazan. When this shrine was opened by its founder, the priest Daitetsu, a pupil of Dōgen (道元, Shōyō Daishi, 1200—1253), the Mountain-god and a Dragon-god assisted and performed all kinds of miracles. Still in the author’s time (the second half of the eighteenth century), every year on the 13th day of the 7th month (probably the date of the opening of the shrine), two lights appeared on the top of a pine tree in the temple garden. One of these lights (that of the Mountain-god) came flying from the summit of Mount Tateyama, the other (that of the Dragon-god) rose up from the sea, and both stopped on the pine tree. They were called the Mountain-light and the Dragon-lantern (*Santō*, *Ryūtō*), and were seen every year by the people of the neighborhood. “Although,” says Tachibana Nankei, “there are many cases of dragon-lanterns coming out of the sea, they rarely appear simultaneously and on the same pine tree with a mountain-light, as is the case at this temple.”

### § 7. Kwannon’s dragon-lantern at Ryūkōji.

On Itozaki yama, in Echizen province, Hannan (the present Sakai) district, there is a Buddhist temple called *Ryūkōji* (龍興寺, “Dragon’s Rise Temple”), which was built by a Chinese priest who came from China on the back of an enormous tortoise, carrying a precious Kwannon image. When approaching the coast, the tortoise emitted a strong light, and the fishermen, seeing this, went out to meet it and carried the image ashore. A temple was dedicated to this Kwannon, and every night a blue dragon appeared there in a so-called “Dragon-lantern pine tree,” carrying a light in honor of the deity. When he appeared, there was always a large number of holy priests, clad in magnificent robes,

<summary>This page describes miraculous phenomena at Gammokuzan and Ryūkōji temples, where "dragon-lanterns" and "mountain-lights" were observed by locals. These lights were attributed to Mountain and Dragon deities and were often associated with the temples' founders or sacred images.</summary>
<keywords>Gammokuzan, Etchū, Dragon-lantern, Ryūtō, Mountain-light, Santō, Tachibana Nankei, Ryūkōji, Echizen, Kwannon, Daitetsu, Dōgen</keywords>

making heavenly music in the air. The priests could see them, but ordinary people could only hear their music <note>*Yūhō meisho ryaku*, Chapter V, page 16.</note>.

### § 8. **Tōmyō-dake, Kumano Gongen at Nogami, Kwōmyōji at Kamakura and Zenkwōji at Nagano.**

Sometimes a mountain peak is named after a dragon-lantern, such as the *Tōmyō-dake* (燈 明 嶽), or “Light-Peak,” in Kawachi province, Ishikawa district (the present Minami Kawachi district), where such a light appeared at Kōkidera, a Buddhist temple whose guardian deity was the Shintō mountain-god Iwabune Myōjin <note>*Ibidem*, Chapter IV, page 59.</note>.

On the last night of the year, during the hour of the ox (1–3 a.m.), a dragon-lantern was often seen near the shrine of Kumano Gongen at Nogami village in Suwō province, while at the same time another “sacred light” (*shinkwa*, 神 火) came flying, swift as an arrow, from the neighboring “Dragon-mouth Mountain.” While worshipping these lights, the villagers entered the New Year <note>*Shokoku rijindan*, Chapter III, Section VI, pages 928 and following.</note>.

Another dragon-lantern was said to rise annually from the sea to the clouds near Kwōmyōji (光 明 寺), the “Shrine of Brilliant Light” in Kamakura, on two nights during the temple festival which lasted ten days <note>*Ibidem*.</note>. And from the 14th to the 16th of the 7th month, a similar light flew up from the Saikawa, a river in Shinano province; jumping from treetop to treetop, it alighted on the southwestern gable of the main building of Zenkwōji, the famous Buddhist sanctuary at Nagano <note>*Honchō zokugenshi*, Chapter III, page 8.</note>.

### § 9. **The light of Yotsukura.**

A celebrated dragon-lantern was that of Yotsukura, a village on the coast of Hitachi province. It is described as a glittering fireball, at least one <term>shaku</term> in diameter, and spreading a very clear light. Fishermen explained this (as well as all other so-called dragon-lanterns) to be a mass of flying insects born on the water, which dispersed and disappeared as soon as they heard people approaching. Therefore, they never appeared in storm and rain (because they were afraid of noise). “Sometimes,” they said, “these insects cluster into one mass, which is seen hanging on”

<summary>This page lists several Japanese locations and temples where miraculous dragon-lanterns and sacred lights were observed, including Tōmyō-dake, Kamakura, and Nagano. It also provides a rational explanation from fishermen who believed these lights were actually clusters of luminous insects.</summary>

<keywords>dragon-lantern, Tōmyō-dake, Kumano Gongen, Kwōmyōji, Zenkwōji, shinkwa, Yotsukura, Hitachi province, shaku, bioluminescence</keywords>

the top of a high tree or on the eaves of a temple, and which looks like a ball of fire. The so-called *shiranu-bi* (不知火, "unknown fire") is the same <note>*Ōshū-banashi*, 奥州波奈志, *Onchi shōsho* (温知叢書), Vol. XI, pages 50, 52.</note>.

More details about the Yotsukura light are to be found in the *Tō-ō kikō* <note>東奥紀行, written in 1760 by <term>Nagakubo Genshū</term>, 長久保玄珠, and quoted by <term>Kurihara Ryū-an</term>, 栗原柳菴 (1793—1870) in his *Ryū-an zuihitsu*, 柳菴隨筆, written in 1819; *Hyakka setsurin*, Vol. 續下二, page 487.</note>, which says that it moves, floating on the water, from the sea along the Kamado river up to the valley brooks. At the foot of Mount Akai-dake it flies up and is soon seen hanging between the branches of big cryptomerias, until it disappears into the depths of the wood, continually followed by other lights, in an endless row, from evening until daybreak. In bright moonlight the lights are small, but on dark nights they are large like fireflies or torches. A strange thing is that they are only visible from the so-called Enseki (Swallow-stone) on a projecting part of the mountain. The author calls it *inkwa* (陰火, *Yin*-fire), an expression borrowed from Chinese books, and compares it with the "Sacred Lights" (神燈) and the "Cold Flames" (寒炎) mentioned by Chinese authors.

### § 10. The lights of Ushijima, Ishidōzan and Kurikara.

On the last night of the year — a time when many dragon-lanterns were said to appear, as the above legends have taught us — three strange lights used to arise from different spots near the island Ushijima and join into one mass, which flew to the "Dragon-lantern pine tree" of Asahizan Jōnichiji, a Buddhist temple at Himi (a little place in Etchū province, Himi district), and was seen hanging between its branches <note>*Sanshū kidan kōhen* (1779) (compare above, page 174, note 1), Chapter VII, page 990.</note>.

It was also a dragon-lantern which the Buddhist priest Nansan saw on an old pine tree when he crossed Mount Ishidōzan in the year 806; Amida Nyorai appeared there, seated on a wonderful cloud. Nansan built a Buddhist temple on the spot and placed Amida Nyorai's image in it. Four centuries later, when the Emperor Juntoku (1211—1221) went to Sado province and his ship was tossed on the waves by a severe storm, all of a sudden a dragon-lantern arose in the South on the same spot and served

as a beacon to the Imperial ship, which safely reached the coast <note>See *Sanshū kidan* (1764) (compare page 172, note 7), Chapter IV, page 815.</note>.

In the Kurikara <note>Compare above, Chapter V, § 8, page 204: Kurikara Myō-ō, the dragon-shaped Fudō Myō-ō.</note> mountains, which form the boundary between Etchū and Kaga, there was a Shingon temple called Chōrakuji or *Kurikara-san*, with an image of Fudō Myō-ō. This sanctuary was miraculous beyond description and famous for its wonderful "Mountain-lights" and "Dragon-lanterns" <note>*Sanshū kidan*, Chapter V, page 835 (*santō, ryūtō*, 山燈 龍燈).</note>.

### § 11. Ignes fatui in general. The dragon-lantern is the only one which arises from the sea and flies to the mountains.

Not only regarding the dragon-lantern, but also in other respects, old pine trees were especially famous for their ignes fatui. For instance, we read of the "gold-fire pine tree" on the road from Komatsu to Kanazawa, where phosphorescent light, the so-called "*rinkwa*" (燐火) or "*kin-kwa*" (金火, gold-fire), was seen to fly up and down. This fire, however, did not come from the sea like the dragon-lantern, but was attributed to the fact that in the past, criminals used to be beheaded under this tree. Their blood, penetrating into the ground, had become the so-called "*ki-rin*" (鬼燐) or "demon's fire"; or someone had committed suicide on this spot in great anger, and "the fire of his heart made the pine tree burn" <note>Ibidem, Chapter II, page 713; "Hachiman's gold-fire."</note>.

The idea of blood causing these mysterious lights is borrowed from China; we read in <term>De Groot</term>'s *Religious System of China* <note>Volume IV, page 80.</note> that blood, identified with the *tsing k'i* (精氣)—the breath or *yang* soul possessed by vital energy, especially the blood of men killed by weapons and that of horses and cows—forms ignes fatui. They are soul-flames, especially visible on battlefields. The identification of blood and soul is not only a Chinese concept <note><term>De Groot</term>, cited above, Volume I, pages 217, 268, note 2.</note>, but is also found among some Indian tribes of North America, as we learn from <term>Frazer</term>'s *Golden Bough* <note>Volume I (second edition), page 353.</note>. As for China, the ignes fatui there were believed to be produced especially by old trees and old blood <note><term>De Groot</term>, cited above, Volume IV, page 80.</note>.

Also, demons were considered to cause will-o'-the-wisps, as the names "*ki-rin*" and "*oni-bi*" (鬼火), or "demon-fire," clearly show. Moreover, old bewitching animals, like *tanuki* and *mujina*, were

<keywords>Kurikara, dragon-lanterns, ignes fatui, Japanese folklore, Fudō Myō-ō, Shingon Buddhism, rinkwa, Chinese influence, De Groot, Frazer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of mysterious lights, specifically the belief that blood transforms into spirit-fire, and categorizes the various types of ignes fatui found in Japanese folklore.</meta>

notorious in this respect <note>Compare my treatise on “The Fox and the Badger in Japanese Folklore," *Transactions of the Asiatic Society of Japan*, Vol. XXXVI, Part III, pp. 151 and following, 156.</note>. Besides *tanuki-bi*, *kitsune-bi* (badger and fox-fire) and *oni-bi*, the ignes fatui were called *inkwa* (陰火, or *Yin*-fire, Fire of Darkness), *kumo no hi* (蜘蛛ノ火, spider-fire), *kaigetsu no hi* (海月ノ火, sea-moon-fire <note>*Sanshū kidan kōhen*, Chapter VI, pp. 955 and following.</note>), *susuke andō* (煤行灯, sooty lantern) <note>Ibidem.</note>, or *bōzu-bi* (坊主火, monk’s fire) <note>*Sanshū kidan*, Chapter III, p. 752.</note>.

Not always, however, are demons <note>*Mami*, 魔魅, compare *Sanshū kidan*, Chapter III, p. 770.</note>, or old animals, or dragons believed to cause the Jack-o’-lanterns, nor are these only considered to be angry souls of the dead <note>*Sanshū kidan*, Chapter I, p. 664; Chapter V, p. 840.</note>, for also Buddhas and Shintō gods may be the producers of these wonderful “*burari-bi*”, or “dangling lights.” Amida Nyorai himself, as we have seen above <note>Page 212.</note>, appeared with the dragon-lantern on Ishidōzan, and the name *Butsu-tō* (佛燈), or “Buddha’s lights,” is sufficient evidence of this belief. As to the Shintō gods, we may mention the ignes fatui near Gofuku village in Etchū, which were said to be caused by the jealous spirit of the goddess Fukura-hime no Mikoto, whose consort, the god Noto-hiko, took a second wife during her absence; whereupon she pelted his temple with stones <note>*Sanshū kidan*, Chapter V, p. 840.</note>. And in the year 1770, the god Sannō made a sacred light (神燈, *shintō*) appear in the dead of night in the worship hall of his temple in Sebamachi, at the western mouth of the Namikawa; after two nights, he stopped it as a result of offerings made to him and *kagura* dances performed in his honor <note>*Sanshū kidan kōhen*, Chapter VIII, p. 1001.</note>.

So we see that there is a great variety of ignes fatui in Japan. The dragon-lantern, however, is the only one which arises from the sea and flies to the mountains; all the others start and remain in the woods, or fly from there to the sea coast, where they sometimes fall into the water <note>Compare *Sanshū kidan kōhen*, Chapter VI, p. 956: a fisherman catches them in his net, but the countless small lights escape through the gaps, fly up, and join into one massive ball of fire which soars away through the air; perhaps, says the author, it was a transformation of old blood.</note>. The reason for this difference is clear: the dragon-lantern is believed to be an offering sent by the dragons of the sea to the deities, Buddhas, or Bodhisattvas in the mountains, while the other lights, on the contrary, are attributed to these divine beings themselves, or to demons, animals, or spirits of the dead, all of which have their abodes in the mountains and woods or on the grassy plains of the battlefields.

# CHAPTER VII.

## THE CHINESE DRAGON’S EGGS IN JAPAN.

**§ 1. The dragon fetus remains in the egg for three thousand years.**

In the sixteenth century of our era, a Japanese author <note>1. *Kii zōdanshū* (1532—1554) (compare above, p. 208, note 2), Ch. III, p. 16.</note> spoke of an old (certainly Chinese) tradition, according to which a dragon’s fetus lives for a thousand years in the sea, for a thousand years in the mountains, and, after having lived among men (“in a village,” says the text) for the same long period, it is finally born, becomes a dragon, and ascends to the sky <note>2. Compare above, Book I, Ch. III, § 16, pp. 88 and following.</note>. During these three thousand years, the fetus lives as a very small snake within a stone—the dragon’s egg—which first lies at the bottom of the sea, then moves to the mountains (how it arrived there is not explained). After a thousand years, it is picked up by someone who carries it home and preserves it because of its beautiful colors, or uses it as an ink-stone (<term>suzuri</term>, 硯). As it invariably has the remarkable quality of constantly producing water (the dragon’s element), it is a very convenient ink-stone indeed <note>3. In the *Hyakka setsurin* (Vol. 續下二, p. 487) we find the following names of ink-stones: *Ryūringetsu-ken*, 龍 鱗 月 硯, or “Dragon-scales-moon-ink-stone,” and *Ryūbi-ken*, 龍 尾 硯, “Dragon’s tail-ink-stone.”</note>. But woe to whoever possesses such a stone at the end of the thousand-year period which the fetus must pass among mankind, for then the stone splits and a small snake creeps out of it. In a few moments, it grows larger and larger, and with a terrible noise, it forces its way to the sky, smashing the roof amid thunder and lightning and ascending in a dark cloud. The little reptile becomes an enormous four-legged dragon, which leaves the narrow dwellings of men and frees itself in this terrifying way.

->Digitized by Microsoft®<-

<summary>This page describes a Japanese and Chinese legend where a dragon fetus spends three thousand years inside a stone egg before hatching. The egg passes through the sea, the mountains, and human homes, where it is often mistaken for a magical, water-producing ink-stone.</summary>
<keywords>dragon's egg, suzuri, ink-stone, Kii zōdanshū, Japanese folklore, dragon metamorphosis, Hyakka setsurin</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragon fetuses living inside stones, providing specific historical accounts of dragons emerging from ink-stones in Japan.</meta>

# § 2. **Dragons born from beautiful stones picked up in the mountains.**

A remarkable ink-stone was kept in ancient times, according to the *Kii zōdanshū* <gloss>1</gloss>, in a Zen monastery at Kanagawa in Musashi Province. Drops of water were constantly dripping from this stone, but no one understood the reason for this strange phenomenon. One day, on a very hot summer afternoon while the monks were sitting together in a cool room, the ink-stone suddenly split open on its own, and a small worm, about 2 bu (0.24 inches) long, crawled out of it. The monks were about to kill the creature, but the head priest forbade them from doing so; he carefully carried it on a fan to the garden, where he placed it in the lotus pond. All the monks followed him, and as they watched the worm, they saw with astonishment how the little creature, by contracting and stretching its body, grew larger and larger. Terrified, they ran back into the house, but even there they soon felt unsafe, for the sky—which had been perfectly clear—was suddenly covered with clouds. Thunder and lightning raged, and a pitch-black darkness filled the garden and enveloped the building. Then they all fled through the gate and watched from a distance as the dragon ascended to the sky in an immense cloud—first its head, then its four-legged body, and finally its enormous tail. Once it had disappeared, the clouds dispersed and the sky became clear again. The garden, the pond, and the building, however, were all left in a terrible state. In the meantime, people from the neighboring villages came to help, thinking the monastery was on fire.

An eighteenth-century writer, <term>Kiuchi Sekitei</term> <gloss>2</gloss>, recounts the same event but says it happened in *Kanazawa* (instead of *Kanagawa*). Furthermore, he mentions a round stone found by a boy in the mountains near Sammon in Ōmi Province. Because water was constantly trickling from this stone, the boy used it in later years to dampen his ink-slab. Fifty years later, after he had attained the rank of Archbishop—the stone apparently having brought him prosperity—the strange object split open and a dragon rose to the sky after breaking through the ceiling and the roof. The stone still existed in <term>Sekitei's</term> time, and there was a hole the size of a bean in its center.

---

<note>1. Ch. V, p. 1.</note>

<note>2. <term>Kiuchi Sekitei</term> (木内石亭), who lived 1722–1801, in the *Unkonshi kōhen* (雲根志後編, "Records on Cloud-Roots, Continued"), written in 1779; Ch. II, p. 2. The first volume of this work (*zempen*) appeared in 1772, and the third (*sampen*) in 1801.</note>

<meta>Digitized by Microsoft®</meta>

<summary>This page details historical legends of dragons hatching from "ink-stones" that naturally produced water. Two accounts describe a small worm-like creature emerging from a split stone, rapidly growing into a full-sized dragon, and ascending to the sky during a sudden, violent storm.</summary>

<keywords>dragon, ink-stone, Kii zōdanshū, Kiuchi Sekitei, Musashi Province, Ōmi Province, Kanagawa, Sammon, Unkonshi kōhen, folklore</keywords>

--- Page 233 ---
<meta>This page continues the accounts of miraculous stones found in Japan that are believed to contain dragons or dragon-like creatures, often resulting in the creature's birth and ascension to the sky.</meta>

A similar dragon’s egg was used by a Buddhist priest in Moriyama, Ōmi province, in 1774, for grinding his tea, until the dragon was born and ascended, leaving a round hole in the middle of the stone <note>Ibidem.</note>.

In another case, such an egg was recognized beforehand by a great scholar who was thoroughly versed in Chinese literature, the famous <term>Itō Jinsai</term> <note>伊藤仁齋, a *kangakusha* (scholar of Chinese classics) who lived 1626–1705.</note>. He warned a court noble, telling him that a magnificent stone, square and five-colored, in the nobleman’s possession was a dragon’s egg, and that he had better throw it away in some lonely spot. The man followed the scholar’s advice and built a small Shintō shrine in an open field outside the capital, in which he placed the stone. A few years later, the shrine was smashed by the dragon as it ascended to heaven. This stone was a so-called *ryūshō-seki* (龍生石), or “dragon-producing stone” <note>*Unkonshi kōhen*, Ch. II, p. 8.</note>.

The name “dragon-horse-stone” (*ryū-me-seki*, 龍馬石) was given to another remarkable stone, white as crystal and as large as the palm of a hand, which lay on the desk of a samurai in Hizen province. A moving creature was visible in its center, and the stone moved by itself from one side of the desk to the other. One day, the man placed a teacup filled with water on the desk; when he returned, the cup was empty. The next day, he performed the same experiment with a large bowl. While he was talking with some friends in the next room, they heard a noise like wind and waves. They immediately went to see what the matter was and discovered a lizard (*tokage*, 石龍子, literally “little stone-dragon”) running from the bowl back to the stone, which it then entered <note>Ibidem, Ch. II, p. 10.</note>.

Two “snake-producing stones” (*shō-ja-seki*, 生蛇石) <note>Ibidem, Ch. II, p. 12.</note> were found in a hole in Kyoto in 1762, and in 1780, a “golden snake stone” (金蛇石) was picked up in the mountains by a child. Water was constantly flowing out of it until it was cooked, and the dragon inside was killed. It was then split open, and the dead body of a small, gold-colored snake was found within it <note>Ibidem, Ch. III, p. 7.</note>.

Although they were not dragon’s eggs, we may mention here two stones that were believed to be connected with dragons. One of them was a large stone lying in a hollow carved out by a waterfall near Kayao village, in the Inukami district of Ōmi province,

<summary>This page recounts several 18th-century Japanese legends involving stones that contained living dragons or snakes, including accounts of these creatures drinking water and eventually breaking their stone shells to ascend to heaven. These miraculous objects were identified by scholars and local observers as "dragon-producing stones" or "snake-producing stones."</summary>
<keywords>dragon’s egg, Itō Jinsai, ryūshō-seki, ryū-me-seki, tokage, snake-producing stone, shō-ja-seki, Ōmi province, Hizen province, Kyoto, Japanese folklore</keywords>

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of legendary stones associated with dragons in Japanese folklore, specifically detailing stones believed to be offerings or physical dragon eggs.</meta>

which was said to belong to the Dragon-god of the place and was called the "Dragon-god stone" (*Ryūjin-seki*, 龍神石) by the villagers. During the Kyōhō era (1716—1735), five or six men visited the neighboring villages and asked the inhabitants to sell them women's hair to make a rope. They intended to use this rope to carry the stone as an offering to the Dragon-god of Seta. A short time afterward, the stone actually disappeared, but it was far too heavy to have been moved by human hands (the men in question were likely transformed dragons) <note>Ibid., Ch. II, p. 13.</note>. The second stone, which was black and about three *shaku* long, lay in a garden and was said to cause even a clear summer sky to turn cloudy in an instant whenever someone touched it. By 1764, the stone was no longer outside but was kept within the castle, so the experiment could no longer be performed. "Perhaps," says Hotta, the author of the *Sanshū kidan*, "it is a so-called 'cloud-root' (*un-kon*, 雲根)" <note>*Sanshū kidan*, Ch. IV, p. 788.</note>.

We find the following details in the *Shōsan chomon kishū* (1849) <note>*Shōsan chomon kishū* (想山著聞奇集), written in 1849 by Shōsan Sai Shujin (想山齋主人); *Zoku Teikoku bunko*, Vol. XLVII, *Kinsei kidan zenshū*. Ch. IV, pp. 572 ff.</note>. The abbot of a Shingon monastery possessed a so-called "dragon gem" (*ryū no tama*, 龍ノ玉), which was considered an exceptionally precious object. On cloudy days, it immediately became moist, and when it rained, it was quite wet. In reality, it was not a dragon gem but a dragon's egg (*ryū no tamago*, 龍ノ卵). Such eggs hatch during thunderstorms and rain; when they do, they destroy even palaces and uproot large trees. It is therefore advisable to discard them beforehand in a lonely spot in the mountains. The abbot, however, did not think it necessary to take this precaution with the dragon's egg in his possession because it was dead. "Thirty years ago," he said, "the egg became moist as soon as the weather was even slightly cloudy, and its luster was magnificent. However, because it later stopped showing moisture even on rainy days and ceased to grow, it is evidently dead." Miyoshi Shōsan (the author) went to the monastery himself to see this wonderful egg and provided an illustration of it (p. 573), which shows the dragon fetus inside. Its dimensions were: length, 4 *sun*, 8 *bu*; breadth, 4 *sun*, 6 *bu*. It resembled a "diamond-natured thunder-axe stone" (*gyoku-shitsu rai-fu-seki*, 玉質雷斧石), which the people called *Tengu no ono* (Tengu's axe),

<meta>The text ends abruptly at the bottom of the page.</meta>

<summary>This page details historical accounts of miraculous stones associated with dragons, including a "Dragon-god stone" that vanished mysteriously and a dragon's egg kept at a Shingon monastery. It describes the physical characteristics of these stones and the belief that dragon eggs hatch during violent storms, causing great destruction.</summary>
<keywords>Dragon-god stone, Ryūjin-seki, Kyōhō era, Dragon-god of Seta, Hotta, Sanshū kidan, cloud-root, un-kon, Shōsan chomon kishū, Shingon monastery, dragon gem, ryū no tama, dragon's egg, ryū no tamago, Miyoshi Shōsan, dragon fetus, Tengu no ono</keywords>

--- Page 235 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the *Shōsan chomon kishū* (1849) regarding the abbot's dragon egg, comparing its physical properties to other legendary stones.</meta>

天狗ノ鉄, or “Tengu-axe”), but it seemed to be even harder and sharper than these. Its color was red, tinted with bluish-grey, much like the thunder-axe-stones, but its luster was more like glass than is usually the case with the latter. There were some spots on the egg, which SHŌSAN believed to be dirt left on it by the dragon that produced it.

->§ 3. Thunder-stones.<-

In the same monastery, there was a so-called “*thunder-jewel*” (雷ノ玉, rai no tama, or 雷玉, rai-gyoku), which in 1796 had fallen from the sky during a heavy thunderstorm when lightning struck a spot near Haseda. Its color was white, tinted with a slight bluish-grey, just like carnelian or marble. Such thunder-stones were called “*thunder-axes*” (raifu, 雷斧), “*thunder-knives*” (雷刀, raitō), “*thunder-hammers*” (rai tsui, 雷槌), “*thunder-blocks*” (雷梃, raitan), “*thunder-rings*” (雷環, raikwan), “*thunder-pearls*” (雷珠, raishu), “*thunder-pillars*” (雷楔, rai-ketsu), “*thunder-ink*” (raiboku, 雷墨), “*thunder-swords*” (raiken, 雷剣), “*thunder-pins*” (raisan, 雷鑽), and so on. They are found in spots struck by lightning. The black ones are thunder-axes; those which are white, tinted with blue, are thunder-rings; and the purple ones, tinted with red, are thunder-pins. If it is neither stone nor earth, but a lump resembling lacquer, it is thunder-ink. The specimen mentioned above was, in SHŌSAN’s opinion, a kind of thunder-pearl <note>Compare with DE GROOT, *Religious System of China*, Vol. V, p. 866, where “thunderbolt stones” (霹靂梃) and “thunder-nodules” (雷楔, compare the 楔 of the Japanese text) are said to be believed to remove the effects of *ku*-poison. On the following page, DE GROOT mentions thunder-hammers, thunder-awls, thunder-axes (supposed to have been used by the God of Thunder to split things apart), thunder-rings (lost by that god), and thunder-pearls.</note>.

We learn from this passage that prehistoric stone weapons and tools were considered by the Chinese—for all these names were borrowed from Chinese works—and subsequently by the Japanese, to be thunderbolts. This is the same concept found everywhere among primitive peoples. Similarly, meteors are believed to have been thrown by lightning upon the earth or to be fallen stars. As for the dragon, its connection with rain and thunder is evidently supposed to begin long before its birth and to manifest in a terrible way as soon as it is born.

<summary>This page describes various types of "thunder-stones" and "dragon eggs" found in Japan, explaining how prehistoric stone tools were often interpreted as celestial objects associated with lightning and dragons.</summary>
<keywords>thunder-stones, dragon eggs, SHŌSAN, thunder-jewel, raifu, Japanese folklore, prehistoric tools, De Groot, ku-poison, archaeology</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical relics associated with dragons, such as dragon eggs and thunder-stones, to the meteorological phenomenon of the *tatsumaki* (whirlwind) and legends regarding dragons ascending to the heavens.</meta>

# CHAPTER VIII.

## THE TATSUMAKI (龍卷), OR "DRAGON'S ROLL".

Works from the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries explain that heavy whirlwinds—which cause so-called waterspouts and can instantly destroy man-made objects or anything they strike—are the work of dragons ascending to heaven. Accordingly, the enormous columns of water thrown up into the air by these whirlwinds are called "*tatsu-maki*" or "dragon's rolls."

### § 1. Dragons which ascended to heaven.

Aside from the *tatsumaki*, we may refer to two passages in the *Yūhō meisho ryaku* (1697) where dragons are said to have ascended to the sky. The first passage<sup>1</sup> deals with the name of *Tatsuta*, the place where the Wind-god has been worshiped from time immemorial<sup>2</sup>; it ascribes this name to the fact that a dragon ascended to heaven there. It was the Thunder-god himself who, in the form of a boy, had fallen onto *Tatsuta yama* (Higuri district, Yamato province), thirty or forty *chō* southwest of Nara. A peasant adopted and raised the child, and from that time onward, wind and rain were very favorable to that specific village. Afterward, the child transformed into a dragon and flew to the sky.
The second passage<sup>3</sup> explains the name of *Sennin-zuka* (仙人塚, or "immortal's grave") in Narumi village, Aichi district, Owari province, as the spot where in remote ages a Chinese *sien* (*sennin*), who had arrived on this shore floating on a tree, lived for a long time until he finally became a dragon and rose to heaven. His soul was worshiped in the "Heavenly Dragon's shrine" (*Tenryū no miya*, 天龍宮), erected close to the spot where he had lived.

---

<note>1. Ch. III, p. 15.</note> &emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp;&emsp; <note>2. Compare above, Book II, Ch. III, § 1, p. 153.</note>
<note>3. Ch. VIII, p. 47.</note>

<summary>This chapter explains the Japanese belief that whirlwinds and waterspouts, known as tatsumaki, are caused by dragons ascending to heaven. It cites historical records linking the origins of place names like Tatsuta and Sennin-zuka to legends of the Thunder-god and Chinese immortals transforming into dragons.</summary>

<keywords>tatsumaki, dragon's roll, whirlwind, Japanese folklore, Tatsuta, Sennin-zuka, Thunder-god, Wind-god, Yūhō meisho ryaku, Nara, Owari province</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of dragons ascending to heaven, specifically detailing a snake's transformation at Lake Biwa and reports of *tatsumaki* (whirlwinds) in Yedo during the 18th century.</meta>

The *Wakan sansai zue* (1713) ¹ describes how a man at Lake Biwa saw a small snake, about one shaku long, which swam to the shore, climbed onto the water-rushes, and danced around. It then went back down and swam on the surface of the water, after which it repeated these movements several times. Gradually, the snake grew longer and longer until it reached a length of about one jō (10 shaku). It then ascended into the sky, which had become covered with black clouds in the meantime. It became pitch-dark, so that only the dragon’s tail was visible, and a rain shower fell until the dragon had entered the sky, which then became as clear as it was before. "The climbing upon the rushes and dancing about," the author says, "was probably a preparatory exercise for ascending to heaven."

### § 2. Tatsumaki in Yedo ².

The *Ichiwa ichigen* ³ mentions a *tatsumaki* that arose in 1735 near the Detached Palace in the Shiba district of Yedo Bay. It destroyed the roofs of many houses in the Kyōbashi and Nihonbashi districts; at the same time, heavy rain fell and it became pitch-dark.
During the Kwansei era (1789–1800), there was a Buddhist priest in Yedo who went around predicting that a dragon would soon ascend to heaven in a heavy storm; for this reason, he advised people to stay indoors. When a samurai asked him how he knew this in advance, the priest answered, "I know this from experience. Whenever the sky has been clear for a long time and it suddenly begins to rain, as is the case now, a dragon ascends." "Are you perhaps the dragon yourself?" the samurai asked. When the priest answered that he was, the samurai requested that he ascend to the sky at once. "I cannot do so," the bonze replied, "because I have no water." "No water?" the other exclaimed, "there is plenty of water in the nearby river!" "That is of no use to me," the priest remarked, "for that is flowing water, and what I want is heavenly water (rain)." "Well, then I will give you some rainwater," said the samurai,

<note>2. I am using the traditional transcription of this name instead of "Edo" because the name Yedo has become familiar to readers of older works on Japan.</note>
<note>3. *Ichiwa ichigen*, written by Ōta Nampo (1748–1823), Ch. 40, p. 41.</note>

<summary>This page describes various accounts of dragons ascending to heaven, including a transforming snake at Lake Biwa and a 1735 whirlwind in Yedo. It also shares an anecdote about a priest in Yedo who claimed to be a dragon waiting for rainwater to ascend.</summary>
<keywords>Wakan sansai zue, Lake Biwa, dragon, shaku, jō, Tatsumaki, Yedo, Ichiwa ichigen, Shiba district, Kyōbashi, Nihonbashi, Kwansei era, Ōta Nampo, bonze, rain, whirlwind</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the anecdote of the samurai and the priest who claimed to be a dragon, concluding the story of the priest's ascent and providing further historical accounts of dragons and whirlwinds (tatsumaki) in Japan.</meta>

and he gave him a bottle of ink-stone water (used for wetting a *suzuri*). The priest took it and went away rejoicing, declaring that he would now ascend to the sky. In fact, a few days later, a violent thunderstorm suddenly broke out, accompanied by heavy rain and wind. When it subsided, the trees and the grass had become quite black. Only the samurai knew the reason for this: it was the ink-water he had given to the priest, who had used it to rise into the clouds. The author of the *Miyakawasha mampitsu*¹ heard this tale from the samurai’s son, whose father had told it to him.

In 1744, a tidal wave that destroyed a small Shintō shrine near Yedo Bay, as well as several houses and trees in Yedo, killing many people, was attributed to a dragon².

Another *tatsumaki* occurred during the Temmei era (1781–1788), when a dragon rose from the famous Shinobazu Pond in Ueno (Yedo). A black cloud rose from the pond and destroyed the houses in the vicinity. This is recorded by Ogawa Kendō³ in his *Jinchōdan*⁴, who adds that such a dragon often ascends on summer days in the seas of Sado, Echigo, and Etchū provinces.
> “Then,” he says, “a black cloud descends from the sky, and the sea water rises, whirling like a reversed waterfall, and joins the cloud. Tradition says that a dragon passes from the water into the cloud . . . Considering the fact that a dragon rose from the Shinobazu Pond, we conclude that dragons lie at the bottom of even small ponds, and that according to the weather, the water rises and a cloud descends, so that heaven and earth are connected and the dragon can ascend to the sky.”

### § 3. Tatsumaki on the sea.

In 1796, four fishing boats sank and the crews all perished while pursuing a whale in the sea near Kashima no ura in Hitachi province. They were caught by a “dragon’s roll,” which all of a

In the *Shōsan chomon kishū* <note>2. Ch. II, p. 460; concerning this work, see page 218, note 3 above.</note>, a sea-otter that rose up from the sea into a black cloud and ascended to the sky is said to have done so in the same way that "dragon-snakes" usually fly to heaven. The incident is described as follows: In a clear sky, a black cloud suddenly appeared and covered the sea in a moment. A heavy storm stirred up the waves and raised the sand, the rain fell in torrents, and the mountains shook. A hunter saw a mysterious creature rise from the sea into the cloud and fly to the sky. Immediately, with a thundering noise, the cloud came straight toward the hunter, and he saw a dazzling light in the middle of it. When he hit the cloud with a bullet, it dissipated, the rain stopped, and the storm subsided. A few days later, a large sea-otter was found dying on the shore with the bullet in its eye.

On the next page, the author quotes the *Koji inenshū* <note>3. 故事因緣集, by an unknown author; probably a work from the Tokugawa period.</note>, which states that in the sea of Iwami, fish ascend to the sky and become "fish-dragons" (魚龍). In a note, we find the remark that "there are several thousand dragons, messengers of the divine *sennin* (immortals), and among these are 'fish-dragons' and 'otter-dragons' (獺龍, *datsu-ryū*), which can assume all kinds of shapes" <note>4. A "dog-dragon" (狗龍), a kind of mole that lived underground, haunted houses, and devoured old women, is mentioned in the *Sanshū kidan* (Ch. IV, p. 821). In Ch. All snakes belong to the dragon species.</note>.

that a dragon was about to lift up the ship and carry it to the sky. In order to scare the dragon away, they all cut off their hair and burned it. And look, the terrible smell was apparently too much for the dragon, for the cloud dispersed at once.

Dragons are fond of money <gloss>1</gloss>. One day, when a *tatsumaki* (waterspout) was raging, an empty string of cash fell down; the coins had evidently been taken off by the dragon, which had then thrown the string away. Another time, a ship with much money on board was attacked by dragons in the form of a fearful storm. It sank, and all efforts to raise the box of money from the bottom of the sea were frustrated by the greedy dragons, which caused a storm to arise each time human hands tried to deprive them of their prey <gloss>2</gloss>.

### § 4. Snakes rise as dragons up to the clouds.

A strange tale is found in the *Fude no susabi* <gloss>3</gloss> concerning a woman who had a severe headache on a day when a violent thunderstorm broke out. During the storm, a little snake came out of her head, fled away through the door, and ascended to the sky in a black cloud which suddenly descended.

The *Mimi-bukuro* <gloss>4</gloss> relates a legend of a large snake that lived under the veranda of a house and was daily fed by the residents. If a girl who was waiting in vain for a husband gave food to this snake and prayed to it, her prayer was heard and she was soon married. One day, in the third month of the second year of the Temmei era (1782), the animal crept upon the veranda and lay there as if it were ill. While the man and his wife were carefully nursing it, clouds gathered and it rained continuously. The snake raised its head and looked up to the sky just as a cloud descended upon the garden. Then the animal stretched its body and, in a heavy rain, ascended to the sky.

# CHAPTER IX.

## JAPANESE, CHINESE AND INDIAN DRAGONS IN GEOGRAPHICAL, TEMPLE AND PRIEST NAMES.

In the preceding chapters, we have often mentioned mountains and temples named after a dragon that was said to live there or to have appeared at the time when the temple was built. There are a large number of similar names to be found throughout Japan, which are given in Yoshida Tōgo's *Dai Nihon chimei jisho*, or "*Geographical Lexicon of Japan*" <note>1. **大日本地名辭書**, by **吉田東伍**, published in 1907.</note>. The following details are derived from this work.

->§ 1. The Japanese dragon (tatsu).<-

*Tatsu no kuchi*, or "Dragon's mouth" (**龍口** or **辰口**) is a very frequent name. It is, for example, given to a hot spring in Nomi district, Kaga province <note>2. P. 1912.</note>, to a small waterfall in Kōjimachi district, Tōkyō <note>3. P. 2884.</note>, to a hill in Kamakura district, Sagami province <note>4. P. 2715.</note>, to a dike in Kuji district, Hitachi province <note>5. P. 3731.</note>, and to two mountains in Bizen and Rikuzen provinces <note>6. Pp. 921 and 4208.</note>. On the hill of this name in Kamakura district, criminals were put to death during the Kamakura period, and it is famous due to the legend concerning Nichiren's miracle, whose life was saved because the sword refused to cut off his holy head. Tradition said that a hill was formed by the dead body of a dragon whose mouth was at this spot and who in ancient times had inhabited a large nearby lake <note>7. *Enoshima engi*, **江島緣起** (time and author unknown), quoted by YOSHIDA, op. cit., p. 2715.</note>. Even in the Anei era (1772–1780), a five-headed dragon was worshipped there in a small Shintō shrine <note>8. *Nichiren chūgwasan*, **日蓮註畫贊**, quoted in the same source.</note>, and even today a "Shintō temple of the Dragon's Mouth" (*Tatsu no kuchi no sha*,

<summary>Chapter IX begins by exploring the prevalence of dragon-related names in Japanese geography and religious sites, specifically detailing the many locations named "Tatsu no kuchi" (Dragon's Mouth) and the legends associated with the site in Kamakura.</summary>
<keywords>Japanese dragons, Tatsu no kuchi, geographical names, Kamakura, Nichiren, Yoshida Tōgo, Shintō shrines, Enoshima engi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the list of Japanese geographical locations and shrines associated with dragons, specifically focusing on place names containing the word "Tatsu" (dragon), following the discussion of the "Dragon's Mouth" legend in Kamakura.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>

Ryūkō-sha (龍口社) is found at this spot, while a Buddhist shrine of the Nichiren sect, called *Ryūkō-dera* (龍口寺), proves how the Buddhists adopted the old belief <note>1</note>. On the aforementioned mountain in Rikuzen, a large rock in the shape of a dragon's head is worshipped in a Shintō temple called "Tatsu no kuchi jinja," or "Shrine of the Dragon's Mouth" <note>2</note>.

*Tatsu ga hana* <note>3</note> ("Dragon's nose") is the name of a cliff in Ōmi Province, Sakata District; *Tatsu-kushi* <note>4</note> ("Dragon's skewer") is that of a rock in Tosa Province, Hataya District. *Tatsu-yama* <note>5</note> ("Dragon Mountains") are found in Harima, Innan District, and in Owari, Higashi Kasugai District; a *Tatsu-ko-yama* <note>6</note> ("Little Dragon Mountain") is mentioned in Hitachi, Taga District; and *Tatsu-zaki* <note>7</note> ("Dragon's Capes") are found in Shimozuke, Sarushima District, and in Iwashiro, Ishikawa District. In Mutsu Province, Higashi Tsugaru District, we find a *Tatsu-hama-zaki* <note>8</note> ("Dragon Beach Cape"), also called *Tatsubi-zaki* <note>9</note> ("Dragon's Flight Cape"), and in Shinano, Saku District, a *Tatsu-oka* <note>10</note> ("Dragon Mound"). Furthermore, a *Tatsu-no* <note>11</note> ("Dragon Field"), also called *Tatsu no ichi* <note>12</note> ("Dragon Market"), is found in Shinano, Ina District, and another *Tatsu no ichi* is in Yamato, Soe no Kami District, where a Shintō god, Tatsu no ichi Myōjin, is worshipped. In Harima, Iiho (or Iho) District, there is a *Tatsu-no* <note>13</note> with an old castle of this name, built by Nitta Yoshisada in 1334. Finally, we find villages called *Tatsuta* <note>14</note> (Dragon Rice Field) in Higo Province, Akutaku District, and in Yamato, Ikoma District. Near the latter place is the well-known ancient Shintō shrine called *Tatsuta jinja* <note>15</note>, which is dedicated to the Wind God and where prayers are offered for wind and rain. Also, a *Mount Tatsuta* <note>16</note> in the same vicinity may be mentioned, as well as a river called *Tatsuta-gawa* <note>17</note>. On the aforementioned *Tatsu-yama* in Owari stood an old Buddhist temple of
