### § 2. The Chinese and Indian dragons (ryū or ryō).

#### A. Names of mountains.

The mountains are called *Ryū-zan* or *Ryō-zan* <note>龍山, pp. 49 and 4393.</note> (in Iwashiro and Uzen; near the latter is a place called "Sacred Tail" <note>神尾, Kan-o.</note>, which probably means a dragon’s tail <note>Compare above, Book II, Chapter III, § 12, p. 177.</note>); *Ryū ga mine* <note>龍峰, pp. 1721 and 2234.</note> ("Dragon’s peak," in Higo—resembling a reclining dragon—and in Hida); *Ryū no* (or *ga*) *saki* <note>龍崎, pp. 1358, 3571.</note> ("Dragon’s cape," with a Buddhist "Blue Dragon temple," *Seiryūji* <note>青龍寺.</note>, in Tosa, and another, in the vicinity of which is a Buddhist shrine called *Kinryūji* <note>金龍寺.</note>, or "Gold-dragon-temple," in Hitachi); *Ryū* (or *Ryō*) *ga take* <note>龍嶽, pp. 606, 4414.</note> ("Dragon’s peak," in Ise and Uzen); *Ryūzu-zaki* <note>龍頭崎, p. 1353.</note> ("Dragon’s head cape," in Tosa); *Ryūten-yama* <note>龍天山, p. 912.</note> ("Dragon-Deva mountain," in Bizen); *Ryū-ō-zan* <note>龍王山, pp. 959, 311 and 1256.</note> ("Dragon-king’s mountain," in Bichū, with a small Shintō shrine <note>Compare above, Book II, Chapter III, § 12, p. 176.</note> dedicated to the Eight Great Dragon-kings on the top, and two others in Kawachi and Sanuki). A *Ryū-ō-take* <note>龍王嶽, p. 1452.</note> ("Dragon-king’s peak") is found in Chikuzen, and a *Ryū-zō-san* <note>龍爪山, p. 4455.</note> ("Dragon’s claw-

### B. *Names of springs, waterfalls, and rivers.*

A hot spring in Kii, famous for its curative powers, is called the "Spring of the Dragon-god" (*Ryūjin-sen*) <note>龍神泉, p. 739.</note>. In Ōsumi, Yamato, and Higo, we find "Dragon-gate waterfalls" (*Ryūmon-daki*) <note>龍門瀧, pp. 1781, 290, 1652; compare above, Book II, in the place cited.</note>, and in Shimotsuke a "Dragon's head waterfall" (*Ryūzu-daki*) <note>龍頭瀑, p. 3517.</note>. The ancient Chinese considered the dragon to be so closely connected with waterfalls that they represented them by using the character for "dragon" combined with the radical for "water" (瀧). Rivers named after dragons include the *Ryūge-gawa* <note>龍華川, p. 327.</note> ("Dragon-flower river," also pronounced *Tatsu-hana-gawa*) in Kawachi, the *Tenryū-gawa* <note>天龍川, pp. 2361, 2505; compare above, Book II, Ch. V, § 7, p. 203.</note> ("Heavenly Dragon's river") in Shinano and Tōtōmi, and the *Ryūkan-gawa* <note>龍閑川, p. 2886.</note> ("Dragon's rest river") in Tokyo.

### D. *Names of Buddhist temples.*

Among the names of Buddhist temples connected with the dragon, Ryūzōji⁶ (“Dragon’s receptacle [womb] or hiding temple”), Ryū-senji⁷ (“Dragon’s spring temple”), Ryūkōji⁸ (“Dragon’s rise temple”), and Ryūmonji⁹ (“Dragon’s gate temple”) are the most frequent. Furthermore, we find temples named after the dragon's horn (Ryūkakuji¹⁰), belly (Ryūfukuji¹¹), mouth (Ryūkōji¹²), and head (Ryōtōji¹³). Moreover, mention is made of temples named after the dragon's cloud (Ryūunji¹⁴), pool (Ryūenji¹⁵ and Ryūtanji¹⁶), sea (Ryūkai-in¹⁷), valley (Ryūkeiji¹⁸), spring (Ryūgenji¹⁹), river (Ryūsenji²⁰), palace (Ryūgūji²¹), canopy (Ryūgaiji²²), flower (Ryūgeji²³), treasure (Ryū-hōji²⁴), felicity (Ryūfukuji²⁵), and rest (Ryūanji²⁶ and Ryūonji²⁷).

### E. *Names of Buddhist priests.*

Buddhist priests often have similar names; *Ryūzan*⁴ (“Dragon's mountain”) and *Ryūshū*⁵ (“Dragon’s islet”) are especially frequent. Furthermore, we find *Ryūsui*⁶ (“Dragon’s water”), *Ryūsen*⁷ (“Dragon’s river”), *Ryūtaki*⁸ (“Dragon's waterfall”), *Ryūchi*⁹ (“Dragon's pond”), *Ryū-en*¹⁰ and *Ryūshū*¹¹ (“Dragon's pool”), *Ryūshin*¹² (“Dragon's depth”), *Ryūsho*¹³ (“Dragon's islet”), *Ryūden*¹⁴ (“Dragon's rice-field”), *Ryūtō*¹⁵ (“Dragon's ascending”), *Ryūhō*¹⁶ (“Dragon's peak”), *Ryūbi*¹⁷ (“Dragon's tail”), and *Ryūmin*¹⁸ (“Dragon's sleep”), and so on. The large number of names referred to in this chapter provides strong evidence of a fact which the legends have also taught us: namely, the great popularity of all three kinds of dragons—Japanese, Chinese, and Indian—in old Japan.

## CHAPTER X.

### CONCLUSIONS.

The previous chapters have once again shown how great China's influence was on Japanese legend and superstition from the beginning of the spread of Chinese civilization in the Land of the Rising Sun until the present day. We have also seen how Buddha's powerful doctrine brought the Indian Nāgas to the Far-Eastern seas, rivers, and ponds, just as it peopled the Japanese mountains and woods with their deadly enemies, the Garuḍas. The idea of serpent-shaped semi-divine kings, living in great luxury in their magnificent palaces at the bottom of the water, was strange to Chinese and Japanese minds; however, the ability of these beings to assume human shapes and grant rain to the thirsty earth, as well as their nature as water-gods, formed the links between the Nāgas of India and the dragons of China and Japan. Chinese Buddhists identified the Indian serpents with the four-legged dragons of China, and this blending of ideas was easily introduced into the minds of the Japanese people, who did not hesitate to associate their own—mostly serpent-shaped—gods of rivers and mountains with the Western deities of the same kind.

<margin>/</margin>
In the Introduction, we have seen that the Nāgas were, as a rule, favorably disposed toward Buddhism, but that they were dangerous creatures on account of their quick temper, deadly poison, and great magical power. They possessed countless jewels and mighty charms, which they bestowed upon those to whom they were grateful and who often stayed for a while in the splendid <note>The following phrase is underlined in the original: Nāga palaces at the bottom of ponds, rivers, or seas.</note>Nāga palaces at the bottom of ponds, or rivers, or seas. The Mahāyāna school speaks of eight Great Dragon-kings, mightier than the others, one of whom, Sāgara, was well-known as a giver of rain. The rain-giving ability of the Nāgas, which is not mentioned in the Jātakas, was apparently emphasized more in Northern than in Southern Buddhism. According to the original concepts, these semi-divine serpents, who had their home in Pātāla land, *beneath the earth*, could raise clouds and thunder or

<summary>This chapter summarizes how Indian Nāgas, Chinese dragons, and Japanese water gods merged into a singular mythological tradition through the influence of Buddhism. It describes the characteristics of Nāgas as powerful, rain-giving beings who live in underwater palaces and possess magical jewels.</summary>

<keywords>Nāgas, Garuḍas, Mahāyāna, Sāgara, Jātakas, Pātāla, Dragon-kings, Japanese mythology, Chinese Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how Indian Nāgas and Chinese dragons were interpreted in Northern Buddhism, as well as their role in Chinese folklore and imperial tradition.</meta>

appear as clouds themselves to terrify mankind. Northern Buddhism, however, made these frightening beings the rain-giving benefactors of humanity, to whom prayers for rain were offered by means of special ceremonies. These rites were also performed in China and Japan. Regarding the division of the Nāgas into four castes: “Heavenly, Divine, Earthly, and Hidden Nāgas,” this is probably also a Northern feature, for I have not found it mentioned anywhere in the Jātakas. Indian Buddhist art represents the Nāgas as serpents, or as men or women with snakes coming out of their necks and rising over their heads, or as snake-tailed beings with human upper bodies and snakes appearing above their heads. Hot winds and hot sand, sudden violent storms, and Garuda-kings are what the Nāgas fear most. When strictly observing Buddhist fasting, they may be reborn as humans.

In Book I, we stated how the oldest Chinese books spoke of dragons in divination, as ornaments on clothing, and as river-gods who caused high floods by their fights. As they belonged to the four *ling* (“spiritual beings”), full of *Yang* (Light), they were omens of the birth of great men—especially emperors—and of happiness in general, like the dragon-horses, but also of death and ruin when they were seen fighting, or when their dead bodies were found, or when they appeared at wrong times or in wrong places. The Emperors were not only called dragons and compared to them, but were sometimes even considered to be their offspring, or to have them in their service. The dragons ascended to the sky, riding on winds and clouds, and were ridden by the *sien*, or they descended into the deepest wells. Their transformations were limitless. They could become as small as silkworms or so large that they covered the world¹. Their wisdom excelled that of all other animals, and their power to bless was great. Alongside these ideas, which made them the favorite subjects of poets and artists, many popular beliefs are found, which have been common among the people since ancient times.

The principal water-god is the *kiao-lung*, the scaly dragon; other important dragons are the *ying-lung* (which has wings), the *k'iu-lung* (which has a horn), and the *ch'i-lung* (which is blue and has no horn). Then, there are several other kinds of dragons, but all of them are afraid of iron, the *wang* plant, centipedes, the leaves of the *melia azederach*, and five-colored silk thread, while their principal enemies are tigers and the demons of drought.

<summary>This page explains how Northern Buddhism reimagined Nāgas as benevolent rain-deities and details the diverse characteristics, symbolic omens, and varieties of dragons in Chinese cultural tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Nāgas, Northern Buddhism, rain ceremonies, Chinese dragons, ling, Yang, dragon types, imperial omens, folklore</keywords>

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chinese dragon lore, specifically their dietary preferences, reproductive habits, and their role in weather control, before transitioning into the influence of Chinese and Indian mythology on Japanese dragon legends.</meta>

who devour them. They are fond of beautiful gems, hollow stones with water inside (or the vital spirit of copper), and swallow flesh. Male and female dragons differ in shape. Because the dragon is very lustful, he mates with all kinds of animals; in this way, he produces nine different classes of offspring, which are depicted as ornaments according to their specific natures.

Causing rain is the Chinese dragon's most important function, and mankind compels him to do so through several magical means. This is done especially by making clay images of dragons (and placing them in water), or by throwing poisonous plants or the bones of the tiger (his deadly enemy) into his pools, or by annoying him with terrible noises, or by using utensils adorned with dragons when praying for rain. The dragons are called the "Rain-Masters," and people pray for rain in front of their dens.

<margin>✓</margin>
They transform themselves into old men, beautiful women, and fish, or sometimes assume the shapes of trees and objects, such as swords. They have a pearl under their throats or in their mouths. As for their eggs, these are beautiful stones found in the mountains or at the riverside; water constantly drips from these stones until they split and a small snake appears. In a very short time, this snake grows larger and larger and, in the form of a dragon, ascends to the sky amid thunder, rain, and darkness. Hurricanes and whirlwinds are all attributed to ascending dragons. Their bones are considered to be a very effective medicine, and their saliva is the most precious of perfumes; their shed skins emit a brilliant light. Dragon boats were pleasure vessels used by the Emperors, which had the shape of a dragon and the head of a *yih* bird; quite different, however, are the dragon boats of the water festival on the fifth day of the fifth month, which are probably intended as sympathetic magic to bring rain. As for Buddhism, it introduced legends into China concerning transformations into dragons after death, Dragon Kings, palaces, and so on.

The first chapter of Book II, in which I discussed the original Japanese dragon, mentioned no dates later than the tenth century (*Engishiki*). Even the eighth century adorned its legends with Chinese and Indian features, as we saw in the tale of Toyotama-bime and Hiko-hohodemi. This was very easily done because the Japanese sea and river gods—having the shape of a dragon or a serpent—resembled the Chinese *lung* or the Indian *Nāgas*. It is no wonder that the simple, rain-bestowing Japanese gods of rivers and seas, mountains and valleys, were identified with and replaced by the similar but

<summary>This text details the folklore surrounding the Chinese dragon's habits, transformations, and role as a "Rain-Master," as well as the historical merging of Japanese river gods with Chinese and Indian dragon myths.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese dragon, Rain-Masters, dragon eggs, dragon boats, Buddhism, Japanese mythology, Toyotama-bime, Nāgas, transformation lore</keywords>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Japanese dragon lore, transitioning from the description of dragon-shaped vessels to the influence of Chinese and Indian water deities on indigenous Japanese beliefs.</meta>

...more fantastic Chinese and Indian gods of water and rain. The "water-fathers" (*mizuchi*), dragon-shaped river-gods who—much like Chinese dragons—obstructed men during the construction of embankments but were appeased with human sacrifices rather than being driven away by iron as in China, soon had to make way for the Rain-masters and Dragon-kings of the West. Gradually, foreign elements were added to the ancient legends, and their original form became hardly recognizable.

The second chapter shows how all the Chinese concepts regarding the appearance of dragons and dragon-horses as omens were adopted by the Japanese and preserved by them from the ninth century until the nineteenth.

In the third chapter, the dragon's primary function is discussed: the granting of rain to mankind. Among the eighty-five Shinto shrines to which messengers were dispatched by the Imperial Court in times of drought, there were many dragon-shaped river-deities. Regarding the offerings made to the Shinto river-gods to obtain rain or to cause them to stop an excessive supply of heavenly water, these consisted of hemp and fiber, and black, white, or red horses (the latter used only for stopping rain). Yet, even the Emperors as early as the eighth century no longer fully believed in the power of these gods, for at the same time Buddhist rites were being performed in the three great temples of Nara. In the ninth century, especially, Buddhist priests gained more and more influence in this regard as well, and the famous "Sacred Spring Park" in Kyoto became their special territory for praying for rain. Kōbō Daishi proclaimed that the pond in this park was inhabited by an Indian dragon, and sutras were recited on its banks by crowds of monks (*bonzes*), sometimes to pray to the Dragon King and sometimes to threaten him with persecution by his mortal enemy, the Garuda. However, if they had no success, the ancient river-gods enjoyed a temporary triumph and were elevated to higher ranks. But their glory was short-lived, for soon the powerful foreign "invaders" prevailed once more. The blessing of rain was expected either from the Chinese dragon—who had to be awakened by sounding bells and drums and by singing and dancing on a dragon boat on the pond in the Sacred Spring Park (or by being deprived of his element, water)—or from the Indian Naga-king. The clever monk Kūkai (Kōbō Daishi) knew how to defeat his opponents, including both the Shintoists and his rivals among the Buddhist priests. This was experienced by the most powerful of his colleagues, Shūbin, the abbot of the "Western Monastery." In addition to prayers,

<summary>This page details the displacement of indigenous Japanese river-gods (mizuchi) by Chinese and Indian dragon myths and describes the evolution of rain-making rituals. It highlights the competition between Shinto traditions and the rising influence of Buddhist monks like Kōbō Daishi in the Imperial Court.</summary>
<keywords>mizuchi, Shinto, Buddhism, Kōbō Daishi, Kūkai, Shūbin, rain-making, Sacred Spring Park, Kyoto, Nara, Dragon-king, Garuda, Naga-king</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of rainmaking rituals in Japan, contrasting Shinto, Buddhist, and Chinese traditions, and begins an overview of legends involving Indian Nāgas (Dragon-kings).</meta>

Through incantations and the recital of sutras, Kūkai—who strove to spread his doctrine through the extraordinarily impressive art of rainmaking—used a magical image of a dragon (reminiscent of the clay dragons used by the Chinese). His success was marvelous.

Furthermore, we have seen how during the thirteenth century, in times of drought, the Buddhist “Five Dragons Festival” was celebrated in that same Sacred Spring Park or elsewhere. Alternatively, sutras were recited before the Dragon-hole on Mount Murōbu in Yamato to compel the Dragon-king who lived there to provide rain. The remarkable fact that a *Buddhist* priest was said to have built a *Shinto* shrine for the Indian dragon on this spot seems to indicate that the *Nāga* had replaced a Shinto dragon, a mountain god believed to have lived in the hole since ancient times. In the same century, the Emperors still offered horses to the famous rain-gods of *Nibu* (the “Rain-Master”) and *Kibune*: white ones to request rain, and red ones to stop it. Even the Court officials themselves went to the Sacred Spring Park and prayed to the “Sea-dragon-king,” while simultaneously performing “sympathetic magic” by sprinkling water on the stones near the pond. Many miracles were performed by Buddhist priests in forcing the dragons to obey their will. In later times, however, especially in the eighteenth century, we see Chinese methods of rainmaking gaining ground again. The Chinese concept—arousing the anger of these rain-gods by making noise or by throwing iron utensils, metal shavings, or filthy objects into their ponds to make them ascend and produce rain—differed from the *Shinto* practice of prayer and offerings to river-gods, as well as from the *Buddhist* method of persuading or forcing dragons to benefit humanity with abundant rain. As I noted earlier,<note>Book II, Ch. III, § 13, p. 178.</note> these *Chinese* methods, which became dominant in later centuries, are still prevalent among Japanese country folk today.

The fourth chapter presented Japanese legends concerning *Indian Nāgas* (Dragon-kings). Since these Indian tales reached *Nippon* via *China* and *Korea*, it is logical that their Japanese versions displayed many Chinese features. Among the eight Great Dragon-kings, *Sāgara*, who was believed to live in a magnificent palace at the bottom of the sea, is the one most frequently mentioned. Like other Dragon-kings, he possesses the “Precious pearl that grants all desires” (*cintāmaṇi*). During storms, sailors tried

<meta>Digitized by Microsoft®</meta>

<summary>This page describes the historical evolution of rainmaking in Japan, highlighting how Buddhist and Chinese practices gradually integrated with or replaced indigenous Shinto dragon worship. It also introduces the Indian Nāga Sāgara and his wish-granting pearl as a key figure in Japanese dragon lore.</summary>
<keywords>Kūkai, rainmaking, Five Dragons Festival, Mount Murōbu, Nāga, Shinto, Nibu, Kibune, Sāgara, cintāmaṇi, sympathetic magic, religious syncretism</keywords>

--- Page 252 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Dragon-kings and their influence on Japanese religious traditions, specifically focusing on their identification with mountain ponds, Buddhist relics, and ancient Shinto deities.</meta>

to pacify the Dragon-kings by throwing all kinds of precious objects into the sea; they succeeded if the object these water-gods desired was offered in time. Ponds, especially mountain ponds, were very often believed to be the homes of Dragon-kings, who in many cases had likely replaced ancient Japanese dragon-shaped gods. Sometimes one of the eight kings took the form of a famous Buddhist high priest, or a man's spirit became a dragon-god. The temple bell of Miidera is said to have been obtained by Tawara Tōda from a Dragon-palace. Azure dragons (a Chinese feature) were often said to have appeared during the founding of Buddhist temples and to have served as the guardian gods of these shrines ¹. Sometimes dragon relics—for example, a few scales or a tooth—were preserved among the treasures of a Buddhist sanctuary. Finally, eight- and nine-headed dragons were described as the inhabitants of mountain lakes, sometimes as reincarnations of Buddhist priests; and until the Restoration, Buddhist priests made offerings of rice to the dragons of some of those lakes.

The powerful influence of Indian and Chinese ideas on this subject upon the Japanese mind is also shown by how these concepts were applied to ancient Shinto gods. In Chapter V, some examples of this were given, found in books from the thirteenth and eighteenth centuries. In the former, the eight-headed serpent called Yamato no orochi (slain by Susanowo), as well as the tragic young Emperor Antoku (who drowned in the Battle of Dan-no-ura in 1185 and whose spirit is said to be the Shinto god Suitengū), are identified with the goddess of Itsukushima, the daughter of the Dragon-king Sāgara! And the precious Kusanagi sword, found in the tail of the eight-headed serpent, belonged to this king’s Dragon-palace; or, according to another legend, it was carefully guarded by a Dragon-king and returned to the Atsuta shrine, from where it had been stolen. The Thunder-god, who according to an old legend was caught by Sukaru, was called a "Dragon-king" by the author of the *Gempei seisuiki* (thirteenth century); this was all the more plausible because the version in the *Nihongi* spoke of a huge serpent. Furthermore, several old Shinto shrines where snake- or dragon-shaped gods were likely worshiped since ancient times were, by the eighteenth century, considered to have connections with Chinese or Indian

<summary>This text describes how Indian and Chinese dragon myths were integrated into Japanese Buddhism and Shintoism, identifying local deities and legendary figures with Dragon-kings. It highlights the transformation of ancient Japanese spirits into dragon-gods and the preservation of dragon-related relics in temples.</summary>
<keywords>Dragon-kings, Buddhism, Shinto, Yamato no orochi, Emperor Antoku, Kusanagi sword, Miidera, Azure dragons, syncretism, Japanese folklore</keywords>

--- Page 253 ---
<meta>This page concludes the study of dragons in Far Eastern folklore, transitioning from the identification of Shinto deities with dragon-kings to the specific phenomena of dragon-lanterns, dragon's eggs, and the influence of Chinese and Indian traditions on Japanese culture.</meta>

dragons, and even old tree-spirits in snake form were called dragons and said to cause thunderstorms.

The *Dragon-lantern*, discussed in the sixth chapter, was not mentioned in works dating before the fourteenth century. It always rose from the sea and was mostly a sign of a dragon-shaped sea-god’s protection of and reverence toward a Buddhist temple or, in a few cases, a Shintō sanctuary. The Chinese “azure dragon” was often mentioned in these tales, and sometimes it was said to have been seen carrying the lantern, which nearly always descended upon some old pine tree standing near the shrine and hung between its branches. These “dragon-lantern pine trees” remind us of the Chinese ideas of old trees producing *ignes fatui* <note>phosphorescent lights or will-o'-the-wisps</note>.

The “Dragon’s eggs”—beautiful stones picked up in the mountains from which water constantly dripped and which, for this reason, were often used as ink-stones—were dangerous treasures indeed. For sooner or later they split, and a little snake crept out of them; it increased in size within a few minutes and finally ascended to the sky as a dragon, breaking through the roof and causing a terrible thunderstorm. Book I, Chapter III, § 16, in connection with Book II, Chapter VII, have shown that this is a Chinese conception introduced into Japan, where it was prevalent from the sixteenth century down to the nineteenth.

The idea of whirlwinds and waterspouts being caused by ascending dragons winding their way to heaven was also very popular. We find this in both China and Japan, and in the latter country especially from the seventeenth century until the present day. The Japanese name “*tatsu-maki*” perhaps indicates that it was not borrowed from China; but on the other hand, the fact that we did not find it mentioned in works before the seventeenth century leads me to think that the general inclination of these later ages toward Chinese conceptions—which we observed also in the methods of making rain—may have caused the spreading of this idea as well.

Finally, in the ninth chapter, the geographical names were evidence that the original Japanese dragon was worshiped mostly in Central Japan, and of the popularity of the Chinese and Indian dragons throughout the Empire. The large number of names of Buddhist temples and priests connected with the Indian dragon showed the important part played by the Nāga in Japanese Buddhism.

With this, I conclude this treatise on the dragon in the Far East, in the hope that it may throw light upon its complex nature as an Indian, Chinese, and Japanese god of water, thunder, rain, and wind.
